# Book One

# Chapter One — The Stars That Fell

*I was happy. Whatever else is said of that house and the two who kept it — and I have heard it said every way the world can say a thing — no one takes that from the boy in the loft. I was happy, and I was loved, and both were true the whole time, under everything else that was true in that house.*

Before anything else, there was the fire.

It lived in a hole in the floor, down where the wind could not find it. The wind wanted the fire. Kael knew this because the wind came to the door every night and pushed, and pushed, and the door held, and the fire stayed small and orange and his.

The fire was not allowed to be big. That was one of the things that were true, like snow being cold, like the goat hating him. Fire lived in its hole and was fed small pieces and it never, ever went outside. Once he asked why, and his father looked at him for a while, the way he did, and said, "Because outside belongs to the wind."

That was a good answer. Kael thought about it often.

The house had one room and a loft and the fire-hole and the door that held. In the room there was the table his father had made, and the beds, and the chest of woolens, and the wall where the tools hung in their places. Every tool had a place. If Kael took one down and put it back crooked, his father would cross the room later, not even looking, and make it straight. His father's hands did things by themselves. They straightened tools and they took stones out of the path and once, when a slate slid off the roof in a storm, his father's hand went up and the slate was in it, and his father kept talking to the goat the whole time, because the goat was upset about the storm.

His mother's hands were quieter. They were the hands that undid knots.

His mother sang while she worked, but not words. His father said words were extra. His mother's songs went up at the ends like questions, and sometimes a bird outside would answer, and his mother would stop and listen with her whole body, and then laugh, and sing the answer back. Kael did not know you could talk to birds. Then he did know, and it was one more true thing: snow is cold, fire lives in its hole, his mother speaks bird.

His father called his mother Neris. It sounded like a wave going back down the beach. His mother called his father Vask. It sounded like a stone put down on another stone. Kael tried both names in his mouth when he was alone with the goat. The goat did not care.

In the day there was the mountain. The mountain was everything the door opened onto: the path, and the split rock the path was not allowed past, and the pens, and the high meadow where the wind lived when it was not at the door. The mountain went up behind the house until it turned white, and it went down in front of the house until it turned blue, and the blue part was the sea, and the sea was the biggest floor in the world. Boats walked on it. They were small and slow, and they leaned, and Kael's mother counted them.

She counted them in the morning and she counted them at dusk. She counted them the way Kael counted his knuckles, not because the number changed, but because it was good to know it. Some evenings there were three boats, and some evenings there was one, and some evenings there were none at all, and his mother stood in the door and counted the none, taking just as long about it.

Nights were for inside. That was true the way winter was true. But one night — and this was the beginning of everything, though he did not know it then — one night his mother woke him and wrapped him in the big wool and carried him out into the dark, and the dark was enormous and mild, and she stood with him at the flat rock and said, "Look."

He looked up, because up was where looking went at night. The stars were out. He knew stars.

"No," his mother said, and turned his chin down.

And there, far below, at the bottom of the mountain, at the edge of the biggest floor in the world, was a little spill of stars. They lay in a heap where the land met the sea. They were warmer than the sky stars. They flickered like the fire flickered. Some of them were doubled in the water, and the doubled ones moved.

Kael understood at once, the way he understood most things, completely and wrong.

"They fell down," he said.

His mother did not say anything.

"The stars fell down the mountain," he said, "and they landed there, in the corner. By the water." It made sense. Things that fell on the mountain always ended up at the bottom. His ball. The slate. Him, once, and his father's hand had been there before the ground was, the same hand that caught slates.

"Can I touch them?"

"No," his mother said.

"When can I touch them?"

His mother was quiet for a time. Under the big wool her heart was going. He could feel it against his back, a small hurrying thing, though her voice when it came was slow as always.

"When the sea freezes," she said. "When the sea freezes over, all the way, so you could walk on it like a floor. Then."

Kael looked at the fallen stars for a long time. One of them went out while he watched, and he felt it go out in his own chest, a little cold spot.

"Is the sea cold?" he asked.

"Very cold."

"Then it will freeze," he said, satisfied. Cold things froze. The water in the goat's pail froze. This was simply a bigger pail.

His mother made a sound that was almost her laugh and not quite, and carried him back inside, and the door held, and the fire was small and orange in its hole, and that was the night the waiting started.

He waited well. Waiting was a thing his father taught, like carrying wood was a thing his father taught: you did it with your whole body and you did not spill any. All that winter, Kael went out in the mornings to test the world. He put his hand in the snow and counted how long. He broke the ice on the pail and held the broken piece up to the sun. And he looked down the mountain at the sea, the whole cold shining size of it, and it moved, and moved, and would not be a floor.

"Not yet," his father said, splitting wood. His father split wood in one stroke, always. Crack. "Not this winter."

"Next winter?"

"Perhaps." Crack.

Kael nodded and went to tell the goat.

At night, sometimes, he woke in the loft and heard nothing — the wind gone home, the fire asleep in its ash — and in the nothing he would hear his mother, downstairs, moving from the window to the door. Not going anywhere. Just standing at the window, and then, after a while, standing at the door. He never wondered what she was doing, because he knew. She was counting the boats.

There were no boats at night. She counted them anyway. It was good to know the number.

And far down the mountain the fallen stars burned in their corner, low and warm and doubled in the black water, and Kael lay in the loft with his hands behind his head, four years old, rich with certainty, waiting for the sea to freeze.


---

# Chapter Two — Part of the Mountain

Some things were true about the mountain, and you did not ask about true things. You asked about other things — why the goat's eyes went sideways, why honey didn't freeze — and his mother would answer until his head was full. But true things were not for asking. True things were the floor you asked from.

The path ended at the split rock. That was one. The path went down from the house through the pens and along the shoulder of the mountain, and at the split rock it ended, the way the roof ended at its edge. There was more ground past the rock. There was more air past the roof. That was not the same as there being more path.

Fire lived in its hole. That was two. Kael had known that one his whole life.

And when a boat turned toward the island, there was the game. That was three.

The game was the only thing his father played. His mother played many things — the counting game, the herb game, the game where she sang a bird wrong on purpose and they listened to it get angry — but his father played one game, and it was the best game, because his father played it too. Not the way his mother played, letting Kael win a little, laughing when she lost. His father played beside him, the whole way, every time. Whatever Kael was asked to be, his father was next to him being it longer.

The game went like this. His father made the sign — two fingers dropped, like something small being set down — and wherever you were, you stopped being Kael. You became part of the mountain. A stone was good. A stump was good. A shadow at the foot of the woodpile was very good. Parts of the mountain do not itch. Parts of the mountain do not look at the interesting thing going by. Parts of the mountain breathe so slow and small that a bird landing on them does not know it has landed on anything that eats.

When he was little, the game was little. His mother would hunt for him around the pens, calling his name in a voice that was pretend-worried, and he would be a stone until his laugh spilled out of him, and being found was warm like bread. That was the game when he was little, the same way his boots when he was little were boots he now could not get his feet into. The game grew the way clothes grew.

His father always won. This was another true thing. Kael had never seen his father lose at anything, but the game was the only thing they played, so it was the only place you could watch the winning happen. It happened like this: nothing. His father set himself down beside Kael and became nothing at all, and stayed nothing, for as long as the game lasted, and then stood up out of the nothing without a single stiff step, as if being nothing cost him nothing. Kael had decided that when he was big, it would cost him nothing too. He was working on it.

The day of the long game was a white-sky day at the end of summer.

He saw the boat before his father said anything, and was proud of seeing it, and the pride was the last loose thing he felt for a long time. It was a wide boat with six oars, and it was doing the thing that made a boat matter: it was not fishing. Kael knew fishing the way he knew the goat. Fishing boats worked. They crawled along their lines, they stopped and fussed, they leaned when the nets came up. This boat did not work. It came along the coast slow and even, and then the oars went up out of the water all together, like a beetle folding its legs, and it just stayed. A man at the front held a dark stick up to his face, pointed at the mountain.

Two fingers dropped beside him.

Kael was at the corner of the pens. He went down along the fence post and became the bottom of it, where the shadow pooled, knees to chest, hands quiet, face down but eyes up, the way you were taught: parts of the mountain do not show the whites of their eyes. His father was four steps away and then there was no father, only a shape by the woodpile that the eye slid off. Kael knew exactly where he was and still had to tell himself the shape was him.

The boat stayed.

He counted his breaths, long ones, the size his father's hand had taught his chest. He counted to the end of his numbers and folded the count and started again. The sun came through the white sky as a warm pressure on his left side, and then slowly it was on the back of his neck, which meant it was moving, which meant a long time was passing, and the boat stayed.

An itch lit on his ankle and asked, and asked, and he did not answer it, and after a while it stopped asking, which was a thing itches did if you were not their friend.

Then his leg began. Not an itch. A wire. A hot wire in the folded muscle, tightening as if a hand were winding it, and this one did not stop asking. Parts of the mountain do not have legs, he told himself. This is not a leg. This is the bottom of a fence post. But the wire wound, and his breath wanted to come up short and fast, and he made it stay long, and his eyes went hot and spilled — but that was allowed, or not allowed, but not counted, because water is not movement. Tears were like rain. Rain fell on stones and the stones were still stones. He let his face rain without letting his face move, because the face is movement too, the face is the loudest thing you own.

His father was a shape by the woodpile. He did not come. He did not whisper that it was almost over. He was nothing, beside a fence post that was trying so hard to be nothing, and he let it try.

The sun was on Kael's right side now. The boat had put its oars down twice and moved a little and folded them again. His whole leg was made of wire. He needed to make water and then, after a forever of holding that too, he could not anymore, and the warm went through his trousers and down, and the shame of it came up his throat like a stone he could not cry out around, because crying out is movement, shame is movement, everything he was, was movement, held.

His father's eyes came to him once. Kael felt them the way you feel the sun through cloth. There was nothing in them that was angry. There was nothing in them that was anything. They went back to the sea.

Water is not movement, he told the wire. Rain falls on stones.

The oars came down. The boat unfolded, turned its nose slow along the coast, and walked away on its six legs, and Kael's whole body rose toward the ending like a fish toward air — and no fingers dropped. The shape by the woodpile stayed a shape. Because the game did not end when the boat went. The game ended when his father said, and his father said it with the sky going grey and the first bat out and stitching over the pens:

"Done."

Kael's legs did not work. He got up the way the newborn goats got up, in pieces, and went down, and his father stood near him — close enough to catch him, and did not catch him — while the legs remembered. Then they walked up to the house, Kael in his cold wet trousers with his steps coming back one at a time, his father beside him carrying nothing, offering nothing, matching his pace exactly.

At the door his father said, "Again tomorrow."

That was all. It was nearly praise. *Again* meant the game was not over for him, that he had not been taken out of it, and Kael held that the way he had held the stillness, with everything.

His mother was in the doorway and had been, he understood, for some part of the afternoon — from the door you could see the pens, and the woodpile, and the sea. She said nothing. One of her hands was up on the doorframe, the knuckles pale, and Kael thought she was holding the door up, and thought no more about it. She took him in and stripped the cold trousers off him and washed him with water warmed at the fire, all without a word, not the washing-words she usually had, and there was honey in his milk at supper. There was not usually honey.

In the loft that night he did his numbers. Last summer, on the short game with the two fishing boats, he had spilled — moved his whole arm at a wasp — before he had even filled one count. Today he had filled his count more times than he could keep, and the only things that had escaped him were water, and water was not movement. He was getting bigger inside, where the game was played. Nobody had said so. Nobody had to. *Again tomorrow.*

He fell asleep proud, the way you fall asleep after winning, with his leg still humming its wire-song under the blanket.

He hoped the boat would come back. He was ready to be nothing for as long as it took.


---

# Chapter Three — Chores

The day had two halves, like the mountain. The mountain had a sun side and a shade side, and the line between them moved but was always somewhere. The morning belonged to his father. The afternoon belonged to his mother. Kael was the line that moved between them.

He was eight, and he had theories about most things now. The theories came out of questions, and the questions never stopped; they hatched out of each other like flies. His mother said his questions had questions. A day with a new thing in it was a better day than a day without, and the empty days made him restless in a way he had no proper name for. It was an itch, but not on the skin. Skin itches he could out-wait; he had out-waited itches beside a fence post for half a day. This one you could not out-wait. It just moved deeper in and kept asking.

His father was already a shape by the cold hearth however early Kael came down the ladder, and they went out into the dark together, because the mountain in the dark was a different mountain and you had to know both of them. They ran the scree first. You could not run scree by looking — by the time the eye told the foot, the foot had already decided — so you ran it in the dark until the foot stopped asking the eye at all. Kael had fallen on the scree more times than he had numbers for. His father had watched him fall from two steps away, every time, and never once been falling himself.

After the scree there was wood, and water, and the snares. The snares were where the knife lived. Kael had his own now, a short one, and the first true thing about the knife was that the knife was the smallest part of the lesson. The lesson was everything the cutting was not: where the other hand lived while the blade worked, what the feet were doing under you, what your eyes kept doing — going up, going out, coming back — because a boy with his whole face in his work had stopped being on the mountain, and you were never to stop being on the mountain. His father taught the way he split wood. One correction, one stroke. He would move Kael's elbow in by the width of two fingers and say nothing. The silence was not empty. The silence meant: again, and this time it is yours.

That morning there was a hare, stiff in the dawn snare, and Kael skinned it on the flat stone while the light came up grey out of the sea. He was good at it now. He knew he was good at it, and the knowing was the mistake, because the hide gave all at once where he expected it to hold, and the blade skated off the breastbone toward the soft of his own wrist —

— and stopped. His wrist was inside his father's hand. He had not seen the hand leave the woodpile. He had not seen his father at the woodpile. The hand was simply there first, the way it had been there before the ground was, years ago, the way it had been under the slate. It held his wrist the exact time it took the blade's want to go out of it. Then it put his wrist back where it had been, and his father said, "Elbow," and moved it in by the width of two fingers, and that was all. The correction was the same size as every other correction. Kael finished the hare with his heart going loud and his hands going quiet, and was proud of the quiet hands.

His father was fast the way weather was fast. You did not see it start. You were just in it.

Later, at the trough, his father stripped to the waist to wash, and Kael read his back the way he read the goat paths. He knew the marks by heart: the long pale seam under the left shoulder, like a place where his father had been let out to grow; the round puckered one with a brother on the front, so that something had gone in and kept going; the short ones on the forearms, many, all the same length, all the same slant, like tallies. Once, this summer, he had asked what work made those, because work made all marks — the rope its stripe, the scythe its notch.

His father had wrung the water out of the cloth before answering. "A bad employer."

Kael had not known the word, and had it explained: someone you work for. "Did you leave?" he'd asked, and his father had said, "Yes," and Kael had said, "Good," and gone back to his rope, and so had missed the thing that crossed his father's face, having no reason to look for it. The marks went on the list of true things. Snow was cold. Fire lived in its hole. His father had worked for a bad employer, and left.

At noon they ate, and the day crossed its line, and he was his mother's.

His mother's school left no marks, and Kael could not have said why it was harder. It was the school where you were never told whether you were done. In the afternoons the table became wherever she said it was — a market, a ship's deck, a garden — and she spoke to him in one of the languages, and the rule was that the language was the only door: if you wanted anything, even water, you knocked on that door or went without. The bird language had been the first, when he was small, but there was the one that clicked and cracked like stones, for market days, and the soft one that ran downhill all its length, that she was strictest in, and sang in sometimes at the ends of afternoons, low, when she thought the lesson was over. He knew the languages the way he knew paths: not as things learned but as places he could go. He had asked once who else spoke the stone-click one, and she had said, "Traders," and the story had a shut door in it.

History was the smallest of her schools, and the only one he was hungry in. The others she poured; history she rationed. It came in scraps, never on asking — a war mentioned sideways while her hands worked, a city with towers she had seen once and would not say when, how a king was a kind of weather that happened to countries. The scraps were bright and exact and did not touch each other, and between them the world down there stayed dark. There were rooms in her stories she walked him around the outside of, and by eight he knew the shut doors the way he knew the split rock: you could stand near them. You did not knock. He had a theory, which was that she was saving the rest, the way you saved the honey.

There was far-counting, his own name for it: counting things you could not walk to. The day she showed him that a stick's shadow and a crag's shadow were the same story told in two sizes was one of the great days of his life; he had spent the rest of that summer taking the height of everything that would hold still — the crag, the tall pine, the house, the goat (the goat would not hold still), his mother, who stood for it in the yard with her arms out, laughing, while he paced off her shadow like a surveyor of small countries. "Well?" she would say, arms out. "Taller than the door. Not by much." "Then measure the not-by-much," and she held still until he had. He could find himself by the stars now, which she said mattered more than finding north, though he had never yet been anywhere else to be.

And there was the standing game, about why things stood and why they fell, and it was the school his hands liked best. In the spring a stone had come off the slope into the gate of the upper pen, a stone with the weight of the house in it. His father had gone for the iron bar, and his mother had come out and said, "No. It's his." And it had been his: his all morning, while they went about their work and did not help. He had cried at it once, quietly, and stopped, because crying was asking and there was no one to ask. Then a pole, and a log under the pole — and where the log sat was the whole of the secret, he found out by doing it wrong; the pole did not care how strong you were, only where you stood on it. By noon the stone was out of the gate, walked over the ground in stiff little tips and shoves by a boy who came up to its shoulder. His mother watched the last of it from the doorway and said, "Everything heavy is lazy. Remember that," and went back in. He had the feeling from that morning filed where he could reach it: the mountain was stronger than everyone, stronger than his father even, and the mountain could be tricked.

That afternoon was an herb afternoon. The basket had been out in the wet wood with her since first light, and she turned it out on the scrubbed boards: the good greens, the roots, and, on a cloth of their own, the mushrooms.

The herb game had grown teeth over the years, the way the stillness game had. When he was five it had been smells and rhymes. Now she set the caps out in pairs, each beside its shadow, the eater beside the killer, and went through them with him, her finger moving, her voice even. This one feeds you. This one, its twin, stops a heart. Not the cap — the cap lies. Look under. Look at the collar on the stem. Look at what the flesh does when you break it, what color it turns, slow or quick. Ask it questions with the vinegar, with the salt, with a hot iron — things that cannot lie make the best witnesses. You learn them both, the eater and the killer, one beside the other, so you never pick wrong.

Then she cleared them all but two. The two were the same mushroom twice — brown caps, dry, a little shine; the same smell when she broke the edge of each, or almost: one had a sweetness far down under it, like something too polite. She set the pot on its hook, and put the onions in, and said, without turning around: "One of them goes in. Choose."

And she went on with the onions.

That was the whole of it. She did not watch him choose. That was the part that made his chest go tight, tighter this year than last, because he understood more this year than last: the pot was the pot they would all eat from, and she trusted the lesson all the way, further than he wanted to be trusted. He took the caps to the window light. He turned them under. He broke the flesh and watched what the color did, and smelled again for the politeness, and stood there long past the point where he was sure, because sure was the feeling that had skated the knife that morning. He put one cap on the cloth by the pot and the other in the slop pail, and his mother added his choice to the onions without looking at it, and sang, later, in the downhill language, while the pot worked.

Supper was the answer. They ate, and the fire sat small and orange in its hole, and nobody died, and nobody remarked on it, because you did not get remarks for doing what was expected. He was eight. Other children — down there, wherever children were — must be learning the same things from their own mothers, he supposed. How else would anyone live to be big?

In the loft that night he did his numbers. Not counting-numbers; the list. The scree in the dark, both legs sure. A hare out of its skin in the time the light took to come up. Nothing beside a fence post for as long as asked — longer than the asking, now. Four languages like paths. The killer told from the eater by what the broken flesh confessed. The height of anything from its shadow, himself from the stars, a stone the weight of the house tricked out of a gate. And still the split rock, and still the sea not frozen.

The wind came to the door and pushed, and the door held.

The best thing about the list was that it was not finished. He could feel the unfinished part, the way you feel the dark past a lamp — all the things not yet known, waiting. And when he tried to feel where they were, the new things, they were not up the mountain, where he had been everywhere. They were down. Down at the languages' far ends, down in the shut rooms of his mother's stories, down where the fallen stars burned in their corner, low and warm, the way they had burned all his life.

He could wait. He was very good at waiting. Everyone he loved had made sure of it.


---

# Chapter Four — The Visitor

The boat came at midmorning, and no fingers dropped.

Kael saw it from the upper pen: a small boat, one sail the color of old canvas, cutting a line for the island the way no boat was permitted to cut a line for the island. He was nine, and the rules lived in his body now, deeper than deciding — his knees were already bending toward the fence post, his breath already going long — and he looked to his father for the sign, and his father was leaning on the gate, watching the boat come, the way you watch weather you know.

"Go wash," his father said. "Both hands. Comb your hair."

Kael stood very still, and not in the game way. Somewhere a floor he had stood on his whole life moved half a finger's width.

He had theories about the exemption before he reached the trough — the game was for boats that watched, and this boat sailed like a sentence plainly spoken — and he knew, scrubbing his knuckles, that they were the kind of good that wrong theories were.

The house, when he came in, had changed the way the light changes before snow. His mother had the good bread out, the one with the seeds, that rose on feast days and no other days, and it had risen, which meant she had started it last night, which meant she had known last night. His father had put on his other shirt. His father's shoulders had come down from wherever they usually lived. His mother was singing, not bird, not downhill, just humming, loose, the way the goat wandered when the gate was open.

They were not afraid. That was the wrongness. He had no word for how he knew that a stranger should have made them afraid, but he knew it. A stranger was coming up the mountain, and the two of them had gone soft as summer, and the door stood open.

The man who came up the path was old the way the split rock was old — worn to what mattered. Tall, gone lean, grey all the way through, in a coat too plain to be a villager's and too well-made to be a villager's, carrying a wrapped bundle on a strap. He walked with a scholar's stoop that sat on him the way a borrowed tool sits in the hand, and the wrists coming out of the sleeves were too thick for a man whose work was paper; Kael filed him with the boat that did not fish. Then the man stopped in the yard and looked at Kael for a long, unhurried moment, the way Kael's mother looked at the sea.

"So," the man said. "You're the reason the honey's always gone."

His father laughed. Kael heard it and filed it and could not file it: a short, real laugh, spent like coin, and his father did not spend. "He counts the jar," his father said, "so mind your spoon."

His mother came out wiping her hands and the man said something to her in the soft downhill language, the strict one, and she answered in it, easily, three sentences with a smile inside them, and the man bowed his head a little.

"This is the master," his mother said. "He has come before. You were on the mountain."

You were on the mountain. Four words, given like bread, and Kael took them and began, without wanting to, to count. The days he had been sent up with his father before light for no season's reason. The days of the far snares, the days of the high meadow. The days he had come home and there was paper in the house that had not been there — the books. The books came from the master. The books had always come from the master. The best things he owned had been carried up this path in that bundle by this man, over years, on days arranged so that Kael would not be standing in this yard, and no one had ever lied to him about it, because he had never known to ask.

The wrongness of that went by him fast and quiet, like something small running the treeline, seen and gone.

Inside, the bundle opened. Two books — one fat, one thin — and a roll of real paper, and a stick of ink, and under those, wrapped separately, a cord of dark silk tied in a knot Kael had never met before. The master set the knot on the table between them like a plate of food.

"Your mother says you like questions," the master said. "I brought one."

The fat book was about engines of water — wheels and screws and the ways a river could be made to work — and Kael wanted it so much his hands ached. But the knot was in front of him, and the knot was now, so he took it up, and while he turned it the master asked him things. Not teaching-things. The master's questions were doors with nothing written on them.

"What is the goat afraid of?"

Kael considered it, fingers working. "Nothing that's there," he said. "She spooks at the shadow and sleeps through the storm. Her fear has bad eyes."

The master put the answer away somewhere, and asked on. "And if you could ask the sea one question, and be answered. What would you ask?"

No one had ever asked Kael what he would ask. The itch, the deep one that could not be out-waited, came up under his ribs and sat there like a second heart — and the answer was already there, because it was the oldest question he owned.

"When it will freeze," he said. "All the way over, so you could walk on it like a floor. I test it every winter. The pail freezes. The sea just moves." And then he was talking, he could hear himself doing it and could not find a reason to stop, and his mother went on slicing the bread, evenly, all the way through his answer, and the master listened the way he looked, unhurried, and Kael was in it the way you are in warm water.

And all the time his fingers worked the knot, which was a liar's knot — it showed you three ways in and all of them tightened it — and somewhere in the middle of saying what he would ask the sea, his fingers stopped believing the knot. It was not thinking; thinking he would have noticed. It was the way his feet were on scree in the dark: the hand knew before the eye told it. There, said his fingers, and he felt the little architecture of it, the one loop that carried all the others, the lazy place — everything heavy is lazy — and he drew it, and the knot came open all at once, soft as a mouth, a whole breath before his fingers could have reached the loop they pulled.

The room went still.

Kael did not notice the room. He was nine and the knot was open and he was mid-sentence about the sea. He noticed only, in the way you notice a door close somewhere in a house, that the master had set his cup down without drinking, and that his mother's hand had come to rest on the table, flat, and that his father was looking at his mother, and none of it was anything, and he kept talking.

Then his mother said there was wood that wanted splitting, which was true — wood always wanted splitting — and Kael went out, and the door shut behind him, and he stood in the yard holding the axe and did not split anything.

Because the voices through the wall had changed shape.

Not the words. The wall kept the words. But sound has bones, and he knew his parents' voices the way he knew their footsteps — his mother's that went up at the ends, his father's that put things down — and through the wall their voices were doing neither. They were quick, and flat, and even, all three voices taking turns without stepping on each other, like drill. Strangers' voices, in his house, wearing his parents' throats.

The axe stood against the block. Kael went down beside the woodpile, into the shadow at the foot of it, knees to chest, hands quiet, breath long — and became part of the house. It was the first time he had set himself down inside the game unasked. The stillness came to him like a friend, and he pointed it, all of it, at the wall, and the wall gave him nothing but shapes, and he stayed anyway, until the shapes stopped.

He was splitting wood, honestly and loudly, by the time the door opened.

The master left in the grey of the afternoon. At the yard's edge he stopped by Kael and said, "Read the fat one first. The thin one will still be true when you're older." And then, to no one: "He unties things." And went down the path.

Supper was quiet. The good bread was eaten. Everything was the same, and Kael lay in the loft doing his numbers and could not find the list, because the day would not file. The books were the master's. The absences were arranged. His parents had a third voice. A knot had opened before his fingers — he had already stopped believing that one; fingers were fast, that was all; his father's hands did things by themselves, why not his.

He woke deep in the night to the sound of his parents arguing in a language he did not have.

He knew all four of his languages the way he knew paths, and this was no path of his. It was low and fast and it had edges, and it was coming up through the floorboards of the loft in hard little runs — his mother the more, his father the less, the same three sounds coming back and back like a knot that would not give. He moved to the ladder without deciding to, one board at a time, breath long — and the third board creaked.

The house went silent the way a fire goes out.

Kael lay where he was, part of the loft, part of the dark, for a hundred long breaths. The silence held. Then, below, ordinary sounds: the shift of the chair, the small iron noise of the fire being settled for the night. He slid back to his blankets and lay with his eyes open, and after a long time he came down, water was a true reason, everyone was allowed water — and saw her.

His mother sat at the window in the dark with her back straight and her face to the sea, and across her knees, unsheathed, lay a blade he had never been shown, long and thin and older than the tools on the wall, and her hands rested on it the way her hands rested on bread dough, at home.

"Why do you have that?" Kael whispered.

"It keeps the cold out," his mother said, not turning.

He took his water and went back up. It was a strange answer, and he kept it, because it was hers. Blades kept the cold out. Mothers sat facing the sea.

In the morning his mother was at the table as always, and the blade was nowhere, as always, and when the chores were done she did not set the table to be a market or a garden. She stood in the doorway with her shawl on and said, "Leave the basket. Come."

She took him up, past the pens, to the flat rock where she had once turned his chin down to show him the fallen stars. The morning was cold and clean. She sat, and sat him in front of her, facing the sea, and for a while she said nothing at all, and the itch in him climbed and climbed and he held it.

"You untied the master's knot," she said at last. "Before your fingers did."

Kael began to say that fingers were fast, and stopped, because her voice had no question in it, and because he had known. Under his own theory, in the dark of him: there had been a moment when the knot had no more secrets, and his fingers had been told, and something in him had done the telling.

"There is one more school," his mother said. "It has been waiting for you to knock."

She held her open hand between them, palm up, empty. "You will not move," she said, in exactly the voice of every lesson he had ever had. "You will not ask. You will watch my hand, and you will find the thing in this that is like the loop in the knot. Not with your eyes. Your eyes lie like caps lie. Watch the way your fingers watched."

Kael watched.

The sea moved below them, the whole cold shining size of it. Her hand did nothing at all, and did it the way the boat had not-fished, and slowly, at the edge of what he was, in a sense he had no name for, something in her empty palm was — carrying. The lazy place. The loop that held all the others.

The wind came up the slope and found them and went on.

"There," his mother said softly, watching his face. "Now we begin."


---

# Chapter Five — The Year

The new school had no name, and that was the first strange thing about it, because everything else his mother taught had a name, even if the name was his own. Far-counting. The standing game. This one she would not name, and when he tried names on it — the carrying school, the loop school — she let them fall on the floor between them and did not pick them up.

The second strange thing was that she rationed it.

He knew rationing. History came in scraps, and the honey theory had held for years. But history was stories; you could live without stories. This was not stories. This was the thing his fingers had done to the master's knot, the thing her empty palm had shown him on the flat rock, and it was the largest thing that had ever happened to him, and she gave it to him in drops. One lesson, some weeks. One sitting, some days, and then nothing, the basket, the languages, the ordinary schools, while the new one sat in him and asked and asked.

The lessons themselves were small on purpose. He understood that much by midwinter. She never asked him to do anything. She asked him to feel where things carried. The pail of water: where it carried. The wind at the door: where it pushed from, which was not where it seemed to push from. The fire in its hole — this one for weeks — where the fire was lazy, which was a thing he would not have believed fire could be, fire that ate all day and was never full. And then one evening he felt it, the way you feel a stair in the dark that is shallower than the others.

"The low side," he said. "Away from the draft, where the log sits down on the coals. It isn't eating there. That's where it carries."

"You felt that. Or you worked it out."

He had to think, which was the answer, and she saw it. "Worked it out," he admitted. He reached again — too fast, lost the whole of it, made himself slow — and this time it was there without any working, shallow and sure under the whole burning weight of it. "There. Felt."

His mother looked at him for a moment and said, "Yes," and put the lid on the lesson like a pot.

"But why is it there? Does it move when the log burns down? Could you—"

She was already at the basket.

That was the whole of it, for eleven days. He counted.

He asked, of course. His questions had questions, and the new school bred them faster than any school before it — what the carrying was, why the eyes couldn't find it, whether the goat could feel it. His mother had always answered until his head was full. That was a true thing about her, floor-true, old as the door holding. And in the new school, for the first time in his life, she answered until *she* stopped, which was different, and the difference had a shape he kept bumping in the dark. Her answers went so far and then went gentle and then went nowhere, like the path going down the mountain, which went so far, and then was not allowed to be a path anymore.

He did not have a theory for it. The honey theory did not fit, because you saved honey for later, and there was no later in the way she stopped; the stopping was the lesson's own wall. The best he could do was a fact, filed without a place: in every school he had, being good was the thing that made his mother's shoulders come down. In the new school it was the other way. On the days he felt nothing, the days the pail was just a pail and he said so, angry at himself, ashamed — those were the days her hand rested a moment on his head, and something in her went loose, the way the house went loose when a watched boat finally leaned back to its fishing. He filed it and could not file it. He was nine, and he assumed the fault was his: that feeling nothing was, in this school, a kind of manners, and some days he was polite by accident.

That was the year everything else sharpened too.

His father's mornings grew a third longer, and stopped ending at noon so much as being interrupted by it. The scree in the dark was old work now, so there was scree with a load, and scree with a load and a count, and ways of falling — falling had rules, it turned out, a whole grammar, the body hitting the ground and coming up out of it in one motion, again, until the ground was just another thing the body talked to.

And the body's own school, the one with no first day in it, grew teeth. It had been play before it was anything — the oldest game he had, older than proper remembering: his father's arm a snare across him, and a small Kael squirming loose into laughing, before memory had proper edges. The snare had grown as he grew, the way the stillness had grown, and that year the play finished folding over into work, so gradually that there was no day he could have pointed to and said: there. It began the way it always began, with him caught — wrist taken, collar taken, folded under the patient mass of his father with the frost finding his cheek — because every lesson in that school began with him caught, and every lesson was the same lesson. Get loose.

There came a morning he could not. He tried strength, which was always the first lie, and then speed, and the grip rode him the way weather rides a slope, and his shoulder began to say things, and then to shout them, and his face was raining and nobody counted it.

"Too much," he said, into the grass.

"Then say so."

He tapped the frost twice with his free hand, flat, and the weight was gone — all of it, at once, the morning sky standing where his father had been.

"Your mouth may be busy," his father said. "The hand talks. Again."

The fourth time down, with the world gone small and the grip a fact of weather, he stopped asking his arm and asked her question instead — where does it carry — and the grip, like everything heavy, was lazy: the iron of it lived in the fingers, and the thumb held the door. He turned against the thumb, not hard, exactly, and his wrist fell out of his father's hand like a thing set down.

His father looked at the wrist, and then at him, and what crossed his face was nothing Kael had a name for, there and gone.

"Again tomorrow," he said, and went to the wood.

The knife-work moved from the snares to the body that year too: where a man carries his weight; where he is lazy; how you stand so that his strength is spent on standing. His parents did not attend each other's schools. But that year Kael began to hear each school inside the other, the way you heard the same wind in different trees, and he filed that too.

He was too full to be restless, most days. Most days.

The itch had not gone. It had changed registers, was all. It lived in the new school now, down at the bottom of the rationed answers, and it lived where it had always lived, down the mountain, in the corner where the fallen stars burned. He was nine, and he still owned the promise the way he owned his knife. When the sea froze — he'd walk on it like a floor — then. It was the oldest thing in the waiting part of him. He did not think about it so much as keep it, the way his mother kept the sea counted.

What fire eats came in the spring, and it killed the promise in four lessons, and she never knew.

It was the last of her schools to arrive and the first one that felt like the new school's cousin: it was about what things were made of, underneath, where the eyes lied.

"Fire eats," she said, first lesson, her hands going on with the wicks while she taught. "But it is a picky eater. It takes the fat of a thing and leaves the bone. It takes the spirit out of the wine and leaves the water. What fire will not eat tells you as much as what it will."

"What won't it eat?"

"Ask it," she said, and gave him the tongs.

Then the cold, the second lesson, because the cold was an eater too — the other mouth, the slow one. The cold ate the difference out of things. It found water wherever water hid and made it hold still.

And then salt. Salt was the third thing, and salt was not an eater; salt was a keeper. Salt in the meat kept the rot's mouth shut. Salt in the barrel water kept it sweet longer. And salt in water — this was the fourth lesson, done with two pails on the bench on a hard-frost night, one pail plain and one brined for the goat, set out together, found together in the iron morning — salt in water kept the cold's mouth shut too. The plain pail wore its lid of ice. The brine pail moved when he rocked it, dark and obedient and unfrozen, and his mother said the thing she said in every school, the sentence that was the school.

"The more salt the water carries, the deeper the cold must bite to hold it."

And went to the fire to see about breakfast, because to her it was Tuesday.

Kael stood with the light coming up grey out of the sea, holding the fact.

He did not do anything with it that day. That was the strangest part, after; the fact just went in and sat where facts sat. It was two nights later, in the loft, doing his numbers, that the fact stood up and walked over to the oldest thing in the waiting part of him and put its hand on it.

The sea was salt. That was floor-true; he had tasted it on the wind his whole life; the fish came out of it wearing it. The sea was the saltiest water there was — and far-counting finished the rest, because far-counting was for things you could not walk to, and he could not walk to the bottom of this, so he counted it from where he lay. How deep the cold would have to bite. How the winter here bit the pail, bit the trough, and left the moving sea alone every year of his life, not once making it even quiet. He lay very still, doing what she had trained him to do, following the thing all the way down to where it carried, and at the bottom of it was the lazy place, the one load-bearing loop, and it was this: *the sea can never freeze. She always knew it.*

The promise was not a promise. It was a door painted on a wall.

He did not cry. He noticed that, distantly, the way he noticed the wind change. What he felt was closer to the morning with the stone in the gate — the mountain-sized weight of a thing, and, arriving underneath it, unwanted, unstoppable, the cold beginning of leverage. She had answered a small boy the way you shut a gate. Very well. It had worked for five years; it was a good gate. But he was not asking to be answered anymore.

At breakfast she was at the table as always. He watched her pour, and a little grey shrike came to the fence post, and he said its word in the bird language — said it wrong, on purpose, the way she sang wrong on purpose, the old game.

His mother looked up. She said the word back, properly. Then she added a second word he didn't have.

"What's that one?"

"What the shrike calls a boy who does it wrong on purpose." She was pouring again. "It is not a polite word."

And she laughed, and he laughed too, and it was real, both laughs were real — that was the thing he could not have explained to anyone: nothing was different, and one thing was. He had a room of his own now, with a shut door in it, and she walked past it every day and did not know the house had grown.

He never asked her about the sea again. She never noticed that he stopped.

The planning began before he admitted it was planning. That was true the way most true things about himself were true: visible first from outside, and there was no outside, so it went unseen. He was simply *interested*, that spring and summer, in things he had always lived beside. When his father woke, exactly, and what woke him — a dropped spoon, a held door, tested without deciding to, filed by what brought footsteps and what didn't. His mother's nights: when the window-and-door counting happened and how long it lasted and which nights it didn't happen at all, and he found the pattern in eleven weeks of lying awake — it followed the boats. Days a strange sail had passed, she stood her watch. Days the sea had been all fishermen, she slept, and the house at the black bottom of the night went as unwatched as it ever went, which was not very, and was enough.

The moon: he knew the moon from far-counting, but he attended to it now the way she attended to the sea, and it was the only piece he ever caught himself holding openly — standing in the yard at dusk working out the dark of it, the nights it would rise late and thin — and his mother came out beside him and looked up too, and said, "What do you see?" And he said, "The moon is lazy this week," in the voice of her own schools, and she laughed, and rested her hand on his head, and went in. Both things were real. That was the year he learned that both things were always real, and that this, not the stillness and not the knife, was the family trade.

The master came in the autumn — no absence arranged, not anymore; those were over — and the visit was books and questions and the best day of the season, and one more thing, this year: a fact, offered across the table like the knot had been.

They had been talking of lamp-oil, of all things — what the village paid for it, some loose thread of the evening — and the master turned his cup and said, "They'll burn a winter's worth of it in one night, soon enough. The feast. The village keeps it on the dark of the year — the longest night, the whole of it, lamps down the waterline end to end, every soul at the water till morning. You could read by the harbor at midnight, I'm told." And he asked Kael something else entirely, about the fat book's water-wheels.

Everyone. Kael took the fact and thanked the school in him that had taught his face to be a stone's face, because his heart had gone loud enough that it seemed impossible the table could not hear it.

The dark of the year came six weeks later.

He lay in the loft and did his numbers one last time, because he was his mother's son and you did not move until the count was done. The sea had been fishermen for three days; no watch tonight. His father slept on the far wall from the ladder, and the third board creaked, and the count knew it, and the count went around it. Wool socks over the boots' voice. The door that held against the wind all his life — he had learned its holding from the inside, the iron tongue, the lift-and-turn that kept the hinge from speaking; he had practiced it in daylight, in pieces, over months, each piece innocent, and that was the standing game too: nothing is moved by strength; everything is moved by where you stand.

The night was clear and enormous and mild, the way it had been the first time, when she carried him out in the big wool. The stars were out. He knew stars. He went down the path he was allowed on, through the pens, past the fence post whose shadow he had once been for most of a day, and the mountain in the dark was a mountain he knew, because his father had made sure of it, and his feet were sure on the frozen ground, because his father had made sure of it, and he was silent, and patient, and warm in his weathers, and could find himself by the stars, because everyone he loved had made sure of it.

The split rock stood up out of the dark where it had always stood, at the end of the path, at the end of the world.

Kael put his hand on it, the way you put your hand on the goat, on the door, on a thing of the house. It was just a rock. It had been the edge of everything for so long that he had expected — he did not know. A wall's holding. A door's weight. It was a rock, split long ago by ice or lightning, cold under his hand, carrying nothing.

Below him, far down, in the corner where the land met the sea, the whole heap of fallen stars was burning — more of them than he had ever seen, doubled and trembling in the black water, the longest night of the year lit end to end — and they were not stars, he knew that now, the way he knew the sea would not freeze; he had traded every promise for every fact, one by one, and this was the last one, and he was going to go and see what burned there.

He stepped past the rock.

The mountain did not notice. His foot came down on ground that was only ground, path that had never been path, and it held him like any ground, and he stood there on the far side of the only law he had ever been given, nine years and a winter old, with his breath going long and easy the way they had taught him, going down.


---

# Chapter Six — The Night

Below the split rock the mountain stopped being his.

It was the same slope. His feet knew scree, and this was scree; his breath knew the cold, and this was the cold. But every step was a step no one had made sure of, and the difference went up through his boots like the difference between the pond ice you had tested and the pond ice you had not. He went slowly, the way his father went when the ground was new — you did not learn this ground, you asked it — and the night was long, the longest of the year, and he had all of it.

Partway down, the world began to smell.

That was the first thing, and no one had told him it would be a smell. Tar. Fish, but old fish, fish that had been kept — salt, he thought, the keeper — and under it middens, and animals that were not his animals, and woodsmoke that was not his woodsmoke, many smokes braided, from fires he had never fed. The mountain's air had always been one clean thing. This air was crowded. He stood in it a while with his mouth open, tasting it, and his heart went strange and quick, because a smell that crowded could only be made by numbers of things, and the things were people, and they were near now, and real, and no rule stood between him and them anymore except all the rules he had ever been given.

Then the lights, close up, through the last of the pines.

They were lamps. He knew this now the way he knew the sea would not freeze — he had traded the stars for lamps, promise for fact, and had thought the trade was finished. It was not finished. Because no fact had told him about the *people*. The shore ran with them. Lamps end to end along the waterline, doubled and trembling in the black water exactly as they had always been from above, and moving among the lamps, dark against them, more people than his numbers had ever been asked to hold. They made a sound all together that was not any sound he had a name for — under the pieces of it, voices, laughing, a thing that might be singing done wrong or done by many — a low warm roar like the fire would make if the fire were happy. The plural of person. He crouched at the treeline with his knees to his chest and his hands quiet and watched them the way he had watched the six-oared boat, except that the boat had been one thing, and this was everything.

He wanted to go down into it so badly that his body leaned. And yet, he did not go down into it. The wanting and the not-going were both at their whole strength, neither winning, the way the wind pushed the door and the door held; and he understood, crouched there, shaking a little in his warm weathers, that he was afraid. Not game-afraid. He had no drill for this. Every stranger he had ever met was one old man, vetted and arrived on a known path, and here were hundreds, and any of them could turn, and see him, and *speak* to him, and there was no count for what came after that.

So he did what his training did with everything it could not enter: he went around it.

He worked along the rim of the lit world, high, in the dark the lamps made deeper, learning the village from outside the way he had learned the wall from inside — its shapes, its lanes, where the light pooled and where it failed. And it was there, at the failing edge, that the pattern snagged him.

A cluster of roofs stood apart from the rest, downshore, past a stand of boat-sheds. No lamps. On the night of all lamps, the night the whole world had carried its fire outside, a handful of houses sat in their own dark, and the dark had a shape, and the shape was wrong. He knew wrongness in a pattern before he knew anything else about it; it was the family trade. One star had gone out while he watched, once, and he had felt it in his chest, a little cold spot. This was the cold spot. He was moving toward it before he had finished deciding to, because a gap in a pattern was the most interesting thing in the world, and everyone he loved had made sure of it.

The first wrong thing was a door.

It stood open. Winter, night, and a door standing open, and no one closing it. His whole life a door was a thing that held — that was what a door was *for*, the way a floor was for standing — and this one hung loose on its dark, swinging a little when the sea-wind found it, knocking softly against its own frame like a thing asking to be let into itself. Kael stopped at the corner of a shed with his breath gone long and thin. The wrongness of the door went into him deeper than the crowd had, deeper than the smells, because the crowd had been a new thing and this was an old thing broken.

The second wrong thing lay in the doorway's spilled dark, half in, half out.

He knew dead. He was not confused about dead; he had unmade too many hares. The man lay the way nothing alive lies, the weight all given up at once, everything the body did to hold itself, stopped. Kael read him from the shed's corner, coldly, exactly, in the vocabulary he had — the dark under him had poured out and finished pouring some time ago; the cold had already gotten into him — and it was the exactness that was terrible, though he could not have said so. He had a school for this. He had never once been told what the school was for.

And the house was not quiet.

Voices, several, men's. And laughing — and laughing was a good sound. It was his mother when the bird got the song wrong; it was his father's one spent coin. It lived on the list of true things: laughing meant the day had gone right. The laughing came through the wall with the lamplight, and under it there was another sound, smaller, and the bones of it were wrong, and he had been taught his whole life that when a thing was wrong you did not turn from it. You looked. The eyes go up, go out, come back. He went along the wall to where a shutter hung as broken as the door, and he looked, because everyone he loved had made sure he would.

There were men in the lamplight, more than his two hands wanted to count, and they had a woman down on the table the way — and here his drawers began opening and closing, fast, finding nothing — the way a goat is held for shearing, except a goat was held quiet and kind and this was not; the way his father pinned him in the grammar of falling, except drills had a shape and a teacher and an end, and you tapped the ground and the weight came off, and she was tapping the ground, her hand was doing it against the table, open, again and again, and no weight came off. The men who were not holding were drinking and watching. One sat on the table's edge with his back to her head, loose, easy, the way you sit on a wall in the sun. She was making the small sound. It wanted to stop being made. It was being made anyway.

Kael's body did something then that no one had trained into it. His stomach rolled over slowly, like something turning in deep water, and his hands went cold in a way the winter had never managed, and a feeling came up through him that had no name and no drawer and no school — a hating, arrived whole, aimed at everything in that room and at nothing he could point to, because he did not know what he was seeing. He could not file it. It would not wait. He tried to out-wait it the way you out-waited an itch and it did not stop asking, and that was how he knew, without knowing he knew, that it was not an itch. It was the first thing his body had ever known before he did that no one had put there.

His eyes came off the window not by decision. They came off the way a hand comes off iron in the cold — pulled loose by the body, for the body's own reasons — and he was against the wall in the dark, breathing wrong, breath short and high and refusing the count, and the lamplight lay in the yard in a long yellow blade, and the laughing went on inside exactly as before, because nothing that had happened in him had happened anywhere else.

He should go. Every rule he had, from the split rock down, said go. He was turning to go when he saw the girl.

She sat against the wheel of a cart in the yard's dark, knees up, smaller than him or his size, he could not tell — and she was tied. Wrists behind, to the wheel, a line taken twice around and finished low where her hands could not follow it. Kael's eyes did what they had been trained to do before he could stop them: they assessed the knot. It was a good knot. Tied by a hand that had tied it many times, economical, no waste, the finishing loop set exactly where fingers could not — and something in him rose against the assessment like water against a wall, the same nameless hating, aimed now at a knot, at a good knot, at the goodness of the knot. His body objected. It would not say to what.

The girl's eyes found him in the dark. They were the first stranger's eyes to ever land on him, and they went wide, and her mouth opened —

— and closed. She looked at him and was silent, silent the way he knew how to be silent, and later, much later, he would understand what it cost a taken child to make that choice in that yard, and would not be able to think about it.

He was at the wheel without deciding. That was the truth of it, the truth he would keep afterward in the room with the shut door: there was no moment when he chose. There was a knot on a person, and he was the boy who untied things, and his fingers were already reading it, finding the architecture, the lazy place, the one loop that carried all the others — *there* — and he had it, he had the whole of it, the line already giving —

Light fell across the yard. A man stood in the open door.

For one breath nothing happened. The man was big the way the stone in the gate had been big, and he held a jug, and he looked at Kael crouched at the wheel with the line half-drawn, and his face did something slow, and he said a word Kael did not know — a word that needed no knowing — in a voice gone loud.

"What," said a voice from inside, unhurried, a chair going back. "What is it."

"A boy." The big man had not moved. He was looking at Kael the way you look at a tool lying where no tool should be. "At the wheel."

"Whose is he?" The doorway was growing men, dark against the lamplight, one and then three.

"Nobody's." A short laugh came out of the light behind them. "Look at him. He's nobody's."

They were not afraid. His body filed that even then, under everything, the way it filed weather: the voices were the voices of men sorting a strayed goat, a fallen rail — a small thing, found where small things get found, already sorted. And then the yard was full.

What happened next happened the way scree happens.

The first man reached for him, and Kael was not where the hand arrived — his feet had already answered — and the man's weight went on past its own lazy place and Kael helped it, two fingers of push at the loaded knee, no more, and the man met the ground with all of himself at once and did not immediately get up. It worked. It was the standing game and it *worked*, on a man, the mountain tricked, and somewhere far under the roaring in him a cold clean voice was counting.

Somebody laughed at their own man on the ground and the laugh was still in the air when the second man came with his hands wide, herding. There was iron in Kael's hand — his own short knife, arrived from his belt — and the knife-lesson met the world: *not the body, the carrying*, the cabled place above the wrist where a man's grip lives, and the blade told the truth there, one short honest line, and the man's hand stopped being a hand that worked and the man made a sound and folded around it. The eyes go up, go out, come back. His father's voice, calm as bread. Two men down. The cold voice counted two.

And then the wall of the house gave him the small sound again — the one that wanted to stop being made — and Kael *heard* it, heard it all the way, and the count broke.

It broke like the pond ice you had not tested. One breath he was inside the game, and everything was carrying and lazy places and lines; the next he was a nine-year-old boy in a yard of grown men in the dark of the world, and he could not find the long breath, and his eyes stopped seeing where the men carried and started seeing the men — their faces, their bigness, their number, the jug still rolling where it had dropped — and behind the men, the table, the lamplight, the open hand tapping, all of it at once, everything spilling, he was spilling, the whole held house of him coming down —

a hand had his collar. He went where it sent him. The ground came up without his father's hand between them, the first time in his life, the whole weight of the world arriving at once, and the sky swung, and something with a man's weight in it found his ribs and the air left him and would not come back. He did the drills. Somewhere in him the drills were still trying, hands moving in the grammar of falling, cover, turn — but the machine needed the count and the count was gone, and they were angry now, at the two of theirs on the ground, at the smallness of what had done it, and the anger came down on him in boots and fists with nothing between him and it but the body they were breaking. Iron came into it somewhere — a line across his guarding arm, cold and then very hot, the blade telling its one truth the way his own had told it — and even that got filed, far away, by the cold voice counting things that no longer helped.

He thought: *not enough.* He thought it without words, in the place where the lists lived. All of it, the scree and the stillness and the knife and the standing game, and it was not enough, he had spilled, he had —

Someone was laughing still. That was the last whole thing: that one of them was laughing, and his body hating it, all the way down into the dark.

After that, the night came to him in pieces, and the pieces did not touch.

A sound that stopped in the middle of itself.

Weight gone off him. The cold arriving where the weight had been, and the ground very close to his face, one ear full of his own blood-beat, and in the yard, nothing — an absence with edges, a quiet the size of several men, and no part of him could lift to look, and no part of him needed to.

Hands. He knew them before he knew anything else again; he had known them before the ground, before the slate, before the blade's want went out of the blade. They went over him the way they went over a tool, finding what was bent, and then he was up, in against wool and woodsmoke, the world tipped into a rhythm of walking, and the walking was climbing, and the cold got enormous and clean again and the crowded smells let go of the air one by one until there was only the mountain's one smell and his father's, which were nearly the same smell.

He surfaced once, partway. Stars — the true ones, cold and high. He could find himself by the stars. He was too broken to find himself and it did not matter; he was found; he was carried, the way everything on the mountain that fell was carried, and the arms were not gentle and were not rough, they were exact, and they did not shake, and he went under again inside their count.

The split rock stood up out of the dark, and past it, against the paler dark of the sky, his mother.

She did not run to them. She stood where the path ended, at the edge of the oldest law, straight and still, the way she sat at the window, and as his father carried him past the rock Kael felt her hand arrive at his head, and stay, the whole way up — one hand, resting there, riding the rhythm of his father's steps — and nobody said anything, the whole climb, and the silence was full of the third voice without any voice at all.

Then warmth. The door, closing, with him on the inside of it. The bar going down into its stone with the sound that meant held.

They laid him where the light was and their hands worked on him together, exact, unhurried, four hands that knew what was bent and what was broken and what would keep. His mother's needle went through the arm in small exact bites, herb-smell and thread, the mending school arrived at last at the thing it had always been for, and he watched it happen as if the arm were the table's, or a hare's, or the goat's. And the fire sat small and orange in its hole, eating what it was given, and Kael lay under their hands with his eyes closed and could not stop shaking. He was not cold. They had made sure of the cold years ago. He shook anyway, in small runs, like a line paying out, and behind his closed eyes the lamplight lay in the yard in its long yellow blade, and no one told him to stop, and no one said it was over, and downhill, far down, in the corner where the land met the sea, the feast burned end to end all the long night — every lamp lit, every light carried down to the water — and one small cluster of roofs sat apart from it in its own dark, and nothing about the dark, from up here, looked any different than it ever had.

He never learned whether the line had held.


---

# Chapter Seven — The Opening

The winter measured him in mendings.

First the arm, which itched under its thread and then itched worse without it, a tightness he was told not to test and tested anyway, alone, in the loft, making a fist until the new skin spoke up. Then the ribs, which measured slower. Ribs kept their own calendar. For a long while there were two kinds of breath, the shallow kind that cost nothing and the deep kind that cost. He paid for the deep kind sometimes just to know the price, and the price came down by the week. That was the calendar.

Nobody punished him.

He waited for it the way he had once waited for the sea. The law he had broken was the oldest law there was, older than the fire in its hole, and he lay those first days building what the punishment might be out of the materials he had — more work, less supper, some new stillness with no boat to give it a shape — and the days came up the ladder one at a time, and none of them had it. His mother changed the dressing on his arm with her mouth set and her hands soft. His father brought his meals up while the ribs still owned the ladder, set the bowl down, took the old one, said what there was to say about the goat or the weather, and went. The house was gentle the way the house was gentle after the long game, years ago: honey in the milk, and nobody saying why.

He had a school for everything else. He had no school for this. Guilt, like the dead man in the doorway, was a thing he had been given all the tools for and none of the names.

The knife was back on the wall.

He found it there the first morning he came down the ladder on his own — his knife, in its place, in the order of tools, and it had been cleaned. Not wiped. Cleaned, the way his father cleaned, back to the metal's own weather. Kael stood in front of the wall for a while with his ribs holding his breath down to the shallow kind, and did not touch it, and did not ask, and the not-asking was a thing he did with his whole body. Someone had gone down for it. Someone had brought it up the mountain and made it right and hung it where it lived. The wall said what the honey said, in the wall's language: *we know everything, and here is your knife.*

Those weeks his father sat by the fire in the evenings with the drawknife and a stack of seasoned wood, and made swords.

Not swords. Kael corrected himself the first night, and was wrong, and knew better by the third: they were swords the way the game was a game. Wooden, full-weight, the balance fussed over the way his father fussed over nothing else — shavings taken off in whispers, the thing hefted, turned, hefted again, held out level on two fingers to see where it wanted to tip. Two of them, one to Kael's arm and one longer. Then a spear with its head blunted round. Then, from the corner where it had always leaned being a hunting tool, the bow came into the evenings too, and got a second string made, and a dozen new arrows with their feathers set in his father's exact, unhurried spiral.

Nobody said what any of it was for. The boy healed by the fire, and the father built the things they were going to fight each other with by the same fire, and the wind came to the door and pushed, and the door held.

---

The audit began before the ribs were done, at the table, on a morning of hard rain.

His father put the door on the table. Not the door — the door's *work*: he set his hands in the air the way the hinges sat, and said, "Show me."

Kael showed him. There was no not-showing; a question from his father was a lifted stone, you got under it. He walked it through in the air the way he had done it in the dark — the iron tongue, the lift, the lean that took the door's weight off the hinge before the turn, so the hinge had nothing to say. His father watched with the stillness he watched everything, and at the lean he said, "Earlier." Kael moved the lean earlier. His father nodded once, and the nod was the size of every other nod, and something in Kael that had been braced since midwinter came off its brace so suddenly that he had to put his eyes on the table.

They did the whole of it that way, over days. The third board, and the count that went around it — "Slower past the count," his father said. "A count you hurry is a wish" — and the socks over the boots, and the going down, ground by ground. His father found four mistakes on the mountain. He named them the way he named a crooked elbow, one correction each, and each correction was a door quietly opening onto the size of the thing: his father was not teaching him to be caught. His father had never once, in any school, taught him to be caught.

Then, last, the yard.

"The first man," his father said.

Kael's breath went shallow, and not for the ribs. But the yard, in his father's mouth, was made of the same air as the door: work, on the table, to be gone through. So he went through it. The knee at its lazy place, two fingers of push. The second man, the wide hands, the short honest line where the grip lives. His father took him along both exchanges twice, moved the line on the wrist a half-thumb ("Here. It ends the argument the same and costs him less"), and asked no question about anything that was not movement, and Kael answered in movement, and between them the rest of the yard — the lamplight, the window, all of it — stood in the room like weather outside a wall, present and unentered.

"Then?" his father said.

"Then I spilled." The old word, the truest one he had. "The count broke and I spilled."

His father was quiet. His hand came over and rested on the stitched forearm — on the scar, exactly on it — the length of one breath. Then it went back to being a hand on a table.

"The body held," his father said. "We will build the rest."

And that was the sentence the wooden swords had been for. The new school started the day the ribs released him. His father put the shorter waster in his hands, hilt first, and watched him find the weight of it.

"Heavier at the tip than steel," his father said. "So that steel, when it comes, will feel like a gift."

Sword, spear, the bow at distance, his father beside him in everything the way his father had been beside him in the stillness, being it longer, being it better, corrections one stroke at a time. *You have the feet,* the school said, in the school's wordless way. *You have the eyes. Now we put something in your hands.* Kael took to it the way dry ground takes rain, and did not tell anyone, least of all himself, that some of the gladness was that in this school, at last, there was something to swing.

His mother's watching had changed too. He noticed it the first week he was well enough to lie awake properly: the window, the door, her feet in the dark — every night now. The boats no longer decided. He lay in the loft with the old arithmetic in his hands and nowhere to put it, because the pattern he had cracked was gone, and she had never said a word, and this was her answer the way the knife was his father's: *I know what you read. I have made myself harder to read.* He felt the cost of it arrive in him and sit down. She stood two watches now where she had stood one. He had done that.

And her answers went deeper. That was the strangest mending of that strange winter. He asked her, over the pairs of caps one afternoon, why the killer and the eater wore the same face — he had asked it before, years before, and gotten *so you never pick wrong*, which was a fence, and he had known it was a fence — and this time she stopped her hands, and thought, and answered him: what the two shared, what the flesh was doing differently underneath, how you could ask the difference to show itself, and when her answer reached the place where her answers had always gone gentle and then gone nowhere, it went on. It went all the way to *I don't know*.
