She gave him her not-knowing with both hands, like a tool, and he took it, and it was worth more to him than most of what he knew, and he could not have said why — except that you did not give a fence to someone you thought could not be trusted past it. Some of her schools stayed fenced. The newest school did. But the rest opened like the winter opening, and he filed the difference between the opened schools and the fenced ones, without knowing he was filing it, the way he filed everything.

---

The conversation happened in spring, in the evening, with the light long and the supper done, and it began with his father not going out.

There was an order to evenings, old as everything. His father went out last, checked what was to be checked, came in, barred the door. That evening he stayed at the table, and his mother sat down across, and Kael, between them, understood from the arrangement alone — two of them, both facing him, nothing in their hands — that this was a lesson with no school attached, and went still.

"Everything we have, you will have," his father said. "It is not everything there is."

His mother's hands were flat on the table, the way they had been the day of the knot. "The rest," she said, "is people. It cannot be learned here. We cannot—" and she stopped, and it was not the gentle stopping of the fenced schools. "It is not ours to teach," she said, which was a different sentence than the one she had started, and truer, he thought, in some way he had no floor under yet.

They did not ask him what he had seen. They did not say the word for where he had gone. The night stood outside this wall too, present, unentered, and inside the wall his parents opened the future by a crack — a school, down the mountain, where the island's children learned; a master he already knew; a road.

Kael sat with his heart going the way her heart had gone under the big wool, that first night, hurrying under a slow voice.

"When," he said, because *why* was too big to lift and his hands had gone to the question they could carry.

"Autumn," his father said.

---

The master came a month later, and Kael read the house the way he had read it at nine: the seed-bread rising in the night, the other shirt, the shoulders down. This time he knew the tells for tells, and knew they knew he knew, and no one performed anything for anyone, and the not-performing was its own strange comfort, a house full of readers all reading, at rest.

The talk after the meal happened without him. Wood wanted splitting; he went. And in the yard, in the shadow at the foot of the woodpile, the old option stood open — he knew the shapes voices made through that wall, he had pointed the stillness at it once and taken what it gave — and he stood with the axe a moment, feeling the door of that open, and did not go through it. He could not have said all of why. Some of it was the room he kept now, with its shut door, and what keeping it had taught him about the cost of doors. Some of it was simpler and had no dignity at all: the last time he had listened to that wall, the world had ended within the year. He split wood, honestly and loudly, the whole time.

At the table, after, the master spoke to him plainly, the way you speak terms.

"Down there," the master said, "you are mine. My charity case — a mountain boy I have taken on, fed books, taught letters. That is the whole of your story, and you will find it is enough story for anyone. What you know, you will be surprised by slowly, the way a boy taught in a kitchen is surprised. Do you understand?"

He did not, entirely. He understood it was a knot, and that he was being handed the tied side of it, and that everyone at the table could see the whole of it and he could see the outside. Rules were the floor you asked from. "Yes," he said.

Then his parents gave him the law, his father saying it and his mother's stillness saying it under him:

"Home to the temple. The temple to home. Never beyond."

No reason came with it. Reasons had never come with the laws; that was what made them laws. But this one sat differently in him than the split rock had ever sat, and he turned the difference over that night in the loft until he found its faces. The old law had been a wall around the itch. This one was a road cut through the middle of it. They had taken the thing in him that could not be out-waited — and aimed it.

---

He left on a morning in autumn, early, the light coming up grey out of the sea.

His mother put bread in the bag and a word in his ear, in the downhill language, low — a blessing or an order, built like both. His father walked him as far as the pens and stopped where his work stopped, and said, "Evening," which meant *by evening*, which meant *come home*, and placed his hand, briefly, on Kael's shoulder.

The master was waiting at the split rock.

He stood on the near side of it, on the path, leaning on a stick he did not need for leaning, and he watched Kael come down through the pens the way he watched everything, unhurried, and he did not say good morning. He said, "Who are you?"

"Kael," Kael said.

"Kael of what?"

"Of—" and there was nothing to put there. He had never needed anything to put there. The master let the nothing sit exactly as long as it wanted to sit, and then turned and stepped past the rock, and Kael put his hand on the stone as he went by — the way you touch the goat, the door, a thing of the house — and followed him onto the lawful road.

"You are Kael from the mountain," the master said, walking. "A herder's boy. I found you three summers ago with your letters already in you — your mother taught you those, that much is true, keep what is true, it carries the rest — and I have brought you books since, because I am an old man with money for books and no sons, and this year I have grown foolish enough to school you. That is your whole story. Tell it to me."

Kael told it to him.

"Again. You are not defending it, you are wearing it. A story is not a wall, boy; walls get climbed. *A story is a coat*. Loose. Warm. Nobody wonders at a coat."

He told it again, and the master stopped him three sentences in. "Who taught you letters?"

"My mother."

"You said it like a fact you were handing over. It is not a fact. It is *your* mother. Say it that way the whole way through."

He told it once more, all the way, the herder's boy, the books, the old man's foolishness — and it was different in his mouth this time, and he could not have pointed to where.

"There," the master said. "Now it hangs off you," and moved on as if from a finished chore. "You will be surprised slowly. You know what fire eats. You do not know what they call it. Their names for things will be new to you, and that is convenient, because it is even true."

The road bent, and below it, for the first time in daylight, lawfully, close: the village.

---

Roofs, and smoke standing up from them, and the harbor with its boats at their work, and sound coming up the slope loose and ordinary — a dog, a hammer somewhere, a woman's voice carrying two notes of something, gulls. People, small and clear, moving between the houses at the pace of chores. Kael's eyes went out, took the whole of it, came back — and his hands had gone quiet on the strap of the bag, too quiet, the stillness arriving in him unasked. Downshore, past the boat-sheds, a cluster of roofs stood with their smoke standing up like all the others, ordinary, indistinguishable, and he did not look at it longer than he looked at anything, and looking away cost what it cost.

The master, beside him, watched the harbor and said nothing at all. Then he said, "The fish here is better than the mutton. You will hear the opposite. It is villagers being loyal to sheep," and walked on, and Kael came off the stillness like a held breath let go, and followed, and was grateful in a direction he could not have pointed to.

"You like questions," the master said, further down, where the road left the village behind and ran out along the shoulder of the coast. "So. A question. You will meet children down here who have never carried water. Not once, from a well to a house. What do they know that you don't?"

Kael thought about it for the better part of a mile, because the question had doors in it, and every door he tried opened onto another, and each answer was true and none of them was the answer, and the master walked beside his thinking and let it work the way his mother let a knot work.

"I don't know," Kael said at last, and gave it the way she had taught him that winter to receive it: with both hands, like a tool.

"Good," the master said. "Hold that. It is the only thing I am worried you'll lose."

The road climbed a last shoulder, and turned, and there was the Temple.

He had seen it his whole life the way he had seen the moon — a pale shape south along the coast, a fact of the island, filed. From the road it was not a shape. It was walls out of the headland like the headland had decided them, courts inside the walls, roofs inside the courts, and over the whole of it a tower with the morning sun on its face — and it was full. That was the thing the shape had never carried up the mountain: thin smoke from its kitchens, banners moving, and in the outer court, figures crossing between the buildings, dozens of them, children, walking in twos and threes and clusters at the pace of belonging there.

The sound of them came up the road before the road went down to them. Voices, many, loose, and threaded through the voices, laughter — children laughing somewhere inside the walls, an ordinary morning's worth of it — and Kael's body answered before any of him was asked. His breath found the long shape on its own. His eyes went out over the court and came back having worked: the twos and the threes, which clusters moved and which held ground, where the walls gave and where they didn't — the yard read as a yard is read, by a boy who had only ever been in one. His hand had come up to the strap of the bag, near where the knife was not.

None of it showed. He knew none of it showed; everyone he loved had made sure of it. He stood on the road with the coat of his story on, ten years old, everything they had given him packed inside everything they had hidden, wanting to walk down into that court more than he had ever wanted anything, and ready, in the oldest part of him, for the ground.

"Come along, charity case," the master said, not unkindly, and went down toward the gate, and Kael followed him.


---

# Chapter Eight — Enrollment

The gate stood open, and it was meant to.

That was the first thing the Temple taught him, before any master spoke: a door could stand open on purpose, all day, and be right. He walked through it behind the master into a court the size of the upper meadow, walled in stone that had been asked to be flat and had agreed. The court was full of the sound he had heard from the road — voices, many, loose, weaving — and his eyes went out and came back with the count before he had asked them to. Two dozen children crossing on nine errands. Three clusters holding ground. Colonnade north and east; the walls gave nowhere. His breath found the long shape. None of it showed.

The air was crowded here too, but crowded differently than the one other crowded air he knew: ink somewhere, and wet stone, and bread in quantities no one family could want, and under everything the smell of many many people. His body checked it the way you check a pail lid, and let it pass.

"The roll first," the master said. "Then I am done carrying you."

---

The roll lived in a long room off the first court, in a book the size of a door, in the keeping of a clerk with ink on the side of his hand like a scar he renewed daily. Ahead of Kael a boy was being entered, and the boy's father stood behind him in a coat worth more than the goat, saying the name for the clerk to take down, and the name went on and on. It had the boy's own name in it, and then a name for honor, and then a name for the house, and the pen walked the whole length of it across the page like a fence being built around something valuable.

Kael listened the way he listened to a new language. So that was what the chains were for. You could hear, inside the name, everything the boy came with — the house, the coat, the fence around the whole of it. When the master had asked him, at the split rock, what he was *of*, he had had nothing to put there. He had thought, then, that nothing was a lack, like a missing tooth. Standing in the roll-room he understood it better: nothing was also a thing the pen would have to write.

His turn came. The clerk did not look up.

"Name."

"Kael." He heard his own voice put the name down plainly, a stone on a table, and then, because the room asked and the coat was on, he wore the rest of it: "From the mountain. The First Master brings me books." Which was true, and carried the rest.

The pen took *Kael* and then stood, patient as a heron, over the long empty stretch where the other names had kept going.

"He is entered under my sponsorship," the master said, from the doorway, already half gone. "My charity case. Leave the space as it is. Empty is true."

The pen came down and made the emptiness official with a small line, the way you close an account. And Kael saw, because his eyes did what they always did, that the line took the clerk less time than any single name of the boy before him, and that two children waiting behind him had heard the whole of it, *charity case* and the line and all, and were already telling it with their faces to a third.

The master left without a farewell. That was its own kind of lesson. On the mountain, the master's leaving was the end of the best day of the year. Here he went the way weather went. The Temple closed around the space where he had been, and Kael stood in it alone — entered, blank where the fences were, the day not even at its noon.

---

They put him with the ten-year-olds, in rooms.

The rooms were the strangest part of the morning. The table became a market when his mother said so; the scree was a school because his feet were on it. Here the school was nailed in place — benches, a lectern, a wall of slates — and the lesson came to you, at its hour, like the tide. Boys and girls his own age filed in and draped themselves on the benches with the particular boredom of people who own a thing, and Kael sat where a space was, and learned quickly to be furniture, which was one of his oldest schools.

History came before noon, and history nearly undid him.

At home history was rationed. It came in scraps, sideways, and never — never once in his life — on asking. Here a master with a chronicle open on the lectern *poured* it, reign after reign, in quantities that would have lasted his mother a decade. Around him the heirs of the story dozed on their fists. Kael sat with his hands flat on the bench and took all of it, every reign, and had to remind himself, twice, to blink like furniture blinks, because the hunger in him had come all the way up to his face.

No one had ever answered him on asking. Here they answered before he asked. He filed the strangeness of that where he filed everything, and under the strangeness, far under, something else went by — small, quick, like a shape running a treeline — and was gone before he could turn to it.

Between the rooms, crossing the second court in a line of ten-year-olds, he saw a master going the other way with a streak of white through his dark hair, one clean seam of it, like quartz through rock. Nobody looked at it. So Kael didn't either.

The last room before the meal was natural philosophy, and the natural philosopher was called Priscian. Priscian was old in a different way than the master was old: not worn to what mattered but stuffed to the seams, a man with too many books inside him and the bindings straining. He took the lectern the way Kael's mother took the table, as territory, and what he lectured on, that first day, was the smallest thing in the world.

"The minima," Priscian said, and wrote it on the great slate in letters a hand high. "The smallest grains of the world. Division has a floor, gentlemen — split the log to splinters, the splinter to dust, and beneath the eye's failure the world is still made of pieces, and the pieces have a smallest, and the smallest is where the Art lives. All matter carries them. All matter *is* carried by them. The Resonant is one who feels the grains as the hand feels warmth through cloth — not seeing, mark me, feeling — and having felt them, sounds them, as a struck string wakes its silent neighbor: his own minima move the world's, and the world answers. That is resonance. The whole of the Art is in the word."

Kael sat very still, and this time the stillness was not furniture.

He knew this. He had known it for a year, the way he knew the stairs in the dark. And he had never once had a word for it, because she gave that school no words at all. She had given him the pail, and the wind, and the fire's — the *feel* of things, where they carried, and here was an old man in a room nailing a name to it, hand-high, in chalk. The word arrived over the feeling like a roof arriving over a room he had lived in all his life, and the word was not hers, and he thought, without meaning to, of the master on the road: *their names for things will be new to you, and that is convenient, because it is even true.*

Priscian lectured, and Kael attended him the way he attended the sea.

The trouble came at the end, and he brought it on himself. Priscian, closing, threw a question at the benches: "What, then, is flame? Anyone. What is fire?"

The benches offered him what benches offer.

"It's hot," a boy said, and got the quiet agreement such answers earn.

"It's bright."

"It is the minima in haste," said a third, who had clearly been fed the doctrine at home and wore it like his coat. "Quickened past their nature by resonance, whereupon the fiery nature—"

"Whereupon," Priscian said, "you will tell me what nature they had before they hastened." The boy did not. Priscian took the silence with the face of a man tasting thin soup. And the itch came up in Kael, the old one, the deep one, because a question was standing in the room with no one under it, and a question from a master was a lifted stone —

"It's the wood being eaten," Kael said.

The room turned. He heard it turn, felt it on his skin, and kept going, because the stone was up and he was under it now. "Fire's an eater, but it's picky. It takes the fat of the wood and leaves the bone — that's the ash, the bone. And it doesn't start everywhere the same. It starts where the wood is —" and he heard, coming up the path of his own sentence like someone he recognized at a distance, a word of his mother's, *lazy*, her word, from her school, and he stepped around it the way you step around a board that creaks — "where the wood is weakest. Where the load of it sits. You can watch it choose."

Silence, one breath of it, with a new temperature.

"You can watch it choose," Priscian repeated, slowly, the way you repeat a thing to keep it still while you look at it. "Who taught you to watch fire, boy?"

"No one," Kael said. "We have a fire."

It was true, and it carried the rest, and it made the benches laugh — a short crop of laughter, many throats at once, and his breath went long on its own and his hand found the strap of his bag, near where nothing hung, and none of it showed. But Priscian did not laugh. Priscian looked at him for a moment the way the master had looked at him in the yard, the first day, unhurried, and said, "Everyone has a fire," in a voice that meant the opposite, and let the benches go.

Kael filed the whole of it walking out: the too-far answer, the turned room, the one crop of laughter, and the way the girl at the end of the second bench — dark-haired, straight-backed, with a face that gave out nothing it hadn't priced first — had not laughed. Her stillness had changed when he spoke; that was all. Everyone else in the room he had learned nothing about. About her he had learned one thing, and it was the only thing all day he knew how to read.

---

The meal was in a hall that held more people than he had ever seen under one roof, and he did his eating the way he did his blinking, at the local speed, and it was there that the golden ones came to look at him.

He knew the leader before the leader spoke, the way you know the lead goat: by where the others put their feet. A boy of eleven or twelve, tall for it, built a year ahead of his age, with dark-gold hair and a chain of a name Kael had heard twice already that morning traveling ahead of its owner — Aurelian Corvane. Kael's body priced him on the way over: unspent strength, carried high, the stand of a boy who had never once been put on the ground by anything. And the table priced him too, in its own language: room arriving around him where no one had moved, so that he crossed the hall the way weather crosses water, announced by the surface. He came with four behind him and stopped across the table with the pleasantness of someone doing a thing he has done before and enjoys.

"The charity case," Aurelian said, to his four, presenting him. Then, to Kael, kindly, the way you speak to something in a pen: "So what does a herder study up a mountain? Goats?"

"Say yes," one of the four said, low, to the others. "Please say yes."

And Kael — because a question was a question, and this one had an answer — considered it honestly and answered it honestly. "Yes. Goats teach a lot. Where they'll put their weight, what they'll spook at, what they can eat that you can't." He thought a moment more, because the answer was still coming, and gave the rest of it: "And weather, and snares, and mending. And letters, from my mother."

The laughter came down the table like a wave down a beach — many throats, one animal — and his body did its arithmetic under it, breath long, eyes out and back, the hand quiet, and the arithmetic came home with a strange sum, the same sum it had been finding all day and now finally showed him whole: *nothing here is heavy.* The boy across the table carried no weight anywhere it could fall from. His four were the same. And Kael was still there when the laughter died, unmoved the way the split rock was unmoved, not because he was brave — he could not have said what he was — but because his body, which made these judgments without him, had judged, and declined to spend anything.

Aurelian's face changed by a degree. Whatever he had come to collect, it had not been paid, and he was not old enough to name what had been paid instead. "It talks," he said, to his four, a little too late and a little too loud, and took them off down the hall, and Kael went back to the local speed of eating and filed the change in the boy's face next to the change in the room's temperature that morning, two entries, same drawer, a drawer with no name on it yet.

"That," said a voice beside him, arriving along the bench together with its owner — a soft-built, round-faced boy, quick-eyed, whose hands were already talking before the rest of him sat down — "was Aurelian Corvane, and you shouldn't answer him, or you should always answer him, I honestly don't know which is worse for you, but it was wonderful. Ostrian Vessaline." The boy said his own house's name quickly, the way you step over a known bad board, and put out his hand. Kael took it, because you took what was offered with both hands or not at all, and filed, in the taking, that everything the boy wore was slightly too new: none of it had learned him yet, the way a tool new from the maker has no hand worn into it. "Everyone says Oss. You should too, it's faster."

"Kael."

"I know. Everyone knows. You're the day's whole news; last week it was a boat with a two-headed fish, that's the standard you've beaten." And Oss talked, the hands drawing the Temple in the air while the mouth drew it in words. He talked the way the downhill language ran, all its length without a stone in it: who the four behind Corvane were, and which houses mattered and which thought they did, and what the masters were called behind the masters' backs, and that the girl who didn't laugh in natural philosophy was Elarine Sedrane, "and don't bother, she's priced everyone in this Temple and no one's met it yet" — and Kael listened the way he listened to rain on the roof, learning the shape of the country by where the water ran. It cost the boy nothing to talk, that was plain; the words came off him like heat off a wall. It was also plain — filed, not judged — that no one else had sat down.

---

He left when the light said leave, because the light was the law now.

The road home ran the day backward: the gate, the shoulder of the coast, the village drawing itself up the slope roof by roof — downshore a cluster of roofs stood among the others with its smoke standing up like all the rest, and he did not look at it longer than he looked at anything — and then up, and up, the world going quiet by degrees, shedding its smells one by one until the air was one clean thing again.

His father was at the lower pens.

Kael saw him from the last bend and filed it without weighing it: the lower pens, at dusk, where the evening work was not. The rail his father was mending was a rail Kael had watched him mend in the spring; it did not need a man. But there the man was, at the bottom of everything, with the light going long and red behind him, working on a mended thing, and when Kael came up the path his father straightened and looked at him — at the whole of him, once — and Kael said, "Evening," and his father nodded, one nod, the size of every other nod, and fell in beside him, and they walked up through the pens together with the tools swinging in his father's hand, and neither of them said anything, and his father's pace, the whole way, was exactly his.

His mother was in the doorway, and the good smell came out past her, and on the table there was the seed-bread — the feast bread, the one that rose for the master and no one else — and Kael stopped in the door and looked at it, and looked at her.

"Sit," she said. "Eat. And tell me."

So he told her. And here was the strange thing: she asked. All his life the questions had run one way, downhill from him, and she had answered until his head was full, and tonight she sat across from him with her hands still and her supper cooling and asked.

"The rooms first. Where does the light come in? Where did they put you?"

"Wherever there was a space. The lessons stay in their rooms — you go to them. The room *is* the lesson," he said, trying the thought aloud for the first time, and something small and pleased crossed her mouth, and she asked on: how they taught the letters, whose hand they taught; what the philosopher said the world was made of; and what the other children — and stopped, and started differently — "And what are the masters called?"

And he answered and answered, he talked the way Oss talked, he heard himself doing it, and she listened with her whole body. Once, when he was telling the part about the book the size of a door, and the pen, and how his name had gone in short with the empty place after it, her eyes went to his father's, one glance, quick as the shuttle of a loom, and came back, and Kael read the glance the way he read everything and filed it wrong: *they are checking I did it correctly.* Their faces through the whole of his talking were the faces of people at work, attentive, weighing. And then he came to the fire question, and what he had answered, and that the room had laughed and the old man hadn't, and his father made a sound.

Kael stopped. It had been short, low, one note — and his father's hand came up and covered the lower half of his own face, thumb and knuckle, a man rubbing his jaw at the end of a day, and above the hand his eyes had gone bright and creased at the corners, and his mother was not looking at either of them, she was looking at the bread, cutting it, and her mouth was pressed the way she pressed it when the bird got the song wrong on purpose, and the lamplight was moving on both their faces.

"You told a room of the empire's sons," his mother said to the bread, evenly, "that everyone has a fire."

"We do have a fire," Kael said, and his father stood up and went to see about the goat, who did not need seeing about, and was some time in coming back.

It was the best supper Kael could remember, and he could not have said what had been in it besides bread.

Only at the end, gathering the bowls, his mother stood a moment behind him and put her hand on his head — not the resting it did after lessons, but cupped, the whole hand, the way you hold something you are carrying across ice — one breath, two — and took it away, and said, in the voice of her ordinary evenings: "The lamp's lit. Bring the slate. Show me what this philosopher of yours thinks the world is made of."

And that was how the third school of the day began, and how the days had their shape from then on: the dark before light his father's: the wasters, the spear, the bow at distance, the body's school moved earlier and grown no smaller; the light itself the Temple's; and the lamplight his mother's, her schools gone to evening the way the birds went to the eaves, the slate between them, her questions running under the Temple's answers like water under a boat. He was ten. He crossed the whole of his known world twice a day, at its two speeds, wearing its two coats, and in the loft, past the edge of sleep, on the good nights, he could hear them below him, the two voices, low, trading the day back and forth — his day; he could tell by the shape of it — and the sound went up at the ends, and put things down, and he fell asleep inside it the way you fall asleep near a fire, in the winter, when the door is holding.


---

# Chapter Nine — The Ledger

The yard took the young cohorts together. It belonged to the master with the seam of white through his dark hair, a man built like old rope, lean and hard and used, the yard's weather in his face and knuckles like a counting-frame, who walked the way a cadence is counted. His name was Ruthen, which Kael heard exactly twice all winter, both times from a clerk. The children said *the yard-master* the way you said *the sea*.

He did not begin with weapons, though the racks stood along the south wall with wood enough in them to arm the island. He began with the roller.

It was a drum of grey stone the length of a man, kept for flattening the yard after rain, and the yard-master put his hand on it and said, "Move it to the wall," and stepped back.

They went at it the way the benches answered Priscian: severally, loudly, and with the first thing that came. Boys shoved, in twos, in fives, slipping on the frost, until Aurelian Corvane came through them and made it work: he picked the six biggest, set them low along one side, gave them a count — a real count, well given; Kael filed that — and on the third try the roller ground the width of the yard, all seven of them roaring, and reached the wall. The cohorts cheered. Aurelian stood off it with his breath tearing and his face bright, a boy standing where he stood best.

"Good," the yard-master said. "Again. Alone."

The bright went off the face by degrees, and the high carriage came down with it, half an inch, the way a banner comes down when the wind quits. It could not be done alone, and everyone knew it, and the yard-master let the knowing sit exactly as long as it wanted to sit. Then he said, to the line: "Anyone."

Kael went out, because, of course, a question was standing. There was a pry-bar in the well-shed and a chock of wood by the racks, and the rest was the spring he was eight: where a boy who came up to a thing's shoulder stood so that the thing's own bigness went to work against it. The roller came back across the yard in stiff little tips and shoves, one boy walking it the way you walk a barrel, and the yard went quiet in a way it had not been for the seven.

He set the bar down. He was not much out of breath, and he heard how that sounded in the quiet.

"Now the arithmetic," the yard-master said. "Seven of you spent everything you had, and it worked, and tomorrow your backs will tell you what it cost. One of you spent almost nothing, and it worked, and he could do it again all day." He did not look at Aurelian, and he did not look at Kael, and somehow that was worse for both of them. "Strength is a purse. Every one of you was born with one and no two are the same size. The ones who like spending always think the purse is the man."

In the line, Kael did his own arithmetic, because a piece was standing in the yard that the yard-master's had not counted. The roller had crossed the yard the first time because six of the biggest had pushed as one body, and they had pushed as one because Aurelian had set them, and given the count, and been Aurelian while he gave it. Kael could have given that count. He knew it was good; he had filed it. And nobody would have pushed. He set the fact where it belonged, beside the pry-bar: six backs, one voice — that was a purse too, and no eye could see into that one either. His own, he filed, plainly, the way he filed the weather, was empty.

Then the yard-master straightened his back, and proceeded.

"In two years, three for some of you, your art will come in, and you will want to spend it the way you wanted to shove that stone. So before I let any of you spend a grain, you will learn what spending is." He walked the line slowly while he said the rest, even, unhurried, a thing worn smooth by being true. The Art was spending. Every Resonant carried a purse of it, and no eye could see into the purse. Spending was effort. Deep spending was pain. And spending near the bottom — the well-rope's creak came into the quiet — the body wrote down forever: the hair went white, from that day, in the measure of how deep the spending had gone, and it never went dark again. The body kept the receipt. And past the bottom there was no pain and no receipt and no Resonant, because past the bottom was death, every time, however large the purse or beloved the house.

Nobody looked at the seam of white. Forty children stood in the cold and did not look at it so hard that the not-looking was a thing they did together, one animal, and Kael stood in the middle of them not looking with everyone else, and understood that this too had been taught, without one word spent on it.

---

The first resonances came at the turn toward spring, when the yard-master judged they knew what a purse was.

They were given candles. The whole apparatus of the empire's Art, that morning: a bench of tallow candles in the lee of the south wall, one child before each, and the instruction, which was to reach for the flame the way you reached for warmth through cloth, and push.

"Your art is in you already, leaning," the yard-master said. "It settles when you are grown — twelve, fourteen, when it settles it settles — but it leans from the start, and the strong lean hard. Today you spend one grain, so your body learns the door." And then, flatly, a wall being pointed at: "The Art runs in the blood, and the blood runs in the houses. That is why the Temple takes who it takes." His eyes went down the bench and passed over Kael neither faster nor slower than over anyone, and the bench understood the exception without a word.

The candles began.

For most of the bench, nothing came, or almost nothing. But down the line the leanings showed, small and unmistakable, like the first minutes of weather. A girl from a river province made her flame gutter and stand, gutter and stand, and the tallow wept too fast, and her sleeve came away damp when she wiped her face, and she looked at the sleeve. Elarine Sedrane, three down from Kael — ink-dark hair bound severe, nothing on her that did not work — made hers do exactly what was asked of it and nothing more, one push, one bright answering stand of flame the size of the push, and folded her hands.

And Aurelian's caught.

Not the wick — the wax. The whole cup of the candle's top went up in a soft orange sheet, a fistful of fire standing off the bench, sudden enough that the boy beside him went backward off his heels; and the bench had turned to him before the flame finished standing, and for one breath, before the yard-master's hand came down over it with a wet cloth that was clearly always ready, the fire lit Aurelian's face from below, and it was the face from the roller morning with the world put right again. The bench cheered him — the roller's cheer, Kael filed, come home to its owner — and something in the yard settled back into its accustomed place with an almost audible click.

"A large purse," the yard-master said, neither warmly nor otherwise. "Learn the price of it." But the bench had seen fire, and had stopped listening to arithmetic.

Kael's turn had come and gone before that, third from the end. He reached the way she had taught him to reach without ever calling it reaching: he found where the flame carried, which took no time at all; it was a candle, it carried where candles carry. He pushed, one grain, the way you press a fingertip to a pail lid to know it is down.

The flame did not move. Something happened — he would have sworn it and could not have said what: a prickle across the back of his hand, a taste in the air for half a breath, gone before it was anything — and the flame stood there, and that was all. The child beside him had already looked away. Behind him someone said, not quietly, "What did the mountain expect," and someone else said: "Somebody's blood in that line somewhere. Someone's bastard, then." A short crop of laughter, many throats. His breath found the long shape on its own; none of it showed.

The filing took longer than usual that evening, because two entries would not sit still. The doctrine said the Art ran in the blood and the blood ran in the houses. And up the mountain sat a woman born in no house any book of the Temple's had ever heard of, who had been teaching him to feel where a fire carried since before this yard had seen his face. The two facts stood in him side by side and did not touch, and he did not put them together. He put them behind the shut door, and did not stand near it long.

---

Twice a year, it turned out, the Temple wrote its children in order.

The midwinter examinations lasted four days — letters, sums, the chronicle, the natural philosophy, a day in the yard — and on the fifth day a clerk nailed the standings to the colonnade of the first court, cohort by cohort, every name in earned order, and the whole Temple came past before noon the way the village came to its water. Kael came late, in the crowd's ebb, and read up his cohort's column from the bottom, and found his name at last four lines from the top, above names with three parts to them, plain and short as a nail among rings.

In the ebb behind him a voice said, "Which one is Kael?" and a second answered it — "The charity case" — and a third read the arithmetic aloud, "Four lines from the top," in the voice you use for a wrong answer that will not stop being right.

The bench that had filled itself around him began, that same week, to have room in it.

It was not one thing. The bench. His boots, gone from where boots went during yard-work, found in the well-shed. *Charity case* arriving now from older mouths, the new word riding under it: *the bastard*, said like a thing everyone had agreed on while he was elsewhere. His body priced each as it came and declined to spend, and the declining, he began to understand, was itself the answer they could bear least.

Oss kept sitting down. That was the fixed thing in those weeks: the bench emptied around Kael and filled around him at the same time, one boy's worth of filling, arriving with the day's news and the day's names. It was Oss who told him, low, across the bread, which of them actually did things: "Corvane doesn't fetch and carry — that's what the four are for. And of the four, the one to watch is Drusane. Lucan Drusane, the long one. He works; the other three laugh where he points. Watch the pointing, not the laughing." Kael took the sentence with both hands, and it proved, like most of Oss's sentences, to be exactly true.

The spar happened because the yard ran spars, wasters out of the racks, pairs called by the master's flat voice, and in the natural course of a term the voice called "Drusane. The charity case." The yard found reasons to slow down and watch.

Lucan Drusane came to the middle a careful dresser even in yard clothes, all reach, a fencing-master's posture worn like inheritance, and did a thing with his waster: brought it up before his face, hilt at the chin, a small crisp ceremony of it, and stood waiting. "When you're ready, charity case," he said, into the waiting. "There's a form to it."

Kael stood, holding his wood the way you hold a tool between jobs, and waited for whatever the thing had been to finish, and it appeared it had finished, because the yard made a low sound and Lucan's face went from ceremony to appetite. Kael filed the gesture with the roll-book's chains: a fence, built out of movement this time. Nobody on the mountain saluted the weather.

He was good: a proper guard, a proper measure, lunges that came off a straight back like bolts off a crossbow. And at first he was strange — wasteful, Kael's eye said: the back held straight where bending was free, the blade kept to one line while whole roads stood open. But the number came back wrong, and he knew it was wrong, because the movements repeated too exactly to be waste. Waste wanders. This was built. The measure was priced for a blade with an edge and a length; the straight back was reach, spent along a lane both men were supposed to share. It was a man moving very well inside fences Kael had never stood in — a school built to fight its own graduates, asking only the questions its graduates asked. So Kael gave ground and let the school spend itself, and learned it while it spent. And when the longest lunge came — the form's best question, arriving on one straight beautiful line — Kael was a half-step off the lane the school assumed, and his boot was against Lucan's planted foot, at the ankle, where all that weight stood on one narrow decision the form had never been asked to defend. He pushed. Lucan Drusane went over his own front foot and met the yard with all of himself at once, waster ringing away over the stones.

Lucan sat up where he had landed, and did not reach for the waster. "What was that?" he said — not to Kael; to the yard, to the form of things. "That wasn't anything."

"Luck," the yard said, in several voices, deciding quickly, because the alternative did not bear standing near — and it was agreeing with him. And under the deciding ran the second offense: nothing the charity case had done had a name. No guard anyone could point to, no answer to the salute, no shape a fencing-master had ever sold. You could forgive a boy for beating you. It was harder to forgive him for doing it in no language at all. Kael did not argue with the yard. The yard-master, who had watched from the well with his arms folded, did not argue with the yard either; he looked at Kael for the length of one breath, the unhurried look, and called the next pair.

Kael went back to the line with the thing he had actually taken out of the middle, which was not the fall; the fall had been one push. It was the school. A second language of the body, whole, provable, learnable — and the itch was already at it. The yard had watched him win and decided he had no language. He had gone into the middle with one and come out carrying the first words of theirs.

Lucan's answer came in pieces, none of them signed.

The first piece was a waster with a cracked core, put back in the rack where Kael's height took its wood from: the crack seated deep under a whipping of cord, waiting for a hard parry to let it go. Kael found it in his hands in the instant of taking it up: the balance sat wrong, a dead place answering along the grain where live wood spoke whole; his father's fussing by the winter fire had taught his palms the difference. He worked that day's spar left-side-forward, keeping the dead wood out of the hard knocks — a herder-clumsy day of it — and won anyway, gracelessly, and put the waster back crack-up, so the next boy's hands would have a fair chance of finding it, and watched no one while he did it. In the corner of everything, a long boy watched him, face working through something it did not like.

The second piece landed. On the morning the spring examinations opened, Kael found his slate — the term's fair-copy sums, taken in first — wiped and fouled, the work gone, a smeared obscenity where it had been, done early and simply. There was no rebuilding four weeks of fair copy. He handed in the slate as it was, took the mark a fouled slate took, and said nothing, because he had no witness and knew exactly how far the sentence *the charity case says a lord's son crept into a room* would travel. The mark pulled his standing down one line, and the line stayed pulled, written, official — a small permanent receipt for someone else's spending.

He filed it, in the drawer with the room's changed temperature and a boy's changed face and a bench with room in it and a dead place under cord — the drawer with no name on it yet, getting heavy.

That night his mother asked what the marks had been, because she asked everything now, and he told her the number, and not the why. Both things were real. It was the family trade.

---

Spring filled the philosophy room with flies, and Priscian lectured through them.

The morning it happened he was on the elements — the six of them, their tidy fences — and he closed, as he closed every morning, with a question thrown at the benches. "The kettle, then. Water over the fire, and the water becomes air. Tell me the resonance of it, in the minima, properly. Anyone."

Elarine Sedrane answered, and her answer was the best thing Kael had heard in that room since the first day. She built it the way a wall is built, course on course: the fire's minima quickening the water's; the water's loosened, rising, taking the airy nature; each element keeping to its fence. It was complete. Priscian nodded along the length of it the way a man nods at good masonry, and had his mouth open for the next thing, and Kael — because her wall was so good; a wall that good showed you exactly the shape of what it stood on —

"Then whose is the mist?"

The room turned. He was under the stone now, so he kept going, at his own pace. "When the kettle breathes, there's mist over it. If a water Resonant can call mist off the sea, and an air Resonant can call mist out of a valley" — doctrine, both elements claimed it, the room had recited it a week since — "then the same mist comes from two arts. So when the mist is standing there, whose is it? What does the mist know that it is?" One breath more, because it was still coming: "The fences are drawn around the resonances. But the mist is only the mist. It has never heard of them."

Silence, one breath of it, with a temperature he was coming to know.

"The mist has never heard of them," Priscian said, slowly, not to Kael, not to the benches. Then he gathered himself, said, "The elements of the Art are the elements of the Art; we will come to the mixed resonances at the proper time," which answered nothing, and knew it, and let the benches go with the flies.

Elarine Sedrane had not moved through any of it. What Kael carried out was her stillness, which had changed when he asked, and then changed further, going in, the way his own went in when a pattern would not sit. She was not looking at him. She was looking at the fence-diagram on the great slate the way you look at a knot, and her face gave out nothing it had not priced, and the pricing was still running when the room emptied.

He was last out, being furniture. From the door, in the corner of everything, he saw her stylus come down on her own slate — quick, small, five words' worth of moving — and stop, and her hand lay flat over what it had written, the way you put your hand over a coin on a table.

---

The sea-wall walk was Oss's discovery, or Oss's gift; with Oss it was never fully one or the other.

It ran along the Temple's ocean side from the kitchens to the old beacon stump, out of the courts' sight, and nobody went there in the cold term but gulls. Oss took Kael out along it one grey afternoon on the strength of a mutton pastry extracted from the kitchens, and that was the afternoon Kael learned how Oss talked when there was no one to be seen talking well in front of, which was more, and lower, and true.

"You know what they say about my house," Oss said, to the sea, in the voice of a joke carried so long the joke had worn off it. "Vessaline. You can hear it, everyone can — a name bought to sound like the crown. My grandfather chose it off a list. I've seen the list." He stepped over his own house's name the way he always did, fast, a known bad board, and kept walking. "The old houses have been laughing at it since before I was born. You get good at knowing where you stand with laughing people." He said it lightly, and it was not light, and he did not look at Kael, and Kael filed, not judged, the true answer to a question he had never asked: why, on the first day, one boy had sat down.

"What's it actually like?" Oss said, at the beacon stump. "Up there. The mountain." And he stopped, and waited — really waited, the words all the way off him for once, his face open and turned — and Kael stood with the whole of it behind his teeth, the fire in its hole and the split rock and four languages and the woman at the lamp, everything true and none of it wearable, and what came out, after a silence one breath too long, was, "Quiet."

"God, I'd die," Oss said, relieved, refilled, off again like water finding its slope, "no, truly, dead in a week, the quiet alone—" and he was away, and the moment closed over itself, and both of them were served, and neither of them was met, and the gulls went over.

It was on the sea-wall, that same afternoon, that Kael got the stories. Oss kept ghost stories the way he kept house-gossip, sorted and current, and the cold term was their season. He did the witch-white one first, offhandedly, a coin already spent — "that one's just true, mind; my uncle's seen a hedge-wife gone white to the roots at forty; they spend too deep and the hair writes it down" — and then, warming, lowering, with the sea behind him doing the sound a story wants: the old one. The eyes.

"Red eyes," Oss said. "The eyes of the devil, the fishermen say — and my grandmother's people say the same from the far side of the archipelago, so it's everywhere, it's old. A Resonant's spending writes white in the hair. But there's a thing that isn't a Resonant, and when it works, the eyes go red as a coal — and where it walks, whole rooms of men die, doors and walls between mean nothing, and it never leaves one alive to say what it was. Every telling has that in it: no one left. Whole houses. Whole crews." He was enjoying himself immensely. "No one's ever seen one, of course. Because" — the grin of a boy delivering a story's oldest hinge — "seeing one is the last thing you do."

Kael watched the sea do its enormous grey work while his mind, without asking him, built a shelf and put the eyes on it: the body's two marks, one taught in a yard by arithmetic, one told on a wall by a grin. White, the receipt of spending. Red, the rumor of something that was not spending — that the fences had never heard of. He did not know why it was a shelf. Both things had gone onto it the moment the second arrived, and that evening it rode up the mountain with him, not heavy, present, all the way home.

---

"From the beginning," his mother said, when the lamp was lit and the slate was out. "Who taught?"

"The yard-master."

"How long did he sit you, before he let you spend?"

"Most of the hour."

"And his word for it?"

"Reach. Reach for the flame the way you'd reach for warmth through cloth. Then push." Kael turned the instruction over. "But it isn't reaching. It's more like listening — for where it carries."

Her stillness changed, the way still water changes when something passes under it far down.

"Does he sit you at it every day now, or just the once?"

"The once, so far. One grain, he says, until the body learns the door." He came back along his telling — the guttering candles, the weeping tallow, the fire that stood up off the wax — and then to his own. "Mine did nothing. I found where it carried and spent a grain on it, and nothing."

Her stillness changed again, at the same place, and her face gave out nothing.

"Show me," she said, in the voice of her ordinary evenings.

The candle came to the table, and his hand came up.

"Nothing yet," she said. "Feel it. Only that."

He felt it. It was a candle; it carried where candles carry; the knowing arrived inside a minute and sat there.

"Still nothing. Until the knowing is in your hands, not your eyes."

The lamp burned. The knowing went old, and boring, and sure.

"Now," she said at last. "The smallest thing you can do. Smaller than that. Spend a thread."

The flame leaned. The width of a hair, once, exactly when he asked it to, exactly the direction he asked, and stood back up.

In the yard it would have been nothing. At the lamp it was the most exact thing his hands had ever done: the yard measured what came out; she measured what it cost and where it landed. Two schools, one candle. He looked up with the next question already forming — *why smaller, when the yard wants bigger* — and met her face, and did not ask it.

"Smaller," she said, gathering the bowls, as if it were a remark about the weather. "Always smaller than you can. The ones who spend big learn what things cost. The ones who spend small learn what things are."

In the loft, one part of the day would not stay filed. The candle had answered her way and ignored the Temple's — and he did not know which difference had done the work: the long feeling, the smallness of the ask, the lamp, the being home, her. Too many things different at once.

He rolled over to sleep on it, and the dry wool crackled its small winter crackle against him, a prickle and a nothing, the way wool did.

---

# Chapter Ten — The Asking

The Temple had given him the word the autumn before, and it had sat over his mother's school ever since like a roof: the minima. The smallest grains of the world, Priscian said — all matter made of them, all matter carried by them. Kael kept the doctrine the way he kept any tool: the part his hands could test. That the grains were real he knew; two years of her school had put the feel of them in his hands before the Temple put a name to it. Whether they were truly the smallest thing, or the whole of what a thing was made of, his hands could not say — so that part went on the shelf, and he kept the part that worked: a thing's grains stood some way, and could be felt standing, and could be moved.

The Temple had words for the rest of it too. Between a man and the minima of a thing a connection could stand — his own grains waking the world's, Priscian said, as a struck string wakes its silent neighbor — and the connection was resonance, and making it was resonating, and those who could were Resonants: fire Resonants, water Resonants, each strongest in the element of his proficiency. Kael liked the words better than most of the Temple's words. They said what they did.

---

The summons came at the term's turn, the way weather came: a clerk at a door, the First Master will see his charity case. It happened each term now, Kael learned — the sponsor auditing the investment — and the Temple found it exactly as interesting as a man checking his accounts.

The study was a room with too many rooms in it. Books to the ceiling on two walls; a table of instruments — a balance, glass, a thing of nested brass rings he had no name for; and on the sill, in a row of clay pots, green things growing that had no business being green in that light. The master set out bread, and took down a jar of honey and put it by the bread without one word being said about it, and Kael looked at the jar, and the master looked at him looking, and neither of them said the thing, and it got said anyway.

It was the one room below the split rock where the coat came off.

He poured out the winter — the candle run her way at the bench, the trial that had worked, the questions stacking up behind it — and the master listened the way he had listened in the yard at nine, unhurried, putting answers away somewhere and questions on the table.

"I asked Priscian whose the mist is — the water Resonant's or the air Resonant's," Kael said. "He said the elements of the Art are the elements of the Art."

"So they are. Who drew them?"

Kael sat with it. "Men."

"Resonants, which is worse." The master turned his cup. "A fence tells you what a man owns, boy. It has never yet told you what the ground is."

Kael took that the way he took tools. Then, because it was the question under all the winter's questions: "Can a resonance be too small to see?"

"Hm." The master did not move. On the sill, the driest of the green things — a fern gone tired in its pot — lifted. All its small leaves turned together, like a bird waking, stood a breath in the thin light, and settled. Kael's eyes went from the fern to the master's hands, which had not left his cup, and back to the fern. Nothing in the room said a resonance had happened; whatever had been spent had gone wherever the smallest spendings go.

"Smaller than that," the master said, "I cannot show you indoors."

The master was a great Resonant. The fact arrived the way the biggest facts arrived, having clearly been standing there for years: the head of a school of the Art, and Kael had filed him with books and questions and honey, and the file had been true, and short.

When Kael left, the master took a book down from the shelf's end and put it in his hands. Bound, plain, thick. Kael opened it: empty, every page.

"Full books answer," the master said. "Empty ones ask. You need the second kind more." And, as Kael reached the door: "Mind the company you keep it in."

That night the book took its first pages: the candle at the bench, written in his own hand with its one changed thing, and the writing turned out to be its own kind of trial — a question put to paper came back with edges it had not had in his head. He thought about what to write at the head of the first page, because his mother's schools all had names, and his own things had names, and then he wrote nothing there. It was all one school now. It did not need a name. It needed pages.

The mist was the first thing the book hunted.

The kettle made mist every morning of his life: fire under water, and the water climbing out of itself into the air. No Art in it at all. So mist could be made without the Art — and the question of that spring was whether he could make it with the Art, and what one road knew about the other.

He tried resonance first, because it looked like the shorter road: a bowl of cold water, a thread spent into it, and wanting mist. Nothing came. Evenings of nothing. He took the failure to the lamp, because failures went to the lamp too.

"I can't make mist with a thread," he said. "There's no mist to resonate with. The kettle knows something I don't."

"Then say what the kettle does," his mother said. "Not what it makes. What it does."

"It burns under the water."

"Does the fire touch the water?"

"No. The iron is between."

"Then what reaches the water?"

"The warmth—" and he stopped, because the road had moved under him while he stood on it. "The fire never makes the mist. It only warms the water. The mist is the water leaving."

"Leaving from where?"

"The top. Where the warmth stands."

"Then you know what to try," she said, and went back to the mending, and it was as if nothing had happened on her side of the lamp, and everything had happened on his.

The next evening he cupped water in his palm and spent a thread of fire into it, at the top, the way the kettle would have — and a ghost of mist stood off his hand, one breath's worth, and was gone. He sat looking at his own palm. Then he wrote it in the book, and the writing shook a little: *the Art does what you understand.* He had not been able to make mist until he knew how the world made it without him. Two roads, one mist — and the second road had only opened because he had walked the first. He thought of the yard, pushing at its candles. Then he thought of the fences: a thread of fire, spent into water, and the air over his palm gone white. Whose was that?

Only one thing ever answered before the resonance was fully made. The dry-wool spark — the little winter crackle that lived in blankets and cold air — came when he called it. Quick, glad. Everything else in the world had to be leaned into answering; the spark arrived like it was waiting for its name to be said. He did not know what to do with that, so he did what he did with everything that had no shelf. He kept it.

---

That spring his curiosity turned to the garden. What made one bean plant stand taller than its neighbor, out of the same handful of seed. What made a row come up early, or come up late. Why the plants along the midden fence grew half again as big as the plants ten steps off, year after year, with nobody doing anything different to them.

The last question had an answer a boy could dig for: the midden plants got what the midden leaked. So he made the difference himself — a feeding, mixed out of her own schools turned around: ash, because ash was the wood minus what the fire had eaten, and the soil could eat what the fire could not; bone from the butchering, ground small; the fish-water nobody wanted.

Then he made it a trial. One hand of seed, mixed blind, split three ways; three rows in the same bed: one fed, one plain, one threaded — a thread of nature, spent in passing every evening, the way you greet a neighbor. The world's road, the bare ground, and the Art's.

And the rows had to drink alike, all season, or the trial was no trial — which was half of why the waterer got built.

The fat book had waited four years to be needed. Engines of water: wheels, screws, a river made to work. He read it differently now — not for the pictures, for the trials in it, some dead man's questions still standing — and built the garden its waterer out of it. A split-log channel from the runnel above the plot; a trough on an iron pin, hung a little off its middle; a counterstone lashed at the short end. The trough filled, and passed its balance, and tipped — one measured pour down the cuts between the rows, the same to each — and the stone stood it back up to fill again. Water, watering the garden, all day, in fair portions, with nobody there.

His father came to see it work. He stood over the plot with his arms folded and watched the trough fill, and tip, and right itself with a knock of wood on wood, and fill again — the whole round of it, three times, saying nothing. Then he crouched, stopped the trough with one finger, tested the pin's seat, and moved the counterstone in by the width of two fingers.

"It will work itself loose at the pin by summer," he said. "Ash there. Not pine." And then, standing, looking down at the thing working: "Who showed you?"

"The book. And the stone in the gate."

His father nodded, one nod, the size of every other nod, and went back up to the wood. It was the longest Kael had ever seen him stand in front of a thing Kael had made. Kael stayed crouched by the plot a while, in the sound of the water arriving in small fair portions, and let the pride sit where it sat, because there was nobody to see him let it.

His mother had watched from the doorway. When he came up to the house she was still there, one hand on the frame, and she looked at him a moment longer than the doorway needed, and what was in her face was pride, whole, and under the pride, folding itself away as he came near, the way the blade went away in the mornings, something else. That night the two voices under the loft went on longer than his day could account for, low and even, past the edge of his sleep.

The rows came in at summer's end, and the book took the answer. The fed row stood biggest, half again its neighbors. The threaded row had woken earliest — up days ahead, leaning to its light — and ended no bigger than the plain. He sat with the columns a long time, because there was more in them than growing. The thread could be replaced by dung, and dung was free. The Art and the world could do one another's work — and for a boy with a small purse, knowing which road was cheaper was worth more than either road.

His mother walked the bed with him when the count was done.

"Same seed?"

"One hand, mixed blind, split in three."

"Same sun?"

"Same bed."

"The outside rows take the wind differently."

He looked at the bed and was quiet a moment. "They do. I'll run it again next year, changed round."

"And the water?"

"The waterer. The same portions to all three, all season. That's why I built it."

She stood at the head of the rows, going down his columns with her eyes the way she went down the boats. "The feeding," she said. "Where did you learn it?"

"Nowhere. It's what fire eats, turned around. The fire takes the fat of the wood and leaves the bone — so the bone is still food, for a slower eater. The soil eats what the fire couldn't."

She looked at him the way she listened to birds, all of her. "I did not know this," she said. Plainly, a fact handed over, the way she handed him all facts. It was the first time in his life he had heard her say it of a thing he had made.

His father kept the fed row's yield for next year's seed. He did it without a word, the way he did everything — carried it up in one arm and set it in the seed corner — and it was the loudest thing anybody said about the garden all year.

He asked her, one evening at the lamp, whether the whole of it had a name — the trials, the one changed thing, the witnesses that could not lie. Her schools had names. A thing this large ought to have one.

"Science," his mother said, not looking up from the mending. "Knowing built by trial, and tested until it holds." The word sat smooth in her mouth, an old tool, worn to the hand.

Then she looked up. "Do not carry the word down the mountain."

He waited.

"The Temple teaches the Art. To the empire's houses, knowing built by trial is a servant's knowing — a thing for smiths, and hedge-doctors, and men who mend milldams. A Resonant stands above it. A scientist" — she said the word the way the village said charity case — "stands beneath one. In Priscian's room you may say natural philosophy. In the yard, say nothing."

"But their natural philosophy is—"

"The same thing in a better coat. Yes." She went back to the mending. "Wear the better coat."

Nobody in the village said science. His mother had not only the word but the map of where it could not be worn. He filed that, and when he brought her a trial half built, she met it with the next course already in her hands: hold the rest still, change one thing, run it again and see whether the answers quarrel. He had thought the method was his, grown out of one candle. She had known its shape before he built it. Somewhere, sometime, somebody had taught his mother science — the word and the way of it, in order, as a system — and village wives did not learn science as a system. He set the thought down at the door of one of the shut rooms, and did not knock.

At the summer's turn he carried the book down the mountain, because the one room where the coat came off deserved the whole of it. The master turned the pages with one finger, unhurried. At the mist he stopped.

"You made it twice. Once with a kettle, once with your hand."

"The hand only worked after the kettle. I couldn't do it until I knew what the kettle did."

"Say that again," the master said. "Slowly." And Kael said it again, slowly, and the master sat looking at him the way he had looked at him in the yard at nine, and what was in the look was not put away anywhere this time. "Hold on to that," he said at last, "the way you hold the not-knowing. Most men of the Art go into the ground without it."

At the rows he stopped again, longer. "The threaded row woke early. Who taught you the thread?"

"No one. I tried it the way I try everything."

"Hm," the master said, and turned the page, and his finger came back to that column once more before he closed the book, the way a tongue comes back to a tooth.


---

# Chapter Eleven — The Dish

The second autumn began with the dish.

He was eleven, and the Temple had decided it was time to learn what its children were. The cohort was walked into the first court on a grey morning and formed into the roll's order, and at the head of the court stood a table Kael had never seen: an iron dish the size of a cartwheel hub, and around it, in a half-ring, the plain things of the world — a candle, a bowl of water, a box of dry sand, a green sprig in a pot, a copper rod on a cloth. The yard-master stood behind the table. Priscian stood to the side with the registry clerk, and the clerk had the book, and the book was open.

"Your art has been leaning since before you knew you had one," the yard-master said, to the line, in the voice worn smooth by being true. "Today it says its name. You will spend one grain — one — at the dish, and we will watch what answers. The dish does not flatter and it does not lie." He looked down the line, unhurried. "What settles, settles. The dish only asks early."

They went up one at a time, in the roll's order, and the Art said its name in each of them, loudly or in a whisper.

For most of the bench it was a whisper — a gutter in the candle, a shiver on the water, readings the masters bent close to catch and the clerk wrote small. But the leanings that had shown early showed now like weather. The river-province girl's bowl climbed its own sides and stood in a ring, and she looked at it with her sleeve pressed to her mouth. Oss's sand rose in a slow mound and settled, like a held breath let go, and Oss beamed down at it as at a relative. "Earth," said the clerk. Lucan Drusane's grain went into the air itself: the dust on the dish lifted and wiped itself outward in one neat ring, a lane swept clean, and the clerk wrote air, and Lucan stepped back with his posture already wearing it.

Then Elarine Sedrane went to the table, and the Temple learned what her discipline had been holding.

She spent her grain the way she did everything, exactly, and the candle did not gutter. It stood. The flame rose off the wick in one straight governed column as tall as her forearm, bright enough to put color on the iron, and held there, not wavering, the size she had set it — and then she shut it off clean, all at once, the way a door closes. The court was quiet. Everyone had priced Sedrane small, because she was small, and neat, and never spent. The flame said what the purse said: there was a great deal in there, and it was all obedient.

"Fire," said the clerk.

Aurelian Corvane's fire surprised nobody and was cheered anyway — the wax of the whole candle going up in the soft orange sheet the yard remembered, the yard-master's cloth coming down over it with the patience of long habit. Two fires in one cohort, the benches whispered, and both of them Corvane's tier; the order of the world held.

Then the clerk read the next name, and it was his.

Kael went to the dish through a court that had already decided what it would see. The mountain's candle was a settled matter; the whisper had explained him years ago. He stood where the others had stood, and found the dish and its half-ring of the world already familiar — water, fire, sand, the sprig; he had spent threads into all of them in a loft and a garden nobody here could imagine — and he did what he had done at the workings bench for two years. He felt first. Then he spent his grain.

The crack was small and white and absolutely clear.

It came off his fingers and went to the copper rod like a thing going home — one thin stitch of light, a hand's width, there and gone, with a sound like a knuckle against glass. Kael stood looking at his own hand, and in his chest something opened like a door onto a room he had owned all his life without the key: the dry-wool spark. The little winter crackle that had answered before the resonance was fully made, that had waited for its name to be said. It had been saying its own, all along.

At the book, the clerk's pen paused over the word, having so little habit of it.

"Lightning," said the yard-master, for him.

Kael walked back to the line with his face at the court's temperature and his heart doing something entirely different, and the whisper was already remaking itself behind him — lightning, said the benches, lightning — because lightning was rare, rare enough that no one in the cohort had ever seen it declared, rare enough that the old whisper had to be rebuilt to hold it. Somebody's blood in that line somewhere had been a sneer for two years. He heard it said again now, behind him, in a new voice. It had stopped being an insult and started being a theory.

He carried it up the mountain that evening the way you carry a coal in a pot.

He poured the day at the lamp — the dish, the half-ring, the column of Sedrane's fire, the white stitch off his own fingers — and his mother listened with her whole body, and asked her close exact questions, what the masters said and what the clerk wrote and who stood where. His father sat with the harness strap he was mending and did not stop working it, except once. Kael did not see what his hands did, because he was talking. He saw the strap lie still for a breath, and then go on being mended.

"Lightning," his mother said, the way you check the last knot of a load.

"Lightning." He heard his own voice do something around the word, and did not try to stop it. "It's rare. The clerk's pen didn't know how to write it. It — of everything at that dish, it's the only thing that ever answered me like it wanted to."

His parents looked at each other — one look, level, like a weight being set down after a long carry. His father put the strap aside.

"Mine as well," his father said. "Lightning."

The room did what a room does when a wall shows a door in it. Kael sat very still. His father held up one hand, palm cupped, and for a breath nothing — and then a thin white crack stitched itself across the hollow of it, twin to the one at the dish, there and gone, with the same small glass-knuckle sound. His father looked at it the way he looked at weather, and picked the strap back up.

"Air," his mother said. No demonstration came with it. She poured his milk. "You will not ask the next hundred questions tonight."

He did not. He sat between his two parents — two Resonants, who had never once in eleven years shown him the Art, whose one school of it had come to him in drops, small, smaller than you can — and the whole of his childhood turned over once in his head and lay back down in a different position. The telling had been given the way her tools were given: one tool, whole, on the day his element got its name, and nothing said about the years it had waited in the drawer. He slept the sleep of a boy who had been told by the world itself that he was something, and that he was theirs — and below him, past the edge of that sleep, the two voices traded the day long into the dark.

---

The yard changed within a fortnight, because the empire did not teach its children the Art in order to watch them wrestle.

The measure was drawn fresh and wider. Sand barrels came to stand at its corners. The declared were taught the stance the fencing-masters sold — weapon hand and art hand, the waster forward, the off-hand open and empty at the shoulder, for the Art — and the yard-master walked the line adjusting elbows exactly as he had always adjusted them, as if fire were a kind of footwork. The rules were three, and he said them once: nothing above the shoulders. Nothing the healer cannot mend by supper. And his own presence, which was not called a rule but was the largest of them: he stood the duels the way the split rock stood the path.

The first resonance duels of the second cohort were, mostly, bad. Fire that missed. Air that shoved its own man. A girl who spent her whole purse in the first exchange and had to sit down on the ground, grey-faced, and be walked through the purse lecture again from the beginning, kneeling. Kael watched every one of them from the line the way he had once watched the sea, and the itch in him was awake the whole time, because this was new, all of it — the Art walking around in bodies, making mistakes, having costs — and new things could be read.

Elarine Sedrane went to the well before the pairings on a rain-grey morning and spoke to the yard-master, three words, maybe four. The yard-master looked at her the way he looked at the sky. Then the flat voice called: "Sedrane. The charity case."

The yard slowed down the way it had slowed for Drusane, and differently. Aurelian came off the rail to watch, his four arranging themselves. Lucan stood at the edge, very still, face already at its sums. Behind Kael, Oss said, low, to nobody, "Oh no."

She came to the middle with her waster in one hand and the off-hand open at her shoulder, and made the taught salute — and before its echo finished, her open palm was burning.

She initiated: a flat fan of flame sweeping from her palm at knee height, crossing the lane his next step wanted, so that the step went elsewhere, and her wrist was already turning, and the elsewhere was crossed too. She walked him. Sweep after sweep, unhurried, each the size it needed to be, cutting his lanes like a woman closing shutters, and the heat of them reached his face from three strides off. The rain hissed into her fans and took nothing from them. Between sweeps the fire did not go out. It stood at her palm, a handspan of flame burning on nothing at all — no wick, no wood, rain falling past it — the way a lamp burns when somebody is paying for it and intends to keep paying all night. Her breathing had not changed since the salute.

Kael gave ground and read. She had watched every duel of the fortnight from the same line he had, and it was all here. When he slid off-lane the way he had against Drusane, her line had already turned to meet it — the answer bought before he had asked the question. When he feinted at the gap by the north barrel she declined it and spent nothing, her eyes doing over the flames what his own were doing: pricing. That gap stayed open exactly long enough to be almost trusted, and when his weight leaned toward it, it closed with a sweep of flame that scorched the air where his chest would have been.

He went through the next gap because it was that or the barrels, and paid for it: the fan that closed it caught him across the shoulder — a wet-cloth slap of heat, cloth scorching, pain arriving real and entire. The yard made its sound. Kael's count did not break. He filed the pain the way he filed weather, at a distance that had taken two years to build, and he took the burn's price for the burn's information: her fans rose no higher than his ribs. Nothing above the shoulders. She fought inside the rules the way she answered inside the doctrine. Correct, all the way down.
