Fire resonance is bad close. Everyone knows it the way everyone knows the sea is wide; she knew it best of all, and had spent the whole fight making sure close never happened. So he bought close with the two smallest threads he owned.

The first went into the ground — not across it. He could no more set the Art down at a distance than anyone in that yard could. He put it down through his own boot. The ground was touching him; the ground was touching her; it was one connected thing the whole way between them, and a thread spent down through his heel ran along the packed dirt the way sound runs along a beam, to the one place her lead foot kept returning to. Her reset came down — the same reset, the third time, exact as everything about her — onto a thumb's depth of dirt gone loose as garden soil, and rain-slick. Her ankle wrote the surprise before her face did; the reset became a stumble the size of half a step.

The second thread went into the back of his own hand, into the rain that stood on it, as he closed and struck at her face — a strike that was never going to land, thrown slow enough to be read, because she read everything. She read it. Her guard came up correct; her eyes tracked his hand all the way in — and the rain on it flashed to steam. It burst off his knuckles in a white breath, into her face, scalding — a kettle's worth, no more — and for half a breath Elarine Sedrane was blind and stung and her weight was on a foot that had stopped being ground.

The waster took her below the ribs, flat and hard, and swept through her planted leg, and she went down onto the rain-dark yard with the fight knocked sideways out of her — and came up onto an elbow with her teeth showing and her off-hand already rising, and the air over her palm had begun to shiver with heat when the yard-master's voice arrived like the bar of a door.

"Enough."

Everything stopped. The rain fell into the measure. Kael stood with his sleeve smoking faintly and his shoulder screaming quietly, and Elarine sat propped in the dirt with a red bloom coming up across her cheekbone and her governed column of a temper banked back down by visible effort, one notch at a time.

"The match is the charity case's," the yard-master said, flat, to the clerk-of-the-yard, and then, to the line, in the voice of a man adjusting an elbow: "Sedrane fought the best duel this yard has seen this fortnight. She lost it to the ground and a kettle. Go and think about that." And he called the next pair.

The laughter came late, and wrong-footed, and less sure of itself than it wanted to be — a girl put down by the charity case was a verdict, but the yard had seen the fans, and the column at the ceremony, and the yard was no longer entirely sure what it had seen since. Aurelian Corvane, at the rail, did not laugh at all. Kael filed that without a place to put it. And Lucan Drusane watched Kael's smoking sleeve with his face doing an arithmetic that had stopped liking any of its numbers a long time ago, and added the line anyway.

They ended side by side at the healer's bench, which nobody had planned, on the plank worn smooth by generations of the mended, his shoulder and her cheek taking the same salve out of the same pot.

"I had you priced," she said, to the wall in front of them. "Range. Pace. The rules. The wet." Each word set down like a weight proved false.

"You did," Kael said. "You read the strike right, too. That's why the steam worked." He considered whether to say the rest of it, and said it, because it was true and she had paid for it: "I aimed it at your reading."

Her head turned. The red bloom on her cheek was going to be a bruise, and her eyes over it priced him for a long moment the way she priced everything — looking for the gloat in it, finding none, recalculating.

"The ground," she said. "That was earth. Your dish said lightning."

"The dish said what's loudest. The others still answer, if you understand what you're asking them to do. Small." He looked at his hands. "Everything I do is small."

"Hm," she said, in the voice of someone declining, on principle, to be fascinated. Then she looked back at the wall, and Kael looked at her looking, and something went wrong in his chest on the way to nothing at all.

He had read faces all his life — for weather, for danger, for the size of the trouble he was in. He had never yet stood this close to one and lost his footing on it: pale, severe, one cheek ruined by his own steam, a stray lock of ink-dark hair come loose from its binding and ignored as if it had personally failed her — and his chest filed a report he had not asked it for, and when she said, "Tomorrow you will show me the ground trick," his answer came out in the wrong order.

"Yes. The — after the yard. If you show me the fans. Slowly. I want to know what having that much fire to spend is like, deciding — how you decide what not to spend." He rebuilt the sentence the way he rebuilt a knot. "You have reasons for everything you do. I want to learn the reasons."

It was the longest thing he had ever said to anyone at the Temple, and she priced it, visibly, and it came up honest.

"Tomorrow," Elarine Sedrane said. "Not here." And she took her salved cheek off the bench and away down the colonnade, no thanks given, nothing agreed, and an arrangement existed between them the way weather exists, without anyone having signed it.

That evening he poured the day at the lamp, most of it. The duel he gave whole — his mother's questions ran close and exact over the ground-thread and the steam, and twice her stillness changed at the same place, the way still water changes when something passes under it far down. One part he did not pour, because he could not find the handle on it: the moment at the bench when his words had come out in the wrong order. He filed it, in the end, on the shelf with the mist — the shelf of things that answered before he had finished understanding the question. The spark had lived there two years, and the spark had turned out to be his own element calling him by name. He fell asleep without wondering what that meant for the other things on the shelf.

---

By the cold term the hall had rearranged itself, the way halls do, without anyone moving a bench. The story of the duel wanted telling, and Oss was the best teller alive, and the middle of the hall was where telling paid — so that was where Oss mostly was now, his hands drawing fans of fire in the air over the tables, giving the yard back to itself with better timing. Kael ate at the bench's end and watched the performance the way he watched weather, and did not judge it. The seat beside him stayed Oss's. It just stood empty more than it had.

But the sea-wall stayed theirs. That was the thing about Oss that Kael filed and kept filed: the middle of the hall got the timing, and the wall got the truth.

"Two houses have asked my father about you," Oss said, out at the beacon stump, with the gulls wheeling on the grey and a stolen pastry divided two ways. He said it low and fast, the way you hand something over while it is hot. "Don't ask me which — no, ask me, I'll tell you this much: Corvane wasn't one of them. Houses with lightning somewhere back up the line, both of them. They asked what the charity case's sponsorship costs. Whether the First Master holds you or the Temple does. What your mother's name is."

The last one went through Kael like the cold. "Why my mother's name?"

"Because that's where blood-theories go, mountain boy." Oss pulled his cloak tighter, and looked, for a moment, entirely unlike a boy at his trade. "Lightning is rare. Rare gets explained. A herder with a rare art is a story with a hole in it, and holes get filled — with a name if they can find one, with a better story if they can't. My grandfather bought a name; I know how this works from the inside. They will not ask you anything. They'll ask around you. They already are."

"Why tell me?"

Oss opened his mouth for the joke, and set it down. "Because my father would sell them the answers," he said. "If he had them. It isn't even wickedness. It's just the trade." He shrugged, the way he stepped over his own house's name. "I wanted you to hear the water moving before it reached you. Nobody did that for us."

The gulls went over. Kael thought about thanking him and could not find the shape of it, so he gave what he had instead.

"Ask me the mountain question again."

"What?"

"From last winter. What it's like. Up there."

Oss stared at him. Then he sat forward the way he had sat forward at this same stump a year ago, the words all the way off him, his face open and turned — and Kael told him. Not the whole of it; the coat stayed on. But true things, arranged true: the loft and the ladder, and the goat who had hated him from birth on principle, and the winter the snow reached the eaves, and the waterer — the trough and the stone that watered the garden all day in fair portions with nobody there. Oss laughed at the goat, the real laugh, not the trade one, and then asked four questions in a row about the waterer, every one of them good, and for the length of the cold walk back neither of them was performing anything. Both of them were served — and this once, a little, met.

At the door of the hall Oss stopped him, a hand on his arm, which Oss never did. Kael's body filed the hand and let it stay.

"One more thing, and then I'm done being wise, it's exhausting." He let go. "You know where you stand with laughing people. You learned that fast; I watched you learn it. Now comes the other lesson. Smiling people. Laughing people cost you standing. Smiling people cost you things you don't get back."

Then his face put its trade back on the way you put on a coat, and he went toward the middle of the hall, and Kael went toward the end of the bench, and that was the shape of that winter: the wall and the hall, the truth and the timing, both things real.


---

# Chapter Twelve — The Telling

The storm-weeks shut the island at the heart of the cold term, the way they did every winter: the sea got up and walked, the yard went to standing water, and the drills moved in under the colonnade until the yard-master himself conceded the measure to the weather.

It was the stories' season. In the storm-weeks the stories came indoors where the audiences were, and grew to the size of the rooms.

The one that mattered came in with the carts.

A kitchen boy brought it to the middle tables, hot from the source and proud of the carrying: his uncle carted for the salters, his uncle had it from the village, and the village had it from no one, because it had happened to them. "Two winters gone," the boy said, the tray still on his hip. "The houses down past the boat-sheds. A whole crew of men, and the morning after the feast night every one of them dead where he sat. The one night of the year the whole village is down at the water." He set the tray down. "Not a mark of a fight on them. Not a thing taken. And no one left to say what came. Not one."

Kael was at the bench-end, being furniture, and furniture hears everything.

The arithmetic ran before he could decline it. Two winters gone — he had been nine years and a winter old. The houses down past the boat-sheds. The one night everyone was at the water. The sums arrived into rooms that were already furnished, the date and the place each walking to its chair and sitting down, the fact he had never had, two years of never having it: *the men died.* All of them. Dead where they sat, said a boy with a tray, at midday, over bread. The end of his own night, arriving by cart.

Kael went on eating.

The middle tables, meanwhile, had begun to work.

The raider theory stood up first and fell first: raiders mark what they touch and take what they come for, and this had done neither. Fever went down slower — somebody's father had once ridden past a fever port, and the telling of that took its own while — but fever did not empty a crew between supper and morning and leave the village standing around it. Somebody said bad fish, and got what he had asked for, a crop of laughter, quick and glad to be let off the strangeness, and it went over the dead men and was gone, and Kael's hands stayed quiet on the bread, and none of it showed.

"It was the *red eyes*," a girl said, from down the board. Not loudly. It did not need saying loudly; half the tables had been waiting for somebody else to say it.

And the comparing, when the comparing began, went where the trading of stories went now — to the middle of the hall. To Oss.

Kael watched his friend take the story the way his mother took mushrooms out of a basket. It was exactly that, and he filed the exactness and kept it: every telling of the eyes the hall owned, laid out cap beside cap against the carter's tale, spine matched to spine. Whole rooms of men, said the legend; a whole crew, said the cart. Doors and walls between them meaning nothing; dead where they sat, in their own houses, on the far side of their own walls. And the spine of spines, the one the legend never told without: no one left. "Everything else changes with the teller," Oss said, his hands doing the tellings for him, one after another, the same shape every time. "Where it walks. What it wants. That, it always has. No one walking away is how you know."

The hall had its answer then, and set about improving it. By the second telling of the afternoon the doors had been barred from the inside. By the third, the lamps in the dead houses had burned until dawn, which no one could have known and everyone now knew. A boy from the north stair supplied the light itself: a glow off the windows the color of a coal, seen from the boats — seen by no one, which was the seeing. The story ate from every bench and grew, the way stories grew in the storm-weeks, and the kitchen boy, watching his property improve in other hands, took it back for the finish that was his by carrying-rights.

"It came down off the mountain," he said. "On the longest night." And he pointed — through the wall, up, at the weather.

The hall looked where he pointed, at stone and lamp-smoke, at the mountain every one of them had walked beneath for years, and somebody said the obvious thing.

"The charity case lives up there." A beat, the length it took the hall to see it too. "Ask him to introduce it to his goats."

The laugh went the length of the hall, and it was a big laugh, the joke being the size of the gap in it: the goat boy, the empty purse, the smallest resonance the yard had ever measured, keeping house on the devil's own mountain. The order of the world held by laughing. Kael's breath found the long shape on its own; his face stayed at the hall's temperature; and not one face, in all the laughing length of the room, turned to him with anything in it but the joke.

He sat inside the laugh until it spent itself, and thought, in the cold clean place where the counting lived: the hall had a story with his night inside it the way a pearl has sand inside it, and no one in that hall knew but him.

He had done one other thing with the afternoon, the one thing that was his to do: he had listened to every version once, all the way through, the way you walk a snare line. He had been listening for two people.

The story had no girl in it. Not the carter's version, not any bench's. No woman either; *no one left* had gone through those houses and taken everyone, tidied the living into the dead for the sake of the spine. And the question he had carried out of that yard two winters ago, the one his night had never answered, stood up in him at the second telling and stayed standing the rest of the afternoon: whether the line had held. Four words would have answered it. *Was there not a girl?* Four words, and no way on earth to say them, because the boy who asked would be the boy who knew the story had people missing from it, and there was no such boy in the hall; there was furniture, eating bread. It was the hardest stillness anyone had ever asked of him, and nobody had asked. He held it.

And the story had no boy in it. In no version, from no bench, did anything see a boy — at a wheel, in a yard, on the long dark of the mountain road. The story had tidied him away with the others.

The road home ran up out of the weather with the day in its files. Where it bent above the village the roofs lay under their early lamps, and downshore, past the boat-sheds, the cluster stood the way it always stood, and he did not look at it longer than he looked at anything. The village had put his night up in salt, he thought, at the bend — wrong in every grain, and keeping.

It came down off the mountain on the longest night.

He did not stand near that one long.

The lamp was lit when he came in, and the slate was out, and he gave her the day: the colonnade drills, the drowned yard, the yard-master conceding the measure to the weather, a hall with nothing to do but talk. The day whole, and in the middle of it a hole, cut clean, the shape and size of one story. He was good at holes. And if her questions passed near the hole, nothing in her face or her hands said so, and something in him went easier at that, and he let it.

---

The storm-weeks ended overnight, the way they always ended, as if the winter had been called home. The sea lay down. The yard drained. Within days the first sails of the turn came up the coast — earlier than the fishing wanted, more of them than the fishing needed.

The news came with them, and it did not walk up the island. It ran.

Kael met it on the road before any master said a word: the village below the bend had changed its sound. New timber stood on the harbor front. Pitch rode the morning air with no boats hauled out to earn it. The village was readying — early, in every corner at once, for something not yet in sight — and the pattern had a hole in it exactly where the name should be.

Oss had the name by midday. Of course Oss had it; the news had come by water, and everything that came by water passed through a Vessaline ledger before it reached a Temple clerk.

"A Grand Tournament," he said, at the sea-wall, over the opened water, dividing the pastry. "Here. Two springs from now. All three schools — the capital, the north, us. The First Master will announce it soon." He handed Kael his half. "In my house it's called what it is: the biggest market this island will ever see."

Kael took the pastry and the fact together. Two springs. He would be thirteen.

"And after that it's done, you know," Oss said, to the water. "For us. A few more years and the Temple hands us on. Corvane to the armies. Drusane to the armies. Half the benches to the armies, and the ones who can't fight, to the coin and the councils. That's me, in case you were wondering. I fight like a sack of flour."

"You count well," Kael said, because it was true and somebody should say it. "You see where things stand before anyone moves. That's most of fighting."

Oss looked at him for a moment with his mouth still open around the next joke. "You're the only person on this island who would say that and mean it," he said, and looked back at the sea, and when he spoke again his voice had gone to the wall's register, low and true. "You know what it's all for, don't you. The yard. The boards. Cost before craft, cohort by cohort, year over year." He tipped his head back at the whole of the Temple behind them. "My father says it with the shutters closed: the empire doesn't keep three schools to make philosophers or simple resonants. It's a forge that calls itself a school. They're not teaching us, mountain boy. They're making us. And the tournament is the empire coming to look at the blades."

The gulls went over. Kael turned the sentence the way he turned a knot, and could not find the wrong in it, and filed it where the true things went.

"So," Oss said, lighter, but not much. "When they hand everyone on. No house holds your paper — you're the only boy here who gets to be asked. What do you want to be?"

Kael looked at the water, all the way out, to where it stopped being color and started being distance.

"I want to go past it," he said. He had never said it out loud before, to anyone, and the saying did something in his chest that the wanting alone had never done. "The sea. I want to see what's on the other side of it."

"And then?"

"I don't know." He gave the not-knowing the way she had taught him to give it, with both hands.

Oss was quiet for the length of a wave. "I'm going to stand in a counting-house in the capital," he said, "watching other men's cargo. And one day somebody will tell me some mountain boy wielding lightning went past the sea, and I'll say I split a pastry with him once, and nobody will believe me."

Footsteps came along the wall, exact and unhurried, and stopped.

"You're late," Elarine said.

"The bell hasn't rung," Kael said.

"It should've. It's late. You're late."

Oss waved them off grandly, restored to himself at once. "Go on, then. The secret school. What is it you two even do up there — hold hands with a kettle?"

"Learn," Elarine said, without turning.

"Terrifying," Oss said, and went back to the pastry.

"We trade," Kael said, because it was true. "What she knows for what I do." And Oss's laugh — the real one — followed them the whole length of the wall, and Kael filed that a joke had happened somewhere in the exchange, and could not find where.

---

The Temple assembled on the third day.

The whole of it was called to the first court, cohort by cohort in the roll's order, and the First Master came out onto the steps and stood there until the stillness had finished arriving. He did not say it grandly either. He said that in the second spring from this one the Grand Tournament would convene; that the three schools of the empire would gather to hold it; and that they would hold it here — the island, the Temple, this court and the yards and the harbor below. The words went out over the ranks plainly, one fact at a time, a man setting stones on a table.

"You have two years," the First Master said, at the end. "The tournament is sure to bring great glory to you, and your families. *If* you're prepared. Spend them well."

The court held its stillness one breath past him, and then broke.

It did not stop breaking for days. The benches reweighed themselves overnight; the boards stopped being twice-yearly weather and became a ladder standing against something; every hour in the yard had a date on it now. The best of the capital school and the best of the northwest were coming here — to be seen, and to see — and the hunger that had always lived in the cohorts quietly came up and walked around in the open. Aurelian Corvane took the news the way a banner takes wind. Elarine's stillness changed at the announcement and did not change back.

"Two years," she said, at the well, to no one in particular, pricing something far off.

"The biggest market this island will ever see," Oss said. "Well, I'll probably lose early." He smiled toward Kael. "If you make it all the way, do remember me when you're famous."

And Kael stood in the middle of the broken stillness of the court with the want standing up in him at its full height, and let it stand. Three schools. The whole empire's children, the ones its houses had been building since birth, brought to one island to be measured — and a dish had already said what he was, and the dish did not flatter and did not lie. He wanted it the way he had wanted the lights: with his whole body, leaning.

---

The mornings with his father had changed, and the one thing changed all of it: the threading.

"Lightning is a tricky element. It is the element most truly spent from the body," his father said, the first morning, in the voice of moving a lesson from one shelf to another. "The body is busy. You and the storm will learn to be busy together."

So now the wasters worked while small threads of lightning lived in his off hand — the smallest things he could hold, held — and the first mornings taught him the size of what he had been asked. The threads died at every hard parry. The sword slowed whenever the threads lived. His father watched the two of them fail to share one boy with no expression at all.

"If the threading dies, you are a boy with a sword," he said. "If the sword stops, you are a boy with a trick. Neither wins. Now look."

His father began. He threaded the lightning through his own hand, and put the hand through the trunk of a tree, wrist-deep, as if the wood were wool, and drew it out. The tree stood there with a hole through it.

Kael read the holding, not the hole. Lightning kept inside the shape of a hand, spent at exactly the rate the hand could bear, an edge finer than anything on the wall at home. His father had not carried a blade to that tree. He had made one out of a part of himself, and then unmade it.

The next question came up on its own, the way they did. If it could be done to a hand, it could be done to what the hand held.

*What could it not cut?*

By the turn of the month there had come one exchange — one — where the parry landed and the threading lived through it, and his father saw it, because his father saw everything, and nodded, one nod, a half smile, and called the next stroke.

---

That evening he carried the announcement up the mountain whole. The day was too big for holes.

He gave it at the lamp with his blood still up — the court, the steps, the three schools, two springs, the island itself the host — and his mother's questions came close and exact, the way they always came: who had announced it, and in what words. How many would come. How long the island would hold them. Which houses. He answered and answered, and somewhere in the answering he heard his own voice lean forward, heard the want get into it, and did not call it back. Two years was enough. Two years of the yard and the boards and the dawn school and the lamp — the dish had said what he was, and in two springs the whole empire would be standing in the one place built to see it.

His parents looked at each other, one look, level.

"You will not enter it," his father said.

The answer arrived whole, and instantly, and from both of them at once — his father saying it, his mother's stillness saying it under him — and it arrived fast. Faster than any answer he had ever been given in that house, to anything, as if it had stood ready behind the door for years, waiting only for the knock. He filed the speed of it, far down, under everything else that was happening in him, because the speed was wrong and there was no room in him to look at wrong things now.

"Why," he said.

No reason came. Reasons had never come with the laws; that was what made them laws. He had traded the floor for facts, board by board, year by year, and they had traded with him — the caps opened all the way, the not-knowing given like a tool — and now the oldest register in the house came down off its shelf, bare, and was set between them. It was meant to hold. It had always held.

Kael looked at the law lying on the table, and did not pick it up.

"Then I have other questions," he said. His voice stayed level, because they had built it to stay level, and his hands went flat on the table, because hers went flat when a thing mattered. "Nobody at the Temple learns what I learn. Nobody anywhere. Four languages no one on this island speaks — one of them you keep between yourselves and the master. I have no surname; the clerk drew a line where the other boys have fences. You are both Resonants, and in eleven years I never saw one thread until just recently. My mother sits the dark with a blade across her knees, watching a sea she has already counted." He set the items down the way his father set stones on a table, one at a time, his heart hurrying under the slow voice they had built him, and he did not stop. "Why?"

The fire ate what it was given. Nobody moved.

"What are we hiding from?"

His father looked at him for a long time, the way he did. Then he said, "Everything you have said is rational."

Kael waited. Waiting was a thing his father had taught him, and he spent all of it back now, at this table, on him.

"You have never been punished for a question in this house," his father said. "You will not be now. So hear the whole of what we can give you." His hands came together on the table and were still. "All of it is one answer. The languages, the rules, the watch, the No — one answer, not many. We are not saving it for when you are older. It is not a story. It is a weight, and it kills. It has killed people who carried it. It has killed people who only stood near it."

"Two springs from now," his mother said, "this island will be the most watched ground in the empire. Every eye the empire owns, in one court, for a season." Her voice was the voice of every lesson he had ever had. "This house lives because it is not looked at."

He looked from one of them to the other, and the itch and the want and the anger — he had not known until that breath that it was anger — stood together in him with nowhere to go.

"And if I need more answers?"

"If the day ever comes that you must have them," his mother said, "you will have them whole. And you will wish, that day, that you could give them back. Pray the day never comes."

And she let him see her face while she said it. All his life the fear had lived under the slow voice. Tonight she set it out where he could look at it, deliberately.

He ran it, because running things was what he was. Years of rules stood up one by one and walked to the answer and sat down around it — the fire in its hole, the boats counted, the blade in the dark, the smallness of every lesson — and nothing in him could find the lie. The answer had no face, no name, no shape, and it fit everything. That was the worst thing at that table: he believed them.

"You will stay enrolled, of course," his mother said. "The Temple. The yard. Every school in this house. Everything we should give you, you will have." He heard it all and yet he knew; the Temple, the yard, his parents' teachings: it is not everything there is. And he yearned for more. For more and more every passing day. He yearned so deeply it scarred him deep inside where no one could see.

He sat very still, and none of it showed, and both of them read all of it anyway.

"May I go up," Kael said.

He lay in the loft with the want still standing at its full height and nowhere to put it. There was nothing to count. No pattern to crack, no board to go around, no lazy place — because the wall was not a wall this time. They had cut him a road once, through the middle of the itch. Now the road ran two springs out to a gate, and the gate was them. And behind the gate there was something real now — shapeless, nameless, large enough to kill — and knowing it changed nothing about the want. Both things were real.

Below him, the two voices did not trade the day.

---

The master came up the mountain nine days after his own announcement, at dusk, and stayed past dark.

The boy was asleep when the three of them finally spoke. They had waited him out — the loft gone quiet, the breathing above gone long and even — and even then the master turned his cup once and listened to the house before he began.

"Two houses have written to me," he said. "Formal inquiries. The sponsorship, its terms, whether it can be bought out from under me." A pause the length of a swallow. "A third did not write. A third sent a man, who asked the clerks nothing at all and stayed three days."

"Asking what," Vask said.

"Everything one asks without asking. Where the boy sleeps. Who feeds him." The cup went around once more. "They ask after his mother and father."

The fire ate what it was given. Neris's hands lay flat on the table and did not move.

"He declared lightning in front of the whole roll," the master said. "Lightning is rare, and rare is a story, and stories get finished by somebody. You know that better than I do."

"Eleven years," Vask said. "The door has held."

"The door has held because nobody pushed it. In two springs the empire itself camps below your mountain. Officers. Courtiers. Men whose trade is faces."

"We told him no," Vask said. "He will not enter it."

"I know. I watched the no walk around my Temple on him for a week."

"He should have a life," Neris said. Her voice was even. "A safe one. Small, if small is the price. He was not—" She stopped. When she went on, it was a different sentence. "He will not be spent."

"The tournament comes," Vask said. "The tournament goes. The island empties. He finishes what schooling there is to finish, and then he is free of all of it — the coat, the counting, all of it. That is the whole plan. Sit still while it passes."

The master was quiet for a while. Above them the roof beams ticked with the cold.

"You have built better than you will let yourselves see," he said at last. "I read what he writes. I have watched him take apart children the empire spent fortunes making, using dirt and a kettle. He is stronger than either of you says aloud, and his mind—" he set the cup down "—his mind is not going to sit still while anything passes. You know what is in him. You put it there. He will go out into the world. Not because he disobeys you. Because he is even more than what you made, and what you made does not keep on a shelf. The only question any of us is fit to answer is how he goes. Held, or loose. Prepared, or surprised."

Neris answered him. She answered in the downhill language, low and fast, three sentences with no smile inside them, and the master listened, and replied in the same tongue, one word.

Whatever the word was, neither of the others took it up. Vask rose and barred the door, iron into stone, the sound the house made when it meant held.

And above them, past the boards, the boy slept the way the trained sleep — still, one arm over the blanket's edge — while below him, low, in the small orange light, the three people who had carried him this far moved the world by inches around his bed.


---

# Chapter Thirteen — The Coat

The fever did not break with the spring. It set.

The tournament was two years out, and the Temple lived as if it were two months: the boards mattered in a new way, the yard mattered in a new way, and boys who had never volunteered for anything now stood extra hours in the rain because somewhere in the capital and the northwest, other boys were standing them too. The masters fed it. Ruthen ran the duels longer. Priscian assigned the chronicle's accounts of tournaments past, and for once nobody dozed. Aurelian Corvane trained like a banner in a steady wind, and his tier trained around him, and the whole island leaned toward the second spring the way a bench of candles leans toward a draft.

Kael climbed the boards one line that spring and held it, and worked, and said nothing, and the want stood in him at its full height all year and was given nothing to eat.

The dawn school fed something else, and it fed on him.

The threading, it turned out, was a school with a price of admission his father had never paid. A thread held quiet in a still hand was one thing; a thread held through the shock of a parry was another animal entirely, and that winter he learned what the difference cost. His father's purse bought margin: a thread of his could waver, lose its line for half a breath, and the purse absorbed the slop the way a big man absorbs a stumble. Kael had no margin. His threads were the smallest things the Art could make — that was the whole of his style, smallness made exact — and a small thread that lost its exactness under impact had nowhere to go. It did not die. It burst.

The first time, it took him at the wrist. His own lightning, arriving home — a white bite up the forearm that dropped the waster out of his hand and dropped him after it, and he sat in the frost with the arm ringing like struck iron and his eyes streaming, and learned in one lesson what the stakes of this school were. The burn was small. It was the size of the thread. That was the small purse's whole mercy and its whole cruelty in one: he would never have enough to maim himself. He had exactly enough to pay, and pay, and pay.

His father stood over him and did not help him up.

"I can be wrong by a finger's width," his father said. "The purse forgives me. Nothing forgives you. So you will be exact instead."

He was burned three hundred and twenty times that spring. He counted, because counting was his. Little white-pink speckles came up on the wrist and the back of the hand, the Art writing its own receipts.

---

The judge came with the first ships of the season, two springs early, and the Temple found this perfectly natural, because preparations had made early natural: surveyors on the headland, factors in the village, and now a judge of the games, come to walk the ground the empire would be measured on. His name was Halden. The hall said it once and then said "the judge," the way you said "the sea."

Kael met him in the first court, which was to say the judge met Kael, in the way that things kept happening to Kael that had the shape of accidents and the timing of appointments. He was a broad, comfortable man with grey at his temples and a voice like a warm room, and he stood watching the young cohort's drills from the colonnade until the yard-master called water, and then he was somehow beside Kael at the trough, remarking on footwork.

"You put your feet down like a man twice your age," the judge said, pleasantly. "Where does a boy learn that?"

"Goats, mostly," Kael said. "Scree. If you put a foot wrong on the mountain you learn, and if you don't learn you stop being someone who puts feet anywhere."

The judge laughed — a real-sounding laugh, easy, the kind that invited you to spend one back. Kael gave him the smile the coat wore, which cost nothing and said nothing.

It should have ended there. It did not end there. The judge turned up the way weather turns up — at the edge of the yard, in the colonnade between rooms, once at the gate at day's end with the road behind him — and he never asked anything a coat could catch. He did not ask where Kael slept or what his mother was called. He talked. He talked about the capital.

He talked about it the way Oss talked about the Temple — a map drawn in the air, a country explained by a man who owned the explaining. The library with more books than the island had people, where a reader could be lost a year and counted well spent. The bridges lit at night, lamp to lamp, so that from the water the city lay doubled like a second city drowned. The halls where the empire's judges trained, and the men of the Art who could be watched working at scales the island would never see. He was from nowhere himself, he said — a wool town in the north, a father who could not write — and look: a judge of the games, who had seen every province the empire kept.

"The empire is hard on the small," he said, comfortably, watching the drills. "Everyone will tell you so, and everyone is right. But it is also the only ladder ever built that goes all the way up. A boy with a rare art and good feet — the capital finds uses for a boy like that. Finds patrons for him." He said it to the yard, not to Kael, the way you set food down near a careful animal and look elsewhere. "If such a boy ever wanted the road shown to him, there are men who show roads. I am one of them. That is nearly the whole of what a judge is."

And the want — the want that had been standing all spring with nothing to eat — turned and looked.

Kael said something that fit the coat, he could never afterward remember what, and walked up the mountain that evening with the whole conversation filed in three places at once. Beyond the sea. The words had not stopped being his oldest compass because a stranger had gilded them. Somewhere far down, in a drawer he did not visit, something had noted that the man's warmth ran level no matter what Kael answered, the way a lamp burns the same for anyone who walks past it; the note went in the drawer, and the drawer stayed shut, because there was no room in him to look at wrong things standing next to the one right thing anybody had said all year.

At the lamp his mother said, "And the new faces? The tournament men?"

"Surveyors," Kael said. "They measured the yard for stands. A judge watched drills." And then the day's remainder, whole, warm, in order — with a hole in it. She would never allow it. He did not decide this the way you decide things; it was simply present, load-bearing, older than the conversation: the capital was on the far side of a law, and the law had a face now, two faces, and the faces had said it kills. To pour the judge out at the lamp was to watch the door close on the one direction the want had left. So he was good at holes.

He did not examine what he had just done. There was no room in him for that either.

---

The arrangement, by summer, had a shape: the hour after the yard, twice a week, at the old cistern court nobody used — her fans, his reading, and a bench between them with whatever the day's argument was laid out on it.

"Again," Elarine said. "Smaller."

Kael stood with his palm up and a thread of fire on it the size of a coin, which for him was not small, which was the argument.

"It wobbles because you are holding it," she said. "You hold everything. Fire is not held, it is *paid* — you set the rate and it spends itself. Watch." Her own palm opened and a flame stood up on it, exactly coin-sized, exactly still, insultingly casual. "I am not holding this. I am refusing to buy more than this. That is a different hand."

"That's the purse again."

"Everything is the purse. You fight like a man spending his last coin because it is your last coin. I fight like—" she considered herself, fairly — "a treasury with discipline. Which is why your fire wobbles and mine is bored." The flame went out, all at once, the way a door closes. "Your turn. The back foot."

"Your back foot tells everyone everything," Kael said. "You reset to the same print, every time. It's why the ground could take you. Anyone who watches four of your exchanges owns your fifth."

"Nobody watches four exchanges."

"I watched two."

The look she gave him would have withered the well. "The correction is *good*," she said, in the voice of a person paying an invoice, and moved her foot, and made him drill her lanes until the light went — and somewhere in the middle of it, dry as dust: "The judge asks about you."

The word went through him and did not reach his face. "Everyone asks about me. I'm the goats' delegate."

"Hm," Elarine said, in the voice of someone declining, on principle, to be amused, and there was the almost-laugh anyway, one corner of it, priced and permitted. "He asked me what you *read*. Judges score bouts. Bouts do not read." She spent one more fan, smaller than the last, exact. "I told him you move your lips over the easy words. Mind the rate. You're buying again."

---

The joke happened at midday, in the autumn, in the long hall, and Oss told it.

He told it well, because he told everything well: up on the bench-end with his hands drawing, the middle tables turned to him, some story about the Temple's ghosts — and then, riding the laughter of the last one, easy as a man dealing cards: "Ghosts, nothing. You want mysteries? Watch the charity case and Sedrane. Twice a week, gone — *phht* — the two of them, off past the cistern court, very grave, very secret." His hands made a solemn little procession. "Nobody knows. Kettle-worship. A society of two. Ask them what they do up there — no, truly, ask them, I've been asking for a year and I get *Learn*." He did Elarine's flat voice, and did it perfectly, and the hall went up.

It was a crop of laughter, many throats, one animal, and it came down on Kael at the bench-end where he sat being furniture, and for the first time in a year the furniture failed.

Heat arrived in his face. His breath missed the long shape, caught it, missed again — the count, the actual count, needed for the first time since the first winter — and his eyes did the thing he had trained out of them and went to the room: the turned faces, the pointing, a girl saying something behind her hand to another girl, both looking at him. At the far table Elarine sat straight as a mast with spots of color high on her cheeks, and the laughter was running over her too — *Sedrane and the goat boy* — and that was the part that put the iron in it, the part his body priced before he could stop it: she was being spent, and the spending was his fault for existing where he was not supposed to exist.

Aurelian's tier poured it on for days, in the way of children who have been handed a new coin: the solemn procession mimed in the yard, kettles left on their bench with flowers in them, *the betrothal of the century, a herder and a house*. Lucan Drusane did not laugh; he watched, and added lines to a column. And Aurelian himself — Kael filed it from the middle of the noise, without a place to put it — Aurelian laughed once, the first time, shortly, and then not again, and by the third day was visibly bored with it in a way his tier took a while to notice.

What Kael carried out of the hall was none of that. It was Oss's face in the half-second after the laugh crested — the performer's smile holding its line while the eyes went somewhere else entirely, the way a man's eyes go along a wall when he is standing in a place he has chosen not to leave. Kael read the wrongness whole and could not read one word of it. He filed it as the herd, finally, winning. It did not sit right in the drawer, and he shut the drawer anyway.

He tried at the sea-wall two days later. Oss was there with two boys from the middle tables, and Kael came along the wall the way he had come along it for two years, and Oss saw him — Kael watched him see — and turned, laughing at something, and led the two boys off toward the kitchens at exactly the pace of a boy who had not seen anything at all.

He tried again the week after, alone, at the hall door. "Oss."

"Later, mountain boy," Oss said, bright, past him, already gone. There was no later. Kael stood with the word in his hands, and then filed it, because there was nothing else to do with it, in the drawer with the room's old temperatures — the drawer that had no name and was getting heavier by the season.

The seat at the bench-end stopped standing empty. It stopped being Oss's at all. It was just a seat.

---

"I despise them," Elarine said.

The cistern court, the cold term, the two of them and a bench of theory nobody was reading. It was the first thing she had said in a quarter hour that was not a correction, and she said it the way she said everything: exactly, with the price already computed.

"All of them. The laughing ones especially. Babies wearing names — they will inherit fleets and provinces because of their grandfathers, and not one of them has ever held anything they could not afford to drop." She was not looking at him; she was looking at the flame on her own palm, the coin of it, steady as a nailhead. "You think I chose fire? I did not choose the Temple. My house chose. My house chooses everything — the school, then the seat, then the man. I am as bound to my fate as they are to theirs. The difference is that I do not pretend the rope is a sash."

Kael sat still. Some things you did not interrupt for the same reason you did not step on new ice.

"You are worried the rumors cost me," she said. "You have been worried for a month. You worry like a man doing sums — it is very loud." The flame shrank one size, for discipline's own sake. "I'll say it once, and then we will not do this again. They cost me nothing I price. My pride does not take instruction from people I despise. I chose this arrangement because it is the only hour of this Temple where anyone says anything worth the fire it's written by, and I do not un-choose things because babies laugh." She closed her hand; the flame died like a door shutting. "You are the only person on this island who has ever beaten me with his mind. If they knew what that was worth, they would not laugh. They cannot know it. So: their loss, twice."

It was, he understood later, the kindest thing she knew how to say, and she had said it in full, and the collegial fiction that it was all rates and corrections held over it like a coat, and both of them let it hold.

Which is when Aurelian Corvane stepped into the cistern court.

He came alone, in the open, without his four — walked in the way you walk into a room where you are not certain of your welcome and are certain you deserve that — and stopped at a distance measured to the inch: close enough to speak low, far enough to be refused.

"Sedrane," he said. And then, to Kael, with the whole stiff weight of a boy to whom plainness costs more than blood: "Charity case." A breath. "Kael."

Elarine's eyebrow priced him. Kael said nothing.

"I am sorry," Aurelian said. "For the hall. For the kettles. For — the years of it, mine and my people's. Some of what has been done to you lately was done in my house's name, and I did not order it, and I did not stop it either, and Corvane pays what it owes. So." The word sat there, doing the work of several sentences he clearly did not have. He had rehearsed this, Kael realized; he had rehearsed it and it was still costing him at full rate, and he was paying anyway, in front of a witness, which was the part that made it real. "I am not asking you to like it. I am telling you the account is known to me."

"And?" Elarine said, because there was visibly an *and*.

"And I want to train with you." His chin came up on it, pride arriving to collect its due — "Both of you. The tournament is in fifteen months, and the yard teaches me nothing: they salute me, and then they lose to my name before they lose to me. You two are the only people in this Temple who work. I know what I bring. Capacity, and the yard moves when I say move — you have neither, either of you." It was said without cruelty, a man reading a manifest. "And I have watched the charity case win with a boot heel and a kettle, and I am tired of not knowing how."

Silence, one breath of it, with a new temperature.

"Conditions," Elarine said. "Your four stay wherever your four go when you are not performing. What happens in this court is not told in that hall. And here you are not a Corvane, because I am not a Sedrane here, and he is whatever he is everywhere, which is the only honest one of the three of us."

"Agreed," Aurelian said, too quickly, and caught himself, and said it again at his own pace, like a signature.

They both looked at Kael.

He did the thing he did: he priced it. The mass, the reach, the fire, the command — a purse he would never own, standing in front of him asking to be studied at close range. The risk — the coat, the court, the hall. The face — and the face was the thing: he read it the way he read weather, and there was no lie standing in it anywhere he could find, only pride and shame holding each other upright like two drunks, which, he had learned at a table on a mountain, was roughly the shape the truth made when it was carrying weight.

"What do you want to learn first?" Kael said.

Something crossed Aurelian Corvane's face that his tier would not have recognized. "Whatever you actually are," he said. "I've been fighting it for three years. I'd like to finally see it."

"No," Kael said, mildly, and felt the coat settle on his shoulders with its full winter weight. "You'll get what the yard gets, plus the why. The why is worth more anyway." And Elarine made a sound that was almost her laugh, one corner of it, and the court had three people in it now, and that was the shape of that winter.

---

The fever climbed all through the cold term, because now there was a number: at the spring, one year would remain. Lists went up. The older cohorts drilled at dawn — his hour, invaded, so that he watched other boys strike sparks off the wet air where he had learned to hold a thread through a parry, and none of them held anything through anything, and every one of them was allowed to be there in the open, with their names on a list.

And at dawn, in the black above the world, on a morning deep in that cold term, it happened at last: an exchange where the parry landed and the thread did not burst and did not die — it rode the wood. A stitch of white down the waster's length at the moment of the knock, there and gone, unasked, bought with a year of burns and three hundred and some receipts on his wrist. His father looked at the wood, and at him, for the length of one breath.

"Earlier than I thought," he said, which from his father was a speech, and they went again.

Kael sat at the end of the bench in the long hall, at midday, in the middle of the loudest winter the Temple had ever kept, and was furniture.

He was getting so good at it. That was the thing nobody would ever know, which was the point, which was the coat: a year of paying his own lightning its tuition in the dark above the world, and the first stitch of white riding steel where no one would ever see it; an hour twice a week where the empire's two best children of the Art paid his mind its exact worth — and then the bench-end, the small purse, the goats' delegate, the boy the judges' lists would never carry, sitting inside the roar of everyone else's coming glory with his face at the room's temperature. The want stood at its full height. It had stood so long now that standing was its whole posture. He fed it work, and it stayed, and he wore the coat over it, and none of it showed, and everyone he loved had made sure of it, and he had made sure of the rest.

*There is a thing nobody tells you about a coat, which is that the wearer pays for it. I wore mine so well that no one ever guessed the price — not the ones who cut it for me, not the ones it was cut against. I have worn heavier coats since. Much heavier ones. And on the days I have wanted to hate them for it — there have been days — I remember that every coat they ever cut me was cut to keep me breathing, and that both things were true. Both things were always true.*


---

# Chapter Fourteen — The Measure

The advance came with the spring, and this time it was not surveyors.

Three ships stood into the harbor on the first fair week of the turn, riding deep, and what came off them had the empire's grammar in it: quartermasters who walked the village like men reading a bill, clerks with chained books, a banner Kael did not know and filed, officials whose boots had never stood in a boat. The tournament was one year out now, and the island had stopped preparing for something and started belonging to it.

The name came up the mountain road ahead of the ships' cargo, the way names did. It went through the Temple in a morning.

Vel.

There had been a time when a name that came by water reached him early, split like a pastry, warm from the teller. Now it reached him the way it reached the benches and the well-shed and the kitchen stairs, at the room's own temperature, from no particular mouth. He sat at the bench-end, being furniture, and the hall told itself the name all through the meal.

He knew it the way he knew tide-tables. The chronicle's accounts of tournaments past had the Line of Vel standing in the margins of every one of them: royal protectors, eleven generations at the shoulder of the throne, a house that had traded its name to the crown so long ago the name had gone hard as a nail doing it. The chronicle did not say famous. The chronicle said things like *the champion's escort was of the Line of Vel* and let the generations do the arithmetic.

The hall said other things. The hall said unbeaten. The hall said she had been given her first steel at four, which was one version, or had never yet needed steel, which was another, and that her art was fire, or air, or water, depending on the bench — which meant, Kael filed, that nobody in the hall had ever seen it. A daughter of the Line, sent ahead with the advance, a year early. To train where the tournament would be held. On the ground itself.

The benches had a dozen theories about why.

She answered the why herself, in the first court, on the day she arrived, in front of the assembled roll.

---

The Temple received the advance party formally, cohorts drawn up in the roll's order, the First Master on the steps where he had stood to announce the thing that was now arriving. The officials were named. The banner was named. And then the First Master said, plainly, that the Temple would also host, for the year, a student of the capital school: Valeria, of the Line of Vel.

She stepped out from among the grey travel cloaks, and Kael's eye priced her before the name finished landing.

Lean. Exact. Built the way a tool is built when eleven generations have asked it the same question — a strong, working build, nothing spare on her anywhere, nothing worn for anyone's eye. Black hair well past her shoulders, kept clear of ornament. She stood in front of six hundred of the empire's children with a stillness Kael had spent his life learning to see, and it was nothing like his. His stillness was built to disappear. Hers was built the other way. It stood in the court like a drawn blade stands in a room, and the court gave it space without knowing it was giving anything.

Her eyes went along the ranks once, without hurry, and the strangest thing about her arrived last: she was bored. Plainly, evenly bored, the way you are bored by weather you have already had. Six hundred children of the houses, the island's whole gathered gold, and her eyes moved across them the way his mother's moved across a basket with no good caps in it.

The First Master asked if she would speak.

"I am here for the year," Valeria Vel said. Her voice was like the rest of her, cut to fit, nothing spent on it. "At its end I will enroll in the tournament." She paused, not for effect; for order. "I will win it."

The court made its sound. She was done speaking. She had, Kael understood, been done before she began — the words had not been offered to the court at all. They were a fact being entered in a roll, and the entering was itself the display: this is the Line of Vel; it says what will happen, and then it happens. He turned the sentence the way he turned a knot, looking for the boast in it, the spend, the wanting-to-be-seen he knew from every bench in the hall. He could not find it.

The itch sat up.

---

"One year," Elarine said, at the cistern court, in the voice of a woman reworking a whole ledger. "The capital sends its best piece to the board a year early. Learn the ground, learn the field, take the measure of everything worth measuring." A fan of flame stood off her palm, coin-sized, forgotten. "If I were the capital, I would do exactly that. I despise how good it is."

"My brother has seen her," Aurelian said. He said *my brother* the way he paid all his invoices now, at full rate, without flinching. "Two courts ago. She was nine. A fencing-master offered to correct her guard, and she asked him to show her his. He is still being laughed at." He worked his shoulder, where the morning's drill sat. "The stories are wrong the way stories are wrong. Underneath them she is real. The Line does not keep pets."

"And her art?"

"Nobody tells that story twice the same. Which you noticed." He looked at Kael. "You've been quiet."

"I'm listening," Kael said, which was true, and was also the smallest part of what he was doing.

He wanted to see it. All of her. He wanted it the way he wanted the far side of the sea.

"Mind the stating," Elarine said. "Both of you."

---

The yard did what yards do with a legend. It asked.

It asked properly, with the weather's permission and the masters' arrangement: an afternoon of exhibition spars, the measure drawn fresh, sand barrels at the corners, the visiting officials given the colonnade's shade. The First Master came. The judge came, comfortable, early, and took the rail with the air of a man attending exactly what he had come to the island for. The cohorts stood the lines three deep.

Valeria Vel went to the racks and took two wasters, one for each hand.

The yard made its sound. The off hand was for the Art — every child in the lines had been taught the stance, the open empty hand at the shoulder, the boast the empire fought with. Both her hands were full. It read as ignorance, or as insult, and nothing else about her read as either, so the yard did not know what to do with its own sound, and swallowed it.

Ruthen called the first pair.

The first was an older boy, a spear, well taught. She finished it in three exchanges. Kael watched the three ways: the first exchange she spent learning his measure, the second she spent confirming it, and the third arrived through a line the boy's school had priced as safe, both wasters working, one high, one low, a question and its answer asked with different hands. The boy sat down on the stones without quite being put there and looked at his empty hands.

The second was better, a longsword from the senior cohort, and it went three exchanges, which was the message. She was not spending more than the problem cost. Her feet went where his feet had been about to want to go. Her wasters moved the way his mother's hands moved at the loom, each already leaving when the eye arrived.

Kael went looking for the lazy place. Every body had one. Ten years of mornings had built the looking into him, load and lean and the one narrow decision everything else stood on — he had found it in every man, boy, and girl he had ever watched move, and he had beaten everything the yard had fed him by finding it. He looked, and looked, and did not find it. What he found instead he had no name for, and things he had no name for were the things he could not leave alone.

"Corvane," Ruthen said.

Aurelian came to the middle the way he did everything now, without performance, which his tier still had not forgiven. He was one of the best the island had and the yard knew it: a year of real work on top of the name, the mass, the reach, the paid polish, and underneath it the thing Kael had learned to respect at close range, a mind that had begun asking why. He saluted her properly. She returned it, exact, a coin given in change.

It lasted six exchanges, and she was never in one of them.

That was the thing Kael filed while the yard held its breath: not that she beat him — that she was absent from the fight the whole time, the way the sea is absent from an argument with a swimmer. Aurelian fought well. His best line, the long one off the straight back, came out true — and her answer was already standing in it, bought before he asked, and the twin wasters closed on his blade like a gate closing, and the sixth exchange ended with his waster ringing off the stones and both her points at rest, one at his chest, one low, covering the answer he had not made yet.

The yard was silent in a way it had no practice being.

Aurelian looked at the two points. Then he picked up his wood, and did the thing his tier would spend a week failing to understand: he nodded to her, once, the way you pay an invoice, and walked back to the line.

Valeria Vel stood in the middle of the measure, breathing the way you breathe standing still, and looked down the lines with the bored eyes, and asked the yard a question.

"Who is next?"

Nobody answered. The yard that laughed at everything, that had a verdict for everything, that had never in three years run out of currency, stood three deep in the spring light and had nothing to spend. Her eyes went along the ranks, unhurried, the way they had gone along the court on the first day, finding it all weather she had already had. On the colonnade the judge watched, comfortable.

The itch did not ask. It never had. It was the one thing in him that had never once asked.

Kael was stepping out of the line before the arithmetic of the coat could finish running. It did not get to finish. It stood there, half-summed, holding its columns, while his body walked into the drawn measure with a waster in one hand, in front of the First Master and the judge and the officials and the assembled gold of the island, and every eye in the yard arrived on him at once, and all of it showed.

"You're crazy," Elarine said, low, somewhere behind him.

"I know," he said, to the measure, and took his ground.

---

Up close, the boredom had texture, and a color: green — a clear, hard green, the green of deep water over rock. Her eyes came down the length of him, village cloth, one waster, nothing to look at until he moved, and cataloged him at the speed she cataloged everything, and were already leaving —

He moved.

He gave her the opening he gave the yard, the herder's shuffle, the tool held between jobs, and then he was off the lane the way he had gone off it against better fencers than the island knew it had, boot finding the seam between flagstones, weight arriving where nobody's school said weight went. Her waster met him there. Not late, not corrected — waiting. He slid the bind, went low, asked the question at her ankle that had put Lucan Drusane on the stones; her rear blade answered it downward, almost gently, while her lead blade asked him a question of her own that he paid for with two fast steps and the skin along his jaw, the wind of it cold.

Fast. He reset, breathing, and the yard had gone away entirely, and something in her eyes had changed by one degree.

He pushed. He fought the way he fought when it mattered, in no language at all, every answer bought used and rebuilt: the feint priced against her reading and thrown away mid-motion because she read it; the ground asked twice; the barrel's shadow spent; the rhythm broken on the half-count that had beaten every timing the Temple owned. She priced it all. That was the thing that was happening, he understood, in the cold clean place where the counting lived: she was doing to him what he had done to everyone. His fifth exchange belonged to her from inside his second. Nobody had ever read him before — the yard had spent three years refusing to believe there was anything to read — and now somebody was reading him at full speed, answer for answer, and it was the strangest thing that had ever happened to his body, and there was a conversation in it. The only language he had never had to be taught, and here, for the first time in his life, was a native speaker.

The boredom was gone. Her eyes were awake, fully, aimed at him the way his were aimed at her, and neither of them had anything to spare for the yard, and the yard did not exist.

She began to press, and pressing, she began to win.

It was not one thing. It was the sum: reach answered, lanes closed, the twin blades running his single one into arithmetic that got one exchange worse each time it ran, her stillness spending itself into speed with no lazy place anywhere in the spending for him to put a lever against. The measure shrank around him. His jaw stung where she had already spent one answer on it. He was losing, on the numbers, in front of the empire, and the counting in him laid the columns side by side and returned its flat verdict: plain, he could not beat her. Nothing plain he owned would arrive in time.

There was no moment when he chose.

The thread was in the waster before he knew he had spent it, the smallest thing the Art could make, a stitch of white down its length — a year of burns, three hundred and some receipts, the one skill on the island nobody had seen — and her parry came exact as everything she did, priced to the wood, and the wood ended where the wood ended.

The edge did not.

It caught her across the left forearm, through cloth, a hand above the wrist. Blood came out of the Line of Vel and stood along the cut, bright, undeniable, in the middle of the drawn measure, in front of the assembled island.

The yard's sound died in its throat. On the steps of the colonnade the First Master had come to his feet, hand rising, and the word that stopped everything was already in his mouth —

"No." Valeria Vel did not look at him. She did not look at her arm. Her eyes stayed on Kael, awake, wide open now, and something was in them that had not been in them since the ships came in, and she said it again, to the First Master of the Temple, the way you decline bread: "No."

And then the ground changed.

Kael felt it through his boots before he understood a single thing about it — the flagstones taking her weight differently, a settling, like a wall sitting down onto its course — and nothing left her body. That was what his eye kept, all the way down: no fan, no thread, no reach of the Art out into the world. Whatever she had spent, she had spent it inward. Down the line a voice said "*Earth*," half a whisper, and the hall's dozen stories collapsed into one.

Then she moved, and the world got faster.

Her first stroke took his parry out of the air like weather taking a roof. He gave ground; the ground was not his anymore. Stone cracked under her step — under a girl's single step, a flagstone, split — and the twin wasters came in a rhythm his eye read perfectly and his body could not answer, because reading her was no longer enough; the answers arrived and could not be paid in time; knowing was not enough. Each blow he caught came through the wood and up his arms and into his teeth, heavier than anything he had ever been hit with, heavier than anything he had ever priced, and the counting in him, which did not stop even now, laid it out plainly: she had been fighting the whole yard all afternoon at a walk. This was the Line of Vel's resonance. He had asked to see it. He was seeing it.

He lasted eleven exchanges. He counted, because counting was his.

The twelfth took the waster out of his hand entirely — no trick, no lever, just force arriving through his best parry as if his best parry were a curtain — and his arm went dead to the shoulder, and the stones came up, and then everything stopped at once, the whole of that speed ending in stillness, and he was on his back in the drawn measure with her point one finger from his face. Exactly one finger. Not touching. The point did not waver, and neither did anything behind it.

She had all of it in hand. Every grain. At that weight, at that speed, she had stopped a killing stroke at the width of a finger, cleanly, the way a door closes.

He had not stopped his.

The thought arrived on the ground, cold and without permission, while he lay looking up her blade at the awake eyes: she was bleeding because of him. He had not chosen it. He had wanted to beat her, and something older than choosing had opened the drawer and spent what was in it, on a person, in a yard, at a game — and if the thread had been one size larger, if her arm had been one hand lower — his body had done it the way his body did things, below the asking, and it had done it with an edge no one knew existed, and nothing, nothing in him had stood at the door of that drawer and said no. The mountain had built many things into him and had made very sure of all of them. He lay in the measure and understood, distinctly, for the first time in his life, that some of what it had made sure of was dangerous, and did not entirely belong to him.

Valeria Vel took her point away and stood looking down at him.

"Match," Ruthen said, flat, into the silence. "Vel."

The word crossed the yard and went into him looking for a place to land. It had been called a hundred times over those stones, at other boys — never at him. Three years, every spar, every duel, every pairing the yard had fed him: one long unwritten column, and he had not known he was keeping it until the word arrived and ended it.

---

The yard came back the way water comes back behind a ship. Noise, then names, then the healer crossing the stones with her box, aiming first for the visitor's bleeding arm and being ignored for her trouble until the arm's owner had finished what she was doing, which was looking at Kael.

"What are you called?"

The measure still lay between them. Kael got up the way his father had taught him to get up, in one order, nothing showing that could be kept from showing, his right arm hanging its dead weight as if it belonged to someone else.

"The charity case," a bench offered, from safety.

She did not move her eyes. She waited the way stone waits.

"Kael," he said.

"Kael." She entered it the way clerks enter weights, and he watched his own name go into a ledger that eleven generations had kept, and sit there. She glanced once at the waster lying on the stones, at the arm he was not using, at the line of her own blood going into the wrapping the healer was finally being permitted to apply. The awake thing was still in her eyes. He understood, with the part of him that read weather, that she was not angry, and that he had no idea what she was instead.

"A year is long enough," Valeria Vel said, "to learn what that was."

She walked back through the lines, and the lines got out of her way, and the yard fell on itself in noise.

Kael picked his waster up off the stones. When he straightened, they were both there — Elarine by the racks, arms folded, Aurelian a step behind her, and around them the yard emptying itself in a noise that had his name somewhere in every part of it.

"Eleven exchanges," Elarine said. "Corvane went six."

"Thank you," Aurelian said. "I had nearly stopped hearing it."

"I am establishing the scale."

"She is better than my brother." Aurelian said it the way he said everything now, a man reading a manifest, and this line cost him at full rate, and he paid it without looking away. "I have watched that yard flatter me for three years, and today I could not buy one exchange. And the capital is sending a hall of her kind in the spring. Not her equals — I don't believe she has one — but her kind. You understand what happened today. The tournament walked into this yard and looked at us."

"It looked at him," Elarine said. "The rest of us it read in passing." She turned to Kael then, and her eyes did what her eyes did, and he stood still under the pricing because that was the arrangement. "The benches have decided what they saw, incidentally. Wet light. A splinter off the waster. The same way they decided luck, three years running."

"It was wet light," Kael said.

"Of course it was," Elarine said, and both of them let it hold. "One thing." Her voice went down to the register she used for corrections that mattered. "I gave the twelfth exchange away to watch the colonnade. The judge saw a boy open a Vel's arm with wood and rain, and his face spent nothing. Not one grain. A man's face does that because he has seen the thing before, or because he was expecting it. Neither one is good. Judges score bouts, Kael." She did not say the rest. She had said it in the winter, and she was not a woman who repeated her work.

"Twelve months," Aurelian said, into the pause, at the yard, at the year standing over all of them. "The cistern court keeps longer hours from tomorrow." And then, already turning, in the voice his tier would not have recognized: "Now you know how it feels. I thought you should hear that from the first man you did it to."

They went. The yard finished emptying around him, and he stood with the waster in his hand, her blood a thin line already browning along the edge of it, and made himself take the inventory: beaten. Whole — mind level, count intact, every school he owned answering when he called it — and beaten anyway.

He had lost once before in his life. He had been nine, and it had never once been a match, and he had carried the verdict of it ever since the way you carry a stone in a boot: he had spilled, and the spilling had been the loss. Everything after had been built against it — the wasters by the fire, the dawns, the counts, the year of burns — and under all of it, unexamined, a belief had been keeping house all these years: that the boy they were building would be enough. That if the dark ever asked him again, the answer would come out different. He had believed it the way you believe a wall.

A girl his own age had just walked through it.

The old verdict came up its hinge with the taste of that winter on it — not enough — and this time, that was the part he could not stand anywhere near: this time it was not even wrong. Nothing had broken. He had been whole all the way down, and whole, he had lost.

And under the loss, under the anger sitting in him with its new name, something ran the other way, shameless, already counting: the first person who had ever read him back was on this island for a year, with his name in her ledger. The want stood at its full height, and for once it had been fed, and he could not tell which was worse — that he had lost, or that some part of him could not wait to lose again.

He was afraid of what it had eaten.


---

# Chapter Fifteen — The Hours

The road home was long enough to fight the match eleven more times, and he fought it eleven more times.

He fought it along the coast shoulder with his right arm hanging dead and swinging whenever he turned. He fought it at the bend above the village, where the roofs were putting on their early lamps and the water had gone the colour of a slate nobody had written on yet. Every time, he found the place where it had gone wrong. Every time, the place moved. Reach was not the answer; he had taken longer arms apart and enjoyed it. Speed was not the answer, or not by itself, because speed could be spent early and made to arrive at the wrong address.

By the split rock he had the shape of it, and the shape was worse than any of the answers he had been trying on.

She had not been faster than his thinking. She had been faster than his body. His thinking had been the whole of his fortune for three years, and tonight it had arrived on time, at the right place, with the right answer in its hand, and had watched the answer be too slow to spend. There was no lever in that. There was nothing heavy anywhere in it to be tricked.

He went up through the last of the light with his heart going like a boy running, and he was not running.

His mother had the lamp lit and the slate out.

"Your arm," she said, before he was through the door.

"It comes back. It was the shoulder." He sat down without taking his boots off, which he had not done since he was nine. "There's a girl."

And he gave them all of it.

It came out of him in the wrong order and he could not make it come out in the right one. Vel, eleven generations, sent a year early to learn the ground. The twin wasters and the yard swallowing its own sound. Three exchanges for the spear, three for the longsword, six for Aurelian Corvane, and the way she had been absent from every one of them, standing in a fight the way the sea stands in an argument with a swimmer. *Who is next.* The whole yard three deep with nothing to spend. Himself, walking out into the measure with the coat's arithmetic left standing behind him half-summed.

He told them the duel the way his father had taught him to give an account, and the account ran away with him anyway. The herder's shuffle offered and declined. The seam between the flagstones taken and answered, not late, not corrected — waiting. His fifth exchange belonging to her from inside his second. Somebody reading him, at speed, answer for answer, for the first time in his life.

He told them about the thread without slowing down, because the thread was in the middle of the fight and the fight was what he was telling.
