"—and there was nothing plain left that would get there in time, so it went into the waster. The stitch. It held through her parry and the wood stopped and the edge didn't, and I cut her arm."

The fire ate what it was given.

He heard the room change and could not have said which of them changed it. His father had not moved. His mother had not moved. The slate lay where it lay with nothing on it.

"Go on," his father said.

So he went on, and the going-on was worse, because now he could hear himself. The First Master rising on the steps. *No,* twice, the way you decline bread. The ground taking her weight differently, a settling, like a wall sitting down onto its course, and nothing leaving her body — nothing, he said, twice, because that was the part his eye had kept. A flagstone split under one step. Eleven exchanges, counted. The twelfth arriving through his best parry as though his best parry were a curtain.

"And she stopped it a finger from my face," he said. "She had all of it in hand. All of it, at that speed." He looked at the table. "I didn't stop mine."

Nobody said anything for a while. The lamp did what lamps do.

Then his mother said, "How many were in the yard."

"Three deep on all four sides. The young cohorts and both senior ones. Say four hundred."

"Where was the sun."

He had to think, and thinking about it put him back in it, standing in the drawn measure with the light where it had been. "Behind the west colonnade. Low. The barrels had shadows out to about a stride."

"So it was in your eyes and not in hers."

"For the first half. We changed ends at the reset."

"Who was on the colonnade."

"The First Master. The visiting officials, five of them, and their clerks. The judge." He said the word *judge* the way he would have said *barrel*, and nothing in his face did anything, because a great deal in him had been built to make sure nothing in his face did anything. "The masters not standing the yard."

"Where was your hand when the thread was in the wood."

"Low. Under the bind, on the inside line. It would have been behind the waster from three sides out of four."

"And the fourth side."

"The colonnade."

His mother nodded once, as though he had passed something, which he had not. "Who was nearest to you when it happened."

"Sedrane, by the racks. Corvane behind her. The rest of the line, eight or nine of them close enough."

"What did they say. Their words. Not yours."

"Wet light," Kael said. "A splinter off the waster. Somebody at the back said luck, because somebody always says luck." He heard how it sounded, laid out like that, in her voice's own order. "The benches decided before the healer got across the stones. They've had three years of practice deciding about me."

"And your friends?"

"Sedrane knows it wasn't wet light. She said so, and then she said it was wet light anyway, and we left it there." He turned his cup a quarter turn, which was his father's habit and not his. "Corvane didn't ask. He was busy paying for six exchanges."

His mother was quiet.

She got up and went to the window, and did not look out of it, and came back. Kael filed the whole exchange the way he filed everything now, and it went in with an edge on it that he could not get his hand around: the questions had come in an order. Not in the order a mother asks. In an order, the way the mushroom litany was in an order and the tap-sign drill was in an order, built before he was born and carried a long way to get here. Where was the sun. Who was nearest. Their words, not yours. He had a name for what it was in the yard — it was a debrief, they did it after the exhibition spars, badly — and he had no name for what it was in his own house at his own table.

His father had let all of it happen without moving. Now he put both hands flat.

"The thread," his father said.

"It held," Kael said, and could not keep it entirely out of his voice, and knew that he had not, and did not care as much as he should have. "It rode the wood through a full parry from the best blade on this island. A year of burns. It *worked*."

"It did." His father let that sit exactly as long as it deserved and not one breath longer. "Where was your wrist when it went in."

"Low. Under the bind, inside line."

"And a breath later."

Kael went back into the exchange and found the answer standing there waiting for him, and did not like it. "I don't know. I stopped attending to it when the wood stopped."

"Yes." His father took his hands off the table. "You put a live thread into a blade and then went to look at what it had done. On a day when it works, that costs you nothing. It will not always be the day it works."

He nodded once, at the fire, at the whole account.

"The rest was correct."

Kael sat with that.

He had come up the mountain wanting exactly this. He had wanted it the whole way, under the loss and the arithmetic and the dead arm — the audit, the mistakes named in order, and somewhere at the end of it the one sentence that said the school had held. He had it now. *The rest was correct.* His father had listened to a boy describe opening a stranger's arm in a game, in front of four hundred people, with an edge nobody knew was there, and had found a single fault in the account, and the fault was wrist discipline.

He looked at the two of them across the lamp — his mother, who had wanted the angle of the light and the words of the nearest bench, and his father, who had wanted the wrist — and could not find in either of them the thing he had been carrying since he got up off the stones.

He had no name for it. He had been going through his pockets for one the whole way home. It was not guilt, which he had been given all the tools for and none of the names. It was nearer to reaching in the dark for the side of the bed where the wall had always been.

"Leave the wasters in the shed tomorrow," his father said.

It was said in the voice of moving a lesson from one shelf to another, and it went past Kael entirely, because he was still standing in the wreck of the other thing.

There was no punishment. There never was. He lay in the loft afterward with the arm coming back in pins and needles and the day going around and around, and what he found, when he had finished going through the pockets of the evening, was not the thing he had expected to find.

It was not the loss. The loss had a shape and he had already begun taking it apart.

It was that he had been beaten whole, and that he had one thing left in the world that might have changed it, and that nobody in that house was going to take it off him. There was no rule to hold to. There was no law lying on the table this time and no gate to be angry at. There was the drawer, and whatever was in it, and him.

And under all of that, in the cold clean place, something had already begun making lists.

He was awake a long time. He did not once think about being unhappy. He thought about her back foot, which did not reset, which was the whole problem, and about how a thing that never resets must be arriving from somewhere, and about the eleven exchanges he had, and where the twelfth would have to come from.

---

There was steel in the yard before dawn.

Two blades on the block by the woodpile, plain as spoons, blunted along both edges and rounded at the points. They were not new. Kael did not ask where they had come from.

His father picked one up, turned it over once, and gave it to Kael hilt first.

"Wood forgives you," his father said. "It bounces, it bends, and it lies to you about what you did. This does not lie. Everything you have been doing slightly wrong for four years, you are about to find out about." He took the other. "Guard."

The weight was wrong. Not heavier — differently distributed, alive at the far end in a way the wasters never were, wanting to keep going after his wrist had finished with it. Every correction he had built into his body had been built around a stick that forgave him. Twice he came off a bind late and felt the blunted edge go along his own forearm, and it did not cut, and his body understood with total clarity that this was a fact about the smith and not a fact about the exchange.

"Again," his father said. And, one stroke: "Elbow." And, later: "You are aiming at where it will be. It is longer than you think. Aim at where it is."

Then he put the thread in.

He had braced for the worst morning of his life. He had spent a year learning what wood cost, and steel was going to cost more, and he had come out to the block in the dark that morning with the whole of it priced.

The lightning went into the steel like water finding a channel.

He felt it happen and his hand opened by itself, and the blade rang off the block, and he stood in the frost saying nothing at all.

Wood had to be persuaded. That was the thing nobody had ever told him, because he had never had anything to compare it to: every thread he had laid into a waster for a year had been laid into something that did not particularly want it, held in place by his own exactness and nothing else, sitting where he put it because he was standing over it making it sit. Steel did not need standing over. Steel *took* it. It went the length of the blade in less time than it took to decide it had gone, end to end, hilt to point, whole — and it was not thinner for having spread. It was the same thread everywhere at once.

"It likes it," Kael said.

His father picked the blade up off the frost and handed it back.

"Yes," he said.

"You knew."

"Yes."

"Why did nobody —" He stopped, because he could hear the shape of the question, and the answer was in every hour of the last four years. Nobody tells you the next thing. You get given the thing in front of you, whole, and then you get given the next one. "How much easier."

"That is the wrong question," his father said. "Ask the other one."

Kael stood with the blade in his hand and the cold coming up through his boots and ran it, and got there, and wished he had not.

A thing that takes a thread that easily gives it back the same way.

"The burst," he said.

"The burst," his father agreed.

It came on the fourth exchange after that, off a hard parry, and it was not the wrist-bite of the wooden year. It arrived everywhere at once. He was down without any memory of going down, with his arm locked out straight and shaking and his teeth ringing in his head, and it took a long moment before he could work out which parts of him were his.

His father crouched and turned the hand over and looked at the speckling coming up from the wrist to the second knuckle.

"Bigger," he said.

"It's the same size thread."

"It is the same size thread in a better road." His father let go of the hand and stood. "You will not be exact enough for steel this year by being what you were last year. Get up."

Kael got up.

And that was the shape of the summer, and the shape of it was this: Kael asked for more, and his father gave him more, and neither of them ever once said out loud why they wanted it.

The hours went backward into the dark. It had been dawn; then it was before dawn; then it was a stretch of the night with a lamp on the woodpile and the sea invisible below them making its noise. Kael asked for that. He asked for it in the third week, standing in the yard with the light going and his hand shut around nothing, and his father looked at him for a while and then said, "The lamp is in the shed," and that was the whole of the negotiation.

His father, for his part, stopped teaching and started proofing.

There was a difference and Kael found it in about four days. Teaching was corrections at normal size and exchanges built so that a boy could win one. Proofing was his father coming at him at the speed the thing actually happened at, over and over, in the dark, to find out what broke. Nothing was explained afterward. What broke got worked until it stopped breaking, and then he went looking for the next thing.

And through all of it — this was the part Kael did not think about until much later, and then thought about for a very long time — his father was never once hurried. Whatever the speed was that the thing actually happened at, it was a speed his father had somewhere about him, unhurried, all summer, and gave out in exactly the amount the morning required and not one grain over.

He was hit a great deal that summer. The blunted steel did not cut and it did not need to; it went into the meat of the arm and the ribs and the outside of the thigh and left marks that came up black and went yellow and were replaced before they finished going. He learned to dress in the loft with his back to the ladder. And he noticed — he noticed everything, it was the one thing nobody could take off him — that in a whole summer of it his father never once put a mark anywhere above the collar or below the wrist.

Not once. Not by accident, either, because his father did not do things by accident.

The care in that was so exact, and so cold, and so entirely without comment, that Kael turned it over for a week before he understood he was looking at the coat. Everything the Temple would see was left alone. Everything the shirt covered was fair.

He was proud of it. That was the part he could not have explained to anyone at that Temple, and never tried: he was proud of it. He got up faster every week. He came off the ground with the count already running where a year ago he would have come off the ground and had to go and find it. The machinery was holding. He could feel it holding, under the bruises, the way you feel a floor.

Twice that summer he came in from the dark and his mother was in the doorway.

She did not say anything either time. She stood with one hand on the frame and the light behind her, and let him past, and had water and the salve out on the table before he asked for it, and her knuckles were pale on the wood, and she said nothing at all. Kael, going by her with his shirt stuck to him in two places, thought that she was probably tired. He had a great deal on his mind that summer.

Once, in the last week of it, he caught his father not moving.

He had gone down badly off a bind — his own fault, late, the thing he had been doing wrong for four years and had nearly finished unlearning — and he came up onto his hands and knees in the wet and stayed there a beat longer than he needed to, getting his breath, and when he looked up his father was standing over him with the blade at his side, absolutely still, looking at him.

Not at the mistake. At him.

Kael had been read by that face his whole life and had never once seen the thing that was in it now, and did not have anywhere to put it, so he filed it and got up.

"Again," his father said.

He burned four hundred and eleven times between the spring and midsummer. He counted, because counting was his.

---

The Temple came to steel at midsummer.

It came the way everything came there, with a procedure attached: blades issued out of the armoury by cohort, racked and numbered and counted back in at the bell, blunted and rounded to the pattern every school in the empire would fight the tournament to. The yard spent a week being extremely pleased with itself.

Kael took his out of the rack and it sat in his hand where it had been sitting every night for two months.

He did not hide it. He had never once hidden that he was good, only where the good came from, and there was nothing in the coat that covered a boy simply being at home in a weapon. So he was at home in it, from the first hour, in front of all of them — and the yard did what the yard had done for three years and decided it was nothing much, and Lucan Drusane, who had been issued the same blade on the same morning with a paid master's four years of steel behind him, watched the charity case handle it like a man taking back something borrowed.

He added a line to a column.

---

The cistern court kept longer hours from the next day, as promised, and by midsummer it did not keep hours at all. It kept whatever was left over.

They came to it filthy and stayed until the light went. Elarine brought theory in a satchel and never opened it. Aurelian brought bruises he had gone and got somewhere else, on purpose, from older boys, and reported them like a man reading a manifest. And what happened in that court over that summer was the strangest transaction Kael had ever been part of, which was saying something, because he had been raised inside one.

"Do it again," Kael said. "All of it. Don't make it small."

"It is not small," Aurelian said. "That is the entire complaint everyone has ever had about me."

"That's what I want."

Aurelian's fire came off his palm the way a door comes off its hinges, and Kael watched it the way he had watched the men on the six-oared boat: absolutely still, no expression, everything going in.

Because here was the thing he had worked out in the loft with his arm coming back, and it was so simple he was ashamed of the years it had taken. *Everything I do is small.* He had said that at a healer's bench, honestly, meaning it as a limit. It was also the reason he could not see his own art. His threads were the smallest things anybody on the island could make. There was nothing to look at. He had been trying to study a working through a keyhole the size of a working.

Aurelian's fire was the same grammar written a hundred times larger, in a hand you could read across a court.

"There," Kael said. "Stop. Do the first half of that."

"Which first half?"

"Before it's fire. There's a part where it isn't anything yet and you're still deciding."

Aurelian looked at him with a small, dangerous patience. "There is no such part."

"There is. It's about as long as a blink. You do it every time and you don't do it the same every time, which is why your second one is always better than your first." Kael crouched by the bench. "When you go big, everything you do goes big with it. Including the mistake. I can't see my own mistakes because they're the size of a hair. Yours are the size of a house."

There was a silence in the cistern court of a particular kind.

"He has just called you a house," Elarine said, from the wall.

"I am aware of what he has just called me."

"I am only checking. You have a face for when you have been insulted and it is not on."

"It is not an insult," Aurelian said slowly, still looking at Kael. "It is the first useful thing anyone has said to me about the Art in four years." He turned his hand over and looked at it as if it had been lent to him. "Go on, then. What is this *blink*."

That was the trade, all summer. Kael got a magnifying glass. Aurelian got the why.

And the why turned out to cost Aurelian Corvane more than he had budgeted, which he paid, at full rate, in public, in front of a witness, which was the part that made it real. He had never once in his life had to find a working's structure, because he had always been able to afford to miss. Kael took that away from him an inch at a time, and Aurelian let him, and got worse for three weeks before he got better, and did not stop.

"You are throwing money at it," Kael said, in the fourth week.

"I *have* money."

"You'll be in the tournament with people who have money."

Aurelian stood in the middle of the cistern court with soot on his forearms, and something went across his face that his tier would not have recognized, and he laughed. It was a real laugh, short, surprised out of him, the first one Kael had ever heard from him that was not aimed at anybody.

Kael's breath went to the long shape before he could stop it. He caught it, put it back, and hoped the count had not showed.

Some things were not going to be fixed by a good summer.

Elarine, from the wall, was pricing all of it and saying nothing, and when she did speak it was to move the ledger sideways.

"We should ask her," she said.

Nobody had to ask who.

"She fights adults every morning and children never," Elarine said, to the general air. "She has been on this island for a season and she has spoken to precisely no one under sixteen except him, once, to take his name down. She is the best thing on this island by a distance that is frankly rude, and she is standing in a field by herself. I would like to know what she does with her training. I would like it very much."

"She'd say no," Aurelian said.

"Probably. I have been told no before. It has never once killed me." She looked at Kael. "You've fought her."

"Yes."

"So you ask her."

"No," Kael said.

It came out flat and fast and about a beat too soon, and he heard all three of those things arrive in the court and stand there.

Elarine's eyes did what her eyes did.

"Why not?"

And Kael, who could take apart a fencing form in two exchanges and had once cracked eleven weeks of his mother's night watch by writing down which boats went where, stood in a disused cistern court on a summer afternoon and could not find one single word to put in the gap.

"It wouldn't work," he said.

"That is not a reason. That is the shape a reason goes in."

"I know," Kael said.

Elarine looked at him a moment longer. Then she picked up the satchel she had not opened all afternoon and said, "Rates. Both of you. You have been buying all day," and the court went back to work, and she did not ask again, and Kael understood that she had decided not to, and did not understand anything else about it at all.

---

It came up the road with him in the evenings and was there in the morning: *What does she do all day, and how?* Twice he built the sentence that would ask her, and twice something in him put it down, and there was no drawer that fit it.

So he did the other thing. If you needed to know a thing, you looked at it from where it could not look back, for as long as it took; he had been taught that by everyone he loved. If he had been asked he would have said, honestly, that he was studying an opponent a year out from a tournament he was forbidden to enter.

What he learned in the first week was her art, and it was worth every hour.

She did not throw anything. Not once, in six days. Everything went inward. He watched them stand her in the middle of the court and hit her — grown men in the capital's grey, with weighted wasters, hard, on the arms and the ribs and once across the back of the thigh — and watched her take it and stay standing, and understood on the third morning what he was actually being shown. They were not teaching her to endure it. They were *calibrating* her. She spent the resonance inward, and the blow came, and the man who had struck her said a number, and she adjusted, and they did it again. Too much and she was slow. Too little and it cost her a rib's worth of breath. They were walking her down to the exact grain, over and over, at a price he could not imagine paying once, let alone forty times before breakfast.

He filed everything. The weight of her going into a step and the sound the flagstone made when it did not break, which was the sound of her having spent correctly. The way the reinforcement ran up and stopped, so that her hands stayed fast while her forearms went heavy. The half-count of lag on the very first exchange of each morning, before she was warm — which was, he thought, with a lift under his ribs that he did not examine, a lazy place, the first he had found in her, and worth more than a month of anything else.

What he learned in the second week was not about her art.

Nobody spoke to her.

Instructions were spoken *at* her, in the register men use with equipment they respect. When it was finished, the grey coats went up to the town, together, talking, and she wound her wrists and put her blades away in their case and went the other way, alone, and there was nobody at the gate, and nobody at the top of the path, and nobody at all.

She was first onto the court every morning by a quarter of an hour, and Kael, who arrived earlier than that to be in position, watched her come down alone into the empty court in the grey light and begin, with nobody there to see it, at the same rate she would keep for the next four hours.

He watched a woman with a chained book read a letter, out loud, before handing it to her, and watched Valeria Vel take it and read it standing, and put it inside her coat, and pick up her blades.

And in the treeline above the sea court, in the wet grass, with his heart going in a way he could not price, Kael assembled the last piece of the puzzle he had come up the headland to solve, and he assembled it exactly wrong.

*Nobody stands beside her.*

He turned it over and found it good, and the more he turned it the better it got, because he knew what it was worth. All his life there had been a man beside him. In every stillness he had ever been asked to hold, his father had been holding one next to him, longer. Every drill, every dark scree run, every hour of the standing game — beside him, always, at his shoulder, doing it better and staying. That was not comfort. Kael had never once thought of it as comfort. It was *how you built a person*: you put someone next to them who is further along and you leave him there.

Her school had no beside. Her school had above, and her school had at, and it worked, plainly it worked, it had produced the most complete twelve-year-old he had ever seen or heard of.

The looking. It never stopped. He'd spend two weeks looking, from the wet grass, through the gorse, in the grey light, at a child being made by people who did not love her — and never once notice that the only reason he had a word for the difference at all was that he had been made by people who did.


---

# Chapter Sixteen — The Terms

He kept coming. To watch. To learn. But, something more than that was true.

What he wanted to see had a beginning, and the beginning was too small to see from ninety strides. He gave it four mornings. On the fifth he came down.

He came down the way he had been taught to come down anything, which was slowly, on the outsides of his feet, reading the ground before he gave it his weight. The old sea court sat at the Temple's seaward edge where the wall gave out, three sides of broken colonnade around forty feet of flagstone that the masters had stopped teaching on before Kael was born. There was cover to within twenty strides of the stones. He had priced it from above for two weeks and it priced the same from below.

It did not once occur to him that there was anything strange about any of this. He had been taught to look at what he needed to know about from where it could not look back.

And then she did the thing he had come for, which was nothing.

She set her blades against the base of a column and walked out to the middle of the stones and stood there. That was all. From the trees it had read as waiting, as a girl arriving early to no purpose, and he had filed it as the one wasteful quarter-hour in a schedule with no waste anywhere else in it. Up close it was not waiting. Her weight was wrong for waiting. Her breath was wrong for it. Her eyes were open and aimed at nothing at all, and her face had the look his mother's face had over a slate, and whatever she was doing she was doing it hard enough to be tired at the end of it.

Kael leaned forward one inch to see her hands.

The stone under his knee was flat and dry and made no sound, and he made no sound, and he had not made a sound since he was old enough to be told not to.

"Nine strides," Valeria Vel said.

She had not turned around.

"You are too light to be one of mine," she said, to the empty court in front of her. "They come in at forty and they land on their heels. Stand up."

There was nothing to be gained by anything else. Kael stood up out of the gorse with his hands where she could see them, because that was a thing his father had taught him and he had never yet had a use for it, and walked down onto her stones.

She turned around.

He watched her put his face on the shape she already had, and the two things fit, and she did not look surprised. She looked the way she had looked at the end of the exhibition, with the awake thing in her eyes.

"How long have you been watching me."

"Fifteen—"

"Fifteen what?" She said it flatly.

"Fifteen mornings."

She sighed.

"You have yet to miss one."

"No."

"Where."

"The treeline. Two places. The gorse above the west corner mostly, because the sun comes in behind me there after the first hour and it makes the court easier to look at." He heard himself giving the account in the order he would have given it at his own table, and did not stop, because the alternative was to start choosing what to say, and he did not have anything to choose from. "The other one is lower and closer. I only used it twice. It's better for your feet and worse for your face."

"My face."

"I wanted to see when you resonate."

Something crossed her that was gone before he could take hold of it. "And did you."

"No. That's why I came down."

Valeria Vel considered him for a while. Somewhere up the hill a bell went for the first cohorts and neither of them moved.

"You will have to tell me why," she said, without any weight on it whatsoever, as a man says the tide is out.

"You beat me."

"I have beaten a great many people."

"You beat me *whole*," Kael said, and it came out with more of him in it than he had meant to spend. "I've lost twice in my life."

Twice. The thousands to his father he had never thought to count.

"The first time, something in me broke, and I've been building against it for three years. The second time nothing broke. Everything I have, worked when I called it, and it wasn't enough, and I've been trying to find out what the missing thing is, and I can't find it. So I came."

"And what have you found."

"That it isn't reach and it isn't speed the way I understand speed." He was aware, distantly, that he had not spoken this many consecutive words to another child in his life. "You spend before the exchange starts. Everyone I have ever fought spends when the trouble arrives. You have already paid by the time I ask the question, so the question is the wrong price when it gets there."

The silence went on long enough that he began to price the run back to the treeline.

"That is correct," Valeria said.

"I know."

"It took the capital's third master eleven weeks to say that to me and he had my instructors' notes." She walked to the column and picked up her blades, one in each hand, and came back out to the middle, and stopped at the measure without drawing it. "Sit down."

He sat down on the stones.

"There are things I am going to say once," she said. "First. You cut my arm."

"Yes."

"Do not apologise for it. Three people have apologised to me for that arm and every one of them was apologising to my house." She turned her forearm over. The line ran a hand above the wrist, healed pink, exactly where he had put it, and he found that he had known its exact position for four months without ever once having chosen to remember it. "The First Master wanted to stop the match. My chaperone wrote to my father." A pause, exactly the length it needed to be. "My father's letter asked how a Vel came to be standing where a waster could reach her, and instructed me to write back with the answer." She let the sleeve fall. "Nobody asked me what it was."

"Wet light," Kael said.

"Yes. Four hundred people decided that in the time it took a woman to cross a yard with a box." Her voice did not change at all. "You put an edge on a piece of wood. I have been in rooms with the six best-taught children of the capital and not one of them can put an edge on anything, and the empire has looked at what you did and agreed among itself that the sun was on the sea. I have thought about it every day since the spring. I am not going to stop thinking about it because you answer like a door."

"I'm not going to tell you what it is."

"No," she agreed. "You are going to show me."

He opened his mouth and found nothing in it.

"That is the second thing," Valeria said. "You have had fifteen mornings of me. That is an account and I intend to collect it. You will come at the same hour every day, and you will get as close as you can before I have you, and I will tell you the number. Then we fight, and you show me what you can show me, and I show you what I can show you, and neither of us explains anything, because I do not have the words for mine either." Something moved at the corner of her mouth and was gone.

"The same hour," Kael said.

"Every day. I will not be surprised twice." She said it as a fact about herself rather than a threat, which he was learning was how she said everything. "Random is for people who are hoping. I am not hoping. I want to know how close you can get when I am waiting for you."

He was already running it. The hour after the yard was the only hour he had; the road home took what was left of the light; and *Evening* stood at the end of every day of his life like a wall with a word written on it.

"After the yard," he said. "I'm home before dark or I'm not anywhere."

"Then after the yard." She looked at him with her head very slightly on one side.

"Yes," Kael answered, a half-smile forming.

"Very well," Valeria Vel said.

She lifted the left-hand blade to the guard she used and stood there waiting with the bored eyes coming awake.

"Do you know what I have been doing," she said, "for the quarter-hour."

"No."

"Good," she said. "Find out."

---

The terms held for eleven weeks.

He came at the hour and she took him at the door. That was the shape of it, and it never once got easier, and by the third week he understood that easier was not what was on offer.

Her rooms were in the guest range above the second court, and the way to them ran up an open stair, along a colonnade with no cover in it worth the name, and past an outer room where a woman with a chained book sat all day reading letters that were not hers. Kael priced the whole of it on the first afternoon and priced it again on the fortieth, and the answer never changed, which was that there were exactly two ways in and both of them were bad.

The first day she said his name at thirty-one strides.

"Thirty-one," she said through the door, before his foot came off the stair.

"That's the stair."

"That is the stair," she agreed. "It is stone all the way to the sea wall and it is the loudest thing in this Temple and you weigh nothing. Come in."

The second week he took the wall instead and got twenty-two. The third week he got nineteen, then twenty-four, then nineteen again, and lost the whole of the fourth week to a run of days where she had him at the bottom of the stair every time and would not tell him why, and he lay in the loft at night going through it and could not find it, and on the fifth day came up the stair on the outsides of his feet with his weight in the wrong place on purpose, walking like a heavier boy, and got to fourteen.

"Fourteen," she said, and there was something in it. "What did you do."

"Walked like Corvane."

"Say that again."

"You've never met me," Kael said. "You've met a weight and a rhythm. Corvane's is nothing like mine and there are two hundred boys in this Temple whose weight you've never taken, so I borrowed one." He shrugged. "It'll work twice more."

"It will work once more," she said.

It worked once more.

After that nothing worked, because he had run out of body to lie with. He sat with that for four days, and then he stopped trying to be quieter and asked the only kind of question he had ever been any good at, which was not *how do I hide from her* but *what is she actually doing.*

He ran it the way his mother had taught him to run anything, which was twice, with one thing changed.

On the Tuesday he came the whole way along the top of the sea wall and down the packed earth of the walk, off the flags entirely. Twenty-six. On the Wednesday he came the same route at the same speed and stepped onto the flags for the last thirty feet. Eleven. On the Thursday he crossed the second court on the flags at a crawl, slower than he had ever moved in his life, making no sound a human ear could have found in an empty room. Nine. On the Friday he went up the outside of the guest range instead, hung off the sill of a vacant room two doors from hers with his boots off the building altogether, and stayed there for a quarter of an hour.

She said nothing at all.

He hung until his fingers went, then came in through the vacant room and walked four strides of corridor to her door, and she said, "Four," through the wood, sounding for the first time in nine weeks faintly annoyed.

He lay in the loft that night and did not sleep, because the whole thing had turned over.

She was not listening. He had spent the Thursday proving a boy could be silent and still be counted. She was not seeing either; she had her back to a door.

She was reading the floor.

It was the same law. It was the law he had used at eleven years old on a rain-wet yard against Elarine Sedrane, a thread of earth spent off his own heel and run out along the packed dirt to her foot, because the ground that touched him touched her, the way sound runs along a beam. He had used it to reach. She was using it to know. Two people at opposite ends of one road, and he had spent nine weeks walking up it on his own two feet and calling it a stair.

The road was the problem. So break the road.

It took him nine days to do it once.

The idea was small and the doing of it was not. A thread of earth into the stone at the instant his weight arrived on it. Not to move the stone, not to lift or split or throw anything, nothing that would show. Only to trouble the grain of it directly under his boot, so that what went in had nowhere onward to go. Let the step die where it stood.

---

He took it to the cistern court, because he had learned over the summer that two other people thinking was faster than one person thinking, even when the one person was him.

He gave it to them with the entire reason cut out of it.

"I want to cross a stone floor," he said, "without the floor knowing."

There was a pause of a particular quality.

"Say that ag—"

"Do not," Elarine said. "He will say it the same. He always says it the same."

"Without the floor *knowing*," Aurelian said. "Floors do not know things."

"Lie down."

"I beg your pardon."

"Lie down, put your ear on the brick, and don't move."

And so the heir of House Corvane, who would one day hold a fleet, lay face down on the wet brick of a disused cistern court with his cheek in a puddle while a herder's son walked circles around him, and after the fourth circuit said, in an entirely different voice, "*Oh.*"

"Yes."

"I can feel where you are." He got up on his elbows with his hair full of moss. "I can feel where you are with my *face*. That is revolting. Do it again."

"Sit up, you look like a dropped bag," Elarine said, and did not look up from the flame standing on her palm, which had not moved in four minutes and was the only tidy thing in the court. "It is not that the stone knows anything. It is that the stone is all one thing. From the sea wall to that arch it is one piece of ground with joints in it, and you are hitting one end of it, and he is lying on the other end of it. Stop it being one thing and you have your answer."

Kael turned to look at her.

"What," she said.

"Say the middle part again."

"Stop it being one thing." She looked up then, and her eyes did what her eyes did, and she filed something and put it away without a word, exactly as she had been doing since the summer. "Corvane, get off the floor. You are enjoying this too much."

"I am enjoying it exactly enough." Aurelian sat back on his heels and considered the brick with real attention, and then said, "Very well. Put something between you and it. A rug."

"He cannot carry a rug."

"I did not say it was a *good* answer, Sedrane, I said it was an answer. It is more than either of you has produced." He looked at Kael. "What. Why are you doing that with your face."

"I can make a rug," Kael said.

"You cannot make a rug."

"I can make a rug the size of a boot, out of the floor, for about a quarter of a second." He was already crouching, already turning his hand over. "It doesn't have to be a rug. It only has to be a gap."

"Then it is not a rug," Elarine said.

"It's a rug if it works," said Aurelian Corvane, with enormous dignity, from the floor.

It cost him more than anything he had ever done. It was the smallest thing he had ever tried and it had to be built six times in six seconds, each one exact, each one under a shifting load, and every one of them came out of a purse that had never in its life had anything in it. The third afternoon of it put him flat on the bricks with his ears ringing and Aurelian's hand under his arm hauling him up, saying, "Enough. *Enough.* Whatever this is for, it is not worth being carried down that hill."

"It's worth it."

"Then tell me what it is for."

Kael did not say anything.

Aurelian looked at him for a moment. Then he let go of his arm, and said, "Right," in a voice with no injury in it anywhere, and went to fetch the water, because he had decided some time in the summer that this was a person he was going to be owed nothing by, and had not revisited it since.

Elarine watched the whole of it from the wall and said nothing at all.

---

On the ninth day he came up the last of the colonnade in six steps with a thread in every one of them, and put his hand flat on her door.

Nothing came through it.

He stood there with his palm on the wood and the grey coming up at the edges of everything, and counted to five, and then the latch lifted under his hand and he nearly went into the room after it.

Valeria Vel was a foot away, looking straight at him, with an expression he had never seen on her.

"Where are you," she said.

"Here."

"I can see where you are. I am asking where you *are*." She did not move out of the doorway. "There is nobody on my floor. There has been nobody on my floor for six steps. I have been holding it since the bell, the corridor is empty, and you are standing in it."

"I broke it," Kael said.

"Broke what."

"The floor. Under my foot, only while I was on it." He was leaning on the frame and had not decided to. "It's your thing backward. You're not listening to me, you're listening to the stone. So I stopped giving the stone anything to carry."

Valeria Vel stood in her doorway for a considerable time.

"Again," she said. "The last part. Slowly."

She had it diagnosed inside four days, which was the part he had expected. What he had not expected was the week after that, and the week after that one.

"You are not absent," she said, on the Thursday, winding her wrists and not looking at him. "That is the mistake and it is an interesting one. You are a *hole*. Every stone in that corridor talks all day — the woman at the desk, the boys on the stair, the sea, the cart on the upper road. And then there is a piece of corridor eight feet long saying nothing whatever, and it moves." She wound the other wrist. "Nothing is not quieter than everything else. Nothing is only quiet. I should be able to hunt you by the silence."

"Should."

"Should."

Kael sat down on the floor of her room, which he had never done, and put his head back against the wall and waited, because he had learned by then that she finished her own sentences and did not need help with them.

"I cannot," Valeria Vel said.

She said it the way she said everything, flat, priced, without one grain spent on how it landed, and it was the first time in eleven weeks that he had heard her say those two words in that order about anything at all.

"The floor can tell me what the floor is doing," she said. "You have taken the floor out of the conversation, so it tells me there is a quiet piece of corridor, and a quiet piece of corridor is also what a quiet piece of corridor looks like. To find the hole I need something that is not the floor." She turned her hands over and looked at them, and for a moment she was not in the room. "There will be something. Air moves. Air is a medium and it touches everything and it is never still, and if a man walked through it there would be a shape in it where he had been. I would need years and a school that does not exist and an element that is not mine."

"That's a lot of work to catch one person."

"Yes," she said, without any humour whatsoever, and then, a half-count later, with all of it: "You cannot beat me with a sword. You have beaten me with a floor."

"I can't beat you with a sword. Yet."

And there it was, the small hard fact of it, sitting between them on the boards: nine strides down to nothing at all, and she could not have it back, and both of them knew it, and neither of them said one further word about it, that day or any other day of that autumn.

It was the first thing he had ever taken from her.

He walked home that evening turning it over, and what came out at the end of the road was not the winning. He had been nothing to look at his whole life and had thought of that as the safest condition a person could be in. A girl with her back to a door had just explained to him, at no charge, that in a room where everything talks, the silent thing is the loudest thing in it — and that the only reason it was not going to cost him yet was that nobody alive had built the ear for it.

Yet.


---

# Chapter Seventeen — The List

The terms ran on into the autumn, and somewhere in them he learned what they were costing her, because he could not help learning it. She was slower to her guard on the days she had held the sense longest. Twice she came to the court after and did not eat the bread she brought, and once, in the ninth week, on an afternoon when he was doing nothing clever at all, he watched her fail to find him at nine strides in an open colonnade in flat light, and stand very still for a moment afterward with a look on her face he had seen exactly once before, on his own face, in a pail of water, the morning his own lightning had come home through his wrist and taught him what the bottom of a purse felt like from the inside.

He did not tell her to stop. It did not once occur to him to tell her to stop.

"How far down does it go," he said.

"I do not know."

"You've never been to the bottom."

"I have been told where it is." She was standing very straight, which he understood, because he had been taught to do it too. "Nobody has ever asked me to go and look."

"Then go and look," Kael said. "Not today. Tomorrow, at the start, before the yard, where I can watch. Spend until it stops working, and tell me what it does on the way down."

Valeria Vel looked at him for a moment, and the tiredness went out of her face and was replaced by the other thing.

"Yes," she said. "That is the correct question." She bent and picked up her blades. "Do you know what happens to a thing you own and never spend? It becomes a story your family tells. Come on."

They did it on the Tuesday. It took a little under two hours, and at the end of it she could not hold a resonance the width of a hand, and had to sit down on the steps of the sea court with her head hanging. He crouched in front of her and asked her nine questions in a row, and she answered all nine, in order, in a voice going thinner as it went.

Then she was sick behind the column, and came back, and said, "Ask the rest of them."

He asked the rest of them.

At no point in the morning did it occur to either of them that this was an unusual way for two children to spend one.

He wrote the numbers in the blank book that night, in his own hand, under a heading, the way he wrote down everything he was ever given.

The fighting was the other ledger and it ran the wrong way first, which he had not expected and should have.

Eleven exchanges in the spring, in front of four hundred people. Nine, on the third day of the terms. Nine again. Eight.

"You got worse," she said, standing over him, not unkindly.

"You know me now."

"Yes."

"In the yard I only ever had to be strange once." He got up in the order his father had built into him, and something in his shoulder disagreed with the last part of it. "Everyone I've fought for three years, I beat them the first time and after that they were beaten before they started, because the second time they came in trying not to lose the way they lost. You don't do that. You come in trying to find out what I did."

"That is the only interesting part," Valeria said.

They fought at the sea court where nobody went, out of the way of masters and rules and the healer's box, and they did each other real damage, and neither of them ever once discussed it.

The Temple's yard had three laws and Ruthen standing over all of them: nothing above the shoulders, nothing the healer could not mend by supper, and stop when I say stop. There was no Ruthen on the old court. There was a girl who had refused to have a bleeding arm looked at in front of the First Master of the Temple, and a boy whose father had spent the summer coming at him in the dark to find out what broke, and between the two of them the question of whether it was allowed to hurt did not arise, because neither of them had ever met the idea.

She split his lip in the fifth week and looked at it with professional interest while he bled.

"Your head goes to the left when you commit," she said. "It always has. It is very small."

"How small."

"Two fingers."

"Then it's a tell and it's mine and I've had it all my life," Kael said, holding his sleeve to his mouth, "so it's going to cost something to take out. Go again."

"You should have it stitched."

"It's a lip."

"Yes," Valeria said. "Guard."

He gave her the threading in the sixth week and never once said a word about it.

It happened the way it had happened in the spring, which was in the middle of a losing exchange with nothing plain left that would arrive in time, except that this time he had come up the stair knowing he would spend it, and had known for four days, and had not decided so much as stopped declining. The steel took the thread the way steel took it now, whole, end to end, unasked, and the parry came exactly where her parries came, and the blunted edge went along the inside of her forearm and opened it.

She looked at it. She looked at the blade in his hand. Then she stepped back one pace, and put her own point down, and stood there in the middle of the sea court with her arm bleeding onto the stones, and said, "*There* it is," in a voice with more in it than everything else she had said in eleven weeks put together.

"Yes," Kael said.

"Again."

"You're bleeding."

"I have been bleeding since the spring, and I have been thinking about it since the spring, and you are going to do it again while I am watching for it." She brought her point up. "Slower. Do it slower."

"It doesn't have a slower."

"Then do it more."

He did it eleven more times that week and she took every one of them, and by the end of it there were four new lines on her forearms among the old white ones, and she had never asked him a single question about where it came from, and he understood — not quickly; he understood it in the loft, three nights later, the way he understood most things about people, late and in the dark and in the wrong order — that she was not going to ask. That she had made a decision about him in a treeline conversation in the summer and had not revisited it since. That there were, in the world, people who could hold a shut door without knocking, and that until now he had only ever met three of them and they had all made him.

He went cold in the middle of it and did not know why, and lay awake, and got up in the dark and went out to the block and put his hands on the steel until it was time.

There was one thing he did not do.

It came in the ninth week, in a bind, both of them locked at the forte with the blades grinding and her weight arriving through the steel like a door being closed on him, and he felt the thought stand up in him fully formed and utterly clear. *Metal is a road. The road does not stop where my blade stops. There is steel touching steel between us right now.*

He had written it in the blank book in the summer, on a page of its own, with two technical objections under it. The objections were still true. There was a third thing under them that was not technical, and it was a girl's forearm opening in a spring yard in front of four hundred people, and it had been under everything for four months.

He broke the bind and gave ground and lost the exchange.

"You had that," Valeria said afterward.

"I had something."

"You had something and you put it down." She was winding her wrist, not looking at him. "Why."

"Because I don't know what it does," Kael said, "and you're the only person on this island worth doing it to."

She stopped winding for about half a count. Then she went on winding.

"Fifteen," she said. "Today. That was fifteen exchanges."

He had counted, because counting was his. He had known it was fifteen before his back hit the stones.

---

The thing about the hour was that it belonged to somebody.

He worked that out in the eighth week and did nothing about it for three, which he would afterward recognise as the most characteristic three weeks of his life. The hour after the yard was the cistern court's hour. It had been the cistern court's hour since the spring, and the cistern court now met twice a week and found him not in it, and nobody said anything, because one of the two people in it was a boy who paid his invoices at full rate and would not have known how to raise this one, and the other was Elarine Sedrane, who did not repeat her work.

She let it run eleven weeks. Then, on a wet afternoon in the turn toward the cold term, she was standing at the mouth of the cistern court when he came past it, which was not on his way to anything.

"Your lip is healing badly," Elarine said.

"It's fine."

"It is not fine, it is closing over dirt, and you have a green bruise under your jaw the size of a plum, and last week you carried your left arm for four days and told Corvane you fell." She had her arms folded and her voice was at the register she used for corrections that mattered. "I have been counting. Eleven weeks. Twice a week is twenty-two hours, and I have had four of them."

Kael said nothing, because everything he had was a shape.

"I am not asking you where you go," she said. "I asked you a question in the summer and you gave me a reason with nothing in it, and I decided not to ask twice, and I have kept that, which has cost me more than you will ever be in a position to know." Her eyes did what her eyes did. "I am telling you that the court has an hour and you are spending it somewhere. That is all. That is the entire complaint."

"It's the Vel girl," Kael said.

There was a silence in the mouth of the cistern court of a particular kind.

"Say that again," Elarine said.

"Every day. After the yard. Since the summer." He heard how it sounded arriving and could do nothing about the order it arrived in. "She caught me watching her train and instead of having me disciplined she set terms, and I've been keeping them."

"You have been *fighting Valeria Vel* every day for eleven weeks."

"Yes."

"And in the summer, in that court, when I said we should ask her, you said no." She said it very evenly. "You said no in a beat and a half and could not put one word in the gap."

"Yes."

"Why."

And there it was again, the same gap, in the same court, with the same person standing in it waiting, and this time he made himself stand in it too instead of walking around it, because he had spent eleven weeks with somebody who did not walk around things and something of it had got into him.

"Because I wanted it," he said. "Just me. I've never had anything that was only mine before and I didn't want to hand it to a court." He looked at her. "That's the reason. It isn't a good one."

Elarine Sedrane looked back at him for a long moment.

"No," she said. "It is not." And then, having priced it exactly, and having decided something, and letting none of it onto her face: "Bring her tomorrow."

"She won't come."

"I did not ask you whether she would come. I said bring her." She turned to go, and stopped, and did not turn back. "That girl has been on this island eight months and has not once been in a room with anybody her own age who was not being paid to hit her. I am aware that you had not noticed. You notice everything and you had not noticed that."

"I noticed," Kael said.

Elarine paused half a step.

"Huh," she said, and went in out of the rain.

---

He asked her the next afternoon, badly.

He had built the sentence four times on the stair and it came out as none of them. What came out was, "There's a court. Sedrane and Corvane. Nobody comes there and nothing said in it goes into the hall. It's the hour after the yard, which is this hour, and I'd have to give it up to keep it, so." He stopped. "So."

Valeria Vel stood in her doorway with a blade in each hand and looked at him for what felt like a considerable portion of his life.

"Corvane," she said.

"He's better than he was in the spring. He'd tell you so himself, at length."

"I put him on the stones in six exchanges."

"He hasn't stopped talking about it since," Kael said. "It's the first thing that ever happened to him."

She was quiet. And then something occurred which he had no framework for at all, which was that Valeria Vel, who priced a court of six hundred in one pass and had never once in his sight taken longer than a breath about anything, stood in a doorway and could not decide.

"Why," she said at last.

"Because you're better than all of us and it hasn't done you any good," Kael said, and immediately wished he had built the sentence a fifth time.

She looked at the blades in her hands.

"Yes," Valeria said. "All right."

And her face, for about a quarter of a second, did something he could not read at all, and if he had been able to read it he would have seen a girl who had just surprised herself so badly that she had to look at her own hands to make sure they were still hers.

---

The cistern court in the cold term at the day's end was three walls of old brick, a bench nobody sat on, and enough light for another half hour.

Aurelian Corvane got to his feet when she came in.

He did it without deciding to, the way you stand up when the room changes, and then he was standing, and then it was too late to sit down, and Kael watched the tallest, loudest, best-born boy of the cohort stand in a disused cistern court holding his own hands and say, "Vel," in a voice that had somehow acquired a bow inside it.

"Corvane."

"I would like to say," Aurelian said, "that the sixth exchange was not representative."

"It was representative."

"Yes," he said, after a moment, and something went out of his shoulders, and something else came into them that suited him a great deal better. "Yes, it was."

"Sit down, Corvane, you look like a footman," Elarine said from the bench. She had not stood up. She had not, so far as Kael could see, moved anything except her eyes, which had gone over Valeria once and come back with everything. "Sedrane. You will be Vel. Two things, and then we can all stop performing. One: nothing said here is said in the hall, ever, including by me, and I am the worst of the three of us for it. Two: in here you are not the Line of Vel, because I am not a Sedrane in here and he is whatever he is everywhere, which remains the only honest position available."

Valeria looked at her for a moment.

"What are you in here," she said.

"Better than everyone in this Temple at one thing and bound hand and foot regardless," Elarine said. "You?"

"The same," Valeria said. "Different thing."

"Yes," said Elarine Sedrane. "I thought so."

And that, as far as Kael could ever afterward determine, was the entire negotiation.

They fought for twenty minutes and it was a shambles, because four people who have never trained together are four people, and Aurelian threw a fan of fire so large that Elarine put her face in her hands, and Valeria took him apart twice without appearing to be present for either of them, and then made him do the second one again slowly, four times, with her hand on his elbow, until he did it right and looked at his own arm as though it had been lent to him.

Kael sat on the bench with his lip stinging and watched it happen and did not have a word for what he was looking at.

It was Aurelian who brought the rest of it, afterward, in the last of the light, in the voice of a man reading a manifest.

"The tournament. The prize is a city," he said.

Elarine's head came up.

"My brother's letter came Tuesday. It's Ammareth, in the east." He said the name the way his people said names, as an object with a price. "Taken eighteen months ago. The empire has been sitting on it deciding what to do with it, and what it is going to do with it is give it away in the spring. Revenues, governorship, the seat. It goes to the winner's house, held in the winner's name until he comes of age."

Nobody said anything for a moment.

"A city," Elarine said.

"A city."

"For a *child*."

"For a child's house," Aurelian said. "Which is the point."

"Who lives in it," Kael said.

They turned and looked at him.

"Ammareth. It's a city." He was aware of having changed the temperature of the court and could not tell in which direction, which was ordinary, so he went on. "There are people in it. There were people in it eighteen months ago and there are people in it now. What happens to them?"

Aurelian's face did something Kael had never seen on it, which was blank incomprehension without any contempt anywhere in it. He was not being cruel. He was explaining a road to somebody who had never once been on a road.

"Nothing happens to them," he said. "They go on living in it. They pay to a different name, that is all. You do not empty a city — there would be nothing left worth having. You take the top off it and put a new top on."

"And the old top?"

"Depends what the old top did." Aurelian shrugged.

Kael filed the shrug. He filed it whole, in one piece, the way he had learned to file things he had no drawer for, and he did not take it out again for a long time.

"Which is exactly the point." Elarine had come off the wall, and there was a colour high on her cheeks that Kael had seen twice before and both times it had meant something was about to be said properly. "Do you understand what that is? That is not a prize. Nobody gives a province to a thirteen-year-old because he is good with a spear. They are not choosing a champion, they are choosing an *order*, and they are doing it in one place, in one week, in front of everyone, so that nobody can argue with it afterward. Three schools. Every house in the empire that matters, sitting in the stands with a list." She turned on her heel and turned back. "The boards, Kael. It is the boards. It is the boards for the whole empire and it is written once and you carry it your entire life."

"Yes," said Valeria Vel.

They all looked at her.

"My father's letter said the same thing in fewer words," she said. "It said: they will be pricing you. Do not be cheap." She was cleaning her blade on a cloth, unhurried. "In the capital they have been drawing the lists for a year. Who will be watched. Who will be placed. Which of the coastal houses can be raised without offending which of the old ones. The tournament is where they write it down." She looked up. "It is not a secret. It is only never said in front of the children, and I have been in the room, because nobody counts me as one."

"So we are stock," Elarine said.

"You are being priced," said Valeria Vel. "Yes."

"And that does not trouble you."

"No."

Elarine turned round properly for the first time since the conversation had started.

"It is the whole of the point," Valeria said. She had not raised her voice and did not appear to understand that anything had changed in the court. "The empire will need officers in nine years and generals in twenty, and it cannot wait until then to find out which of us is worth a province. So it looks at us now, in one place, at once, and it writes down what it sees. That is not a cruelty. That is a state doing the one thing a state is for." She wound the cloth around her hand. "My line has been read that way for eleven generations. We have never once been asked whether we consented to be useful. I do not know what I would say if I were asked. I have never been able to think of an answer that was not an insult to my father."

Nobody said anything at all.

"You are all speaking as though being looked at were the injury," she said. "Being looked at is the only reason any of this is worth doing. If they stopped looking, what would you be? You would be four children in a wet yard." She finished the cloth and put her hands down. "I intend to be looked at. I intend to be written down first. I will win it."

And she said it exactly as she had said it on the steps in the spring, without a grain spent on how it landed, and this time nobody in the court made a sound.

Kael sat on the bench in the dark with his lip stinging and looked at the three of them: a boy who would inherit a fleet and had just discovered what the fleet was for; a girl whose house had chosen her school and would choose her seat and her man, and who did not pretend the rope was a sash; and a girl forged from birth by a line eleven generations deep, who had heard the same facts they had, and priced them the same way, and come out the other side holding them like something handed down. Two of them had just found out what they were. The third had always known, and was proud, and could not follow the other two anywhere from here.

And not one of the three had answered him about Ammareth. It had not been refused, or laughed at, or turned aside. The question had gone up into the air of the court and found nothing anywhere to land on, and the conversation had walked over the top of it on its way to the part that mattered, which was what the empire was going to write down about them.

Oss had said it a year and a half ago at a sea wall with his hands drawing in the air — *they're not teaching us, they're making us* — and had been laughed at by nobody, because Kael had been the only one there, and Oss was three tables away in the hall now and had not spoken to him in a year.

"Well," Aurelian said. "It changes nothing. I was going to enter and win it before, and now I am going to enter and win it while understanding what it is for." He said it lightly and it did not come out light. "Sedrane?"

"Obviously I am entering," Elarine said. "My house has entered me. There was no conversation."

"Vel."

"You have my answer."

Every one of them had answered with a *because*. Kael went looking for his own and found the work standing where the because should have been.

And then the three of them turned, in the ordinary way of a circle going round, and looked at Kael.

He had known this was coming for eleven weeks. He had known it, in some form, since a winter night at a table with a lamp on it when his father had said seven words in the fastest voice he had ever used. He had eleven weeks and four months and a year and he had never once built the sentence, because there was no sentence; there was a fact, and the fact had the mountain inside it, and everything he could say that was true would open the door on it.

"I'm not enrolling," Kael said.

The light in the cistern court was almost gone.

"Say that again," Aurelian said.

"I'm not enrolling."

"You are three lines from the top of the boards."

"I know."

"You lasted eleven exchanges against—" Aurelian stopped, and started again, at his own pace. "I have spent a year getting worse on purpose so that you could take me apart, and you are telling me you will be standing in the crowd."

"Yes."

"Why?"

"It isn't for me," Kael said.

Nobody said anything.

Elarine Sedrane did not move at all, and Kael, who could not read a room to save his life and had never once been able to, understood with total and unwelcome clarity that she had heard this sentence before, in this court, in the summer, about this girl, and that she was at this moment laying the two of them side by side and finding that they were the same shape, and that she had just been handed the second one and would not be handed a third.

She did not say it. That was the thing he took home. She looked at him with her face perfectly still and she did not say one word of it, and the not-saying went into the drawer with everything else and made it heavier.

"That is a shame," Valeria said.

Kael looked up.

"I wanted the rematch," she said. "In front of them. With everything." She slid the blade home into the case and closed it, and her voice did not change at all, which was how he had learned to tell when it mattered. "It is the only thing on the list I actually wanted."

She picked up the case and went to the arch, and stopped there, and looked back at him across the dark court, and Kael waited for the question, because everyone asked the question, because there had never once been a person in his life who had not eventually asked the question.

Valeria Vel looked at him for three full seconds, and turned, and went out into the rain.

The bell went for the last meal. Aurelian swore softly and went after her to say something about the sixth exchange. Elarine gathered her satchel with more care than it required.

"Tomorrow they nail the lists to the colonnade," she said, at the arch, not looking round. "First court. After the boards. Every name that is going, in earned order, for everyone to stand in front of and read."

Then she went out too, and Kael sat on in the dark with the rain coming into the cistern court, and found that he had known for a year that he was not going, and that it had been a rule, and a law, and a thing his father had said, and a door he had shut himself.

It had never once been a list with his name off it.


---

# Chapter Eighteen — The Entry

The list went up before the first bell, because the Temple liked a thing to be standing there already when the children came out to it.

Kael came in off the road with his boots wet to the ankle and the rain still in his hair, and the first court was solid at the colonnade, three deep the way the yard had gone three deep in the spring, and making the same sound. He went along the outside of the crowd to the west end, where the pillar stood off the wall by the width of a hand and a small boy could get his shoulder into the gap and read sideways, and he read the list sideways, in the grey light, over the top of somebody's collar.

It was two sheets. The Temple's clerks had ruled the columns with the same hand they ruled everything with, and the names came down in earned order, off the standings that had been hanging on that wall since the spring, and it read like the boards had learned to walk.

*Valeria, of the Line of Vel.*

He had known that since the day she arrived and she had said so herself, out loud, on the steps, in front of everybody, and it still went through him.

Then Corvane. Then two of the senior cohort he only knew by their reach. Then Sedrane.

Then, four lines down, in the clerk's flat hand, no taller and no shorter than anything else on the sheet:

*Kael.*

He read it. Then he read it again, from the top, all four names, to make sure he had not come in at the wrong place on the page. Then he read it a third time with his thumb under the line, the way his mother had taught him to hold a column of figures still while he checked it, and it stayed where it was.

And for about the space of one breath, before anything else got there, he was happy.

It came up through him the way the want had come up on the steps a year and a half ago, with his whole body, leaning. The thing he had asked for and been refused and stopped asking for was standing on a wall in a public hand where four hundred people would read it, and it had his name on it. He felt his own face start to do something. He caught it about halfway and put it back.

Because a list is a document, and documents are made by clerks, and clerks make mistakes.

That was the whole of it. That was the only drawer in the house that fit. Somebody had copied a line wrong off the standings, and by the second bell a man with a pen would come out and score through it and write it again correctly, and there would be an afternoon of it in the hall and then it would be over.

He got his shoulder back out of the gap and stood in the wet with the crowd noise going over him and made himself finish reading properly, which meant the columns he had skipped.

Every name on that sheet had a house after it.

*Corvane. Sedrane. Drusane. Vel.* The clerks had put them in the second column with the same flat hand, because a boy does not enter a tournament that gives away a city; a boy's house enters him, and the city goes to the house, and the boy holds it in his name until he is old enough to be told what he owns.

Kael had no house. He had never had one. On the day he was entered in the roll, at ten years old, the space had been left empty and a clerk had drawn a line through it to close it, and the master had said *leave the space as it is, empty is true,* and Kael had carried the line for four years like a thing he owned.

The space was not empty.

There was a name in it, and after the name a word he did not know, and the word had the shape words have when they mean an office rather than a person.

The name was Halden.

---

He stood there for a while with the collar in front of him moving and the rain coming off the colonnade roof in a line, and the drawer he had put the judge in two years ago — the shut one, the one with the level warmth in it that he had filed and not examined and not opened since — came open by itself, the way a bar comes off a door that somebody has been leaning on for a long time.

He did not know what was in it. That was the thing. He had a wrongness with a face on it now and not one single word to put after the wrongness.

"There you are." Aurelian Corvane came out of the crowd with his hair wet and his whole carriage a hand higher than it had been yesterday, and he took Kael by the shoulder with both hands and shook him once, hard, the way you shake a post to see if it is set. "You *lied* to me."

"No."

"Four lines. Four lines, and *below* Sedrane, which she will be unbearable about for a year, and you sat in that court in the dark and told me it was not for you." He was grinning. It was an entirely undefended grin and Kael had never seen it on him and did not have anywhere to put it. "Was it a house? Did somebody take you? Tell me it was not Vessaline, I will not be able to bear it if it was Vessaline—"

"I don't know who it was."

Aurelian's grin came down about a quarter of the way, the way a sail comes down, unhurried, still full.

"You don't know."

"No."

"Somebody has stood for you at the empire's games and you do not know who."

"No," Kael said.

And Aurelian Corvane, who had been raised inside the arithmetic of houses standing for people the way Kael had been raised inside a count, went quiet, and looked at the wall, and then looked back at Kael, and something crossed his face that had nothing at all in it of the morning.

"Right," he said.

Elarine had not come out of the crowd. She was on the far side of it, at the second pillar, and she had been there the whole time, and she was not reading the list. She was reading him. When he found her she did not look away and she did not come over, and she moved her eyes once, deliberately, from him to the second column and back, and let him see her do it.

Then she turned round and went in to letters, because the bell had gone.

Valeria came past him at the arch with her case in her hand and her blades in the case and the grey coats waiting up the path, and she stopped, which she did not do, and stood in front of him for exactly as long as it took.

"You're on it," she said.

"Yes."

"Good," said Valeria Vel, and went.

It was the only clean thing that happened to him all day, and it was going to be a long time before he could take it out and look at it without the rest of the day coming out of the drawer attached to it.

---

He had never gone up to the study without being sent for.

Four years, and every one of the summonses had come the same way — a boy from the clerks' room at the end of the third lesson, the same words every time, *the First Master will see you before you go down* — and Kael had gone up the stair and sat in the chair and had his book read and his year audited and gone home. He had never once climbed that stair on his own account. It had not occurred to him that a person could.

The door was open. That was the first thing.

The second thing was that the master was standing, and the fern on the sill had not been touched, and there were two letters on the desk lying open, and the honey jar was where it always was, and the room had the look of a room somebody had been walking around in since before it was light.

"Come in and close it," Severin said.

Kael closed it.

"You have seen the list."

"Yes."

"Sit down." The master went round the desk and did not sit himself, which Kael had also never seen. He put one finger on the nearer of the two letters and moved it very slightly, as though it had been lying at an angle he did not like. "I am going to tell you three things and then I am going to answer whatever you have. The first is the one you came for, so I will not make you carry it up the hill. I have spent this morning finding out what I can do about that sheet."

"Yes."

"Nothing," said Severin.

The rain went along the window.

"I want you to hear the shape of it, because you will build something wrong out of it otherwise and I would rather you built the true thing." He came round the desk's end and leaned back against it, and folded his arms, and looked at the floor between them the way a man looks at ground he is about to walk over. "The Temple is mine. The tournament is not. The empire holds the games; three schools convene; the island keeps them because the island is convenient and because I asked for them, four years ago, when I thought I understood what I was asking for. Entries are made to the games, under the games' seal, by men the games appoint. A judge may stand for a competitor who has no house. It is a small provision. It exists so that the empire never loses a boy off a farm because his father cannot write."

"And it was used."

"It was used correctly," Severin said. "That is what I have spent the morning learning. There is no error in it anywhere. It is entered, it is sealed, it is lodged in the capital, and it was lodged nine days ago, which means it was lodged before the standings closed, which means whoever wrote it knew where you were going to come on that sheet before you did." He looked up. "I can refuse you a room. I can refuse you the yard. I cannot refuse you the games, and I cannot take your name off a document that is not mine, and if I write to the capital about it I will be writing to men who will want to know why the First Master of the archipelago is troubled about one entry among four hundred, and I will have made the thing I am trying to make small, large."

Kael sat with his hands on his knees and ran it twice.

"Then somebody is above you," he said.

"Yes."

He had expected the man to soften it. Four years of doors with nothing written on them, and this one had a wall behind it and the master had let him walk straight into the wall without putting a hand out.
