"That is the second thing," Severin said. "And the third is this, and I have been carrying it since the spring and I have chosen every single day not to say it to you, and I was wrong, so I will say it now."

He waited.

"In the spring you put an edge on a piece of wood in front of four hundred people," the master said.

Kael did not move.

"I have not asked you what it was. I am not going to ask you now." The old man's voice did not change and did not rise and stayed exactly where it had been, which was somehow worse than if it had done either. "You will notice that everybody on this island agreed within a quarter of an hour that the sun had been on the sea. That agreement was not luck. Four hundred people in that yard wanted an easy answer and were handed one and took it, and I let them take it, and I have let them keep it since." He turned his head and looked at the window. "I am telling you that somebody in that colonnade did not need an easy answer. He had a hard one already. And nine days ago a man wrote your name onto a sheet in the capital."

The rain went along the window again.

"You are twelve years old," Severin said, "and I would give a great deal not to be saying any of this to you, and I find that I am going to say it anyway, because the alternative is that you go down that hill this evening and work it out on your own on the road, and you would, and you would work out three quarters of it, and the missing quarter is the one that gets people hurt."

"Who is Halden," Kael said.

"A judge of the games."

"That's the sheet's answer."

"It is also mine," the master said, "because I have been asking since the first bell and it is the only answer anybody in my Temple has for me, and I do not like a man of whom that is true. He is not one of the great houses. He is not anyone's cousin. He arrived two springs early with a warrant that was correct, and he has walked my grounds for two years being pleasant to everybody, and I have looked at that warrant twice this morning and both times it was still correct." He unfolded his arms. "He is the empire, Kael. Not a house. Not a family with a grudge in it that can be traded with. The empire is not one thing that wants one thing — it is ten thousand men in ten thousand rooms, and any one of them who has a seal and a reason can reach into another man's school and take a boy off a wall, and there is no door to knock on, because it was all done correctly."

Kael looked at the two letters lying open on the desk and did not read them, which took something.

"You told me at the rock," he said, "that you were done carrying me."

"I said it at the gate, and I said it because you needed to walk into that court on your own feet." Severin came off the desk. "I am telling you now that whatever this is, you are not standing in it by yourself. Not you, and not your house. Whatever it costs, and whatever it takes, and however far it has to go — you have that from me, and you will have it in the spring, and you would have had it if you had never once climbed this stair."

Kael sat and looked at his own hands.

He had been given a great many things in that room over four years: a blank book, an empty column, a jar of honey that was never mentioned, a sentence about not-knowing that he had carried since he was ten. This was the first one he could not price at all.

"Go to letters," the master said. "You are late, and being late is a thing people remember about a boy on a list."

---

The judge was leaning on the rail above the yard at the end of the afternoon, in the place where the wall gave the best view of the stones, with his coat done up against the weather and his hands comfortable on the timber, and he was watching the second cohort spar, and he turned as Kael came past as though he had felt the air change.

"There he is," said Halden.

Kael stopped.

It was, he thought afterward, the one moment in the whole day when something might have gone another way, because he could have gone past. The road was there. The bell had gone. And he stopped, because the drawer had come open in the morning and it had been open all day and he could not walk past the only hand in the world that might have something to put in it.

"Congratulations," the judge said, and put his own hand out.

Kael looked at it.

"Ah," Halden said, and took it back without the smallest change anywhere in his face, and folded both hands on the rail again. "You have read the second column."

"Yes."

"Then say it. Go on. You have been carrying it up and down this hill since the bell and it will do you no good in your pocket."

"Why."

"Because it was a pity," said the judge.

He said it easily, in the voice that had made two years of corridors and doorways and accidental crossings feel like a warm room, and he said it looking out at the yard, where two boys of the second cohort were being stood up by the yard-master and made to salute each other again.

"Look at them," Halden said. "That is Talrane's second son and that is an Iscane, and both of them will be entered, and neither of them will last the second day, and both of them have been entered since the week the games were announced, because their houses wrote a letter and paid a fee and the thing was done. Nobody asked whether they were any good. Nobody in the empire has ever asked that question about a boy with a name." He turned his head. "And four lines above them, on the same sheet, at four hundred to one against, there is a herder's son who has been three lines off the top of this Temple's boards for two years and cannot be entered by anybody, because there is nobody to write the letter."

"There's a provision," Kael said.

"There is a provision." The judge looked genuinely pleased, the way he looked pleased at everything. "You went and found it out. Of course you did. Yes. There is a small dusty provision in the games' law, put there by men who are all dead now, and it is used perhaps twice in a generation, and it exists exactly and precisely for the situation of a boy off a farm whose father cannot write his name." He spread one hand on the rail. "What a pity it would be, not to showcase the empire's finest."

Kael stood very still.

"You want to know what I get," Halden said. "Ask it."

"What do you get."

"Nothing at all, and I am aware that you will not believe that, and I would think less of you if you did." He shrugged, comfortably, the whole broad shape of him going up and coming down. "Here is the honest article. A judge who stands for a boy who wins is a judge who was right about something, in front of every house in the empire, in one week, once, and carried for the rest of his life. That is what I get. It is not a small thing and I am not going to pretend to you that it is nothing. But it does not cost you a grain, and it does not cost your master a grain, and it does not put one hand on you anywhere. It buys me a good week in the spring if you are what I think you are." He smiled. "And if you are not, I will have been wrong in public, which men in my position generally take care never to be. I priced it. I am content with the price."

"Take it off."

"I cannot."

"You put it on."

"I lodged it," the judge said, "and it went to the capital, and the capital sealed it, and the capital does not unseal things because a twelve-year-old on an island has changed his mind. Neither would you, if you had ever seen the room it is filed in." He said it without one grain of unkindness. That was the thing Kael kept getting stuck on. There was no cruelty in it anywhere. "I will tell you the truth, since you have asked me for it twice and got it twice. If I could take it off, I would not. You do not want me to. You have wanted this since before I ever came to this island, and everybody within a mile of you knows it, and the only reason you are standing here with that face on is that somebody has forbidden you, and you have not told me who, and I have not asked, and I am not going to."

The yard-master's voice came up off the stones, flat and even, counting a cadence.

"Two years I have been on this rock," Halden said, "and in two years I have watched them price you at nothing four separate ways, and every single time they were wrong, and every single time you let them stay wrong, which is a discipline I have never in my life seen in a child and which I would like, someday, to understand." He straightened off the rail and worked his shoulders, an ordinary man at the end of an ordinary day. "In the spring they will not be able to. That is all I have done. I have taken away their ability to go on being wrong about you, and I have not asked you for anything, and I am not going to ask you for anything now."

He pulled his coat straight.

"Go home, lad," he said kindly. "It is coming on to rain again and you have a long road."

---

He made the man at the second bend.

It was not a thing he did on purpose. His head was full — the four lines, the second column, the two letters lying open on a desk, *he is the empire, Kael* — and none of that had anything to do with his feet, and somewhere underneath the thinking the part of him that had been built first went on counting the way it always did.

At the bend above the village the road gave you two hundred strides of the way you had come. Kael had never in his life turned a corner without buying the corner first.

There was a man on it at about a hundred and fifty, walking.

That was the entire content of it. A man on a public road at the hour when men come off the water, in a wool coat the colour of every coat on this island, going the way half the island went.

Kael came round the bend without changing one thing about how he walked, and thought, in his mother's voice: *ask twice, with one thing changed.*

He stopped at the top of the rise and made a long slow business of a lace that did not need it, and counted forty, and went on.

At the next place the road looked back on itself, the man was at a hundred and fifty.

What happened in him then was not one thing, and he was ashamed of about half of it for years afterward.

The first half arrived at once and it was very nearly joy. Everything he had — every dark scree run, every count, every hour of being made into nothing while a boat sat off the coast — came up under him together and stood there ready, and it was enormous, and it was *fun*. He had been carrying a whole school around inside his body for years and not one single person had ever *needed* him to use it. Now somebody did. His chest was going like a boy running and he was not running, and he wanted, very badly, to be very good at this.

The other half came in underneath it about ten strides later and was the size of the mountain.

There was one road. It went up, and open, past the split rock, and it went to one door, and it had gone to that door every day of his life. Nobody had ever said out loud that a road works in both directions. Nobody had ever needed to.

*This house lives because it is not looked at.*

He had a school for this. He had never once been told what the school was for.

The losing took about four minutes and cost him nothing whatsoever, which was the part he could not afterward get over.

Where the road dipped through a fold, a walker behind you lost the top of your head for four seconds, and on the uphill side the bank gave out into scree, a long tilted field of loose grey stone that a man in town boots cannot cross quietly in the dark and that Kael had been running loaded, in the dark, with a count going, since he was eight years old. He used three of the four seconds. He went up eleven feet of bank on the outsides of his feet and lay down in the stones.

The man came along at the same unhurried pace he had kept for two miles and went by twenty feet under him: wool cap, wool coat, a man about his father's build and nothing whatever like his father's carriage, no badge, no colour, nothing on him anywhere that meant a single thing. Kael never saw the face and did not need it. He took the walk instead, the way he had taken Aurelian's to get to fourteen strides, and put it away in the place where he kept a six-oared boat and a dark stick going up to a face.

The man did not slow, and did not look up, and did not come back.

He had not been sent to follow a boy who could do this. Nobody had told him there was one.

The stillness game ended on his father's word and always had, and there was no one on that hillside to say *done*, so Kael set the rule himself — the lamps at the water, all of them, and a hundred after that — and held it long past any use, because being finished was not the same as being sure. Then he went home the wrong way: up over the shoulder, along the goat line, down onto the path above the split rock from the side that had no road on it, adding forty minutes to a walk he could have done blind.

He came at his own house from above and stopped and looked at it the way he had been taught to look at anything before giving it his weight. The lamp was lit. His mother's shape crossed the window and did not stop at it. The bar would be down, because the bar had been down every night of his life.

Some of it was still going in him. That was what he carried down the last of the hill: that a grown man had walked behind him to within two miles of his mother's door, and that he had beaten him without hurrying and enjoyed it somewhere a long way down — and that he was now going to have to go in and say all of it out loud to the two people who had built the part of him that enjoyed it.

He went down.


---

# Chapter Nineteen — The Winter

The door opened when he was still four strides off it, which meant his mother had been at the window and not at the fire, and had been there long enough to know his walk before she could have seen his face.

Behind her, in the middle of the room, his father was standing.

Kael came in and put his back against the wood and shut it with his weight.

"I was followed," he said.

His mother went past him and put the bar down and it went into the stone the way it had gone into the stone every night of his life, and the sound was the same sound, and everything in the room changed anyway.

"From where," his father said.

"I don't know where he picked me up. I had him at the second bend above the village, a hundred and fifty strides back, walking."

"Sit."

Kael sat. Neither of them did.

"Go."

It came out of him fast and complete, because it was the one part of the day he had a shape for.

"A man on a public road at the hour men come off the water. Wool cap, wool coat, the colour of every coat on this island. My height and a good deal heavier through the chest. Boots not made here — the sole was too flat and he set the whole foot down at once, so he came off a ship or he came off a horse. He never closed and he never fell back. A hundred and fifty at the bend. A hundred and fifty at the top of the rise."

"You tested it."

"Twice. I stopped at the top of the rise over a lace that did not need tying and counted forty, and then went on. A man walking home covers forty strides in that time. He was still a hundred and fifty back. I did it again lower down and got the same number."

"Where did you lose him."

"The fold under the goat line, where a walker behind you loses the top of your head for four seconds. I used three. Eleven feet up the bank on the outsides of my feet and down flat in the scree."

"How long did you hold the stillness."

"Until the lamps at the water went out. All of them. Then a hundred more."

His father came and put one hand on the back of the chair opposite and left it there.

"Did he go past you or did he stop."

"Went past. Same pace he'd kept for two miles. Didn't slow at the fold."

"Did he look up the bank."

Kael opened his mouth and found that he had to go and get the answer, and that it took him longer than it should have, and that his father waited for it without moving.

"No," he said. "He never looked at the bank at all. He didn't check the high ground. Not once, anywhere on that road, and there are four places on it where I would have."

Something went across the table between his mother and his father that Kael could not read, and he had been reading those two faces his entire life.

"Then one of three things," Vask said. "He was bad at the work. Or he was not alone, and did not need to be good at it. Or he was never meant to be hidden from you at all."

The fire moved in its hole.

"What's the third one mean," Kael said.

"It means a man walked up the only road there is, behind a boy, at a distance the boy could see him from, and did not do one single thing to keep himself out of sight. That is not always poor work." His father took his hand off the chair. "Sometimes a man is put on a road to be noticed. You learn a great deal about a house by letting it find out it is being looked at, and watching what it does next."

"And what did this house do."

"Nothing yet," said Vask. "Because you are good, and because you came the long way, and because I have not gone outside."

Kael understood, about a breath later, that his father had been standing in the middle of that room for forty minutes not because he was waiting, but because going out to walk the ground was the one thing in the world he wanted to do and the one thing he could not afford to be seen doing.

His mother sat down across from him then and put her hands flat on the table and began.

Where did you first have him. What was the light doing. What did you change. What did he do when you changed it. How many on the road behind him, how many in front. Was there a boat off the point when you came over the shoulder, and how many oars. Where was the sun. They came in the order they always came in, no matter what the thing was that had happened, and he answered all of them, and somewhere in the middle he noticed for the second time in his life that the order did not change when the subject did, and that he had a name for that in the yard and none for it at this table.

"His face," she said at the end.

"I never saw it. I have the cap, the shoulders and the walk."

"You have the walk."

"I have the walk."

"Then you will walk it for your father tomorrow," Neris said, "at speed, four times, and he will tell you what that man does for a living."

"And nothing on him," Vask said. "You would have said."

"Nothing. No badge, no colour, no ring, no dog, nothing on him anywhere that meant one single thing."

His father nodded once, slowly, at the middle of the table, and did not look pleased and did not look afraid, and said, "Good," and Kael discovered that he had been waiting eleven years to be frightened by that word.

---

"Now the rest of it," Vask said.

Kael put his hands on the table because he did not know where else to put them.

"The tournament lists went up this morning. In earned order, off the standings." He made himself say it at one speed. "Vel. Corvane. Two of the senior cohort. Sedrane. And me, four lines down."

Neither of them moved.

"I did not enter," Kael said. "I want that known first. I have not gone near it, I have not asked anyone, I have not written one word to anybody. Somebody entered me."

"Who."

"There is a provision in the games' law. A judge of the games may stand for a competitor who has no house." He kept his eyes on his father, because four years ago he had learned the reading was better there. "A judge used it. It is sealed and it is lodged in the capital and it went nine days ago — before the standings closed. Which means whoever wrote it knew where I was going to fall on that sheet before I did."

"The master," said his mother.

"Spent this morning finding out what he could do about it." Kael heard his own voice do something and put it back. "Nothing. The Temple is his and the tournament is not. He can refuse me a room and refuse me the yard, but he cannot refuse me the games."

His father sat down.

It was the first thing either of them had done all night that Kael could put a price on, and what it came to frightened him more than the road had.

"There is a second column on that sheet," Kael said. "Every name on it has a house written beside it, because a boy does not enter the games, his house enters him. Mine has never had anything in it. Today it has a man's name and a word after it that means an office."

"Then give me the name," said his mother.

"Halden."

He was watching both of them when he said it. He had decided on the road that he would, and he had spent eleven years being taught by these two people how to read a face while it takes something in, and the last of that schooling had been beaten into him over one autumn by a girl who could read a room of six hundred in a single pass.

He got nothing.

Not stillness — stillness would have been an answer. What he got was two people hearing a name for the first time in their lives. His mother's eyes went very slightly up and to the side, the way they did when she was going back through everything she had, looking for something. Then they came back, and there was nothing in them. His father waited while she did it, which told Kael which of the two of them carried the names.

"No," Neris said.

"No," said Vask.

It had taken four seconds, and neither of them had any idea what they had just told their son, which was that the man who had reached into his life was somebody they had never heard of. Nothing else that happened to him that day came near it.

"Who is he," his mother said.

"A judge of the games."

It came out level and unhurried, and every word of it was true, and it was the smallest true thing he could have said. Behind it stood two years of corridors and doorways and meetings shaped like accidents; a warmth that ran at exactly the same heat for everybody, which was the one wrong note he had ever caught in the man; a judge who talked about the capital the way Oss had once talked about the Temple. He had shut all of it in a drawer and had not opened the drawer since, and it had come open by itself that morning, in the rain, in front of a wall.

"He came two springs early with a warrant that is correct," Kael said. "The First Master looked at it twice today and both times it was still correct. He is not one of the great houses and he is not anybody's cousin." And then, because the rest of it was the part that mattered and he was not going to keep that back too: "The master said — *he is the empire, Kael. Not a house.*"

Vask looked at Neris.

"There is one more," Kael said. "I think it is the worst one, and I have been carrying it."

"Give it."

"He talked to me about the spring. The exhibition. The thing I did to the Vel girl's arm." He had told them that himself, in this room, at this table, in the spring. "He said the agreement was not luck. Four hundred people wanted an easy answer and were handed one and took it, and he let them keep it. And then he said he was not going to ask me what it was, and that he had chosen every day since the spring not to say any of this, and that he had been wrong to." Kael looked at his hands. "And then: *somebody in that colonnade did not need an easy answer. He had a hard one already.* And nine days ago a man wrote my name onto a sheet in the capital."

His mother stood up.

She did not go to the window. He noticed it because he had cracked the window pattern when he was nine years old and had gone on quietly testing it ever since, the way a man puts his hand on a rail he does not trust. She went to the door instead and put her palm flat on a bar that was already down.

"Say the judge's own sentence," she said. "The one at the rail. Not the master's."

"*What a pity it would be, not to showcase the empire's finest.*"

"And he asked you for nothing."

"Nothing at all. He said so twice." Kael turned in the chair. "He was kind about it. That is the part I cannot get past. There was no cruelty in it anywhere."

"No," his mother said. "There would not be."

---

"I've known for a while now," Kael said. "That something, or someone, is looking for us. How long have you known it would come for us?"

Neither of them asked him what he meant.

"For as long as you lived," his father said.

"And you have watched a sea every night of my life for... whoever that *someone* is?"

"Yes."

"Then say the rest of it." None of this had been planned and it was arriving now in the order it had stacked in since he was born. "You told me at this table that whatever we hide from is a weight, and that it kills. You told me this house was alive because nobody had ever looked at it. You told me I would have the whole of it one day and to pray the day never came. A man walked behind me to within two miles of that door tonight, so I would say the day has opinions of its own." His voice went, and he let it go. "You told me once that I would not ask the next hundred questions that night. It has been a year and a half. I have kept every one of them."

"Ask," Neris said.

So he asked them, and they let him ask, and it was the cruelest arrangement anyone had ever made with him, because they let him get all the way to the end.

Who is hunting us. Who are you. What did you do. Who is the bad employer. Whose was the blade that made the seam in your shoulder and where were you standing when it came. Why does a woman in a village have four languages and an order to ask questions in. What is my name — not what am I called, what is my *name*.

His father let the whole of it run out and did not once look away from him, which was worse than any of the answers would have been.

"We are hunted," Vask said.

"By who."

"That is not tonight."

"Then what is."

"That you are hunted because you are ours," his mother said, from the door. "That is the whole of it and it is not the piece I am handing you to keep you quiet. It is the part everything else stands on. Nobody in the world is looking for a herder's son on a hill. They would be looking for you because you came out of us."

"Then tell me what you *are*—"

"No."

"Why."

"Because the day you know it you are carrying it," Neris said, "and a boy carrying it stands differently and answers differently and comes through a door differently, and every one of those is a sentence a stranger can read. You have spent this evening telling me how a man on a road gave himself away by the way he put his feet down. You are asking me to put a thing in you that you cannot set down."

Kael sat with that.

"That is the same answer you gave me last year in a different coat."

"Yes."

"It was a bad answer then."

"Yes," his mother said. "It is still the only true one."

His father had not moved through any of it. Now he put both forearms on the table, the way he did in the yard when he was going to explain a thing once, and the tally-marks came up out of the sleeve and lay there where they had lain in front of Kael for twelve years.

"Three sentences," Vask said. "They are not the history. You will not have the history from me tonight or on any night I can see from here. You are going to hate me for how small they are. Listen anyway."

"I'm listening."

"We were owned. We stopped being owned. People who own things do not forget."

Kael counted to eleven before anybody in that room moved.

"That is three sentences," he said.

"It is."

"There are about forty years missing out of the middle of it."

"There are."

Then his mother said, from the door, in the flattest voice she had used all night, "It may not be them."

His father did not turn round.

"It is them."

"A man walking a boy home is them. A name written onto a sheet in the capital is not obviously them at all." She came off the door. "Your son has been three lines off the top of that Temple's boards for two years. He put an edge on a piece of wood in front of four hundred people and the whole island agreed to look away, and one man on a colonnade did not. The empire looks at children, Vask. It has built a school on this island for the express purpose of looking at children. An empire noticing a clever boy is not the same animal as the one we have been watching that sea for, and you cannot make it the same animal by wanting there to be only one."

"And if it is both," Vask said.

Neris did not answer that.

Kael sat between the two of them with his hands flat on the table and understood, with a coldness that arrived somewhere under his ribs and stayed there for years, that his mother and his father — who had never in his whole life disagreed about anything in front of him, not once, not about a rule or a lesson or a road — did not know. They had no more of it than he did. They had eleven years of watching an empty sea and one man in a wool coat and a word neither of them had ever heard, and the answer changed everything they were about to do, and they could not get it.

"Then I'll ask the other one," he said.

They both looked at him.

"The master. You are going to say his name in about four minutes, because you always do, because every road out of this house has him standing at the end of it, and I have never once asked you why." He kept his voice where his father kept his. "You have hidden from everybody alive for eleven years. Why not him?"

His mother's chin came up very slightly.

"Because he has never once been wrong about us," Vask said.

"He knows us. Truly," said Neris.

And that was the whole of it. Neither of them said another word about it, and neither of them looked at the other one, and Kael sat there and read the shape of the answer instead of the answer, which is what he had been trained to do.

---

"We go," his father said.

He said it to Neris, over the top of his son, in the voice of a man saying a thing that had been decided somewhere else a long time ago and was only now being taken down off its hook.

"With what," said his mother.

"There are four boats in that village that would go out tonight for enough coin."

"And by the third day all four of them have told somebody where they carried three people in the dark, because it is the most interesting thing that will ever happen to a man who fishes." She had not moved from the door. "The hulls that do not talk write instead. A merchant keeps a manifest, and a manifest is a sheet of paper with our names on it in a room in the capital, and we have spent eleven years staying out of rooms in the capital."

"Then we take one and do not ask."

"In the cold term." Neris said it with no weight on it at all. "On that water. One sail, no second hand aboard who knows the passage, past the last of the islands in the dark. You would be putting the three of us in a boat the sea does not have to try very hard to take."

Kael had been running it alongside them from the first word, because he could not help running it, and he heard himself say the next part out loud before he had decided to.

"And the harbours are watched."

They both looked at him.

"He came off a ship or off a horse, and he did not know this island — he never once looked at the ground he was walking on," Kael said. "If somebody can afford to put a man on the road above the village to find out where I sleep, then somebody has men standing on the quays, because that is cheaper and it is the first thing anyone would do. Whichever of us walks down to hire a boat walks past him."

His father was quiet for a moment.

"Yes," he said.

"Then not a harbour." He had it built before he opened his mouth, the way he built everything. "There are beaches on our own side of this mountain that nobody has any reason to walk to. It needs a hull that is not ours and is not anybody's, and somebody to have put it there beforehand, and we go down in the dark and nobody on this island ever knows one thing about it."

Neither of them said anything at all, and about a second later Kael understood that he had just described, out loud, from nothing, a plan they had already made.

"Yes," his mother said. "That is how it will be done."

"Then we can go now."

"No."

"You just said—"

"I said that is how it will be done." Neris came off the door. "I did not say when."

"Why not now."

Nobody answered. Then his mother's breathing changed, and he heard it.

"Because I am carrying a child," his mother said.

The fire went on doing what the fire did. His father did not move. Kael sat with his mouth open around the next word of an argument that had stopped existing somewhere behind him.

"You're—"

"Yes."

"Since when."

"Since the summer."

Silence sat in the room. Then Kael's head came up.

"I'm going to have a brother. Or a sister."

"One or the other," his mother agreed.

It was the second time that day that a thing had got all the way through him before the rest of it could arrive, and this time there was no rest of it.

"Will I be allowed to hold it."

He heard it come out of his own mouth and could not think where it had come from. It was not a count and it priced nothing and nobody in that room needed it, and he was twelve years old and three lines off the top of the boards of a Temple full of the empire's children, and he had asked it in the voice he had asked things in when he was five.

His mother looked at him for some time.

"Yes."

"When."

"When it is dry and it is breathing and I say so." Something came up into her face that had not been anywhere in it since he came through the door, and stood there a moment, and went. "You will be careful with the head."

"I know about heads."

"You know about heads," his mother agreed.

Kael ran the rest of it because his head could not be stopped from running things. The summer, and three months gone, which put the birth into the spring — and the spring was the games, and the games filled this island with hulls and retinues and quiet men on every quay with a list in their hands.

"Then it's born in the spring," he said. "And the spring is the one season we can't move through."

"There will be perhaps three weeks after it comes, before the sea fills up." Neris came off the door. "We will use them. And the master will be here for it."

Kael looked over at his father, because his father had a face for everything and had been wearing one all night.

"The master works nature," Kael said.

He had seen it once, two years ago, in the study: a dead fern standing itself back up out of its pot while the old man sat across the room with his hands in his lap. He had never once put that beside anything else.

"Nature is the Art of mending," his mother said. "Most of what a healer does badly with a hot iron and a needle, a skilled artist does properly." She sat down on the bench by the door, which was not where she sat. "And the Art on its own is not worth very much. A man who can push a bone toward mending and does not know which way the bone goes will push it the wrong way, and there are a great many more of those about than anyone will admit to you. You want both halves in the one head. He has read medicine for as long as I have been alive and he works nature, and there is nobody else on this island who is both."

Kael sat with that.

"He will find the boat as well. Not a hull with our faces written in it — something of his own, kept dry, on our own side of this mountain. We go off a beach in the dark and we are past the last of the islands before the village has its shutters open."

"Where."

"That is not tonight either."

Kael had thought a great deal of the master for as long as he had known him.

He sat and listened to his mother lay out a road built end to end out of that one man, and thought a great deal more.

"Women have children on this island every week of the year," Kael said. "Nobody sends up the hill for the First Master of the Temple."

"Your mother will have the best hand there is on this island," his father said.

It was the first time all night that either of them had answered a question that had been put to the other one. Kael knew what it meant when the *wrong* person answered, and he sat and looked at the two of them and let it go, because there was nothing else available to do with it.

Kael did the rest of the sum before either of them offered it to him.

The cold term. The storm-weeks. The turn toward spring. The child, and then three weeks, and then a beach in the dark.

Six months. Call it six months and be honest about it, because a count you hurry is a wish.

"The games are in the spring," his mother said, to his father, in the same voice she had used about the hull.

"Yes."

"He is written on a sheet in the capital under a seal. A boy who is written down and does not appear is a question somebody asks out loud, in a room we cannot see into."

His father was a long moment answering.

"Then he appears," he said.

"He appears." Neris did not look at Kael once. "He goes as far as he goes. And on the day the boat is ready we take him off that field and down to the water — whatever day it falls on, and whatever he is standing in the middle of."

Kael sat perfectly still, with his hands flat on his own table, and did not say one word.

He had spent four years learning exactly what it cost to be seen, and had built a very good coat out of it, and the best work he had ever done with it was done at his own table, on the two people who had cut it for him, over one clause his mother had said about a day in the spring.

---

After that they stopped talking to him and started talking about him, and did not send him out.

He would think about that for years and never entirely settle what it had been. They had put him out of rooms his whole life for smaller things; there had been a night when he was nine when the world turned on a conversation held behind a wall with a boy on the other side of it declining to listen. Tonight they left him at the table and built it in front of him in the voice they used for salt and weather and the price of a goat, and never once asked him what he thought. He could not decide, then or afterward, whether it was the first time they had ever treated him as a man or the last time they had ever treated him as furniture. Possibly both. It was the family trade.

The list of what went was short and it was mostly not things. The books went, because the books were the master's and could go back to the master. The salt went, because salt is money anywhere there is weather. The slate did not go. The loom did not go, and his mother said that out loud and said nothing else about it, and Kael found that he was watching his mother price a loom and looking away from it at the same time.

His father crossed the room in the middle of it, thinking, his thumb moving along his fingers the way it did when he was counting a thing that was not in the room, and stopped at the foot of the loft ladder and put his boot flat down on the third board and stood on it.

Kael had counted around that board since he was nine years old.

His father stood on it with the whole of his weight and it did not creak.

It moved. It went down about the thickness of a fingernail, all of it at once, end to end, and when the weight came off it came back up all at once as well, and in the black seam along its edge, for about as long as it takes to blink, there was a line of iron.

A nailed board does not move. This one had gone down along its whole length and come back up, which meant it was not fixed to anything, which meant it was lying on top of something.

His father took his boot off it and went on counting on his fingers and said something to his mother about the goat, and his mother answered him about the goat, and neither of them looked at the floor, and there was no reason on earth why either of them should have.

Kael sat with his hands flat on the table and did not ask.

He put it where it went. He did not take it out again for a long time.

---

"Then there is the hill," Vask said.

Kael's head came up.

"He does not go back down it," his father said. "Not tomorrow, not in the cold term. He is sick. Boys are sick all winter. It buys us until the turn and by then we know more than we know now."

"No," said Neris.

"He was followed home."

"He was followed home from a school he has walked to every day for four years, by a man who did not look at the bank." She had not raised her voice at any point in the entire evening and did not raise it now. "Think about what you are asking for. On the ninth day of it somebody in that Temple says *where is the mountain boy*, and a clerk writes it down, and a judge of the games who has just put a name on a sheet in the capital hears that the name has stopped coming to lessons. What have we told him? We have told him the boy has somewhere else to be and somebody at home who decided it."

Vask said nothing.

"A boy who stops going is a boy worth asking about," she said. "He goes."

"He goes down a road with a man on it."

"He goes down a road with a man on it," Neris agreed, "and he goes differently, and he does not go empty-handed, and we do not give anyone on this island one single new thing to look at. That is the whole of the argument and you know it is."

His father stood for a while with his hand on the back of the chair.

"Say it, then," he said at last, to Kael, without turning round. "Since it is your road."

"Not the same road twice," Kael said. "Not the same hour twice. Never the last stretch in the light if I can help it, and never the same way up off the road onto the shoulder." He had built it on the walk home over the goat line without knowing he was building it. "And nothing changes at the Temple. Nothing at all. I go in at the same gate at the same bell and I am exactly as boring as I was yesterday."

"And if you are taken on the road."

"Then it happens on ground I chose."

His father turned around and looked at him for a long moment, and something crossed his face that Kael did not have a category for and never afterward built one for either.

"Yes," said Vask.

Then he went to the wall and took down the knife.

It was Kael's own, the small one, in its place where it had always been, cleaned once years ago back to the metal's own weather by hands that had never said anything about it. His father turned it over twice, the way he turned over everything before he gave it to anybody, and held it out across the table hilt first.

"Not on your belt," he said. "Not where a boy carries a knife to be seen carrying one. You will show me in the morning where you have put it and I will tell you whether it is a good place."

Kael took it.

"It is a tool," his father said. "It has been a tool your whole life and it stays a tool, and if you ever have it in your hand on that road it is because everything I taught you before it has already failed. Do you understand me."

"Yes."

"Say the rest."

It was the oldest lesson in the house and he had been able to say it since he was eight.

"That the knife is the smallest part."

"That the knife is the smallest part," Vask said, and let go of it.

Later, in the loft, with the two voices going below him and the fire down to its coals and the bar across the door, Kael lay on his back in the dark with the thing wrapped in cloth beside his hand and ran the count again, because he could not stop running it and because it was the only part of the whole day he could hold in one piece.

The cold term. The storm-weeks. The turn toward spring. The child. Three weeks.

Six months, and it came out the same every time, and it was the only honest thing anybody had given him since the first bell.


---

# Chapter Twenty — The Word

There were four places on the road where a man could stand and not be seen from below.

Kael had known about all four of them since he was seven, the way a person knows where the doors are in his own house, and he had never once in his life thought of them as places where a man might be.

He went down by the goat line and came onto the road under the split rock, which cost him eleven minutes and put him on a stretch nobody walked before the light was properly up. He did the four places first, out of order, from above. Then the fold. Then the bend.

By the time the roofs of the village came up under their smoke he had priced nine things and every one of them had come back nothing, and he had learned that pricing nothing nine times takes exactly as long as pricing something.

At the boat sheds a man straightened off a hull and looked up the road at him.

Kael spent four minutes of a morning he did not have proving the man was a cooper. He got his proof — the wear on the outside of the right sleeve, the way the shoulder came round before the feet did, a man who spends his life putting a hoop on something that does not want it — and he put the proof away, and went on, and understood while he was going on that he would spend the four minutes again tomorrow on the same man, because a cooper on a Tuesday is not evidence about a cooper on a Wednesday.

His father had done this every day for eleven years.

Kael had grown up inside the result of it and had thought of it the way you think about a man being tall.

The Temple was where it had been. The gate stood open. The first court was full of boys complaining about the cold in the accents of six provinces, and a clerk was rehanging a lamp that had come down in the night, and nothing anywhere in it had changed at all, and that was the part he could not get level.

He had turned everything up. That had taken about ten minutes and had been almost easy.

What he could not work out, going in under the arch with his boots wet, was how you turned it back down.

---

She was in the cistern court twenty minutes before anybody had any reason to be, sitting on the bench nobody sat on, with her satchel on her knees and her hands on the satchel.

"Sit down," Elarine said. "I have four questions and then I am finished with it."

Kael sat down.

"Was the entry lodged before or after the standings closed."

"Nine days before."

"Does the provision require the boy's consent."

"No."

"Does it require the boy's guardian's consent."

"No."

She was quiet for a moment. The rain had stopped in the night and the court smelled of wet brick.

"Can a judge of the games be removed by a First Master."

"No," Kael said.

"Then that is all four." She did not sound satisfied. She sounded like somebody putting down a tool that had turned out to be the wrong tool. "I sat up three nights on this. Do you know what I found."

"No."

"Nothing. There is nothing wrong with it anywhere." She turned her head. "That is what is wrong with it. A thing that is done badly has a place you can put your hand. This was done properly, by a man with the right seal, under a clause that has been sitting in the law since before my house had a name, and every step of it would survive being read aloud in the capital by somebody who hated him. I have been looking for four days for the crooked part and there is no crooked part."

"The First Master said something like that."

"Then the First Master and I agree, which is not a comfort." She looked at the wall opposite. "I do not like it. I want to be clear that I do not like it, since nobody else appears to be saying so. A man arrived on this island two years early and spent two years being pleasant and then reached over the head of the man who runs this school and took you, correctly."

"He asked for nothing."

"Yes," said Elarine. "I had got as far as that too."

Kael did not say the rest of it. There was a quantity of rest of it — corridors, doorways, two years of meetings that had never once looked arranged — and he took it out and looked at it and put it back where it lived, and the putting back took no effort at all any more, which he noticed and did not examine.

She did not ask. He watched her not ask. She had a particular way of doing it, which was to let a silence run one beat past the place where a question would have gone, so that you knew the question had been available and had been declined.

"You didn't have to sit up three nights," Kael said.

Elarine Sedrane turned round on the bench and looked at him properly for the first time that morning.

"No," she said. "I did not."

"I only meant—"

"I know what you meant. You meant it as an apology and it came out as an accounting, which is a thing you do." She put the satchel down on the bench beside her. "I am going to say something now, and you are going to do something strange with it, and I have decided in advance not to hold that against you."

Kael waited.

"You are my friend," Elarine said. "It is not a complicated word. I have been using it about you privately since the summer, and I did not say it, because I could see exactly what you would do with it." Her chin came up a fraction. "That is why I sat up three nights. There is no other reason. I would like it written down somewhere that I did not do it because it was interesting, although it was interesting."

The cistern court was very quiet.

He did not respond immediately. He sat with his hands on his knees and did the only thing he had ever been able to do with a thing he could not price, which was to take it apart and see what it was made of, and he found that he did not have the word. It was not that he disagreed with it. It was that the word had never been handed to him, in the way that certain other words had never been handed to him, and he had reached twelve years old with a very great deal of vocabulary and a hole where that one went.

So he went and got the evidence instead, because evidence was a thing he did have.

"Corvane," he said.

Elarine's eyebrows went up half a hair.

"I think Corvane might be one as well," Kael said. "I don't know when that happened. I've been trying to work out when it happened and I can't find the day."

"There will not have been a day."

"There's always a day."

"Not with him. With him there is a Tuesday when he decides a thing, and then a year in which he behaves as though he had always decided it." She was, he realised, almost enjoying herself. "Do not tell him."

"Why."

"Because he will put it in a letter," said Elarine Sedrane. "To his brother. At length. With a heading."

Kael laughed.

It came out of him before he had any say in it, one short sound in an empty brick court, and it startled him more than it startled her, and it went away again, and what came in behind it was Oss.

He had not gone looking for him. That was the part that stayed. He had been running the word across the people he had, the way you run a rule across a row of figures to see which of them it fits, and the rule had come to the end of the row and gone straight on past the end into a boy three tables away in the hall who had not spoken to him in a year.

Who had given his name away in the first hour, for nothing, before Kael had known that names were things a person had at all. Kael had priced that on the spot and priced it correctly: it cost nothing, so it was worth nothing.

He had been right about the price. He had never once looked at the other column.

Was he.

Kael turned it over and could not get it to sit still. *Was* he — which put the thing in the ground with a date on it, finished, a fact about a year that was over. *Is* he — which was worse, because if that one was yes then it had been yes the whole time, three tables away, at every meal of a year, while Kael sat with his back to it and got on with his work.

He did not know how to find out. That was the entire difficulty. He could price a man off the set of his shoulder at forty strides in bad light, and he had no idea whatever how a person walks across a hall.

The bell went for the first lesson. She stood, and got the satchel onto her shoulder, and stopped at the arch with the light behind her.

"One more thing, and then I am finished with it for good." She did not turn round. "If they draw us against each other in the spring, you will not be kind to me."

"I wasn't going to be."

"Good. Because I intend to beat you." A pause, exactly the length of a pause she had decided on. "And if you hold back I will know, and I will never forgive it, and I am extremely good at not forgiving things."

"I know," Kael said.

---

It took him seven weeks.

He had been carrying the question since the first evening he had gone up the headland and watched grey coats put numbers on a girl, and it had lain in the blank book for most of a year under a heading with nothing under it: *what she does with it after it goes in.*

The trouble was that everything he knew about the Art was about getting something out. A thread went from him into a waster, into a floor, into the wet air in front of a boy's face. Hers did not go anywhere. She spent, and the spending stayed, and a flagstone came apart under her foot while nothing whatever had crossed the space between her and the stone.

He could not do what she did. He had worked that out in the first fortnight and it had not been a disappointment, because it was arithmetic. To do what she did you flooded a limb, and to flood a limb you needed a limb's worth, and everything he had ever had would not fill a cup.

So he stopped trying to do what she did.

*Everything heavy is lazy.* A gate does not resist you everywhere. It resists you in one place, and if you find the place you can move a thing that ought to be beyond you with your hands in your pockets. He had been eight when his mother put a stone in a gate and walked away.

A parry is not the whole arm either. A parry is a line: the point of contact, the edge of the hand, the two bones, the elbow, the shoulder, the hip, the ground. Weight arrives along that line and the line either holds or it folds, and it does not fold everywhere at once. It folds at one place.

He did not reinforce his arm. He had no idea how to reinforce an arm.

He reinforced the line.

It went wrong for seven weeks. It went wrong in eleven distinct ways, all of which he wrote down, because a thing that fails the same way twice is a fact and a thing that fails eleven ways is a subject. Then on a grey afternoon at the end of the cold term, on the old sea court with the water going white out past the point, Valeria Vel came through his guard with her weight behind the steel the way she had come through it four hundred times, and it did not open.

It held for about two seconds.

She stopped.

"Again," she said.

"I can't."

"Again."

"I can't," Kael said, and sat down, because his legs had been consulted and had returned an answer.

It came for him about four heartbeats later, on the stones, the way it always came: the drop, the ears going, the grey coming in from the corners. Six threads in six seconds had put him flat on the bricks of the cistern court in the autumn. This had been one thread, and small, and exact, and it had emptied him to the floor of the purse in the length of one exchange, and he lay on the cold stone of the sea court with his hand shaking against his knee and could not have lit a candle to save his life.

Valeria crouched down in front of him.

"What did you do."

"Wait."

She waited. He got about half of himself back and gave her the whole of it, because there was no version of it that could be given in pieces, and because she had asked the way she asked things, which was without one grain spent on whether he would say yes.

He told her about the flooding, and about the cup. He told her about the gate. Then he put his hand flat on the stones and drew the line for her with one finger — contact, hand, bones, elbow, shoulder, hip, ground — and showed her the place, one place, an inch across, where the line folded when her weight came into it, and told her that he had not strengthened his arm at all, that his arm was exactly the arm it had been that morning, and that he had put everything he owned into an inch of it for two seconds.

Valeria Vel looked at the stones for a long time.

"You did not do it the way I do it," she said.

"No."

"I have never thought about where it folds." She said it slowly, which she did not do. "I have been doing this since I was four years old. They put me in a doorway and put weight on me and I held it, and when I could hold it they put on more. Nobody has ever asked me where it folds, because I have never needed to know. There is always more."

"There's never more," Kael said. "That's the whole—"

"Yes." She looked up. "I understand. I am telling you that eleven generations of my house have not had to have your idea, and that is not the same as my house having had it and set it aside."

Then she said, "You will never be able to do it the way I do it," and it was not unkind, and it was true, and he had known it for seven weeks and had gone on for seven weeks anyway.

"I know."

"Good." She sat down on the stones beside him with her forearms across her knees. "Your wrist was late. It went in a hair after the contact instead of a hair before, which is why you got two seconds instead of four. Do it before."

"How do I know when before is."

"That," said Valeria, "is the part they cannot teach you either."

The light was going out over the water in the long flat way it went in the cold term. Neither of them got up. She had blood on one knuckle from something earlier and was not attending to it.

"You are still shaking," she said.

"It does that."

"How long."

"Couple of hours."

"And then you walk home."

"And then I walk home," Kael said, and heard himself, and found that having started to answer a question he was going past the end of it. "I don't sleep much any more."

Valeria did not say anything.

"I lie down and it starts," he said. "It's not — I'm not frightened. That's the part I can't get right when I say it. I lie there and I go back down the road and I check it. All of it. Every place I went past, in order, and what was standing there, and whether it was standing there yesterday. And when I finish I've usually found nothing, so I start again, because finding nothing once isn't finding nothing." He turned his hand over and looked at it shaking. "I turned it up. In the autumn. I turned everything up as far as it goes, and it worked, it works, I'm better at it than I have ever been at anything, and I can't get it back down. It runs all night on its own. It doesn't need me."

The water went on out past the point.

"I do not sleep either," Valeria said.

He looked at her.

"I am cold," she said. "Not the weather. All the time, and for as long as I can remember, and when I was small I thought it was the stone, because we live in a stone house and I was on the floor of it a great deal." She was looking straight out at the water. "It is not the stone."

"How long is all the time."

"I have just told you."

"No, you—"

"I am not complaining." It came fast and flat and closed. "I want that understood. I am telling you a fact about my body in the way that you have just told me one about yours, and if you make it into the other thing I will stop."

Kael shut his mouth.

"You have been going to say something since the summer," she said, after a while. "I have watched you not say it about forty times. Say it now, or put it down and stop carrying it about, because it has begun to be in the room."

There it was.

He had had it whole since a morning on the headland, when he had counted the same quarter of an hour four days running and got the same number, and had understood that nobody came early with her and nobody left late with her and nobody stood at her shoulder while she did the hardest thing she could do. His father had been beside him for everything. Beside him, and longer, and better, every morning of his life.

"Nobody stands beside you," Kael said.

She did not move.

"On that court. Not one of them. They call the number and they adjust you and they call the number again, and none of them ever gets up and stands in it with you." He was doing it in the wrong order and could not find the right one. "Everything my father ever asked me to do he did next to me, first, and he was always better at it, and that is the only reason I can do anything at all. That isn't kindness. That's the method. It's how you build a person. And nobody has ever once done it with you, and you are still the best of us, and I have spent a year trying to work out what you would be if somebody had."

The water went on out past the point.

"Say the rest of it," said Valeria Vel.

"That's the rest of it."

"No. The rest of it is that you have decided my father does not love me." Her voice had not risen anywhere. "You have thought it since the summer and you have been very careful with it, and watching you be careful with it has been like watching a man carry a lamp slowly around a room he has already set fire to."

"That isn't—"

"There is no word for it in my house," she said. "For any of this. Not the one you have been circling for a quarter of an hour, and not the other one. My father has never in his life said a sentence to me that was not an instruction." Her chin had come up somewhere in it and stayed up. "I have never wanted him to. You do not understand that and I do not require you to. An instruction from that man is worth more to me than every kind word anybody on this earth could put end to end, because he does not give them — so when one arrives, it is *true*. I would not trade one of them for a hundred of the other kind. Not one of them."

Kael did not say anything.

"He wrote to me in the autumn about an arm you opened. You know what the letter asked." She was looking straight out at the water. "I sat up past the middle of the night getting the answer right. And when it went, I was pleased with it." She let that stand exactly as long as she meant it to stand. "That is what my house is. It is not a sad story and I will not have it told back to me as one. It works. There are eleven generations of it and it works, and I am the best of them since my grandfather, and there is nobody on this island who can stand in front of me for eleven exchanges — and you were one of them, and to manage it you had to go away and invent something that nobody has ever thought of."

"I know."

"Then do not stand beside me," said Valeria. "Stand in front of me. It is worth more."

Kael sat on the cold stones and could not tell, in any direction, whether he had just been corrected or whether he had put something into her that would not come out again. There was no way of finding out. He had spent his whole life being able to find out.

They sat. The wind came off the water and went up the court and got in under everything, and neither of them moved, and somewhere behind him in the last of the light the road was going dark all the way up the mountain, one bend at a time.

"I don't have a word for this," Kael said.

"For what."

"This. Any of it. Sedrane sat me down in the autumn and told me I was her friend, and I couldn't say it back. I took it apart instead, to see what it was made of." He was aware that he was doing it badly and went on doing it, because doing it badly was available and stopping was not. "And Oss gave me his name in the first hour I ever knew him. He said everyone says Oss and it's faster. He handed it over before I knew people had names to hand over, and it didn't hold anything, and I think it was supposed to."

"It was supposed to."

"Yes." Kael looked at the stones. "So I've got no word. I've got everybody's word for everything else on this island and I haven't got that one."

"Then do not use a word," said Valeria Vel.

He turned his head.

"You have just told me you do not have one. So do not stand there waiting for one to arrive." She had not moved and her voice had not changed. "Use something else."

The court was nearly dark.

"Val," Kael said.

She did not answer.

It went on not being answered for long enough that he heard it going, the way you hear a stone going down a shaft and keep waiting after the sound should have come back. He sat with his hand shaking on his knee and did not take it back and did not add anything to it, because there was nothing to add to it, and because his father had spent eleven years teaching him that a thing you say twice is a thing you have started to bargain about.

"Nobody has ever called me anything," Valeria said.

Then she got up and got her case and went in off the sea court, and did not say his name either, and Kael stayed where he was on the cold stones with the shaking going out of his hand by degrees.

He could not tell whether that had been permission.

He sat there working on it long after there was any light left to work in, and the lamps came up along the water one at a time, and the last bell went somewhere behind him in the Temple, and *Evening* stood at the end of the day where it had stood at the end of every day of his life.

For the first time, he was going to be late for it.

He stayed a while longer anyway.


---

# Chapter Twenty-One — The Edge

The blades had edges on them.

Nothing had been said about it. One morning in the turn toward spring Kael came down to the block in the dark and the two training swords were lying where they had lain every morning for a year, and the light off them was a different light, and that was the whole announcement.

He stood and looked at them for a moment. Then he picked his up and went to his place, because the alternative was asking a question he could answer by looking, and his father did not spend words on those.

They worked an hour before it came anywhere near him. That was the shape of it now: the first hour was still the first hour, footing and measure and the same corrections his father had been making since he was seven. Then the light got grey enough to see by, and the hour changed.

He caught the second one across the ribs, through the shirt, and it opened him about the width of a thumbnail and stung like salt.

"Where was it," his father said.

"Low. Inside my elbow, on the turn."

"Where was it before that."

Kael went back through it and found it: the blade had been low and inside for a full exchange before it went anywhere, sitting there being nothing, while he spent his attention on the point.

"It was already there," he said.

"It was already there." Vask stepped back to his mark. "Again."

Kael put his hand flat on the cut and looked at what came off it, which was not much, and went back to his mark.

---

He brought the two knives up on the ninth morning.

He had been eleven days getting to it, four of them lying awake with the thing turning over, and he had made himself write it down first, because a thing you can write down is a thing you have actually got.

"I want to try something," he said.

His father looked at him and then went into the shed and came back with two short blades and did not say where they had come from. They were not new. One was heavier than the other by enough to feel, and the grips had been wrapped by somebody who had been in no hurry.

Kael took them and said nothing about that either. It was the family trade.

"Say it," Vask said.

"Steel takes a thread whole. So a sword is one road, and everybody I fight knows where it is, because it's the thing in my hand." He had written it down and it came out in the order he had written it. "Two blades is two roads."

"Two roads."

"And both of them live at once. Either one can be the real one."

His father turned one of the short blades over and looked along the edge of it and did not say anything.

Kael waited. Then he stopped waiting, because the waiting was doing what it always did.

"And a parry costs me everything I have," he said. "A short blade is a shorter line. There's less of it to fold."

"They will not stop anything."

"No."

"Show me."

It went well for about forty seconds, which Kael understood afterward was the worst possible outcome, because it taught him nothing except that he was right about the part he was already right about. The two points did work. They asked two questions at once, from two places, and his father had to answer both, and for four exchanges Kael was quicker than he had ever been in his life.

Then Vask stopped answering the questions and came in past them.

Kael's left hand was full. His right hand was full. His father took him by the wrist, turned him, and put him face down on the wet stone with an arm folded up behind him, and it was done so fast and so entirely that Kael's body had not yet finished the exchange it had been in the middle of.

"Get loose," Vask said.

Kael could not get loose. There was nothing to get loose with. Every escape he had was built out of a hand, and he had spent both of them on being fast.

The pressure came off.

Kael got up. His shoulder was going to be a colour by supper.

Ten years of that school, and every lesson in it had begun with him already caught. Not one had ever begun with him winning. He had spent four nights building a way to walk into a fight with both hands already gone.

"How long would it take," he said, "to build the escapes again, for two hands full."

His father did not answer that immediately, and when Kael looked at him he found that he was being looked at, and that whatever was in his father's face was not on the list of things Kael had names for.

"Years," Vask said.

"I've got about eight weeks."

"Then you have eight weeks of finding out how it fails, which is not nothing, and it is not the same as having it." He nodded at the blades. "Keep them. Do not take them anywhere near a man who is trying to hurt you until I say. Again."

They ran it eleven more times that morning and Kael lost eleven times, in six different ways, and he wrote down all six on the road down with his hand cramping around the pencil.

---

The threading through two blades was worse than he had budgeted for.

He had known it would cost double and had accepted that. What he had not priced was that a threading wants the whole of him, and there were two of them, and he did not go round twice. The first four attempts the left-hand blade simply went out, quietly, somewhere in the middle, and he did not notice it had gone until he was already using it.

The fifth one did not die.

It went out of true on the contact, the way they did, and came home into his own hand, and because there were two of them and they were both live it came home into both of them, and Kael sat down on the wet stone with his arms out in front of him and his fingers doing something he was not asking them to do.

He counted eleven before he trusted his voice.

"That," he said, "was the worst one yet."

"Yes."

"Both at once."

"Both at once," his father agreed. He crouched and turned Kael's hands over and looked at the speckles coming up along the second knuckle of each, and put them back. "You have made it possible to be wrong twice in the same instant."

"I can't hold two."

His father took one of the short blades off the stone and stood up with it.

What came up the steel was not what Kael made. Kael's was a stitch, the finest thing he could manage, laid in and kept quiet and gone within the exchange. This surged. It went up the blade hilt to point and it kept going, the whole length of it running and running, and it did not falter anywhere along the way, and the arm holding it was not doing one thing that Kael could see.

"The problem isn't capacity," Kael said.

"No." His father let it run a while longer, and then it was not there. "My purse means I can be careless with that and pay nothing you could measure. It has never meant one thing more than that." He turned the blade over in his hand. "I am not being careless with it."

Kael did not say anything.

"Where does it start."

"Your shoulder."

"My shoulder." Two fingers, once, against it. "Then here. Then the hand. Then the steel. Fast the whole way and tight to me the whole way and nothing coming off it anywhere between, and I have to know where all of it is at the same time." He set the blade down on the stone. "You do not know where your own elbow is when your hands are full."

Kael opened his mouth and shut it again, because it was true.

"Put them down."

"...The blades?"

"Both. Nothing in your hands." His father waited while he did it. "Now thread. Both hands."

"That's the off-hand drill. I did that at eleven."

"You did it with one hand, holding the smallest thing you could hold, standing still." Vask stepped back toward his mark. "You have never once done it with two, because nothing has ever asked you to. Do it now. When you can hold both of them and still tell me where your feet are, pick the blades up — and it will go out into them, because by then there will be nowhere else for it to go."

That was the whole of it, and Kael took it as it was meant, which was that his father had priced the idea and had not thrown it away.

Then he stood on a wet block with his hands empty and got it wrong for the rest of the morning, and for a great many mornings after that.

---

Later — because it was the turn toward spring, and because the mornings ran long now — his father came at him with an edge on the blade and Kael's guard was a quarter-count late.

The edge stopped across his throat.

It did not touch him. It arrived at the place where it would have gone through and it was not going through, and it stayed there long enough for Kael to feel the cold coming off the steel onto his skin.

"Dead," his father said.

Then the blade turned over in the hand that held it and the hilt came into his chest under the collarbone, hard, and put him back a step and took the air out with it.

He worked out two things while he was getting the air back. The first was that his father had not hit him in the throat and was never going to, because a boy who walks through a Temple gate with a bruise on his neck is a boy somebody asks about. The second was that the arithmetic had been done inside a killing stroke, at speed, and that nothing whatever about it had shown.

"Again."

The guard was better and it was not better enough. The point came in under his arm and stopped against his ribs, over the heart, and stayed there.

"Dead."

The hilt. Lower this time, and harder.

"Again."

Stomach. "Dead." The hilt. "Again." Across the kidney, from behind, arriving from a direction Kael had not known was a direction. "Dead." The hilt. "Again." The inside of the thigh, which he had not known was anywhere at all, which his father did not explain and never would, and which he would go and find in his mother's anatomy that night and then wish he had left alone.

"Dead."

"Again."

By the sixth his heart was doing what it had done in a yard in the dark when he was nine. His hands had that lightness in them. Everything had gone very clear and slightly too bright at the edges, and sound was arriving a fraction early, and he had gone past being frightened somewhere around the third one and come out the far side of it into the place where the count lived.

And he was getting better. That was the part. Not across weeks — inside the same morning, between the fourth and the seventh, his feet stopped arriving late and something in his shoulders finally learned the thing it had been failing to learn for days, because the price of not learning it had now been stated seven times, in a flat voice, by a man who could take his life off him without hurrying.

He trusted his father the way he trusted the ground. It had never in his whole life occurred to him to be afraid of him. He understood, going back to his mark with his chest banging and his ribs already stiffening under the shirt, that this was not the same thing as being safe. His father was not going to make a mistake — nothing in eleven years suggested his father made mistakes — but the entire arrangement stood on that and on nothing else, and there was no second mechanism anywhere in it.

He went back to his mark. He was glad to.

His father could kill him on any morning he chose, and had chosen not to, on every morning of his life, several times a morning, for years.

He found that he was proud of it. Not of surviving it — of being worth it. Of being a thing his father had decided to bring an edge to.

He went in to breakfast and ate two bowls and did not think about it again until he was old.

---

The Temple had stopped pretending.

For years it had been a school with a tournament somewhere out ahead of it, the way a village has a feast out ahead of it, a thing that shapes the year from a distance. Now the thing was the building. The colonnade had lists on it that were not the boards. Rooms that had been store-rooms since before Kael was born had beds in them. There were carpenters on the seaward side from the second week of the turn, and by the fourth week you could hear them from the letters room, and no master ever once told them to stop.

The entered boys were a class of person now. It had not been announced. It had simply arrived, the way the weather arrives: the entered ate first, and got the dry end of the yard, and were excused from the chronicle in the last week, and Ruthen stood their bouts himself instead of handing them to an older boy.

Kael was entered. It took him until the middle of the turn to notice that this applied to him, and he noticed it because a kitchen woman put a second piece of fish on his plate without looking up.

"You get used to it," Aurelian said, sitting down opposite with his sleeve rolled back off a forearm that had gone through green into yellow. "I have been getting extra fish for thirteen years. It is not the great compensation people imagine."

"What happened to your arm."

"Meloran happened to my arm." Aurelian said it with satisfaction. "He is seventeen and he is entered and he has a spear, and I have asked him for the hour before supper four days a week, and he has taken it, because his house and my house are polite to each other and he cannot say no."

"He is much better than you."

"He is *enormously* better than me. That is the entire point of him." Aurelian pulled the sleeve down. "You do not get better by fighting people you can beat. You told me that, in a cistern, when I hated you."

"I said it about form."

"You said it about everything and then pretended you had said it about form, which is a thing you do." He took Kael's bread without asking, which was a thing he had started doing in the autumn and which Kael had decided to allow. "I am going to be pulled apart in the first week. I have accepted this. I intend to be pulled apart at the highest possible level, and my brother is going to hear about it from three people before I write to him."

Elarine came past with a slate under her arm and did not sit down.

"Ledron is gone," she said.

Kael had read that name on a wall in the autumn, two lines above Sedrane and six above his own, and had never known one other thing about the boy except how far he could reach.

Aurelian's face changed. "Gone where."

"Home. Last night, on a boat that came for him." She said it in the flat voice she kept for facts. "His house sent. There was no letter to the First Master. I asked the clerk."

"Ledron is entered."

"Ledron *was* entered. He is off the sheet and there is a line through it." She shifted the slate. "That is the second one this month. Tavrian in the cold term, and everyone said Tavrian's mother was ill, and I believed it."

"Northwest houses," Aurelian said slowly.

"Both of them northwest houses. Both of them called home in the season their sons would be looked at by every house in the empire." Elarine let that sit for one beat, the way she did. "I have no conclusion. I want it noticed, that is all, and neither of you is to say it in the hall."

She went. Aurelian sat and looked at the table for a while, which was not like him.

"My brother's last letter," he said, "had a sentence in it about the eastern roads. That the eastern roads were slow this year." He turned his cup round. "He has never once written to me about a road."

Kael thought about that for the whole of the afternoon lesson, and got nowhere with it, and put it in the drawer where the things went that he could not price, which had a great deal in it now.
