Valeria was on the sea court in the hour after the yard, as she had been every day for eleven months. They did not talk about the tournament. They had never talked about the tournament; the tournament was the thing at the end of the corridor they were both walking down, and neither of them had ever seen the point of describing a corridor.

She put him on the stones twice and he put her on the stones once, which was the best week he had ever had.

"Your left hand is doing something," she said, at the end of it, not looking at him, wrapping her forearm.

"Yes."

"It is doing it badly."

"Yes," Kael said.

She finished the wrap and bit the end of it, and did not ask what the something was, and went up the path.

---

The night still belonged to his mother.

She had been at the table when he came in, with the lamp already trimmed, and in front of her there were nine things laid out in a row: a copper rod, an iron nail, a length of dry oak, a length of the same oak that had been in a bucket since morning, a dry rope, a wet rope, a bone from the winter's goat, a cup of water from the butt, and a cup of water from the sea.

"Sit down," she said. "You are going to spend, and I am going to hold the tow."

She had done the tow herself, before he got there — a wisp of it teased out fine and hung off a splinter at the end of the run, so that whatever arrived at the far end would lift it, and the amount it lifted was the amount that had arrived. It was a better instrument than the one he would have built. He looked at it for a moment longer than he needed to.

"You built me a measure."

"I built you a measure," his mother agreed. "You have spent two years telling me that the answer is in the how much, and getting the how much off your own thumb. Your thumb is a liar. Begin with the copper."

He began with the copper.

They ran it for three hours, and it was the best three hours he had had since the garden.

The same thread. He had to make the same thread nine times, which turned out to be the hardest single thing in the evening, and she made him prove it by running the copper first and last and showing her the same lift. The same length of every material, cut to the copper's length. One thing changed at a time.

The metals took it and were not close to anything else. Copper lifted the tow further than iron, which surprised him and did not surprise her. The dry oak did almost nothing and cost him more than the copper had. The wet oak did a great deal more than the dry oak, and Kael stopped with his hand still out and said, "Wait."

"No," said his mother. "Finish the row."

He finished the row. Bone, nothing much. Dry rope, nothing. Wet rope, a great deal. Fresh water, some.

Sea water lifted the tow nearly as far as the iron.

Kael sat back and put both hands flat on the table.

"It isn't the wood," he said.

"Say it properly."

"The dry oak and the wet oak are the same oak. I cut them off the same branch. If the wet one carries and the dry one doesn't, then the thing that carries isn't the oak, it's the water that's in it." He was going too fast and knew it and could not slow down. "And the water isn't the water either, because these two cups are both water and one of them is nearly iron. So it's the *salt*—"

He stopped.

He had learned about salt when he was nine, in a different room in the same house, out of two pails left on a step overnight. She had taught it to him as a fact about cold. He had taken it away and done his own far-counting with it in the loft, alone, and it had killed the last thing he had ever believed without checking.

His mother was looking at the sea water and not at him.

"Go on," she said.

"It's what's in the water," Kael said. "Not the water."

"That is better, and it is still more than you have." She reached over and turned the two cups so they stood square to each other, which was a thing she did when she wanted him to look at something as a pair. "You have nine results and one of them is interesting. What you do not have is nine results and a law. Say what you have."

"That some things carry a thread and some things don't, and it isn't about how hard they are or how heavy they are, because copper and bone are both hard. And that a thing which doesn't carry can be made to carry by putting something into it."

"Yes."

"And I don't know what the something is."

"No," his mother said. "You do not."

She got up to fetch the second lamp, and it took her a while, and Kael watched her do it and then made himself stop watching, because she had told him in the winter, once, flatly, that she would ask when she wanted a hand.

She was very big now. She had been carrying it in front and high since the storm-weeks, and in the last month it had begun to be a thing that happened to her rather than a thing she was doing. She could not get to the table properly any more; she sat side-on to it, with her left arm along the top and her weight back in the chair, and she reached with the one arm for everything, and she had rearranged the whole surface so that everything she needed was inside that arc. Kael had watched her do the rearranging over about a week without ever once saying anything about it, and had understood while he watched that she had done it early, deliberately, at a stage when she did not yet need it, so that nobody would see her do it when she did.

She got the lamp lit and came back and did not sit down straight away.

"The word is *conductivity*," she said.

Kael looked up.

"The property you have spent this evening measuring. It has a name. The things that carry it well are *conductors*, and the copper is a better one than the iron, which you now know because you measured it and *not* because anybody told you." She lowered herself into the chair in the two stages it took her now. "The property of a thing that decides how readily it carries. Not how much of it there is. What it is."

Kael sat with his hand on the table and did not say anything for a moment, because a thing had just gone through him sideways and he wanted to have hold of it before he opened his mouth.

He knew every word the Temple had. He had spent four years collecting them the way other boys collected slights — the minima, the resonances, proficiency, declaration, the whole flat learned vocabulary that Priscian laid out like cutlery. He could have written the list. *Conductivity* was not on it. Priscian had never said it. Priscian did not have it to say; the man had a fine word for what a thing was made of and no word at all for what a thing would *do*, and Kael had noticed the hole two years ago and had assumed the hole was in the world.

And it was not a village word. There was no version of his mother's life in which a woman came up on this island and learned that a length of copper had a property with a name on it.

His mother was watching him have all of this. He knew that she was, and he knew that she had known he would, and that she had put the word on the table in the same way she had put the nine materials on it.

"You have never used that word with me," Kael said.

"No. This is *science*. The empire hates that word."

He waited. She let him wait, and the lamp did what lamps do, and it went on for long enough that he understood he was not going to be given anything else, and that this was not the same as being refused.

He had a shut door of his own. He had had one since he was nine. He knew exactly what one looked like from the outside, and he knew what it cost to stand near one and not knock, because he had made his mother stand near his for four years.

"All right," he said.

"There will be more of them," his mother said. "A great many more, and I am going to give them to you quickly, and I am not going to slow down." She squared the two cups again, though they were already square. "From tonight you will not be told to make a thing smaller. You will be told what it is called."

"Why now."

"Because you have got as far as anybody gets on words a child made up." She said it without one grain of unkindness, which was somehow the worst available way to say it. "Far-counting. What fire eats. The standing game. You built those because nobody had handed you anything better and you needed somewhere to put what you knew, and they have carried you further than they had any business carrying you." Her hand went flat on the table. "They will not carry you into a room with the empire in it."

"That isn't the reason."

His mother looked at him.

"No," she said. "It is true, and it is not the reason. Ask me in a year."

She went faster after that, and she went faster every night for four weeks. She gave him the words for things he had been carrying around in his own bad language since he was ten, three and four a night, and she did not explain where any of them came from and he did not ask where any of them came from, and twice she gave him one that he could tell, from the shape of it, belonged to a whole country of other words that he was not being given.

She worked past her hour. She had never in her life worked past her hour.

One night late in the turn Kael looked up in the middle of writing and his father was standing in the door of the room with his shoulder against the frame, doing nothing at all, watching his wife lean forward off the back of the chair to reach a cup she could have asked for.

Kael had seen his mother stand in a doorway like that. He had seen it his whole life, at the top of the yard, with her hand on the frame and her knuckles going pale while something was done to her son that she had agreed to.


---

# Chapter Twenty-Two — The Names

The island filled.

Kael had watched the sea his whole life the way other boys watched a road, and he had never seen anything like this.

Hulls he had no names for. A ship with three banks of oars and a gilt beak that came in under sail alone and made the whole harbour rearrange itself to let it stand. Barges. Then more barges, unloading — poles, rope, canvas, and then, over four days, seats.

There were men in the lanes with tally-sticks. There was a horse, which Kael stopped and looked at for a full minute, because he had seen four in his life and never one that had been brought across water to be looked at.

Then the soldiers, and the soldiers did not stop.

They came off the transports in blocks and formed up on the shingle and went where they were sent, and by the fourth day there were more armed men on that island than Kael had known there were men. They took the quay. They took both roads off it. They put a post at the mouth of the village lane and another at the head of the Temple road, and Kael went past that one twice a day and was looked at twice a day by somebody who had been standing there since before it was light.

He counted them for a week out of habit and the count was the largest he had ever had.

---

They opened the games on a bright cold morning, and Kael came down the mountain at first light with his kit on his back and understood, at the bend above the village, that he had never actually seen a crowd.

He had seen the feast from a treeline when he was nine. He had seen the Temple's first court three deep at a wall. He had built his idea of *many people* out of those two things, and it had been like building an idea of the sea out of a bucket.

The arena stood on the flat ground east of the Temple, where four years ago there had been nothing but the drying-frames, and it had gone up out of poles and rope and cut stone in weeks: an oval of banked seating around a floor of raked sand, open to the sky, with the sea behind it on two sides. Kael had watched it built and had thought he had understood the size of it.

Full, it was a different object.

He came in through the competitors' gate with a Temple steward's hand on his shoulder and the noise arrived at him bodily, from above and on all sides, the sound of thousands of people who were not in danger and were not angry and were simply *there*, and every drill he had was the wrong drill. There was no count for it. His body went and got the settlement out of storage and offered it to him, unasked, the way it always did in a crowd, and he stood in the mouth of the gate and found that his hand had gone by itself to his knife.

He took the hand away, and stood there a moment with the noise coming down on him, because at ten he had come through the Temple gate with nothing on him anywhere and his hand had gone to that same place and found nothing at all, and he had not thought about that morning in years, and he thought about it now.

Then he made himself go through the exits: four, three of them narrow, the widest behind the boxes. His hands were cold and he was going to need them shortly.

The north curve was the Temple, because the Temple was hosting and the Temple had brought everyone it had — four hundred children in the school's colours, most of whom would spend the week in the lower bracket, fighting each other for a purse of gold and the right to have done it.

The other two schools had sent nobody who was not going to fight.

Five from the capital's Collegium, in red and black, standing. Five from the northwest, bigger by a hand on the average, in nothing in particular, half of them with their sleeves already off.

In the Gold league the Temple was six lines out of sixteen, and he was one of the six.

The host's box stood on the western curve, and the First Master was in it.

Here he was in robes Kael had no name for, with a chain across his shoulders that plainly had weight in it, sitting very still in a seat that had been built to be looked at, at the top of an island that had been taken apart and rebuilt around a thing he had asked for. *Four years ago, when I thought I understood what I was asking for.* He had said that himself, in the autumn, to a boy, without being asked to.

Then the horns went, and the noise came down like a lid, and everybody stood up.

A girl came in from the seaward side, which meant she had come by water that morning rather than sleeping on the island, and Kael worked out afterward that this had been decided by somebody so that the entire arena would watch her arrive rather than find her already there.

The party ahead of her took a full minute to cross.

The imperial guard went first, white and imperial blue, and they were what Kael had been walking past all week, and he read them the ordinary way: drilled, competent, unremarkable, men doing a thing they had done before and would do again on some other place.

Then twelve who were not that.

They came in a heavy dark grey with nothing whatever on it, six and six, and they were not marching, and not one of them looked at the crowd, and Kael's whole body sat up before he had any word for why. They were not arranged in front of her. They were arranged across the *distances* — the near ones close enough to take her weight off her feet, the far ones out at the edges of the ground, where a thing would have to come from, and every one of them was already standing where he was going to be standing before he got there.

It was the best thing Kael had ever seen a man's body do on purpose.

He looked sixty feet down the curve, because he could not help it. If one of those twelve had been Vel she would have had him by his walk at that distance in half that light. She was looking at the sand in front of her boots and did not turn her head once.

*Stand in front of me.*

He had taken it, at the time, for a cold way of saying something else.

Then her.

Four years in a school full of the empire's best-kept children had built Kael a scale for this without his ever having agreed to have one. Elarine was economical. Valeria was a working blade. Aurelian was beautiful and knew to the grain what it bought him, and the bench moved when he walked past it.

The scale had nowhere to put her.

She was fourteen or near enough, which put her a year above him. She came out onto the stone in a blue so dark it read black until the light moved on it, with gold at the shoulder and the hem in a quantity that had no function except to be a quantity, and her hair up off her neck in a construction that had plainly taken more than one person more than an hour.

Eleven thousand people stood up.

What impressed him was not the standing. He had seen a court stand for the First Master. It was the sound underneath it, which was not a cheer and was not a roar and went on a great deal longer than either, and which Kael understood some while afterward was the sound of a very large number of people being glad about a thing they were never going to be any nearer to than this.

He looked for what he always looked for. Reach, weight, what the shoulders had been taught, where the hands go when nobody has given them anything to do. He got nothing at all. It was not concealment — he knew concealment, he had been raised inside it and could watch his own mother doing it across a room. There was nothing under it to get. She had been made, from the ground up, to be looked at by exactly this many people at exactly this distance, and the making was total, and it was working on him while he stood on the sand and disagreed with it.

*The kind of beautiful that is also a uniform,* he thought, and had no idea where the thought had come from, and it stayed with him.

The herald had a voice that carried to the back of the seaward curve without any evidence of effort.

"Her Serene Highness the Princess Aeliana Cassivara Vessantine, of the House Vessantine, Daughter of the Emperor, Light of the Provinces, Beloved of the Throne and its Ward."

It took him six seconds. Kael counted them.

The First Master came to the rail of his box and said the words a host says, and she said the words that are said back, and the whole of it took a quarter of a minute. Kael watched the man who had told him at a gate that he was done carrying him bow his grey head to a girl of fourteen.

She sat, and the arena sat, and the sound went out of it like water out of a yard.

Then the herald came down onto the sand, and the sand was very white in that light, and read:

"The first bout of the Gold league. Arms as carried. Resonance without limit." A pause that was entirely form. "No mortal intent."

Everybody in that arena knew what the last clause was worth. You cannot weigh what a boy meant; you can only weigh what he did, afterwards, when it is already done. Kael had read the games' law twice over the winter looking for the part that protected anybody, and that sentence was the part.

What actually protected anybody was sitting in a box on the western curve with a chain across his shoulders — six days above that sand with earth in one hand and nature in the other, to stop what could be stopped and mend what could not be. Nobody said it out loud. Saying it would have meant saying the rest of it: that the empire had written a rule it could not enforce and had answered the difficulty by putting one old man in a chair.

Kael discovered, standing in the north curve with his heart already going, that none of that frightened him, and that the not being frightened was itself worth a look.

"Lucan, of the House Drusane, of the Temple of the Southern Isles."

Lucan went out. He had been ready, and he walked out onto the sand with the posture a paid master had spent years putting into him, and it was, Kael thought, entirely good. There was nothing wrong with it anywhere. The Drusane seats found their voice, and some of the Temple's, and the noise went up and around and came back off the seaward curve.

The herald looked at his board.

There was a pause.

It was not a long pause. It was about the length of a man reading a line, arriving at a place where a word should be, and finding that the clerk had written what the clerk had been given. Kael had watched a pen make that space once, four years ago, in a book the size of a door, and had carried the line it drew around with him ever since like a thing he owned.

His eyes went up to the western curve before he had decided to send them.

The First Master was already looking at him. He had been for some time — through the horns and the standing and the whole of the arrival, from the one seat under that sky with nothing in front of it to watch but an empty floor. There were two people in eleven thousand who knew what the herald's pause was, and the other one had stood over that pen himself and told the clerk to leave the space as it was.

Kael could not read his face. He had never been able to read that face and had given up expecting to somewhere around his eleventh year, and what he got now was what he always got, which was a man who had priced the thing in front of him and was not going to say the number.

"Kael," the herald read out, "of the Island."

Kael walked out onto the sand.

He was thirteen years old. He had wanted, since he was four, to be seen; had wanted, since he was ten, to be measured in front of people who could tell; had spent every hour of it being told what it costs to be looked at, by two people who had built him out of not being. He came out into the middle of a white floor with eleven thousand people over him and heard the first sound the world ever made about him, and the sound was a question, because they had just been told everything about the other boy and nothing whatever about him.

Lucan Drusane was already at the measure, his eyes locked onto Kael's.

It had begun.


---

# Chapter Twenty-Three — The Ghost

Forty feet of raked sand, and a boy at the other end of it with a sword.

The draw had gone up on the colonnade at first light. Kael had read it once and had it. Sixteen names in eight pairs, the pairs folded into each other twice more, then a line where the last bout went. His half of the sheet ran Drusane, then whichever of the two in the pair under it was still standing tomorrow.

Lucan Drusane came to the measure the way he had been taught to. The front foot down, the back foot turned out, the sword up into the guard a paid master had spent four years putting into his shoulder. The off-hand open, empty, out to the side, because that was doctrine, and doctrine held that a Resonant kept one hand for the Art.

The whole system stood in front of Kael and told him what it was for.

It was built to fight at the measure. Every part of it — the guard, the straight back, the lunges that came off it, the empty hand — assumed a man standing exactly this far away with a blade of his own, doing the same grammar back.

The air came off the sea, went across the sand, and did nothing whatever for the heat.

Kael's eye went over the north curve once. Four hundred children of the Temple in the school's colours, on their feet, a wall of them. Three rows up and off to the left, a soft-built boy with his hands already going, saying something to whoever was next to him. Kael found him in about a second and a half and moved on. The finding cost him nothing and was not free.

Then he came to his own mark, and he stopped.

He did not draw.

The knife was where his father had put it, off the belt, inside the coat, and it was going to stay there. The two short blades were across his back in their wrap. They had edges on them that would go through a man's forearm if a man were careless with them, they had never once been near a fight, and they were not going to be near one today. He had thought about it for six days and come out of it the same way every time.

He put his hands at his sides and left them empty.

"You have to draw," Lucan said, from the measure. He said it the way you tell a man his lace is undone. "There's a form to it."

Kael looked at him and said nothing.

After a moment Lucan saluted anyway, because the grammar said to. The blade came up and came down in the line it wanted. The Drusane seats made a noise about it, and Kael did not answer that either, and the noise changed shape.

Somewhere up in the cheap seats one man laughed.

Lucan's face did what his face did, which was arithmetic, out loud, where anybody could read it. Kael watched it arrive at *contempt* and stop there.

Then a judge dropped his hand, and the sound came down.

---

Lucan opened correctly.

He took the measure — a step, then a step, without hurry, exactly the distance the master had put in him — and his off-hand came up flat.

The air between them went hard.

Kael felt it a half-beat before it left, because it did not come out of the hand. It came off the shoulder and the ribs and the whole front of the boy at once, the way everything did, and the hand was only where he had been taught to point it. So the shoulder was the tell, and the shoulder went, and Kael was already moving when the line arrived.

It went past his hip the width of a door and hit the rail behind him. Sand came up off the floor in a sheet.

The arena made a sound.

Lucan reset. Front foot, back foot, guard. He did not look at where the column had gone, which was good discipline, and he came forward again.

Second one. Higher, and aimed where a boy would have gone.

Kael did not go there. He went the other way and half a step in, and the air took the empty floor and the noise off the rail came back across the sand.

Third. Lucan did not throw it at him.

He threw it at the sand two feet to Kael's left, and the floor came up in a wall, and Kael's feet did what feet do and took him right, and Lucan was already there, and the measure was already made, and the sword came off the guard in the lunge four years had built.

Kael got most of his arm out of it.

The edge went along the outside of his left arm above the elbow and opened it, and he felt the cold of it before he felt anything else, and then it was warm all the way to his wrist.

The whole arena heard the sound it made.

The noise changed. It was not the noise of a crowd enjoying itself any more. Eleven thousand people had just been reminded, all together and at the same instant, that there was no ceiling on this, and the only thing between the white sand and the far end of it was an old man in a chair on the western curve.

Kael did not look at his arm.

He had been taught what looking at it costs, and had paid for the lesson in his father's own currency. The arm was information he could get later. He kept his eyes where they had been, moved off the line, made Lucan spend a step. The blood went down his hand and off his fingers and made a row of black marks in the sand as he went.

In the north curve Elarine Sedrane had a slate flat on her knees and a stylus in her hand, and while four hundred children stood up around her and shouted, she was writing.

Lucan came again. He was not enjoying it either. He was working — herding with the air and cutting with the steel, and the two things fitted together, and a paid master had fitted them, and it was, in every particular, entirely good.

And it needed a distance.

Kael had it standing on the sand with his arm running: he had spent four exchanges going *around*, because around was what you did with a thing coming at you in a line — and around was exactly where the sword lived. The column and the blade were not two problems. They were a fence and a gate, and he had been running along the fence.

The line the air came in was straight. It had to be. It came off a boy's front, and a boy's front had to be square to what he wanted to hit, and squaring took a half-beat, and a half-beat is a very long time.

So do not go around it.

Go up it.

Lucan's shoulder went, and Kael went in.

Not away and not aside. Straight down the middle of the thing, under the line and inside the release, and the air went over his back with a noise like canvas tearing and blew the rail apart behind him, and he came out of it at half the measure and kept coming, and then there was no measure at all.

Valeria Vel was the only person in the north curve who was not standing. She had her elbows on her knees and her chin on her fists and she was not watching his hands. She was watching his feet, and she had been watching his feet since the second column, and somewhere in the last three seconds she had stopped blinking.

A one-handed longsword is a magnificent object at four feet.

At six inches it is a stick with the handle in the wrong place.

Kael took the wrist. Not the sword — the wrist, in the way his father's school took a wrist, which was the way you take a thing that is going to be in your hand whether it agrees or not. He turned it against itself and the fingers opened because fingers do, and the blade went out and down and stood up in the sand three feet away, quivering, and neither of them looked at it again.

Then he was inside Lucan's arms.

Nobody had ever been inside Lucan's arms. Nothing in years of a paid master's grammar had one word to say about this, because the grammar was a language for talking to a man across a distance, and the distance was gone. Lucan's elbow came round because an elbow will, and Kael was not where the elbow went. Lucan tried to make room, and making room is a thing you do with your feet, and Kael's foot was already inside his and had been for a beat and a half.

Lucan spent the Art at nothing range.

It was the last thing he had and he was right to try it. The whole front of him went hard and the line came up between them out of no distance at all, and it would have opened Kael from the hip to the collar.

Kael turned him.

That was all of it. A hand at the shoulder and a hand at the hip and five years of *everything heavy is lazy*, and the boy's front went nine inches off where it had been aimed and took the line with it, and the column went out of him into the floor and blew a crater in the sand beside them the size of a table, and the two of them came off their feet in the middle of it.

Kael got up first. He had been going down on purpose and Lucan had not.

The elbow went in above the eye and shut it. The knee found the outside of the thigh, in the place his father had shown him in the dark when he was nine and had never once explained. Lucan's leg stopped being a leg. He went down on the sand on his face, and Kael went down with him, riding it, and took the arm and folded it up behind the back, and put his knee across, and stopped.

Lucan tried to get loose.

He tried three times, and he was strong, and he had been drilled since he was six, and there was nothing to get loose with. Both his hands were full: one of them pinned, and one of them still open at the end of an arm that had nothing left to put in it.

He reached for it anyway.

Kael felt him do it, the way you feel a man take a breath under your knee. He reached down for the thing he had been spending since the first step of the first exchange — the columns, the herding, the whole front of himself going hard at nothing range — and he went all the way down after it, past where he had ever been sent, and his body stopped him at the floor of it the way a body does. There was nothing there. He had emptied his capacity into a crater in the sand and the crater was still there and the rest of it was not.

Kael let go and stood up.

He had not decided to. His father's school had no move for a man on the ground with nothing in him, because his father's school did not put men on the ground and leave them there.

Lucan got his hands under himself and got up.

He did it badly and it took him a long time, and the arena watched every part of it. One eye was already closing. The leg would not carry, so he took his weight on the other one and stood crooked, and his sword was standing in the sand eleven feet away with the light going along it, and he did not look at the sword.

He looked up into the seats.

Kael followed it, because he followed everything. Nine rows up on the eastern curve, in the block where the noise had come from when the herald said *Drusane*, there was a man on his feet with his hands on the rail.

Kael read him at sixty feet in bad light, the way he had been reading men off hillsides since he was five, and got it exactly right the first time. There was no anger in it. Anger would have been something. The man on the rail had the face of somebody watching an expense.

Then he sat down.

Something went through Lucan Drusane's shoulders, and he turned round, and came at Kael.

There was no form in it. Four years of a paid master, the guard, the measure, the straight back, the lunges like a crossbow — all of it went off him between one stride and the next, and what came across that sand was a boy of thirteen with his hands up wrong, running.

Kael took him apart.

He did it the way he had been taught to do it, which was the way you do it when the thing has to end and there is no one to appeal to and no bell anywhere in the world. He met him coming, because meeting a man coming is cheaper. He took the wrist that was nearest and turned it and let the running do the work, and Lucan went past him and down, and the sand took most of it.

He got up.

Kael put him down again. Elbow, and then the ground.

He got up.

The third time Kael did not use his hands at all. He put a foot in behind the leg that was still working and took the arm across, and Lucan went down onto his back hard enough that the sound of it came off the seating and came back.

The noise in that arena had been going since the horns. Somewhere in there it stopped.

Eleven thousand people watched a boy with no house and no colours stand over a Drusane in the middle of the empire's own floor, waiting, entirely without hurry, for him to get up a fourth time — and understood, all together, that he was going to keep doing this until it was finished, and that he did not appear to know that there was any other way for a thing like this to end.

Lucan got his elbow under him, and pushed, and got as far as one knee.

Kael hit him once.

It was not a big blow. Nothing whatever about it was big. It went in short and level under the angle of the jaw with about eight inches behind it, into the one place on a man's head that a mountain had taught him about before he was nine, and it did not knock Lucan Drusane down so much as switch him off.

His hands did not come up.

That was it. His hands did not come up, and his knee did not brace, and he did not go down the way people go down — he left himself somewhere between one instant and the next, and what arrived on the sand was the rest of him, loose, with no argument anywhere in it.

Kael stood over him and waited to see whether he would get up, because that was the only question his father's school had ever asked about anybody.

He did not get up.

Eleven thousand people had come to that arena to watch children fight for a city, and they had been loud since the horns went at first light. What happened now was that the noise came off the building the way water comes off a roof when the rain stops. Underneath it there was nothing at all, and the nothing went on for four full seconds, which in a place that size is a very long time.

In the judges' enclosure on the southern turn, a broad, comfortable man with grey at his temples sat with his hands folded on the rail in front of him, looking down at a boy standing over another boy on the sand. He was smiling. He had been smiling for some while.

The First Master rose from his chair.

He did not come to the rail and did not raise his voice, and he did not have to, because he was standing in the one silence that building had made all morning and it carried him to the back of the seaward curve without any evidence of effort at all.

"The bout is decided."

Menders were coming out onto the sand before he had finished saying it.

"Kael, of the Island."

Kael walked back to his mark and stopped there with his hands at his sides.

Empty.

---

The arena erupted out of the cheap seats and off the seaward end and up from the standing ground behind the rails, where the island was, where the village was, where men who had rowed in that morning and gutted fish for their fathers were, and the sound was enormous. It went up and it did not stop going up. It was not a cheer for a house. Nobody up there had a house.

They had seen a boy with no name, no colours and no house take a Drusane apart with his hands, in front of every family that mattered in the empire, and decline to use the sword he had left standing in the sand, with two of his own across his back that he had not touched. Half of that arena had watched a fight. The other half had watched an argument, and had lost it.

He looked out over the seaward rail while the noise came down on him.

From the middle of the floor you could see over the top of the far seating, and past it there was the sea, and on the sea there were more ships than he had known existed, and behind them a horizon that went on being a horizon. He had been looking at that line since he was four years old from a rock on a mountain, and it had never once had anything on it. Now it had banners on it belonging to houses out of a chronicle he had read in a cold room, and every one of those houses had somebody in this building with a list, and they were all looking at him.

He was thirteen, and he was bleeding, and he had never in his life been so happy.

He did not notice that thought arrive. It went into him the way water goes into cloth, and it did not have a name on it, and behind him the menders were kneeling on the sand around the boy he had put on the ground.

The First Master had sat down again before the noise started, and he sat as he had sat all morning, with one hand along the arm of the chair and his grey head level, watching the sand. He said nothing to the steward at his shoulder, and the steward did not ask him anything. Four more bouts were fought on that floor before midday and he did not stand up for any of them.

In the royal box the princess had stopped talking to the woman on her left. She had stopped somewhere around the third column and had not started again, and her hands had gone still in her lap, and the twelve in dark grey stood exactly where they had been standing since she sat down. One of them looked, once, at the sand.

It started in the standing ground, and it was not a word at first.

It was a man shouting something to another man about where the boy had been when the air went, and then it was four of them, and then it had a shape, and then the shape came down out of the cheap seats and along the rail and into the north curve, where four hundred children of the empire's finest houses were on their feet and had not yet decided what they were allowed to think.

It went round the arena in under a minute, and it fitted, and that was the trouble with it.

*The Ghost.*

Kael heard it arrive and did not, at first, understand that it was about him. When he did, what he felt was a kind of narrow cold, because he had heard it before in other words. A girl with her back against a door in a brick court in the autumn had told him that in a room where everything talks the silent thing is the loudest thing in it.

It had taken the world a winter and four minutes to come to the same conclusion and put a name on it.

He had spent his whole life being nothing to look at.

Eleven thousand people had just found a word for it, and were shouting it.

Ruthen met him at the mouth of the competitors' gate with a cloth in one hand and his face doing nothing whatever.

"Arm."

Kael gave him the arm. The yard-master turned it to the light, ran a thumb along the outside of it well below the cut, and grunted.

"Long. Not deep. You will keep the scar and it will be a stupid one." He wrapped it, tight, in four turns. "You went inside the second column instead of the fourth."

"I hadn't worked it out at the second."

"No." Ruthen tied it off. "Sedrane tomorrow, at the third bell. She has been in the stand all morning with a slate."

"I saw."

"Then you know more than most of the boys in this building." He put the cloth away. He looked at Kael for one breath longer than he needed to, which was a thing he had done once before, four years ago, from beside a well. "That was not anything I have a name for."

"No," Kael said.

"Good," said the yard-master, and went to stand somebody else's bout.

They brought Lucan Drusane past the gate on a board a few minutes later, with one eye shut and his jaw already going the colour it was going to go, and somewhere on the way he had come round, because his eyes were open.

His face was doing arithmetic. It was not the same arithmetic. It had stopped being about luck a long time ago and it had stopped being about a charity case somewhere in the last four minutes, and whatever column it was going into now, it was going into it permanently.

He did not look at Kael. That was how Kael knew.

---

The corridor under the seating was cold. It smelled of new timber and old sea, and Kael got about twenty strides down it before the light at the far end changed.

A man was standing in it.

Heavy dark grey, with nothing on it. He was not blocking the corridor. He had arranged himself in it the way the twelve had arranged themselves across that ground in the morning, which was to say that he was already standing where he would need to be standing, and had been for some while, and Kael's whole body came up out of the fight and went cold and quiet in about a quarter of a second.

The man did not put a hand anywhere near him.

"Kael of the Island."

"Yes."

"Her Serene Highness the Princess Aeliana Cassivara Vessantine sends for you." He said it flat, in the voice of a man delivering a thing exactly as it was given to him, with nothing of his own left in it anywhere. "At the third hour after the closing bout. Alone. You will be brought."

Kael stood in a cold corridor with his arm bound in four turns of a yard-master's cloth and the noise of the arena coming down through the timber above his head, and ran every instrument he had at that sentence, and got nothing back off any of them.

*Sends for.* Not asks. Not invites.

He had a school for the road and a school for a room full of men and a school for a hillside at four hundred paces. He had a coat that had held for four years against masters, against a judge of the games, against two of the cleverest children in the empire, and had been cut for him on a road when he was ten by a man who had built it to survive precisely this kind of morning.

He had nothing at all for this.

"Alone," Kael said.

"Alone," said the man in grey.

Then he turned and went up the corridor toward the light, and did not look back to see whether he was being obeyed, because there had never been the smallest question of it.


---

# Chapter Twenty-Four — The Table

They came for him at the third hour, and they were exactly on it.

Two of them, in the heavy dark grey with nothing on it. They did not tell him where they were going and he did not ask. He built the route as they walked it — up out of the arena's under-passages, out along the seaward side where the drying-frames had been, past a line of tents with lamps inside them, and up eleven steps onto a floor that had not existed in the autumn.

The whole of it had been raised in six weeks and would be taken down in one. He had watched the poles go in.

At the top of the steps the taller of the two put out a hand, and Kael stopped.

"Arms."

He took the wrap off his back and held it out, and the man took it without opening it and passed it behind him.

"The rest."

Kael thought about that for exactly as long as it took to understand that the man was not guessing.

He reached inside the coat and brought the knife out and put it into the grey glove.

The guard looked at it. Then he looked, one beat longer than the job required, at the place on Kael's coat the knife had come out of — which was not on the belt, and was not anywhere a boy carries a knife to be seen carrying one. Something in his face moved by about a degree, then went back where it had been.

He did not say anything. He put the knife with the wrap.

"Straight in. Do not turn your back on her when you go."

---

The first thing was the smell, and it arrived before the doorway did.

It came down the six feet of passage and went into him and kept going. Perfume, hot and sweet and heavy, the kind that is put into a room on purpose. Under it, charcoal. Under that, meat — more than one, three at least, and one of them was mutton and one of them was something he had never smelled in his life. Fish, grilled, with the oil still going. Bread. Wine, which he knew from the Temple's high table at a distance and had never been near. Something green and cut and dying.

He came through the doorway and the rest of it landed on him all at once.

The room was hot. There were braziers in it, four of them, burning coals in the spring, in a room already full of people, for no reason his body could find. There were lamps along both walls and lamps on stands and candles on the table, and the light off the gold came back up under all of it, and there was gold on the plate and gold in the cloth and gold beaten flat and nailed along the edge of a screen that was there to stand in front of nothing.

And there was the table.

It ran the length of the room and there was food on all of it. Whole fish laid out in a row with their eyes in. Three joints. Birds he had no name for, small, arranged. Bread in a shape bread has no reason to be in. Fruit that did not grow on this island and did not grow anywhere at this time of year, piled, and some of it was already going. Flowers cut and lying along the middle of it, out of season, wilting in the heat off the braziers. Glass. Glass everywhere.

And in the middle, in a wide silver bowl, packed round a jug and sweating into the cloth, there was ice.

Kael stood two steps inside the door and his count came up on its own, the way it always did.

Nobody was eating.

Twelve people in that room, and a fire in four bowls, and enough on that table for the village, and nobody had a plate.

"Nearer," said a woman's voice.

She was on the far side of the table with two others behind her. Kael had seen her at a distance that morning, on the left, in the royal box.

He went nearer.

The princess was in a chair that had been carried here, because nothing on this island was made like that. She had come out of the arena blue and gold; she had changed since. It was a lighter thing now, there was less of it, and it had still taken somebody an hour. Her hair was down.

She looked at him.

She did it the way you look at a horse, or a field, or a piece of work somebody has brought you — from the top, then across, then back to the top — and she went on doing it well past the point at which a person stops.

Kael let her. He had been looked at by masters, by four hundred children, by eleven thousand people that morning. He stood the way he stood and put his hands where his hands went and did not fill the silence, because filling a silence was a thing he had never once needed to do.

"Turn him toward the lamp," the princess said.

The woman said, "Boy—" but Kael had already worked out what was wanted and turned, so that the light came onto the side of his face. That was somehow worse than being told.

The princess looked some more.

"You will address Her Serene Highness properly," the woman said.

"Your Serene Highness," Kael said.

He said it correctly, once, and then it went out of his head entirely, because it was not information and he had nowhere to keep it.

"There is nothing to him," the princess said.

She said it to the woman.

"No, Highness."

"I watched him for a quarter of an hour this morning and I have been trying since to say one thing about what he looks like, and I find that I cannot." She moved a hand at him, at the whole of him, in the light. "Look. He is a boy who might have carried something up here. There is nothing to look at at all."

"No, Highness."

"It is very annoying," the princess said.

Kael stood in the lamp and understood that he had been sent for the way you send for a thing that has been described to you, and that the description had not survived the object.

"They are calling you a ghost," she said, to him this time. "Did you know that."

"Yes."

"Do you like it?"

He thought about it, because she had asked.

"I don't know what it's for yet," he said.

The princess's eyebrows went up about a quarter of an inch, and behind her the woman's face did something that was not a smile and was in the same family.

"You beat a Drusane with your hands."

"Yes."

"My father's cupbearer is a Drusane cousin and has told me for two years that the house is the finest steel in the southern isles." She reached out without looking and somebody put a glass into her hand. "He was badly taught, then. That is all it was. He was badly taught and you are very quick."

"No," Kael said.

The room did not go quiet, because it had been quiet. It changed anyway. He felt it go through the twelve of them like weather crossing a slope, and he had felt exactly that in a hall four years ago.

"No," the princess repeated.

"He was taught very well." Kael had already started, and the thing that had started him was not courage, it was that a false statement had been made in front of him about a subject he knew. "His guard was right. His measure was right, and he kept it four exchanges when he was frightened, which is hard. He herded me with the air instead of throwing it, and that is what you do against somebody who moves, and nobody had to tell him that this morning. He opened my arm with a lunge his master built. It was all correct."

"And yet."

"And yet all of it needs a distance." He heard his own voice doing the thing it did in the letters room when Priscian asked something real, and he could not get hold of it in time. "He was taught a language for talking to a man four feet away. He was taught it very well. Nobody in that school ever had to explain what he should do when there is no four feet, because everybody he has ever fought was standing in the same place he was."

There was a pause of about two seconds.

"You are saying," the princess said, "that the finest steel in the southern isles is fine only against itself."

"I'm saying he was taught well," Kael said. "I said that twice."

The woman behind the chair drew a breath in through her nose.

The princess put the glass down. She had gone still — not the stillness Kael knew from his mother, which was a thing built out of years, and not Valeria's, which warned. This was a girl of fourteen finding a place in her own chest where a thing had gone in.

"Come here," she said. "Where I can see the arm."

He went round the end of the table and stood where she pointed. She looked at the cloth, and at the blood that had come through it and dried brown, and at the older mark on his forearm going up out of the cuff. Kael watched her look at his body the way she had looked at his face. It went on for a while.

"Does it hurt?"

"Yes."

"Will they mend it properly, or will you keep it?"

"I'll keep it."

"Good," the princess said. "Scars are the only interesting thing about soldiers and they get them so late."

And that was where his own faculty came on, and he had no more say in it than he had ever had.

The itch had never once in his life been out-waited. It had walked him down a mountain on the darkest night of a year. It had put him on a stone floor in front of a girl who could hear him coming through her boots. He was standing in the hottest room he had ever been in, four feet from the daughter of the emperor, and what his head did was what his head always did, which was to go back to the thing it had not finished.

"What happens to the food?"

The princess looked up.

"What?"

"On the table." He was not pointing. He had not moved. "Nobody's eating it. There's fish on there that was killed this morning and it's been in this heat since, and the fruit's going, and there's three joints. What happens to it after?"

Nobody in the room said anything at all.

"It is taken away," the princess said.

"Where."

"I have no idea where," she said. "Why on earth would I know that?"

Kael did not have an answer for that. It was not the kind of not-having-an-answer he was used to. He had spent years collecting the sensation of not knowing a thing and putting it somewhere useful. This one would not go anywhere. The question had gone out of him and had not landed on anything. It was going to go on not landing, and the room had already moved past it.

"And the ice," he said.

"The ice."

"There's no ice on this island in the spring. There's barely any ice on this island in the winter." He heard himself keep going, from a small distance, the way you hear yourself on the second night of a fever. "So it must have come in a hull. Packed in sawdust, perhaps? From somewhere north, and most of it was water before it got here."

"Yes," the princess said.

She was looking straight at him now, and she was not looking at his face any more.

"You have been in this room a quarter of an hour," she said, "and you have corrected me about a boy you knocked unconscious, and asked me two questions about my own table, and you have not once asked me for anything."

Kael, who had had nothing asked of him and had asked for nothing, said, "No."

"Every person who has ever stood where you are standing wanted something from it."

"I didn't know I was allowed to want something."

The princess sat back in the chair that had been carried across water for her.

"You are not," she said. "That is not what I meant and you know quite well that it is not what I meant."

He did not know quite well. He was, at that moment, further from knowing than he had been at any point in the last four years, and everything in his body that was any use to him was reporting a room, and a room is not a person.

"I sent for you," the princess said, "because eleven thousand people looked at one thing this morning and I wished to see it closer than they did. That is all it was. That is the entire of it." She had gone very level. "And I have looked at you for a quarter of an hour and there is nothing to see, and instead of being looked at you have stood in my rooms and counted my dinner."

"I—"

"Kneel. Say your farewells. And leave afterwards."

Kael knelt.

He did not know what the farewell was. Nobody had ever taught him one. He knelt on the boards in front of the daughter of the emperor with the heat of four braziers on the side of his face, and the silence went on for about as long as it takes a room full of people to understand that nothing is coming.

He got up. He went backward the first three steps, because the man on the stair had told him to, and it was the one instruction he had been given that he could still carry out. Then he turned and went out past the braziers and the flowers and the gold nailed along a screen standing in front of nothing. The smell came with him all the way down the eleven steps, and a long way after that.

The tall guard gave him back the wrap, and then the knife.

He watched Kael put the knife away, and where he put it, and said nothing whatever, and stood aside.

---

The cistern court at night was four brick walls and a black square of sky, and somebody had put two lamps on the rim.

"There he is," said Aurelian. "We have started without you and we are not sorry."

They were sitting on the brick with a cloth between them, and on the cloth was most of a Temple supper — bread, cold fowl, a wedge of the hard cheese, a jar of the small beer the kitchens gave the older boys and pretended not to.

"They feed the ones who are fighting first," Aurelian said. "Nobody has yet worked out that they will feed you twice if you go back and look tired enough."

Kael sat down on the brick and looked at it for a moment longer than the food required, and then took a piece of bread and ate it.

"Well?" said Elarine.

"She wanted to see what I looked like."

"And?"

"She was disappointed."

Aurelian put his head back and laughed at the sky, which was the loudest thing that had happened in that court in two years.

"Oh, that is *wonderful*," he said. "That is the best thing I have heard all day, and I have had a very bad day. The daughter of the emperor sent for the Ghost of the Southern Isles and got you."

"What did she say?" said Elarine.

"That there was nothing to look at."

"There is," said Valeria.

She said it from the other side of the cloth without looking up from the cheese she was cutting, flatly, as a piece of information, and Aurelian made a sound like a man hit in the stomach with a cushion.

"Enough," said Elarine. "Aurelian. Tell it."

"I do not wish to tell it."

"You have been waiting three hours to tell it."

"I have been waiting three hours to tell it," Aurelian agreed, and told it.

He told it well. He was good at this and always had been; it was the same instrument that had moved six backs as one in a yard when he was ten, pointed now at his own defeat, and he did not spare himself one inch of it. His opponent in the games, Meloran, on the sand at seventeen with four years of reach and a spear. The first pass, in which Aurelian had done something clever. The second pass, in which Meloran had shown him what the clever thing was worth. The third, in which Aurelian, by his own account, had been "moved about the floor like furniture, by a man being polite about it."

"He apologised afterward," he said. "In front of my father's steward. He said, *your house has been very good to me*, and then he asked whether my arm was all right, and I said yes, and it is not, and I shall be pretending it is until Thursday."

"You asked for him," said Kael.

"I asked for him four days a week."

"You got what you asked for."

"I got *precisely* what I asked for. That is the thing nobody warns you about." Aurelian looked into the beer jar. "I have been telling everyone for a year that I intended to be pulled apart at the highest available level, and I meant every word, and I was correct, and it turns out that being correct about it in advance does not do one single thing for you at the time."

He was quiet for about a second and a half.

Then he said, "He is going to win the whole thing, by the way," and reached for the cheese, and the moment closed over like water.

"He is not," said Valeria.

"He is not," Aurelian agreed cheerfully. "I withdraw it. I was being noble."

Elarine had not eaten anything. She was sitting with her back against the brick, knees up, arms round them, which Kael had never seen her do. The slate was nowhere.

"We are not doing tomorrow," she said.

"Nobody said—"

"Somebody was going to. It was going to be you, and it was going to be in about four minutes, and you were going to make it a joke first." Elarine tipped her head at Aurelian without any heat in it at all. "We are not doing it. It will happen at the third bell and it will happen without either of us discussing it tonight in a cistern."

"All right," said Aurelian.

"Good."

"May I ask one question about it."

"No."

"Then I shall ask a question about something else." Aurelian turned round on the brick. "What is the capital actually like? Everybody who has been will only tell you who they met."

"Ask her about the gates," said Kael. "Val, the seaward gate on that arena is hung to open against the crowd. Is that how they build them in the capital, or did somebody do it here because it was quicker?"

The court went quiet in a way it had not been quiet all evening.

Kael went back over his own sentence and found the word sitting in the middle of it, where he had put it without asking himself one thing about it.

He had said it before, at the end of the cold term, on cold stone in the dark. It was simply the word his mouth had for her now, and it had arrived in the middle of a question about a gate.

"*Val*," said Aurelian.

"Don't," said Valeria.

"A year." Aurelian put both hands flat on the brick and addressed the court at large, in the manner of a man laying out evidence. "A year. I have trained beside her. I have been struck by her. I have written to my brother about her at a length he described as *worrying*. I once held her cloak." He turned. "He has had the same year, and he says it in the middle of a sentence about a *gate*."

"You have never once asked her a question you wanted the answer to," said Elarine.

"That is beside the—"

"It is the entire point." Elarine had not moved off the brick. Her chin was still on her knees. "And you."

Kael looked round.

"Four years," said Elarine. "Four years of *Sedrane*. I have collected you off a sea wall twice a week since we were eleven. I taught you fire. I gave you a principle for a floor and I never once asked what you wanted it for." She let it sit exactly as long as she let everything sit. "And you have not shortened one syllable of me in your life."

"Your name doesn't—"

"Do not tell me what my name does. I am extremely aware of what my name does." There was something happening in her face that Kael could see with total clarity and could not read at all. "Go on. Try it."

"...Elarine."

"That is the whole of it. That is my entire name." She put her head back against the brick and looked up at the black square of sky. "Unbelievable."

"Val," said Aurelian, experimentally, to nobody.

"Stop."

"*Val.*"

"Elarine. Make him stop."

"I am not going to make him stop," said Elarine. "Val."

Valeria cut a piece of cheese and did not look up at any of them.

"It is grey," she said. "It is grey and it is very large and there is nothing to eat that is any good."

"That is the worst description of the capital I have ever heard."

"You asked me what it is like. That is what it is like."

"Elarine. Help me."

"I have never been," said Elarine. "My house sends letters there. It does not send me."

"Then we shall all go," said Aurelian, "and Val shall show us the grey, and we shall find something to eat that is good, and I shall pay, because my house is enormous and my brother has a very poor grasp of what things cost."

And they laughed.

All three of them, at once, easily, at nothing, in a brick court with two lamps on the rim of it — the sound going up off the walls and out of the square of sky, three children of the empire's finest houses laughing at a table on a night with nothing in it.

Kael's body flinched.

It was very small. It had been very small for years. He had stopped being surprised by it somewhere around his eleventh year. It arrived when people laughed. It arrived worst when they laughed together. It had never once been wrong about anything, and never once been right.

He took a drink of the small beer to have something to do with his hands.

And over the top of the jar he saw that Valeria had not laughed either.

She was sitting with her knife in the cheese and her face doing nothing whatever, in the way of a person who has watched a thing happen from outside it and has understood the shape of it perfectly and has no idea at all how the others knew when to start.

Kael looked at her for slightly too long.

Valeria's eyes came up and met his. She saw him seeing it. Neither of them did one thing about it, and she went back to the cheese.

They stayed another hour.

Aurelian talked, mostly. Elarine took the argument apart twice for the pleasure of it. Somebody knocked the jar over and it went into the cistern and Aurelian lay flat on the brick with his good arm down the hole and could not reach it, and described in detail what his house would say if it could see him. Valeria ate about four times as much as anybody.

Kael did not say very much, and was not asked to, and that was the part he had no word for at all.

When they broke it up the lamps were low and the sky had turned.

"Tomorrow," Elarine said to him, at the top of the steps, and that was all of it, and it was enough.

Valeria walked out with him as far as the corner where the sea court path went up.

"I am going to miss this," she said.

Kael stopped.

"After the games I will be going back to the capital," Valeria said. She was looking down the path and not at him, and her voice was doing exactly what it did when she called a number after a blow. "It was always the year. My father wrote in the autumn that it would be the year and that I should not become attached to the arrangement, and I wrote back that I had not. That was true when I wrote it."

"What will you do?"

"Wherever father says I should. It is not a thing I am told in advance." She shifted the strap on her shoulder. "I have been on the island a year and I have eaten supper with other people more times than all of my life combined, and all of them were in that court."

Kael, who had been given no word for this either, said nothing at all, which was the only honest thing available to him.

And Valeria Vel turned her head and smiled at him.

It was not much of a smile. It was there and gone, and it did not do anything a smile is supposed to do to a face. Kael had watched her for many mornings from a treeline and stood across from her every day for a year. He had seen her satisfied. He had seen her bored. He had seen her decide not to hit somebody. He had never once seen this.

"Third bell," she said. "Do not lose to Sedrane. I want you."

Then she went up the path, and Kael went down the road, and it was a very long way home and he did not notice one step of it.


---

# Chapter Twenty-Five — The Door

The lamp was still burning when he came up the last of the hill.

He was three hours past *Evening* and he had known it since the cistern, and he came in through the door with the whole of it ready — where he had been, who with, what had been said, in the order she liked it — and stopped, because his mother was at the table with her hand flat on a sheet of paper and his father was sitting down.

His father did not sit down when he came in. In eleven years his father had not once been sitting when he came in.

"Sit," Neris said. "Eat first. You are grey."

"I ate at the Temple."

"You ate bread in a hole in the ground with three children and I would not give the smallest coin for any of it." She pushed the plate across the table with one finger, side-on in the chair, reaching with the one arm. "Eat, and then tell me all of it, and then I am going to stop asking you questions and you are going to go to bed."

He ate. It was the goat, and the last of the winter roots, and there was one lamp lit because the second one was for work.

Then he told them.

He told the bout first, because that was the order it had happened in. His father took him through the second column and the fourth without a word wasted.

"You went inside a column."

"Yes."

His father looked at him for a moment. "Good," he said, and nothing else.

Kael went on to the arm, and then to the corridor.

At the words *sends for you*, nothing happened in that room at all.

He knew what that meant. He had learned in the autumn exactly what it meant when a name landed on his parents and nothing came back off them, and he kept going, and gave it to them whole: the two men in grey, the stairs, the arms taken, the room, the food, the woman behind the chair, what the princess had said and what he had said back. He did not soften the part where he had corrected her. He did not soften the part where he had asked about the table, though he heard how it sounded coming out of his own mouth at his own table with one lamp on it.

His mother asked three questions.

Where were the guards standing when the door was open. Whether anyone had written anything down while he was in the room. And what, exactly, the woman behind the chair had been wearing.

He answered them. She listened the way she listened to birds.

And then she stopped.

Kael waited for the fourth question, and the fifth, and the sightline reconstruction and the order of it and *their words, not yours*.

His mother put her hand flat on the table.

"That is enough of that for tonight."

"There's more."

"There is always more. It will be there in the morning." She was not looking at him quite. "You have been up since dark and you have been cut and you fight at the third bell. It will keep."

Kael sat and could not work out what had just happened to him.

His father got up, went to the fire, came back with the pot, and poured. The smell of it came up between them. It was the mint she grew by the step, and he had drunk it after every hurt of his entire life.

"The judge of the games was at that arena," Neris said, to nobody. "There were four hundred of them. It is one island and there is one week left of it."

"Yes," said Vask.

"Then we will do the rest tomorrow."

"Yes."

She turned the cup round on the table with two fingers.

"He beat a house today," she said.

"He did."

"In front of all of them."

"Yes," said Vask.

That was the whole of it. Nobody looked at Kael while it was said and nobody said one word to him about it, and he sat in his own chair with his hands round a cup of mint and understood, without any of the equipment he would have needed to say it out loud, that his mother had asked three questions instead of thirty and that his father was sitting down.

"Your sibling is very close now," Neris said.

"How close?"

"Days. A week." She put her hand on the top of it, which she had never done in front of him, and left it there. "He has been sideways since the turn and last night he was not, so he has decided something."

"You keep saying he."

"I do," his mother agreed. "I have no evidence whatever. Do not write it down."

Kael laughed — properly, once, out of the middle of him — and his father made the small sound in his nose that was as far as his father went, and for about four seconds the three of them sat in a one-room house on a mountain with a lamp and a pot of mint between them, and that was all that was happening anywhere.

"Everything is by the door," Vask said, after a while.

Kael looked. Two bundles, canvas, roped. He had walked past them coming in and had counted them and had not let the count arrive anywhere.

"The books," said Neris. "The salt. The instruments. It has been ready since the morning the games opened."

"And when it starts," Kael said, "the master comes."

"The master comes," said Neris.

Nobody in that room said anything else about it, because there was nothing else to say about it. It had been decided in the autumn and paid for over a winter and it was the one part of the whole arrangement that had never needed discussing.

Kael went up to the loft when he was told to, and slept the way he had slept before every important morning of his life, which was badly and then all at once.

---

They were both at the door.

That was the thing. His father was at the door because his father was always at the door, up before him, out at the block before there was anything to see by. His mother was at the door too, with a shawl round her and one hand on the frame and the whole weight of her carried forward, at an hour she had no business being out of a chair, on a stone step in a spring wind.

Neither of them said anything about that either.

His father checked the arm. He did it the way Ruthen had done it and a good deal faster, thumb well below the cut, one turn of the cloth tightened.

"It will open in the first two minutes and then it will stop," he said. "Do not look at it."

"No."

"Sedrane."

"Yes."

"You've said she has been reading you twice a week for two years and she was writing yesterday while everyone else was shouting." His father let go of the arm. "So she has got everything you did to the Drusane boy, and she is not going to give you any of it. What has she not got?"

Kael stood on the step in the cold with the mountain going grey behind the house and found that he had the answer and had had it since the cistern.

"Everything I've learned about fire," he said. "She taught me all of it herself."

His father looked at him for a moment.

"Go on, then," he said.

And Neris took Kael's face in both hands, which she had done perhaps eleven times in his life. She looked at it. She said nothing. Then she let go.

"Evening," said Vask.

"Evening," said Kael, and went down the hill.


---

# Chapter Twenty-Six — The Third Bell

The road was full.

He had come down it alone in the dark for four years. Now there were carts on it, a family from the village walking in the ruts, a man selling something hot out of a basket to people who had slept on the ground. Somebody had painted a bed sheet and hung it off a cart rail. He could not read it from behind.

He went along the top of the ditch where the ground was firm, because that was where you went.

A boy of about six saw him first.

He did not say anything. He took hold of his mother's skirt, pulled it twice, and pointed with his whole arm. His mother looked. The man beside her looked. Then the cart stopped.

After that it went down the road ahead of him faster than he could walk.

He had come down this hill every morning for years past people who never once turned round, and it took most of a cart-length for the road to find out what was on it. Somebody said the word. Somebody further down said it louder. A man with a coil of rope over his shoulder stepped out into his path, and Kael's whole body went to work before his head had any say in it — the reach, the rope, the ditch behind, the four places he could put his feet.

What the man did was take his hat off.

"Go on, Ghost," he said.

That was the whole of it. He stood back into the ruts and let him by, and the road did the same thing the rest of the way down, opening in front of him and closing up behind. Somewhere in the middle of it a woman put her hand flat on his back as he passed, and he did not turn round.

He came level with the cart, and got past it, and read the sheet going away.

Two words, in whitewash, on somebody's bed linen, with a rope nailed through the hem so the wind would not take it.

He walked the last of the hill with his ears hot and a narrow cold sitting under his breastbone, and could not have said which of the two was bigger.

The arena from the landward side was a wall with noise behind it.

He sat in the cold under the seating with his back against new timber and the sound of thousands arriving over his head.

---

The sand had been raked flat overnight. The crater was gone. There was nothing on that floor at all — no wood, no cloth, no grass, not one thing that would take a flame and go on burning by itself.

Elarine Sedrane came out of the eastern gate with her hair bound and a sword in her right hand.

Her left hand was open and empty and held a little out from her body, because that was doctrine, and doctrine held that a Resonant kept one hand for the Art.

She came to her mark, set her feet, and looked at him the whole width of that white floor away, with the face she used on a page she had already read.

Their eyes met.

"Do not make me chase you," Elarine said.

"I'm going to make you chase me."

"I know." She set her feet. "I am asking anyway."

The third bell went.

A judge dropped his hand, and eleven thousand people made a sound, and she paid.

Both hands.

The fire came off her right and her left in the same instant and went three ways at once — one sheet low and flat across the sand at his shins, one wall climbing out to his left where a boy would have gone, and one that simply burst in the air in front of his face, because she had decided the air should burn there.

And she was coming forward behind it with the sword.

He went under the low one and it took the hair off his arms. He got his head out of the burst and the heat went into his ear and his eye began to water and did not stop. He turned off the wall and there was nothing left to turn onto, and the sword came out of the middle of all that light at his throat.

He got a hand on her wrist and it cost him. The edge went along the top of his shoulder and opened it, and her left palm came round flat at his ribs and he felt his shirt go and then his skin.

He broke and ran and she let him go, because she did not need him.

Out at the far end of that floor Kael stood with his shoulder running and his ear singing and the whole right side of him smelling of himself.

*Not with hands. Not enough.*

He had known it for days and had wanted very badly to be wrong about it.

He reached over his shoulder and pulled the wrap.

*Do not take them anywhere near a man who is trying to hurt you until I say.*

His father had said it in the dark on the ninth morning of the turn. Kael had carried it eight weeks and carried the blades through a whole day of these games without touching them, and it went off him now in the time it took to get one hand round a grip, because there was a girl at the other end of that sand who was going to beat him.

The arena understood before the blades were clear.

Eleven thousand people had watched this boy stand with his hands empty yesterday while a Drusane came at him with four years of a paid master behind it, and had made a noise about it that went round the building twice. Now the wrap came off and there was steel in both his hands, and the sound they made was not the same sound at all.

The threading went into the right-hand blade the way his father had taught it. Shoulder, arm, hand, steel.

It sat in there loose at the point and wanting to come home, and he had no time to make it better.

Elarine did not wait for him to be ready. She opened her hand and a fan of flame came across the sand at his legs, wide as a cart, and Kael did the thing he had built the last eight weeks out of.

He went at it.

The threaded blade came up through the middle of that fire and the fire came apart on it.

It did not go out. It broke — split along the line the steel took, went round him on both sides in two ragged halves that had nothing holding them together any more, and closed on empty air behind his back while he was already three strides past where it had been.

Somewhere above him a very large number of people stopped making any noise at all.

Elarine's face did something he had never once seen on it.

Then she was moving.

She threw fire off her left hand at his head and lunged with the sword under it in the same motion, which is a thing that should not be possible and is what a treasury with two years of drilling buys you. Kael took the fire on the threaded blade and it came apart, and took the sword on the other one and it rang, and both of his arms went numb to the elbow, and he was already inside her guard.

The heavy blade went into her thigh.

He felt it go in and he felt it come out and he was moving before she finished the sound she made.

*Cut her.*

*Keep cutting her.*

She burned him for it. She burned him hard, off both hands, at nothing range, and he got most of himself out and left some of his sleeve and a strip of his forearm in it, and the crowd came up off its seats in a wave that ran round the whole bowl.

They fought.

There is no other word for the next part of it. It was not exchanges and it was not passes. It was two children trying to end each other on white sand in front of the empire, and it went on for a long time.

She was fast. She was faster than anybody in that yard had ever seen her be, because nobody in that yard had ever made her be. Fire came off her in every shape she owned — sheets, walls, columns, bursts hanging in the air where he was about to put his head — and behind and through and under all of it came the sword, always, never once late.

He was faster.

That was the thing the north curve could not get over afterward, and the thing the boxes did not want to say out loud. She was a Sedrane with a treasury and a paid master and eleven years of it, and the boy with no house was faster than she was, and he had been faster than she was since the first exchange, and he was not tiring.

She burned him across the back when he mistimed one. He cut her forearm and then the same forearm again.
