She got a wall up under his feet and he took it on the boots and came out of it with his soles smoking and put the heavy blade through her guard into her ribs and did not get depth on it, and she paid the whole front of herself into his face at half a stride and he turned his head and lost his eyebrow and some of his hair and kept coming.

Blood went onto that sand from both of them. It went on going.

In the north curve Valeria Vel stood up.

She had not stood up yesterday, not once, not through any of it. She stood now with one hand on the rail in front of her, and she was not watching Kael's hands, and she was not watching his feet either.

She was watching their eyes.

---

Here is what Kael did, and eleven thousand people saw it happen without one of them being able to say afterward what it was.

He never stopped.

He would not stand and take a thing. Every single time she made him move, he came back — off the turn, off the boot, out of the smoke, at her, with one blade or the other, so that the fire she had just spent on putting him somewhere had to be followed immediately by more fire to keep him from arriving.

She could not stop answering him. He was always arriving.

And she was buying every answer out of her own body.

*Fire is not held. It is paid.* She had said it to him on the rim of a cistern when they were eleven with the whole superiority of somebody explaining her own art to a boy who had none, and she had been right, and there is no floor in an arena and no fuel in the air and nothing anywhere on that white sand for a flame to eat, so every wall and every sheet and every burst came out of Elarine Sedrane and nowhere else.

He was spending his legs.

She was spending herself.

The eighth time he came back at her the fire that met him came up at his knee instead of his chest.

He saw it. He had been waiting to see it, and it went through him with no pleasure in it anywhere.

The ninth time it came slower.

The tenth she did not throw at all — she cut at him with the sword, twice, fast and correct and entirely conventional, and Kael broke her line with the heavy blade and put the threaded one along her upper arm and opened it, and stepped back out, and let her have the distance.

He gave it to her on purpose. He wanted her to spend something to get him back off her.

She stood there breathing.

There was blood coming off her hand from the arm and blood on her thigh from the first cut and blood soaking down the side of her shirt, and her hair had come out of its binding down one side, and she was looking at him across a floor she had owned for the first four minutes of that morning and did not own any more.

She understood exactly what was being done to her. He watched her understand it. It was the worst thing he had ever seen on anybody's face and there was nothing in it he could use.

"Elarine," he said.

"Don't," said Elarine Sedrane.

In the royal box the princess had come forward in her chair with one hand flat on the rail. The woman behind her said something. She did not answer it and she did not sit back.

---

Elarine set her feet in the sand, put the sword down and took her hand off the grip.

Then she spent all of it.

The sand beside her turned to glass.

Not a wall and not a column — the fire came up out of the whole white length of the arena between them and rose and went on rising, and the sound of it went through the seating into the timber and up through eleven thousand pairs of feet, and every one of them stood.

Kael had seen the Art at candle scale thousands of times in his life.

Now the world showed him what the Art of fire truly was.

It came at him wider than the road home.

There was nowhere on that floor to be. No lane. No distance to get. About a second and a half.

He put both blades away, because he was going to need the hand.

Then he held the left one out in front of him, edge on, the way you hold a thing you mean to cut with.

Two at once.

The first was the earth — his cheap copy of the Vel's art, seven weeks and many failures of it. Not the arm. He could not do the arm. He had never once been able to do the arm. He put it into the one line down the outside of his palm, the thinnest place he owned, and he put all of it there.

The second was the threading, and it went into his hand the way it went into steel. Shoulder, arm, hand — and then nowhere left to go, because there was no blade on the end of it any more.

He felt his own minima come together along the edge of that hand, precise and small and absolutely exact, and it emptied him of every grain of capacity he had in about a fifth of a second.

The fire arrived.

---

There is very little to say about it.

It came apart on his hand the way the fan had come apart on the blade. It went round him on both sides and along the inside of his guard, and it took the skin off the back of his left hand and the whole outside of his left arm from the wrist to the elbow, and it went on doing that for a length of time that had nothing to do with a second and a half.

He did not put the arm down.

He held.

And underneath it all, in a part of him that was not doing anything else, a small flat voice that belonged to a woman at a table with two pails observed that fire eats what it is given, and that there was nothing on this floor and nothing in this air, and that she had bought this out of her own body and could not put it down and pick it up again.

The heat went off.

He came out of the far edge of it moving, with his sleeve gone and his arm the colour of a cooked thing and a blade back in each hand.

His own Art had been spent too. No more threading now flowing through his blade.

All he had left was steel, and every hour he'd ever spent learning to swing it.

She had the sword up before he was halfway to her, and her left hand was open and low and there was nothing in it, and there was not going to be anything in it again. He had made sure of that.

Both of them knew it in the same half second.

Whatever either of them had brought onto that sand in the way of the Art was gone into the air and the floor and each other's skin, and what was left standing at the middle of the empire's arena was two fighters bleeding, with edges.

And something came up through Kael that he did not examine and had no name for and would not have stopped if he had.

He was not tired. That was the strange part and he noticed it distantly, the way you notice weather. His arm was ruined and his shirt was stuck to his ribs and he had nothing left to spend and none of it had reached the part of him that was doing this, because the part of him that was doing this had been doing it since before he could remember, in the dark, on scree, against a man who never once hurried. The ability to fight was not a tool.

It was the first language he ever learned.

He came at Elarine Sedrane across that sand with a short blade in each hand.

And Elarine, backing off her lead foot and getting her point into line for a boy who was already inside where a longsword works, saw his face.

She would think about it for a long time and never once say it to him.

He was smiling.

Not a fighter's grin, and nothing to do with her, and nothing in it that was cruel about anybody. It was the face of a boy doing the only thing in the world he had never had to be taught to want. There was blood on his teeth from somewhere in the last two minutes and he did not know it was there, and he did not know his mouth was doing anything at all.

Elarine had spent four years learning that the Art is a tool and a house is a ladder and the games are a market. She had never in her life met somebody for whom the fighting was simply the good part.

She met him anyway.

She was very good. She was better with that sword than anybody in the Temple except the girl on the sea court, and she was faster than she had ever been, and she put the point into his forearm on the first pass and along his hip on the second, and it did not matter at all, because he did not stop coming and she had to give ground to keep the length she needed.

He took the length away from her.

That was the whole of it, and everybody in that building watched it happen and most of them could not have said what they were watching. He came in and he stayed in. Every time she made the room a longsword wants, he closed it before she could stand it up, and there is nothing a long blade can do about a short one at the distance of a table, and there were two of them, and he was faster.

She got one more real thing away — a cut off the back foot that would have opened his face if his head had been where his head had been.

Then he took her wrist.

Not the sword. The wrist. He turned it against itself the way his father had shown him on a mountain in the dark before he was ten, and the fingers came open because fingers do, and the sword went out of her hand and stood up in the sand three strides away.

Elarine Sedrane stayed on her feet for about two seconds.

Then her legs went, and she knelt down in the middle of the empire's floor with her hands hanging and her head down and her breath going in and out of her like something being dragged over stones, and did not get up, because there was nothing left anywhere in her to get up with.

Kael stood over her with a blade in each hand and waited to see whether she would.

The First Master was already on his feet.

"The bout is decided."

The noise came in over the top of it and buried the rest, and it came out of the standing ground first and off the seaward end and up out of the cheap seats where the island was, and then out of the north curve, and then out of the whole building at once.

"Kael, of the Island."

Kael got about four strides toward the gate before his own legs found out what he had spent, and then he sat down on the sand an arm's length from her, with a blade still in each hand.

---

The First Master was coming down.

Not to the rail. Down. Out of the host's box, along the front of the western curve, through the gate the judges used: an old man in robes and a chain crossing the whole width of that white floor at a walk, while the noise came off that building in a wave that did not stop.

He went to Elarine first.

Kael sat on the sand with his ruined arm in his lap, no more capacity in him than a wrung cloth, and watched the First Master of the Temple of the Southern Isles kneel in the middle of the empire's arena beside a thirteen-year-old girl and put both hands on her.

Nothing showed. That was the part nobody in the seats was ready for. No light. No sound. Nothing came off him at all — and the great slow noise of eleven thousand people came down, and down, and stopped. A building that size can be quiet if it wants to be. It wanted to be.

Her fingers moved. Then her feet. Then she said something Kael did not hear, and the old man answered it without stopping what he was doing.

He was there a long time.

When he came to Kael he took the arm and turned it to the light with a thumb well below the burn. A yard-master had done that a day earlier. A father had done it that morning. All three of them had learned it in the same trade.

"Look away, or don't," said Severin. "It makes no difference to the arm."

Kael looked.

It went cold first, then past cold into something with no name in any of the five languages he had. The skin along his forearm crawled, and knitted, and turned over the space of about forty seconds from the colour of a cooked thing to the colour of a healing one. It hurt more coming back than it had going away.

"You will keep some of this one as well," the First Master said. "Nature is not tidy. It is only quick."

"Is she—"

"She is very strong and she went to the bottom of herself in front of the empire." He did not look up. "She will be walking by this evening. She will be no good to anybody for a week, and there is nothing in this world that would make her admit it."

He did not let go of the arm.

"Were you aware of it?" he said.

"Of what?"

"You were smiling."

Kael stopped looking at the arm.

"Through a good part of the end of it," said the First Master. "While you were cutting her. I do not believe anybody in those seats could see it from where they were sitting. I could."

"I didn't know I was."

"No." The old man turned the wrist a little and went on with what he was doing. "I did not think you did."

The noise was still coming down off the building. Somewhere behind them they were getting Elarine onto her feet.

"Your father and your mother taught you to fight," Severin said, in the voice of a man going down a list.

"Yes."

"They did not teach you that."

Kael went after it the way he went after everything, and found nothing at all where the answer should have been. There was no day it had been put into him. No hour, no lesson it came at the end of, nobody standing over him saying *again*. It had been there before the parts of him that kept things.

"No," he said.

"No," said the First Master.

He finished the arm and laid it down across Kael's knee, and did not take his hand off it straight away.

"I am not going to tell you it is a bad thing," he said. "I do not know that it is. I have been wrong about people for longer than you have been alive, and I have been wrong in both directions." The hand came away. "But you will want to be able to see it."

"What do I do with it?"

"You watch it. The way you watch everything else, which you do better than any child I have ever put in that yard." He got his knees under him.

"All right," Kael said.

He meant it, and the old man heard that, and something went across his face and was gone before it finished.

Severin sat back on his heels for a moment before he got up. Kael saw the hands, because he saw everything and had never once managed not to. They were not entirely steady. It was the second child the old man had mended on that sand before noon.

---

They got Elarine up at the eastern gate, sitting, her back against the timber. Somebody had brought water. She had refused to be carried.

Kael came and stood in front of her because there was nowhere else he was going.

Somebody had put her sword down beside her, and she had not looked at it once. Her hands lay in her lap with the palms turned up, the way you rest hands that have been doing one thing for a long time. She looked up at him for a while without saying anything, and there was something happening in her face that he could see with total clarity and could not read at all.

"You were not kind to me," she said.

"No."

"Good." Her voice was a ruin. She took a mouthful of the water and held it before she swallowed it. "You made me buy too many things that were not there."

"Yes."

"I knew by the fourth."

"I know," Kael said. "I saw you know."

"Then you understand that I paid for the fifth one anyway." She put her head back against the timber. "That is the part I would like explained to me at some point in my life. Not today."

Somewhere above and behind them the horns went for the next bout. The noise started up again. Neither of them looked round at it.

"I needed it," Elarine said.

Kael waited.

"I am not going to say the rest of it, because I am extremely tired and it is a great deal less interesting than people think." Her eyes came round to him. "My house chooses the school and the seat and the man. But if I had won it. Perhaps I could have proved to myself that I was still able to write my own destiny."

She said it evenly, the way she said everything, priced and set down.

"But you? The city, the glory, you did not want any of these at all," she said.

"No."

"You wanted to win."

"Yes," Kael said.

"Yes," said Elarine Sedrane. "I have known that about you since we met. It is the only thing about you I have never once had to work out."

She shut her eyes.

"Go away, Kael," she said. "Come and find me tomorrow. It will be the match you really wanted."

He had a half-smile as he went.

---

He was most of the way up the hill before he noticed he was climbing it.

The sun had gone round; the sea was doing the thing it did in the afternoon. Below him the island was full of people, and the arena sat on the headland with the noise coming off it in soft handfuls. The games went on without him. They were four bouts from the end of the second day, and tomorrow there would be four children left.

His arm was a long pink new thing that would be a scar by the turn of the year.

His capacity was a dry hole. Any more spent, and his hair would surely have turned as white as the clouds above him.

He had beaten a house on the first day and a treasury on the second, in front of every family that mattered in the empire. There was a girl sitting against a gate down there who had needed it, and who would be sent wherever her house sent her. He had taken it off her because he wanted it.

*I wanted it.*

He turned it over the whole of the last mile and could not find anything underneath it. There was nothing beneath the wanting. There never had been. Everybody he knew had a *because* standing under theirs: a house, a name, a Line, a city. He had gone down that hill this morning with nothing at all under his, and won.

Then the house came in sight. Both bundles were still by the door, and there was smoke coming off the roof-hole the way there had been every evening of his life.

He stopped on the path.

Then he went up and in, and told them all about it.


---

# Chapter Twenty-Seven — The Fog

He told them all about it, and it was the shortest telling of his life.

He gave them the road first, because that was the order it had come in: the boy who pointed with his whole arm, the man with the rope, the two words in whitewash on somebody's bed linen. His mother's chin came up at the sheet and stayed up.

He gave them the opening — both hands, three directions, the sword coming forward behind all of it. He gave them the wrap coming off his back in the middle of the empire's floor with eleven thousand people watching, and he did not put anything in front of it. His father took that without moving anything at all.

He gave them the fire coming apart on the blade. Then the fire coming apart on his hand. Then the two at once, and the arm, and sitting down on the sand because his legs had found out what he had spent.

Then he stopped.

He had meant to go on. He was fairly sure he had opened his mouth to go on. There was the old man crossing that floor at a walk and going to Elarine first, and there was a hand still on his arm and a question in it, and the whole of it was sitting behind his teeth in the shape of a conversation he had not finished having with himself.

"Tomorrow," he said instead.

Neither of them moved on it. His mother put the cup down in front of him. It was the mint off the step, and he had drunk it after every hurt of his entire life.

"It will be there in the morning," she said.

He looked up at her. In eleven years he had never once been the one who stopped.

"Eat," said Vask.

He ate about half of it and could not afterward have said what it was.

His mother got up to take the plate and did it in two parts, with a hand flat on the table in the middle, and nobody said anything about that either. When she came back she stood behind her chair instead of sitting down in it.

"It is soon," she said.

"How soon?"

"Tonight. Tomorrow. This week." She said it the way she said the result of a trial, which was the only way she had ever said anything. "He has come down. There is nothing left to work out about it."

Kael looked at the door without deciding to. Both bundles were where they had been that morning, and the morning before that, roped and squared and out of the way of the step.

"Go up," said Neris. "You fight the Vel girl in the morning."

---

He did not sleep. He lost to her.

He lost to her eleven or twelve times before the middle of the night, in the dark, with his hands out on the blanket, and every one of the losses was real, because he built them out of real material. He had a year of it. Four hundred afternoons of what she did with her feet when she meant it, what she did with them when she was only spending his time, the way she put her weight into stone before the stone had any reason to expect it.

He had everything except the thing.

Everything he had ever taken off her, he had taken once. The floor had worked once. The gap in the fourth exchange had worked once. There was a way to make her stand still that had worked exactly once, in the autumn, in the rain, and she had come back the following afternoon having solved it, and had not mentioned it, and had never given him the chance again.

Somewhere near the end of the night he stopped hunting and lay still and let himself want it, which he did not usually allow, because wanting it was not information.

Then the fog came up the mountain.

It was in the house before he was out of it. He came down the ladder into a room with grey standing in the doorway. When he put his boots on the step the split rock was not there. The sea was not there. The village was not there. There were about four strides of wet path and then there was nothing, and the whole of the world had been reduced to the sound of his father's block, which came from off to the right and could have been anywhere.

Vask was a shape and then a man.

"Off the water," he said, without stopping. "It will burn off by the third bell, or it will sit on us all day."

"Which?"

"Both are common."

His mother did not come to the door. Kael registered that, and set it down, and went.

The road was full and he could not see any of it. He heard carts. He heard a man swearing at an animal a long way off in a direction the road did not go in. Twice, people came out of the grey at him and were past before he had finished pricing them. Both times his hand had gone to the knife inside the coat before his head had any say in the matter.

They still knew him. That was the strange part. He could not see them. They could not see much of him. The word went down the road ahead of him in the fog anyway, one voice and then another, the way water finds the low ground.

He heard the arena before he saw it.

He came up the last of the rise and the building arrived out of the grey all at once, close enough to touch, full to the top rail, the sound of it standing straight up into the fog like smoke off a fire in still weather.

Two days had taken twelve of the sixteen. Every seat in that place had somebody in it.

He went in under the seating and stood in the cold with his back against the new timber, listening to eleven thousand people spend the last of the waiting on the two names they had left.

"VEL."

It came off the western side, where the houses sat, in blocks, the way men shout who have been taught to shout together.

And then, from the standing ground and the cheap seats and the seaward end where the island was:

"GHOST."

Neither of them was winning. They went back and forth over the top of him for a quarter of an hour. Under it, quieter and everywhere, ran the thing half that building had worked out for itself and had stopped bothering to pretend about: that whatever happened tomorrow was going to be a ceremony, and the thing worth having was happening this morning.

---

The fog had come into the bowl and had not left it.

It lay across the sand in a thin sheet at about the height of a man's knee. It sat over the top rail like a lid. The far side of the building was a suggestion, and the seats above him went up into it and stopped being seats and became noise.

The floor had been raked flat. There was nothing on it anywhere.

She came out of the western gate with her hair bound and both blades in her hands, and she did not look at the crowd once.

The herald had a chain and a voice built for weather.

"Valeria, of the Line of Vel. Of the Collegium of the Capital."

The building came down on the name like a wave hitting a wall.

Kael was standing four strides away with his feet already set, and he saw it happen. Something went across her face and was gone inside half a breath. Not fear. Nothing whatever to do with pleasure. It was what a man's face does when a door shuts behind him in a corridor he has walked down a thousand times.

Then it was not there, and neither was anything else, and she came to her mark.

"Kael," the herald read out, "of the Island."

The noise that came back for that one arrived from further away and lower down and went on longer. He did not listen to it. He had been given four strides and about forty seconds, and there was still a hole where his answer went.

"They are shouting for you," Valeria said.

"They're shouting for you as well."

"Yes." She did not look up. "It is not the same."

He had nothing to put beside that. Being quiet was one of the two things he had ever been good at with her.

She set her feet. Then she said, "So. Figured me out?"

He spoke the truth. There had never been any point to lie to her.

"No."

"No," she agreed. Her chin came round and the green came up at reading distance, bored with nothing, the boredom that had died against him in a yard two springs ago and had never once come back. "You have had a year."

"I know how long I've had."

"You will find it in the first minute or you will not find it." She turned the left blade over in her hand, once, the way she did. "I have thought about that a great deal."

"So have I."

"I know." And then, because she was Valeria Vel and she paid what she owed at the moment it fell due and never afterward: "It is the only thing on this island I have been unwilling to guess at."

The judge came out to the line between them with his arm down at his side.

Kael put his weight onto the front foot and everything in him went quiet, the way it had gone quiet on scree in the dark since before he could remember, and he thought: *first minute.*

The third bell went.

---

The judge's hand came up.

Eleven thousand people made the sound they had been holding since the fog.

The hand did not come down.

Something went over the top of the building. It came in high out of the grey from the seaward side, going fast and going away from him, and it was the size of a cart, and it was burning.

Every face in that arena turned with it.

It came down into the standing ground at the seaward end, into the packed part where the island sat, and the sound it made going in was not loud.

That was the first wrong thing. He had time to notice it and no time to do anything else with it. Thousands of people shouting and one soft crumping thud a hundred rows away, like a sack coming off a cart onto wet ground.

Somewhere behind him a man said, quite conversationally, "What was that?"

Then the building started screaming.

It did not start where the stone had landed. It started everywhere at once, which was the second wrong thing, and it went round that bowl faster than anything Kael had ever seen a crowd do, and by the time he had turned his head to follow it the whole seaward end was standing and moving and no longer had a shape.

The second one came over while they were still getting up.

He saw this one properly. It came out of the fog over the rim with fire going backward off it in a tail, and it went into the western curve about halfway up, into seats, and this time he heard it, and this time it did not stop when it landed. It went down through three rows of new timber and took the timber with it and stopped somewhere underneath and burned there.

"FIRE — fire in the boards, there's fire in the—"

"Sit *down*, you'll have us all—"

"Where's it coming from? Where is it coming *from*?"

A judge went past Kael at a dead run with his chain still on. Behind him, up at the rail of the host's box, somebody was shouting for the First Master over and over, screaming.

*Trebuchets,* said something in him. *Off ships. They are ranging on the building.*

He knew the range of a trebuchet. He had read it in a cold room four years ago, in a book about the reduction of fortified towns, because Priscian had left it on a shelf and he read all that was on that shelf. He knew what threw a stone that size that far and he knew what it had to be standing on to do it and he knew, standing on the raked sand of the empire's arena with his weight still forward on a front foot, exactly and precisely what was out there in the fog.

The third and the fourth came over together.

And through the seaward arch, where the world showed between the columns, the grey moved and did not close again, and there were shapes in it.

Not one. Not four.

They came out of that fog in a line that went as far along the water as the arch would let him see, and behind that line there was another one, and the near ones already had their bows in the shallows and men going over the side into the surf in numbers, and it was not a landing, it was a pouring, and the whole of it was happening in complete silence because the building was making too much noise for anything else to exist.

Then a voice from the seaward end got over the top of everything, one word, torn.

"*BOATS!*"

And after it, a great many voices at once, and none of them saying anything usable.

"—the sea, look at the *sea*—"

"How many? How many is that—"

"They're on the beach. They're already on the—"

Somebody up in the western curve began to make a single high sound and did not stop.

The crowd went.

Eleven thousand people who had come to watch two children fight got up out of their seats and tried to leave a stone bowl through four gates, and Kael stood on the sand in the middle of it.

They went over the rails first. The ones at the front, who had nowhere back to go, went over the rail onto the sand, and that was survivable and most of them survived it. Behind them the mass was moving toward the vomitories and the mass at the vomitories was not moving at all, and the pressure went through the middle of that crowd like weight going through a stack, and people began to be pushed down.

He watched a woman in a good coat go down in the second row of the northern curve and not come back up, and the row above her walked over the place where she was, because it was not walking, it was being carried.

"Get *back* — get back, she's under, she's—"

"Marun. MARUN."

A woman said that one name about nine times while the crowd took her along the rail, and each time she said it exactly the same way, as though the correct number of repetitions had simply not been reached yet.

He found Oss.

It took him about a second and a half. Three rows up, hands already going, exactly where he had been on the opening day, and he was not moving with the crowd yet because he had turned round to get a smaller boy off the bench behind him. Kael saw the hands. Kael saw the round face turned the wrong way against the flow of the whole world.

Then the north curve stood up as one thing and Oss was inside it, and Kael did not see him again.

---

The First Master came down off the host's box.

He did not go to the rail. He did not go to the stairs. He came over the front of it, dropped the height of a man onto the walkway, and went along it into the north curve, into the four hundred, against the flow. The fifth stone came over the rim while he was doing it.

It came down at the north curve. At the children.

And it stopped.

It stopped in the air over the seats with about the length of a man to spare, and it hung there. The fire on it went on burning, going nowhere. People who did not know what they were looking at looked at it anyway, and for one entire second the noise in that building went out like a lamp.

Then the stone went sideways.

It moved off the seats the way a barrow moves when a man leans on it, ordinary, unhurried, absolutely without effort. It went out over the top rail and dropped somewhere behind the building, and the ground jumped under Kael's boots when it arrived.

Nothing had shown. No light. No sound. An old man in a grey robe on a walkway with one hand out.

The next one came over eleven seconds later.

It came down into the western curve. Severin was on the wrong side of the building. It went in among people, and he did not stop it, and that was the whole of it: he could hold one. He could not hold two. There were going to be a great many more than two.

Kael watched him understand it. From the floor of the arena, at the distance of a thrown stone, through fog, he watched the First Master of the Temple of the Southern Isles stand still for about a breath and a half and price the arithmetic and take the only answer it had.

Then Severin turned his back on the sky and started getting the children out.

He went into the north curve with both arms and began moving four hundred students down the walkway toward the northern arch. The ones at the front went because he told them to. The ones behind went because the ones in front were going. It worked. It was the smallest possible thing to be doing while the sky came apart, and he did it, and he did not look up again.

Kael did not move.

He was aware, in the way you are aware of weather, that he had not moved. He was standing on the sand where the judge had left him, on his front foot, with both hands empty, in the middle of the flattest and most open ground on that headland. Every school he had was running perfectly. None of them was producing an instruction.

---

The bowl came apart at the seaward end.

The stone that did it went in low at the base of the standing ground where the timber met the stonework, and the standing ground folded downward into the space under it, four hundred people at once, and the sound of that arrived across the sand as a single soft enormous crump and then nothing, and then the fire from the fourth stone found the new timber and began to go along it under the seats, and the smoke came up through the boards in fifty places at the same time in neat little rows.

The seaward arch went with it. The northern arch had a wall of people in it that was no longer moving and no longer, in any useful sense, people. The western arch was open and burning.

"The east! The east gate's clear — the *east*—"

That left the eastern gate, and there were men standing in it.

They had come up from the harbour and they had come fast, and they were not in a formation and they were not in a hurry, and they were killing everyone who arrived in the mouth of that gate. There were maybe thirty of them. There did not need to be more. They stood in the gateway and the crowd delivered itself to them in ones and twos out of the smoke, running, with its hands empty and its back to a burning building, and the men worked.

Kael watched them work.

They were good. That was the part his hands would not stop reporting. They were not frenzied and they were not cruel about it. They stood square and they let the ground do the arriving and they took what came with the short economical strokes of men who had done a great deal of it and expected to do more before noon, and they stepped over what they had made and reset and waited for the next of it, and he could have named the school. It had a school. Somebody had taught them, one stroke at a time, beside them, longer.

They called to each other while they worked. Short, twice, and then a third time from further back in the arch.

He understood every word of it.

*Two more on the left.* And then, from a man who had stopped to wipe his hands down his own coat: *We are behind.*

In his whole life Kael had heard that language come out of exactly two mouths. One of them had taught it to him on a flat rock above the house, going slower in it than she ever went in anything else, correcting him in it long after she had stopped correcting him in the others. The other had used it once, at his own table, to say hello.

He stood on the sand and heard thirty men use it to organize a morning's work.

He saw a man take a boy off his feet with a spear butt and finish him on the ground without changing the rhythm of anything.

He saw two of them kill a household — a man, a woman, three others — in the time it took the smoke to go across the gate and come back.

He saw a man in very good clothes stop in front of them with both hands out and begin to say *I can pay*, and get most of the way through it.

He saw the fifth one, who was young, hesitate over a girl of about six and then not hesitate.

Under all of it, from somewhere in the packed dark of the northern arch where nothing was moving any more, a boy was calling for his mother, steadily, the way you call across a field.

He knew what he was looking at. Every part of it. He knew the reach of the spears and the weight in the shoulders and which of the thirty was tired and which one of them was going to be a problem and where the ground under that gate went soft. He knew that the smoke was moving left to right and would be at the eastern wall in under a minute and would blind them. He knew that eleven thousand people had come into this building and that a number he could estimate to within about two hundred were going to come out of it, and he knew the two hundred was the part he was least sure of and he could feel his mind going back to work on the two hundred, patiently, because that was what it did.

He knew his mother was six miles away with a child coming down.

He knew his father would be on the ridge above the house by now with the whole of it under him and the fog burning off it, watching men land on his island.

It all arrived at once, all of it correct, all of it useless, and the machine that had produced one clean answer at the end of every problem of his entire life produced ten thousand of them and no way whatsoever to put them in an order.

And Kael stood on the sand and stopped.

He was not frightened. He looked for it later and it was not there. It was simply that there was nothing to do first — that every single thing was first, that the girl at the gate and the smoke and the two hundred and the ridge and his mother's hand flat on the table all had exactly the same size, and that a thing with no order in it cannot be started.

Somewhere above him the western curve went over.

He heard it as furniture.

Then something took a fistful of his coat at the shoulder and turned him bodily out of where he was standing, and his back met the timber of the judges' enclosure hard enough to empty him, and Valeria Vel put her face in front of his face.

She still had both blades. There was blood on her forearm that was not hers and a burn on the side of her neck he had not seen her take.

"Kael."

He looked at her.

"*Kael!*"

She hit him. Flat hand, open, hard, across the side of the head, and it was not temper, it was the correction of a fault; she did it the way the grey coats on the sea court did it, called and delivered and finished, and the whole arena came back into him at once through the ringing.

"There," she said. "I need you."

He heard his own voice say: "Thirty at the eastern gate. The smoke will take their eyes in forty seconds. It will not last."

"How long."

"Twenty. Thirty."

"I need you to be sure, Kael. Please, fast."

Kael steadied his breath.

"Thirty."

"Then we are going in thirty." She had already turned; she was already reading the gate. "Not the harbour road. Not the coast. Inland."

"Inland is—"

"The nobles' district." She said it flat, the way she said the result of a thing she had already checked twice. "It is a mile up from the water and it is walled and it is the only wall on this island that was built to be a wall. My orders name it. They will hold it and they will hold it for days."

Behind them the fire found something under the western curve and the seats went down another course, and the heat came across the open floor and moved the fog in one long sheet.

"My mother's on the mountain," Kael said.

Valeria Vel looked at him for about a second.

"Yes," she said. "And the mountain is behind them. We must regroup with the army at the nobles' district. Please, come with me. They'll help you to the mountain afterwards, I'm sure of it."

Then the smoke came off the western fire and went across the eastern gate exactly as he had said it would, and the men in the gateway put their heads down and their arms up, and she was already moving.

---

He went with her.

He got eleven strides out onto that sand. His body took the eleven strides; his mind stayed where it was for about four of them. Then it came, and it came all the way.

The smoke shut the gate. They went through it low with two blades each and did not use them. Behind him the arena where he had been named twice fell down into itself with the noise of a hill giving way. Ahead of him a road went up off the headland into fog that had not burned off and now never would.

Every third step, he looked left, at the place where the mountain was.

There was nothing there to see. There had been nothing there to see since the middle of the night.

He went up the inland road with a girl of the Line of Vel and eleven thousand people behind him. It was the wrong direction. He had worked that out before he took the first step, and he took it anyway.


---

# Chapter Twenty-Eight — Turning Point

The road climbed.

It went up off the headland between two walls of dry stone, and it was full, and everything on it was going the same way they were. A man carrying a woman. Two men carrying a bench with somebody on it, which was slow, and which they kept doing. A girl of about ten with a fistful of another girl's sleeve, going at a speed the second girl could not do, so that the second girl came along behind her at an angle, half off her feet, and neither of them made any sound at all.

Kael went up it beside Valeria Vel with the fog closing behind them and the noise of the arena coming apart at their backs, and he did the only thing he knew how to do with a road, which was read it.

He did not like what it said.

The people thinned. That was the first of it. A road that is emptying from the front is emptying because something at the front is taking people off it.

Then the second thing, which was that nobody was coming back down.

"Soldiers ahead," he said. "Theirs."

"Yes."

"You haven't looked."

"No. You have." She had not slowed. "Say it when you have it. Not when you have finished checking it."

Four men came out of a side lane forty strides up, unhurried, with things in their hands that they had not brought with them.

They were not a formation. They had been in a house.

Valeria did not stop, did not speak, did not change her pace by any amount he could measure. Kael, who had spent hundreds of afternoons learning the exact contents of that girl's body, watched her put her weight down on the flagstone at the mouth of the lane, and felt the stone answer under his own boot from three strides away.

Then she was among them.

In the yard there had always been a shape to it — a beginning, a middle, a moment where you could see her decide. There was none of that.

She went in at a speed that had nothing to do with her legs.

The first one was still turning. She put the right-hand blade in under his jaw and out the other side of his neck, and what came out after the steel came out under pressure, and went up the dry-stone wall in a long arc, and then a shorter one, and then a shorter one, keeping time with something inside him that had not been told yet.

The second got his arm up, which was correct, and which is the only thing there is to do. The blade took the forearm off through both bones without changing speed and went on into his face.

The third was stepping back with his mouth open. She arrived at him instead of at where he had been, and put both blades in under the ribs on either side at once, and lifted, and a grown man came up off his heels with his boots off the road. Then she let go of him. He sat down in the ruts with the whole of himself in his lap and his hands out on either side of it, not touching it.

The fourth ran two strides. She took one. She put a blade through his back at the height of the heart and he ran three more before his legs were told.

That was the thing the yard had never shown him and had never once suggested. The first was on his knees with both hands up at his neck trying to hold it shut, and he was doing it wrong, and there was no right way to do it. The second was face down and still moving. The one in the ruts sat looking at his lap. And somewhere in it somebody was making a high sound with no words anywhere in it, over and over on one note, and Kael could not tell which of the four it was.

Valeria went back four strides and put a blade in at the base of the second one's skull, and the moving stopped, and she did not look at what she had done while she was doing it.

Three seconds. Perhaps four. The noise went on a good deal longer than that.

The smell came up off the road with the steam. It was iron and it was the inside of things and it was, underneath, a smell Kael had known since he was eight years old, because his mother had taught him what is under a hide on the table by the door with the light coming in, and a man opened all the way down is the same warm room as anything else opened all the way down. He had never once put those two facts beside each other. They went beside each other now and would not be separated again.

There was nothing in any of it that looked like skill. That was the part Kael could not get past afterward. Skill was what she did to him on the sea court, where every exchange had a joke in it somewhere and both of them were pretending the pretending was not there. This looked like a woman going down a row of beans.

His hands had come up. He noticed that they had come up. By the time they had, there was nothing anywhere on that road to put them on.

Valeria stepped over the second man, because he was in the way of where she was going, and reset her feet, and looked up the lane and then back down the road, in that order, and her breathing was not different.

Kael had watched thirty men do exactly that in a gateway an hour ago and had thought, standing on the sand with his hands empty, *it had a school.*

"Kael."

He looked at her.

"That was four," Valeria said. "There will be more of them and they will not always be four. When I go, come with me. Do not wait to be told which one is yours."

He had a great many things arriving at once and no order to put them in, and what came out of his mouth was the smallest of them.

"You didn't ask them anything."

Valeria Vel looked at him for about a second.

"No," she said.

---

They went up.

She set the pace and it was a hard one, and he matched it without effort and would have matched a harder one, and there was a piece of him that noticed he was pleased about that and filed the fact with a certain amount of distaste.

"Their colours," he said.

"You saw them."

"Green at the shoulder. A cord. Not all of them had it — one in three, one in four."

"Officers' men. Not officers." She said it the way she said the result of a thing she had checked twice. "That green is eastern. The dye comes out of the east and it is expensive and nobody west of the middle sea wastes it on a cord. Those are eastern troops."

"Coalition."

"Coalition is a word for eleven of them at once. Those are eastern." She went around a cart that was on its side with nothing under it. "It matters. Eastern means they came a long way and they did not come to hold anything, because you do not hold a thing at that distance. They came to do a job and go."

Kael turned it over.

For four years a fat man in ink-stained cuffs had taught the reduction of fortified towns to boys who would never reduce one, and Kael had read every book on that shelf because it was on that shelf. None of it had ever once been about anybody he could see.

"They speak the downhill tongue," he said.

Valeria's head came round.

"You understood them."

"All of it. They were saying where the left was and that they were behind."

She was quiet for four strides.

"Then you will walk on my left," she said, "and you will tell me every word you hear, and you will not wait until you have decided whether it is important."

That was the whole of it.

She did not ask him. She did not slow down and turn and put the question in the middle of the road where he would have had to stand in it — *and how does a boy off a mountain come to have the enemy's language in his mouth?* She took the fact, and she priced it, and she put it to work, and went on up the hill.

"Valeria."

"Say it."

"How far is it, from the wall to the mountain road."

The pause was not long. It was the length of a person deciding to answer the question that had been asked instead of the one underneath it.

"Four miles. Perhaps five, the way you would have to go now."

"And the wall. How long does it hold."

"I have no idea."

"You said days."

"I said it on the sand to a boy who was not standing in his own head, and I have not been given one new fact since." Valeria went up over the broken part of the road where the ruts crossed and did not offer him a hand and did not look to see whether he needed one. "Nobody was told this was coming. There is no plan for it. What I have is that it is cut stone, and it is high, and it stands on ground with nothing near it worth standing on, and every armed man left alive on this island is going to arrive at it before noon, because there is nowhere else on this island to arrive at."

"Your orders name it."

"My orders name it because somebody once sat in a room and wrote down the only defensible place on a small island, and then went to his dinner. That is the whole of what an order like that is. It does not know what day it is." She did not look round. "I know what you are doing. Stop."

"I'm not doing anything."

"You are working out whether I was right, and you cannot, because you do not have enough, and you are going to go on not having enough all the way up this hill." She said it flatly and without unkindness, which was worse. "I told you they would help you to the mountain and I said I was sure of it. I am not sure of it. I said it because you were standing on that sand and you were not there."

Kael said nothing.

*I'm sure of it* was not a fact. He had known that when she said it. He had known it the way he knew a claim was untested the moment it left anybody's mouth, because his mother had built the machine that knew it and had been running the machine on him since he was four years old, and he had heard the flaw in the sentence and had taken the sentence anyway.

He had wanted to be told. It was the only time in his life he could remember wanting to be told.

They came up onto the flat and the fog thinned and there was a lane going off to the right between two low walls, and at the mouth of it there had been a post.

---

He had seen these lights from the mountain every night of his life.

He had counted them in the dark from a doorway with his father's hand on the top of his head. He had come down past them on the lawful road twice a day for years, high above, within sight and sound, never through, and he had looked away from them so many thousands of times that looking away had stopped being a thing he did and had become a thing he was.

He was in them now.

The lane was narrow. It went down, then up. Smoke came along it at knee height and then lifted, and there was fire in three of the roofs on the left, one on the right; one of the three was going to take the next along the row, and nobody was going to stop it.

He saw a man sitting up against a doorframe with both hands in his lap, holding the place where his stomach was. Kael's mother had taught him what sits under a hand in that position, and how long it takes. He knew going past at a run that the man had somewhere between one hour and four, and that nothing anyone did would change it.

He saw a woman face down across her own step with the back of her head opened. That one had been quick. It had come from behind, and she had not known.

He saw a man on his side with his leg off above the knee, a belt round the stump that somebody had put there properly — wound, turned, tied — the man grey and shaking and alive, done by somebody who knew how. Kael went past him, and the arithmetic went with him: *that one lives if he is carried in the next hour.* There was nobody to carry him.

That was the part. Not the leg, not the head. He went past the man who could be saved, and the machine gave him the number, and the number was useless, and his legs went on doing what they had been told. Somewhere very far back, a faculty nobody in his family had ever built or trained or mentioned once put out a hand and touched him, and was ignored.

He saw the man with the coil of rope.

He was in the ditch on the right with his hat still on. Yesterday he had stepped into Kael's path on the road down to the arena, and Kael's whole body had gone to work before his head had any say in it, and what the man had done was take the hat off.

Then the boy of about six.

He was not sure at first. Then he was, because of the sleeve. A boy of about six had taken hold of his mother's skirt on that road and pulled it twice and pointed at Kael with his whole arm. His mother had looked. The man beside her had looked. Then the cart had stopped, and the whole road had found out what was on it, and he had been the first person on this island ever to point at Kael for a reason that was not a warning.

He was under the wall on the left. There was a great deal of blood.

Kael kept his eyes from staying too long on that thing no longer the shape of a boy, and moved past him.

---

They came up out of the lane into the wider part where the wells were. There were between fifteen and twenty of them working the houses on both sides.

"There's no way around this. Kael, are you ready?" Valeria said.

Kael nodded.

He went with her.

Here is what the next part was, and there is no arrangement of it that makes it into anything.

Valeria took the right and the middle. Kael took her left, the wall, the doorways.

One of them saw them come out of the lane and did not stop what he was doing.

*Two strays,* he said, over his shoulder. *Off the left.*

*How small?*

*Small.*

Kael heard every word of it.

*Please,* he said. *Let us pass.*

It came out in his mother's accent, with the ends of the words where she had put them.

The nearest man turned round to see what had spoken.

As soon as he did, Kael saw her:

"Val, WAIT, we can—"

Valeria opened his throat.

"There's no time Kael. Now, get ready, here they come."

He was faster than every single one of them.

It was not enough, because they were men. Not one of them was under thirty and not one of them was under twelve stone, and a boy of thirteen who is quicker than a grown man is still a boy of thirteen the moment the grown man gets a hand on him. They kept getting hands on him. He went into a wall twice inside the first minute. One of them picked him up off the ground entirely, by the coat, one-handed, the way you lift a chair, and Kael only came down again because he opened the arm that was holding him.

Valeria they could not touch. Two of them tried it in that lane. Neither of them used the hand again.

There were exactly fifteen of them. His count had never stopped.

He got his blades up under one and turned it, and the man behind that one had a hand in his coat and hauled, and Kael went off his feet.

He was on the stones. There was a boot coming, and behind the boot a second man with a hooked thing raised, and there was no roll that came out of that and no lane to take.

He put earth into his own forearm along a line about two fingers wide, and let the hook come down on it.

It went through his sleeve, pierced the skin but did not go into the bone.

The man looked at his hook. Then at the arm. Then at Kael's face.

*Resonants,* he said.

He did not shout it. He said it the way a man says a thing he has just worked out and would rather not have. Then he said it again with the whole of his chest, and it went up that lane and round the corner of it, and at the far end of it men stopped what they were doing to people and turned round.

Two of them went backward before they went forward.

He came up off the stones inside the man's arms, and put his short blade through the top of his thigh and turned it and took it out.

It came out ahead of the blood by about half a second. Then the blood came, and it did not run, it pumped, and it went across the stones in a sheet that was already wider than a man when Kael's second foot left the ground.

His right-hand blade was now carrying lightning.

It showed. It had always showed. White, running the length of the steel. He held it, and let it stay, and every man in that lane could see exactly what was in his hand, because there was no longer one single person on this island he was hiding it from.

Four years of putting it away. It took him no time at all to stop.

It went through the haft of the hook-man's friend's spear and did not slow down for the wood, or for the arm behind the wood, and Kael felt the whole of it go as one weight — the way a blade goes through a thing that has been cooked — and had the man's forearm on the stones before he had finished deciding to cut.

Kael put the thread back down the arm and into the blade and went at the next one, and kept that edge off their steel, and never once let it touch theirs.

He was taking hands and knees and shoulders.

It was slower, and it kept putting him inside the reach of men he could have finished from outside it, and there was nothing clever in it. Killing them would have been faster. It would have been easier. He had known how since he was six years old.

He did not want to do it.

There was no argument underneath that. Nobody had ever put the question to him — not on the mountain, not in the yard, not once in his life — and it had been sitting in him the whole time without anybody noticing, including him, and it stood up now in a lane in the middle of the worst hour of his life and would not move.

He was frightened, and not of the fifteen men.

Then Valeria stopped moving.

She did that perhaps four times in the whole of that morning, and each time it was worse than the moving. She put both hands down at her sides and set her feet and the lane came up.

It came up in a column the thickness of a man's chest — packed earth and old stone and four hundred years of people walking on it — and it took the one standing over the well through the middle of him and did not stop where he stopped. It carried him up to the height of the roofs and left him there.

Then the next one, three strides further on, and the next after that, walking up that lane ahead of her in a line. The ones that did not catch a man threw him into a wall, and the wall did the rest.

Kael had spent eight weeks of his life learning to put a thread into two fingers' width of his own forearm.

She was rearranging the road.

He priced it while he worked, because he could not stop pricing things, and the price was honest and he did not enjoy any part of arriving at it: there was no version of him, at any age, with any amount of study, that could do what she did.

And it was not the purse's fault. He had looked. He had spent a year hunting for the place where Valeria Vel was only gifted, turning her over the way he turned over everything, and there was no such place in her. She was gifted, yes. But she was much more. She had the body. She had the capacity. She had the control to stop a killing stroke at exactly one finger, and he knew the width of it because she had stopped one at exactly one finger on him. And on top of all of that she was the first person onto that sea court every morning of the year and the last one off it, with nobody waiting for her at either end.

---

Too many of them. He had known it since the second minute.

A cut came down from the top. He was late. His shoulder took it.

Two more off the left. He got the first on the blade. The second was a haft and it came in under his ear and put him into the wall.

Up. Turn. A spear at his belly — off it, inside it, and the man's knee went out from under him and Kael was past.

Another cut. Late again.

He was slower than he had been at the mouth of that lane, and he knew by how much, because he always knew by how much.

A hand closed on his right-hand blade at the guard. Something came down across his wrist. The blade went out of his fingers and across the stones and under the lip of the well.

He put the left one into a knee. It stuck. There was no time, and he let it go.

Nothing in either hand.

And a man in front of him with a short-hafted axe already coming down, and it was a good stroke, and it was going to land, and there was no ground behind him and no room to either side.

And Kael knew, in the way he had always known things, all at once and completely: *there is no other way to do this.*

He screamed.

There is no record of what it was. It had no words in it. It came up out of a boy who had been taught to be quiet before he could walk, whose whole childhood was built out of not making a sound on scree in the dark, and it went up that lane over the top of everything, and thirty strides away Valeria Vel turned her head, which she did not do for anything, all morning, except that.

He put the thread into his left hand.

Shoulder, arm, hand — and then nowhere left to go, because there was no blade on the end of it any more, which had happened once before in his life, in front of eleven thousand people, on the day this same hand was burned down to the meat.

The white came up along the edge of it and sat there, on new skin, in the fog, in a lane, lighting nothing.

Then he put it through the man's chest.

The lesson was four years old and it had never once changed.

*Not the body. The carrying.*

He went to the body. He went to the middle of the body, where his mother had shown him the pump sits, on a table, with a hare, when he was eight years old and had asked why the blood came out of that one in a jet and out of the others in a run.

It came out in a jet.

It came up his arm to the elbow and it was hot in a way he had not been ready for, hotter than the inside of a person has any business being, and it kept coming.

And he felt it stop.

That was the part. Not the going in. He had his hand inside a man and the man's heart was working against the back of it — and then it did not, and Kael was still there, and there was nowhere in the world to put his hand down.

A man is heavy. He is heavy in a way a waster and a sack and a goat are not, because he is heavy and he is also *holding on*. Then he stops holding on, and becomes heavy in a completely different way, and the change happens in the hand.

The man's face was a foot from his face. He was young. Not as young as Kael and not old, and there was surprise on him, which Kael could read perfectly, and nothing else on him at all, which Kael could also read perfectly and had no use for.

Kael's own face was doing something.

He could not tell what. He went for it the way an old man had told him to go for it on that white sand less than a day ago — *you watch it, the way you watch everything else* — and he reached for his own face and there was nothing there to reach for. He could not feel it. He had no idea what his mouth was doing. It might have been doing anything.

He had been taught what a body is. The weight of its parts, the order they come in, which of them mend and which do not, and where to put a blade in each of them and why.

Nobody had ever told him that when you take the man out, nothing is missing.

Everything was still in there. All of it, in the quantities his mother had taught him, in the order she had taught him, and he checked, because he could not stop himself checking. What had gone had never had a weight at all. There was no place anywhere in eleven years of instruction to put it, and he stood in a lane holding what was left, which was all of it, and it was not the same and it was never going to be the same again.

Then the weight went off his arm and down, and Kael got his hand back and stood in the lane with it out in front of him, open, not touching anything.

"KAEL."

He turned round.

Everything was where it had been. The well, the slab, Valeria, the fifteen who were now nine or ten. No time had passed. The whole of it — the scream, the weight, the arithmetic he would be doing for the rest of his life — had cost about six seconds, and the world had not waited even that long. Two of them were coming down the lane at her back and she had her hands full.

He went.

---

After the first one they were not hard.

That was the thing, and he turned it over a great deal afterward, in the flat unhurried way he turned over everything, and never found any part of it that was difficult. The first one had taken everything he had and a sound he had never made in his life. The second one took a step and a half. He did the second one from behind, correctly, with the short blade, in the place he had been taught to put it when he was eleven years old on a mountain in the dark by a man who had said *here, and then here, and do not be anywhere near his hands when you do it*, and it worked exactly as described, because everything that man ever told him worked exactly as described.

He went under the lip of the well and got the right-hand blade back, and the left one out of the knee it was still in, and killed the third one going past, across the front of the throat, and did not break stride, and did not see him land.

He could hear his own heart. It had never beat so fast in his life.

He put the fourth one under the slab.

He came round the well and took the fifth one's spear at the shaft and went up it, which was the column — the same solve as the column, the thing he had done to a Drusane in front of eleven thousand people two days ago. The man had no more idea what to do about it than Lucan Drusane had. But there is no yard-master standing at the end of a lane, and there is no rule there about the shoulders, and Kael went up that shaft and put the point of the right-hand blade in through the man's eye and most of the way to the back of his head, and had it out and was turning before the spear had finished coming down.

He noticed that one less than he had noticed the second.

That was all he ever had to say about it afterward, and it was the part that frightened him.

And the cold voice counted.

It had counted at the settlement when he was nine and it had counted two, and it counted now, and it did not care what it was counting, and it was going to be there when he stopped and it was going to have a number.

He did not stop.

*There is a thing nobody tells you about the first one, which is that you may remember deciding. I have heard grown men say otherwise — that their hands did it, that they came back to themselves afterward and it was already done, that there was no one home at the time. Mine was not like that. I know the moment. I know what was in me when I made it, which was not rage and was not fear and was nothing anybody would put in a song: it was that I wanted to go home, and he was standing in the way of it. I have looked for a version of that morning in which I do something else, the way my mother taught me to look at a thing until it gives up what it is, and there is not one. That is not the same as being forgiven. It took me a long while to stop confusing the two.*

---

They came out at the top of the village with nine or ten behind them and nobody following, because there was nothing left in that lane to follow with.

Valeria went about thirty strides up the open road and then stopped, on purpose, in the middle of the ground where she could see three ways, and stood, and breathed, and did not sit down.

Kael stood beside her with his hands hanging.

He looked at the left one.

It was new skin. Severin had put it back on him yesterday at the eastern gate, in front of the empire, and told him nature is not tidy, it is only quick. It had been pink and clean at first light this morning.

There was a great deal on it now, and it had gone into the creases where the fingers folded and dried there, and it was not coming out without water and time, and there was going to be no water and no time.

His father had taken his knife off the wall in the autumn and turned it and put it into his hand hilt first and told him the knife was the smallest part of it. He had not said what the largest part was. Kael had assumed, in the way you assume a thing at twelve, that the largest part was carrying it properly and not being seen with it.

It was still inside his coat. He had not once thought of it.

"Sit if you want to," said Valeria.

"I won't."

"Your hands are shaking."

"I know."

"I understand," she said. "Kael. You had no choice. Those men were not people. You can't see it as such. We have a duty to live. We owe it to ourselves, and the empire."

He looked at her.

She had blood to the elbow on both arms, a cut on her jaw, her hair out of its binding on one side. She was thirteen years old. She stood in the middle of a crossroads on the island where she had spent one year of her life, breathing, in the manner of a person who has done a piece of work and expects to do more before noon.

"How many is it for you," Kael said.

"Today?"

"Ever."

Valeria Vel looked out along the road toward the water, where the fog had gone and the smoke had come.

"I do not have a number," she said. "There was no first one. My house does not do it that way. They take you to see it before you can do it, so that when you can do it there is nothing about it left to find out." She put her hair back off her face with the back of a wrist because the hand was not fit for it. "There were four years of watching before I was ever given anything sharp. Executions, mostly. My father stood behind me for the first of those with both hands on my shoulders so I could not turn my head, and afterward he told me I had done well, and I had not done anything at all."

She said it exactly as she said everything, priced and set down, and it was a long time before Kael understood he had been handed something enormous.

"They did it right," she said. "It is the correct way to raise a Vel. I would not change any of it."

Kael's mother had taught him what a lie is shaped like from the inside. He stood in the road and could not tell, and knew he could not tell, and understood for the first time in his life that this was going to keep happening to him for as long as he lived.

"You were slow on your feet, but," Valeria said. "In the lane. You did not stop moving and you did not stop being useful and I do not believe you noticed you were doing it. That is all right. It is what it is supposed to do the first time." She turned. "It will not do it the second time and you will notice *that* instead, and it will be worse. Come on."

---

They came up the last of it and the fog was gone off the high ground and the wall was there.

It was where she had said it was and it was what she had said it was. The walls were a mile up from the water on the flat shoulder of the ground with nothing near it that could be stood on, and it was cut stone and it was high, and there were men on the top of it who had been put there by somebody who knew where to put men.

It went round the whole of the district in a shape you could not see the end of from the road. There was one gate on this side.

And in front of the gate was the island.

Kael came up over the last of the rise and stopped, and it was his legs that stopped and not any decision of his.

He had believed he knew what a great many people was. He had stood on the floor of a building with eleven thousand of them banked up over his head and roaring, twice, and had gone home afterward and found words for it at his mother's table.

This was not that. This had no edges to it. It began two hundred strides short of the wall and went back down the road and off both sides of it, into the terraces and the ditches and the olive ground, and over the shoulder of the hill and out of sight, and there were people standing in it who could not see the wall and did not know where the gate was.

The sound was not shouting. It was past shouting. It was the noise a very great many people make when they have all been making it for an hour: low, and broken, and going nowhere.

He tried to count it.

He took the front of it and about four hundred strides of the near side, the way he had been taught, and then the number came apart in his hands.

That had never once happened to him. He had counted an army onto a shingle beach for a week out of pure habit. He had counted a dead post off the stones in four seconds, nine and one older one. He had counted, that morning, without wanting to and without being able to stop.

He could not count this.

These people were not whole. The sheer scale of desperation. The screams for help. The blood, the smell, the noise.

He filed it all and did not take it any further, because behind him the island was burning, and in front of him was the only wall on it that had ever been built to be a wall, and because there was a road on the far side of this district that went four miles to the mountain road, and up it to his mother, and his father, and a child that had not been born yet.

"There," said Valeria Vel. "I told you. We did it."

"We did," said Kael.

They went down toward the gate.


---

# Chapter Twenty-Nine — The Gate

They came down off the rise into the back of it.

From up there it had looked like ground — a thing you could cross, given time and a line to take. Eleven strides in, the road stopped being a road. It became a surface that had people standing on it, shoulder to shoulder and back to chest, and the forward you got was the forward the bodies in front of you gave up, which was none.

He had never been inside people before.

His father had taught him crowds the way he had taught him scree and gorse and wet rock, which was as terrain, from above, at a distance, with a stick in the dirt. *Where does the weight go. Where is it thin. What is it standing on.* Nothing in any of that had mentioned the smell, or the heat coming off the backs of strangers in the cold, or what it is like to have four people touching you at once who do not know they are doing it.

His hand went inside the coat and found the knife and stayed there. He made it come out. It went back twice more before he stopped noticing.

"Kael."

"I'm here."

"Stay there."

Then the crowd changed, all down its length, the way a field of barley changes when the wind arrives at the far end of it.

He turned round.

He could not see the road below them; there were too many heads. He could see what the heads were doing. From somewhere back down the hill a piece of information was coming up through thirty thousand people, and it was travelling faster than anybody could walk, and it was arriving as a physical event — a lean, and then a press, and then a sound that had no words at the front of it and picked some up on the way.

"—coming up. They're coming up the road—"

"How far? *How far?*"

"Move. MOVE. Get *up* there—"

"There's nowhere to go, you stupid—"

Kael did the arithmetic while people came apart around him. The lane in the village was four hundred strides below the flat. He had come up it himself inside the hour. Fifteen men had needed a lane and a well to hold two children, and there were not fifteen coming.

"Twenty minutes," he said. "Perhaps thirty. They'll take the road, not the terraces. The road is faster and there's nothing on it to worry about."

Valeria was already looking at the wall.

"Then we go now."

"You can't go through this."

"I can."

"You can go through it," Kael said. "It won't be there afterwards."

She stopped, which cost her something to do, and looked at him properly for the first time since the top of the hill.

There was a version of this where she went up that road the way she had gone up the lane, and everything in front of her moved or was moved, and Kael had watched her do it to grown men with weapons an hour ago. Nobody standing between her and that wall had a weapon. Most of them had a child or a bundle or another person's arm.

"Show me," she said.

So he took her through it.

He had spent eleven years learning ground, and the ground was people, and it worked. The seams were where the seams always are: behind anything solid, because a cart or a wall or a fallen animal makes a pocket downstream of itself; along the ditch lip, where the footing is bad and the crowd thins out of ordinary caution; wherever somebody big had stopped moving, because a stopped man is a stone in a river and there is always slack behind a stone. He went low and he went sideways and he went at angles that were not toward the gate at all, and every one of them bought ten strides that going straight would not have bought.

He talked the whole way, which he had never done in his life.

"Excuse me. Excuse me. Coming past you — thank you — coming past—"

They let him. That was the part he had not expected and had no time to look at. He was a boy of thirteen with blood to the elbow and two short blades across his back, and people looked down at him and made a space that was not there a moment before, and most of them said nothing, and one man put a hand under his arm and lifted him bodily over a low wall and set him down and turned away without waiting to be thanked.

Twice he lost her and found her by the sound of the crowd rearranging itself.

Twice a woman asked him something and he had gone eight strides before he understood it had been a question.

Two hundred strides short of the wall the crowd stopped being a crowd and became a face of people, packed hard, pressed against the last of the open ground and not going anywhere, because the last of the open ground was where the men on the wall had put a line of spears and told everybody to stand back.

The wall went up out of it, cut stone, higher than the arena rail, with men on the top of it standing in the ordinary bored way men stand on walls.

The gate was shut.

It had been shut for some time. You could see that from how the people at the front had settled — nobody at a gate that has just closed sits down.

"They said they'd hold it," Kael said. "You said they'd hold it. Not shut it."

"Yes."

"Val."

"I heard you."

They got to the spear line the way he had got them everywhere else, and then the spear line was the end of it.

Valeria did not argue with the man in front of her. She stepped out of the press into the strip of open ground where nobody was allowed to be, which took a certain amount of doing, and she stood in it, and she let herself be seen.

She was thirteen years old and covered to the elbows and her hair was out of its binding on one side, and she stood in front of a wall of the empire holding two blades she had not put away since the arena, and she did not say a word.

It took about four seconds.

"On the left. *On the left* — that's Valeria Vel."

Somebody up on the wall said something to somebody else. A head went away and came back. The crowd behind them heard the word go along the top of the wall and did what a crowd does with a rumour of a door opening, which is lean.

The spear line dropped its points and leaned back into it and held, and the noise came up out of the ground and off the stone and there was a while where Kael could not hear anything at all.

Then the gate opened.

It opened the width of a person. Not a gate opening — a bar coming off and a leaf coming back about eighteen inches and stopping, with men behind it and their shoulders in it, and the whole weight of that hillside arriving against the front of the spear line in the same second.
