The judge came out into the gap.

He was exactly as he had been in a corridor at the Temple two springs ago and in a judges' enclosure two days ago: broad, comfortable, grey at the temples, wearing the morning like a man who had come out to see about a delivery. Nothing about him hurried. Behind him, inside, there was light on stone and the ordinary sound of an ordinary street.

"Valeria Vel," he said. "Come through, please."

"There is a boy with me. It's Kael. He studies at Temple too."

"I know. Come through."

"He comes through with me."

"He does not." Halden said it the way you say a fact that has already been checked. He had raised his voice enough to carry and no further. "Look at what is behind you, and then look at this gap. It is the width of one person. It is going to be the width of one person for exactly as long as it takes you to walk through it, and then it is going to be shut, because if it is ever the width of two, thirty thousand people come through it and this wall falls at noon instead of tonight."

"Then let them through."

"No."

"There are *children* out here—"

"Yes," said Halden. "There are children in here as well. Come through, please, Miss Vel. I am going to ask you twice."

She did not move.

Kael saw the exact moment it happened, and he was the only person on that hillside who had the frame to read it, because he had spent a year of afternoons learning the contents of that girl's body and he knew what all of it meant.

Her chin came up. Her weight went down onto the front foot, the way it went down before she took a lane apart. And then something came off her face that Kael had never once seen there — not in a duel, not on the sand, not with a blade of his going into her; not when the herald read her name and the door shut behind her in the corridor.

Valeria Vel was angry.

"I was *sent* here," she said, and her voice did something. "My orders name this district. My orders name this *wall*. I have walked six miles with him and he took me through that" — the blade came up and swept the whole hill, thirty thousand people, and came back down — "and you will open it eighteen inches for a boy who weighs eight stone. You know what he is. You *entered* him. Your name is on his line."

"I did."

"Then open it."

"No," said Halden, and there was no more edge in it than there had been in any of the rest.

"I am not coming in."

"You are."

"I am *not*—"

He came out of the gap and took her by the arm above the elbow.

She set herself. Kael saw her do it — the weight going down into the stone, the thing she did, the thing that had held against grown men in a lane inside the hour and against four hundred years of packed road under her feet.

It did not matter.

She came through the gap the way a chair comes across a floor. She got one blade round and Halden had the wrist of it in his other hand before it was anywhere, and he was talking to her the whole time in a voice like a warm room, and none of it was cruel, and he did not appear to be hurrying.

"KAEL—"

He was moving before he decided to. He got to the gap. He got a hand on the edge of the leaf.

"Sir." He had never once called anybody sir in his life. It came out anyway. "Sir. Please. My mother is on the mountain."

Halden looked at him.

There were men putting their shoulders into the leaf and a girl of the Line of Vel being carried backwards up a lit street with her boots off the ground, and thirty thousand people arriving against a line of spears twenty feet behind him, and the judge stood in the gap and gave Kael the whole of his attention, which is what he had always done.

"I know," he said.

"She's having a child." Kael heard his own voice climb and could not get underneath it. "Four miles. The road on the other side is four miles to the mountain road and I can run it. I'm not asking to stay. I'll go straight through and out the other side and you can put a man on me the whole way, I don't care, I'll—"

"Kael."

"—I'll go out the north side, you don't even have to open this again, I can go over from the inside, there's nothing on the north side, I *looked*—"

"Kael."

He stopped.

"The road on the other side of this district," Halden said, "has been closed since the second hour. There is nothing on it going anywhere. If I put you inside this wall you will be inside this wall until it falls or until it doesn't, and it will not be four miles, it will be as long as it lasts. That is the truth and I am not going to dress it up for you. There is no version of this where you get to the mountain through here."

The leaf came back another inch under the men's shoulders.

"The First Master." Kael got it out fast, both hands on the wood now. "The First Master will stand for me. Send for him. He's — he was at the arena, he's coming here, everyone's coming here, he *promised*—" and it was out of his mouth before he heard it, and hearing it did not stop it, "—he said I wasn't standing in it by myself. Not me, and not my house."

Something went across the judge's face and did not stay.

"Yes," said Halden. "He would."

"Then send for him."

"He is not here."

"Then *wait*—"

"Kael." Halden took a breath. "I have nothing to give you. I am not going to pretend I am sorry in a way that costs me nothing, and I am not going to ask you for anything. Get off the road before they reach it. Go high and go east and do not be on any road at all."

"*Please.*"

The gate shut. The bar went across on the other side of it, and it was it.

---

He stood in front of it for a while.

Then he looked up, because there were people on the wall and one of them might be somebody, and that is what a boy does.

There were about forty men on that stretch. Behind them, he spotted some of his fellow students. And among them, Elarine Sedrane.

He saw her for about as long as it takes to see somebody. She was three heads along from a boy taller than the men beside him, with dark-gold hair. Both of them were looking down into thirty thousand people the way you look into thirty thousand people, which is at all of it and none of it.

Then one of them stopped.

Kael could not see her face. He was a hundred and forty strides out and below, with smoke coming across from the west, and he could not have told you at that distance whether her eyes were open. He knew it was her. She knew it was him. That was the whole of the information and there was not going to be any more of it, because the crowd came forward against the spears again, the man beside Kael went sideways into him and took him four strides down the hill, and when he got his feet back the piece of wall he had been looking at had different people on it.

He tried to look for them, and failed.

They were in and he was out.

That was all he had, and he took it, and what he built out of it was finished before he had gone twenty strides.

Whatever there had been between the three of them, it was weaker than that gate.

It was weaker than those walls.

He was glad they were alive. He found that out about himself in the middle of it and it did not change anything else.

---

Nobody at the wall had said a number, so he made one.

He took a section of the near ground where he could see the edges of it — from the ditch to the second olive terrace, about forty strides by thirty — and he counted a strip of it properly, multiplied, and checked the multiplication against a second strip because his mother had made him check every number he had ever produced in his life. Then he ran it back down the road and out over the shoulder of the hill by eye, allowing for the ground he could not see and being honest about it.

It came out between thirty-one and thirty-six thousand.

Then he continued.

Twenty minutes to the flat, which was now perhaps twelve. The road came up onto the shoulder in one place. The crowd began two hundred strides short of the wall and ran back down the hill for a quarter of a mile. There was no third gate. The terraces went up to the north and ended against the district's own wall; the ditches to the south went down into the olive ground and the olive ground went down to the cliff path and the cliff path went to the water, where there were ships.

He came to the answer he wanted to find the least.

He had found it, and it arrived complete, the way findings did to him. Not *it will be difficult* and not *many will not get out.* There was no arrangement of that ground, those numbers and that much time that got thirty-one thousand people off this hill, and there never had been.

He was the only person sitting on that hillside who knew it.

He looked at what was around him while the knowledge sat there being useless. A man with a bandaged hand had gone to sleep against his own knees. Two women were arguing quietly about whether a third woman should be made to drink something. Somebody's grandmother was being fanned with a hat. Further up, four men had got a plank out of a fence and were carrying somebody on it. They had been at it for an hour, and were going to go on, because putting him down was not a thing they were going to do.

None of them were doing anything wrong. That was the part. They were doing the correct things. They were queuing at a gate, and looking after each other, and waiting to be let in, which is what you do at a wall your own side built.

"You're bleeding, love."

He looked up.

She was somewhere past fifty. She had a shawl, one bundle, nothing else at all, and she was pointing at his shoulder with two fingers, at a cut he had taken at the wells and not looked at since.

"It's not mine," Kael said, which was true of most of him and not of that.

"It's yours." She got down beside him with the noise people that age make getting down, and she took the corner of the shawl off her own shoulders, and she wet it out of a stoppered bottle that was the only thing in the bundle worth anything, and she cleaned it. She did it briskly and not very well. She talked the whole time about somebody called Ilva who was not there and who she was quite sure had gone to her sister's.

She did not know who he was.

That was the thing he could not put anywhere. There was no house in it, no name in it, nothing to be got out of it. She had a bottle with about a third of a pint in it, she was going to be on this hill in twelve minutes, and she spent some of it on a boy she had never seen.

"There," she said. "Now don't pick at it."

"Thank you."

"Where's your mother?"

"On the mountain," Kael said.

"Well." She patted his knee and got up. "You want to get on, then."

He got up too. It came out of him with no shape on it at all, because he had spent the last quarter of an hour establishing that there was nothing to be done and had not once thought of saying any of it out loud.

"They're coming up the road. They'll be on this hill in ten minutes and that gate is not going to open again. Go down the ditch on the south side, get into the olive ground, keep going, and don't stop at the cliff path."

She looked at him for a moment with the bottle still in her hand.

"That's kind of you, love," she said.

Then she put the bottle away in the bundle, worked her way four steps back into the press, to the place where she had been standing before, and stood in it.

He watched her do it. He had given her the only thing he owned that was worth anything, correct to the minute and checked twice, and she had thanked him for it the way you thank a child, and gone back to her place, because the gate was at the front of it.

---

He went east.

He went the way Halden had told him to go.

Getting out of that crowd was easier than getting into it. Everything was pointed at the gate; nothing was pointed away from it, and a body moving against a stationary mass is not fighting anything.

He got up onto the high ground and the whole of it was underneath him.

The wall. The road up from the village with its two dry-stone sides. The crowd, which from up here had gone back to looking like ground. The village behind and below, burning in four places now, not three. The headland beyond that with the smoke standing off it. The sea past the headland with the ships on it.

And on the road, coming up out of the village onto the flat, small and unhurried and in no formation at all, the front of it.

Then he turned the other way, to look at what he was walking into.

The ground east of the district went up in terraces to the olive line, broke into scrub and goat ground, and beyond that, standing in the haze with its top in cloud, was the mountain.

It was not empty.

He counted eleven fires, and then stopped counting them and began reading them instead, because a fire out there was a house, and nobody puts a house down at random. The low ones stood where the water stood. The ones going up the shoulder stood along a track. Eleven houses, spread across four miles of ground that had perhaps thirty on it, which meant they had been at it for about an hour and were working from the water inland.

And between the fires, in ones and twos and fours, going along the field walls the way water goes along a gutter, there were men.

They were not marching. They were finishing scraps.

On the eastern water there were more boats coming in.

He stood on the terrace wall with his hands hanging and read the ground between himself and his mother, because reading ground was what he did, and it read the same the whole way to the haze.

Everything from here on was through them. There was no line to take that was not through them. He looked for one for a long time, because looking for one was also what he did, and the ground did not offer him anything, and he was going to go anyway.

Behind him, the faculty nobody in his family had ever built or trained or mentioned once in eleven years put out a hand.

He knew every one of the arguments. He had made them all sitting against that ditch wall and every one of them was correct. He could not fight three hundred men. He could not move thirty-one thousand people. He could not open a gate that masons had built specifically so that men could not open it. Nothing he did on this hill changed one single thing that was going to happen on it, his mother was six miles off with a child coming down.

All of it was true. He turned it over twice looking for the flaw, and there was no flaw in it, and he went anyway with the thing still holding on to his sleeve.

He put the mountain in front of him and started.

The sound behind him changed about four minutes later.

He did not turn round. He had known since the ditch wall what it was going to sound like, because he had done that arithmetic too, unasked, the way he did all of them.

What he had not done, having never in his life held the information required for it, was work out how long it would go on.

It went on.

It did not arrive in a piece. It came the way rain comes onto a roof, with no part of it being the loud part, and every so often something came up over the top of it that had one person inside it.

And the cold voice, which had counted two in a settlement yard when he was nine and five in a lane before noon and did not care in the slightest what it was counting, started on the hillside behind him.

Thirty-one thousand. Thirty-six at the outside. Gone. Forever.


---

# Chapter Thirty — The Houses

He went off the high ground on the north side, where the terraces started, and he went over the first wall at the corner.

He went over flat, on his front, and came down in the dead grass on the other side and stayed there for a count of thirty with his cheek against the ground.

Wind in the scrub. A goat bell somewhere north, still working.

The other thing, behind him, which was not going to stop.

He got up and went on.

There were two ways up off that flat and he stood behind the third wall and priced them both, and hated the answer before he had finished getting it.

The track ran along the shoulder from the corner of the district and joined the goat ground in a mile and a half. It was made ground. It was the fast way and it was in the open for the whole of it, and from anywhere on it a man on a field wall could see a boy moving on it for as long as he cared to look.

The other way went north first. Away. Up the field walls in the wrong direction for the better part of a mile, then east under the olive line where the terraces gave a man a shadow to be in, then down into the runnel that came off the shoulder and up the bed of it. Three miles for one. Two of the three under something.

He took the walls.

There was a trough at the corner of the second field with the rim gone green, fed off a pipe out of the bank. He put his head down and drank until he could not hold any more, and then stood over it and made himself drink again.

He set his pace.

---

The first of them came along the top terrace with the sun behind them, six men, unhurried, going west toward the district.

Kael went down into the corner where the wall met the bank, into the dock leaves, and put his forearm over the bright part of his face. He did not close his eyes. A closed eye tells a body it may relax, and he could hear his own heart already, and needed it quieter than that.

They passed nine feet above his head. One of them was talking. Another one said something short back, and they both laughed, and the boots went on west and got smaller.

He counted to two hundred. Then he got up.

He was shaking a little in the hands. He looked at that, the way he looked at everything, and put it down as information rather than as a problem, and went on up the wall line.

---

He came on them at the top of the runnel, where it opened out and the track crossed it.

A woman with a bundle on her hip and a boy of about ten holding the back of her skirt, and a smaller one on her other arm with its face in her neck. They were going down the track. Down, toward the flat, toward the water.

He came out of the runnel four strides in front of them and she stopped dead and got the boy behind her with one hand.

"You're going the wrong way," Kael said.

She looked at him. Blood to his elbows, two short blades across his back, thirteen years old.

"Down to Kellis' landing," she said. "There's boats."

"There aren't."

"My brother has a—"

"There aren't any boats." He heard his own voice come out flat and too fast and could not do anything about it. "I was on that water an hour ago. There are ships in a line the whole length of the bay and there are men coming off them and they are working inland from the shore. Whatever is at the landing is theirs now. If you go down there you will walk into them in about twenty minutes."

The boy said, "Mam."

She stood in the track and did not move. Kael watched her not believe him, and understood that she was not going to, because she had one plan and it had taken her all morning to make it.

He did not have the time and he took it anyway.

"Come here," he said.

He put them in the fold under the third terrace, where the wall had come down in the winter and the bank behind it had gone under and made a lip. It was blind from the track. It was blind from above unless a man came and stood on the edge and looked down, and there was no reason on this earth to stand on that edge, because there was nothing up there but stone.

"Get in there. Right in, under the lip, all three of you, and put the bundle in front of the small one." He pulled a fall of dead bramble across the outside of it and set it so that it looked like it had come down on its own, because a thing arranged looks arranged. "Stay until it's dark. Not dusk. Dark. If you hear them, do not run. Running is what they see. Go quiet and stay quiet and let them go past."

"How long," she said.

"Six hours. Maybe seven."

"She'll want feeding."

"Then feed her in there."

The woman looked at him out of the hole in the bank with the small one's face still in her neck.

"How do you know all that?"

"I looked," Kael said.

He put the last of the bramble across, got up, went. Thirty strides on, he registered that she had said something to his back. By then he could not have said what it was.

Eleven minutes. He knew because the count did not stop for anything, ever, and it had cost him eleven minutes, and he carried the number up the runnel with him and did not put it down.

---

The ground with the houses on it began at the top of the runnel and went for a mile and a half.

He lay on the lip of the bed and read it and it was bad.

Thirty-odd holdings scattered across the shoulder. Between them, open. Terraces, stubble, vine ground, the odd row of almonds. And on the field walls, in ones and twos, at the corners where two walls met and a man could see down both of them, there were men standing.

They were not searching. They were posted. Somebody had put them there.

He gave it four minutes and it did not improve. There was no line across that open ground that was not under somebody's eye for at least a hundred strides, and a hundred strides is eleven seconds if you are fresh, and he was not.

Which left the houses.

---

The door was open because they had opened it. There was light coming in behind him. The room in front of him was black. He went along the inside wall with his back against it, stopped where the hinge side of the door made a pocket, put his own knife into his right hand, off the belt, out of the coat, and held it low.

He waited.

A pot somewhere in the dark, dripping. Flies already. The smell in that room was the smell of every room on this island by now and he had stopped registering it about an hour ago, which he noted.

Boots in the yard.

Two men. One of them said something and the other one did not answer. Then one set of boots came across the yard toward the door and the other set went away round the side of the house.

A shape came in and stood in its own light with its head turning.

Kael did not wait for their eyes to meet.

He came off the wall behind him, put his left hand flat over the mouth, pulled the head back, put the knife in under the jaw on the near side and turned it out. The whole of it made about as much noise as a boot in gravel. The man went rigid. Kael went down with him, holding him, riding the weight so that it did not arrive on the floor as one sound, and knelt in somebody's kitchen with a stranger against his chest, a hand over the stranger's mouth, and felt the arms stop.

He waited eleven seconds. Nothing came.

Then he got the body by the collar and dragged it backward into the black part of the room.

His arms were sluggish. He had gone down that lane before noon, up a hill, across three miles of walls. Dragging twelve stone of dead man four feet took him longer than it had any business taking.

He went out the back of the house through the store and over the yard wall.

*One.*

---

The second holding had a fire in one of its roofs, and the light off it was the problem. He lay in the vine rows on his front for eight minutes and let the men in the yard finish what they were doing. While he was lying there, two of them stood on the other side of the wall from him and talked.

They were three feet away. He could hear one of them shift his weight.

*My feet are finished,* one of them said. *I have said so twice.*

*There is water at the top.*

*There is water here.*

*Not in a cistern I have not looked into.* A pause, and then the sound of a man spitting. *Rolan went in one at Tremar's place. There was a dog in it. Three days.*

*So don't drink it.*

One of his mother's languages. Every word of it.

They went. He counted to sixty and got up.

---

The third was a stair.

He came in low through a window and got up the inside stair with his back against the wall, and a man came down it toward him with a lamp he did not need.

There is very little to say about that one. Six feet of stair, no room to either side, and the man had a lamp in the hand he needed.

Kael took him off the stair sideways into the wall, killed him there, caught the lamp before it landed, set it upright on the tread, still burning.

*Two.*

He stood on the stair with his heart going and made himself breathe out longer than he breathed in, four times.

Then up, and out through the roof hatch, and flat on the tiles.

---

The roofs were the best of it.

Nobody looks up. He had known it for years. They came out of the house and stood about. One of them went to the wall and looked out along the terrace for a long time, and Kael lay eight feet above his head watching the back of his neck, close enough to count the man's hair.

He went along that roof-line and got onto the next one off a wall that touched it. Then a wall-top for thirty strides, on his hands and the balls of his feet. Then a stock pen, out the far side of it, up the bank.

He was moving well.

That was the part that started to frighten him, somewhere in the middle of that mile. His hands went where he sent them. He did not have to decide anything. There was a boy going through somebody's home in the dark with a knife who was doing every single thing correctly, and there was another one somewhere further back watching him do it, and the second one was getting quieter.

It was not that the killing had stopped costing him. He knew exactly what it was costing. He had put it down — the first one and every one since — the way you put down a thing you cannot carry and work at the same time, and it had stayed where he put it, and it had not once asked him anything.

That was the frightening part. Not that it was light. That he could set it down at all.

He put a man down in a doorway with the short blade because there was no time for the knife.

*Three.*

One of them heard something, came round to see what, and made the mistake of standing still under the eaves to listen. Kael came off the roof onto the top of him. The point went in behind the collarbone on the way down, and came out, and he was moving before the noise of the landing had finished.

*Four.*

---

Then it went wrong twice inside two minutes.

The first time was a man coming back for something. Kael was in the open between two walls with sixty strides to cross when a man came out of a door behind him and shouted. He did not run. Running is what they see. He went flat behind a stone trough instead, and lay there while the man came out into the yard, stood, looked at everything except the trough, then went in again.

The second time was a tile.

He had a foot on a roof edge and a hand on a beam-end and there was a tile out of line under his boot, and it went. It did not fall. It moved, three inches, with a sound like a plate, and every man in that yard turned round at once.

He got off the roof on the blind side, into the almond row, along it doubled over with his lungs on fire, into the dry ditch at the end of it. He lay there with his mouth open in the dirt for the better part of a minute while six men went along the wall above him at a run.

---

At the top of the shoulder the last of the holdings sat across his line, and past it the ground broke into scrub, and the scrub went up into the goat ground, and that was the end of the houses.

He could see it from the wall. Two hundred strides of stubble, and then nothing on it but gorse and stone all the way up.

He had been reading ground since first light and he knew his reading had got slower in the last hour, because he always knew by how much. He gave that yard ninety seconds instead of four minutes. There were men in it and men on the track beyond it, and there was a line along the north side that put two buildings between him and both of them, and it was a good line, and he took it.

He went over the wall at the end of the byre.

His right thigh had been asked for one thing too many since the third bell, and it picked that wall to stop answering. He got the top of it. He did not get the drop. He came down on the far side with nothing under him on that side and went over onto his shoulder, in the open, in daylight, four feet short of the corner.

It was not a large sound. It did not need to be.

The fire came before he was back on his feet.

He got out of the line of it by going on falling. It went past his back close enough to move his coat, and it took a section of the byre wall out behind him and laid it across the yard as gravel.

Then he was up, on the one leg that was working, with his back against stone, looking to see what had thrown it.

He was standing on the threshing floor with his hands down at his sides.

He was big, and it was not the size. Kael had priced big men all his life. He was forty, or near it, in a plain coat with nothing on it, and he had been standing there looking at a piece of ground the way a farmer looks at his produce, and when he came off his heel he moved light. All of him at once. No shuffle, no settling. A man who had never in his life had to think about where his own weight was.

Kael went for the corner, because the corner was the only part of that yard that was not open.

The man was standing in it. He had not hurried to get there.

They looked at each other across about fifteen feet.

Kael watched him take it in. The blades, the coat, the blood to the elbow, the height, the face.

Something went across the man's own face and stayed there. Kael had spent his whole life failing to read faces. He read this one without any trouble at all, and wished immediately that he had not.

*I'm sorry, little one,* the man said. *But all of you must go.*

He said it in the downhill language.

He said it gently, the way you say a thing to a child in a doorway when you have brought bad news to the house, with the ends of the words where Kael's mother put the ends of her words. It went into him and lodged somewhere and stayed.

Then he came.

---

There was no first exchange.

Kael got one blade up and got it in the way, which was correct, and which was the only thing there was to do, and the fist came through it. Not past it. Through it. The blade went out of his hand and away across the threshing floor. The fist arrived in his chest with fire on the front of it, and Kael went off his feet and hit the yard wall with his shoulders and the back of his head.

He got up. He got up because the alternative was to be on the ground, and he had been taught what happens on the ground before he could read.

He came off the wall inside the next one and put his second blade at the man's hip. Right target, right line, good stroke. He felt it go in about an inch and stop against something.

Then the knee came up.

Fire came off the knee.

It was not a working thrown from a hand. It was in him. It came out of the point of contact at the moment of contact, out of the knee, out of the heel, out of the knuckles, the way sparks come off a wheel — every place the man touched anything, a burst of it, at the instant, and the instant was the only warning there was.

Kael was in the air. Then he was on the threshing floor.

He rolled. Something came down where he had been and the stone cracked and went white-hot and he could feel it on the side of his face as he went past it.

He got to his knees.

He reached for the thread, and there was almost nothing there to reach for.

He found the end of it and put it into his right hand because there was no blade in it any more, and the white came up along the edge, thin, thinner than it had been all day, wavering the way a flame wavers when somebody opens a door, and he put the hand up as the boot came in.

The threading took the boot.

It broke the fire off the front of it. It cut two inches into the leather, and into the shin behind the leather. It did not stop the boot. The boot weighed eighteen stone and it was moving, and Kael went backwards across that threshing floor, off the edge of it, into the wall a second time.

He heard something in his own chest break.

The purse was empty. Not low. Empty, the way it had been in front of eleven thousand people, and the bottom of it was right there, and he could feel exactly where the next grain would have to come from and what it would cost him to take it.

He tried to get up and his arms took his weight and then did not.

Boots on the threshing floor, coming, unhurried, his face against the stone of somebody's yard, his parents six miles up a mountain, both blades gone, nothing at all left in him. He opened his mouth and screamed.

There was no craft in it. There was nothing in it that had been taught to him. It went up out of that yard and over the roofs and out across the whole of that shoulder — *help — please — PLEASE* — a boy, a child, calling in the plain flat words of the village, over and over, to nobody, because there was nobody, because there had not been anybody since the gate.

The boots stopped.

Kael got his head round.

The man was standing over him, and he had not raised his hand, and he was looking down at him, and there was something on him that Kael had no filing place for at all. He was not enjoying it. He was not angry. He looked like a man doing an errand he had been dreading to do.

*I know,* he said. *I know.*

He raised his hand.

---

The men at the gate end of the yard stopped being where they were.

That was the first of it, and Kael got it in the wrong order, the way you get things when your head is on a stone: the sound came after. Four men were standing at the mouth of that yard. Then the mouth of the yard was empty and something was going through it. Then the noise arrived.

Then there was white in the yard.

Not on a blade. Everywhere. It came in through the gate at the height of a man. Kael, lying on his side on somebody's threshing floor with his ribs broken, saw it clearly for perhaps a second and a half, understood half of what he was looking at, and would have given a great deal to understand all of it.

It was a man.

It was a man with lightning in him. Not only in his hand. Not only down a blade. In the whole of him. Down both arms, across the shoulders, through the chest, into the ground at every step. Held in. Running the length of every part of him at once and *not coming off* — not sparking, not spilling, not one thread of it going anywhere it had not been told to go. He carried a longsword in his right hand with the same white standing the whole length of the blade, and the sword was the least of it.

Kael knew what it cost to hold that in one hand. He had spent a winter learning to carry it from a shoulder to a hand without losing it at the knock, and he knew, the way he knew the weight of things, that this was much more of that.

It was not going along him. It was going *into* him.

Three men came at him out of the corner.

Kael did not see the sword move. He saw three men go at somebody, and he saw one of them arrive, and then there was nobody standing in that corner and the man was eleven feet further on than he had been. Not blurred. Not quick. *Absent* — as though a piece had been taken out of the middle of the second and the two ends put back together.

It was a third thing, made out of two he already knew, and there was no name anywhere in him to put on it.

A spear came in from the left. The left hand took the shaft, bare, and the white went up the wood in front of the hand and the shaft came apart in the middle and the man holding it went down without being touched by anything Kael could see.

Then the yard was quiet except for the fire in the roof, and there were two of them standing on the threshing floor.

Kael's father stood eight feet from his son, in his own working coat, with his sleeves rolled the way they were rolled every morning of Kael's life, and the light coming off him put the shadow of the wall halfway up the house.

He was breathing hard.

The big man had not moved. He was standing where he had been standing with his hands loose at his sides, and he was looking at Vask, and he was looking at him the way a man looks at an exotic animal in the wild.

Then he did something that made no sense at all. He put his head very slightly on one side.

*Well,* he said. *The Phantom.*

Nothing came back.

*You have aged,* the man said.

He came off his back foot and the yard came apart.

Kael saw perhaps a third of it. Fire off both hands at once, out and low, and the white went through the middle of it and the middle of it was not there any more. A boot came round. The sword was in the way of it, and the shock of the two of them meeting went through the ground, through Kael's ribs, and he made a sound. His father turned inside something and came out on the other side of the man. The sword went across the back of the coat. The coat opened, and there was no blood in it, because the man had already moved four feet that Kael never saw him move. Fire came out of the stone under Vask's feet in a line and Vask was not standing on the line by then.

Four exchanges. Perhaps five. Neither of them gave any ground at all and neither of them took any.

Then they were fifteen feet apart again. The big man stood with his weight square and his hands open. He was not smiling. He was not hurrying either. There was blood coming down the outside of his sleeve.

And there were men coming in over the far wall. Six of them. Then more behind those.

Vask did not look at them. He did not look at the man in front of him either. He turned round, came across the threshing floor, and got Kael off the stones with one arm under his back and one under his knees, the way you pick up a thing that has been dropped. Kael's head went against his father's shoulder and stayed there.

He got one thing out. It cost him everything he had.

"Mother. Is she—"

"The baby. It's coming. I'm sorry I couldn't find you sooner. I... We must go."

The yard went.

Not the way ground goes when you are running. Kael had run all his life, on scree, in the dark, downhill, and it was not that. The wall went past. The gate went past. The almond row went past, and the noise of all of it arrived somewhere behind them, late, chasing. The white was in the arm under his shoulders. It ran down through his father's chest against his cheek, and it did not burn him. It did not do anything to him at all. It went round him.

He could not lift his head. He did not need to. He knew every foot of the ground that was going past underneath them, every wall of it and every turn, and he had walked it twice a day for four years, and it had never once gone past like that.

Above them, black against the last of the light, with its top in the cloud, was the mountain.


---

# Chapter Thirty-One — The Line

The street was lit.

Behind her the bar went across.

The judge let go of her arm. She got her feet properly under her. She put the blade away. The street had a gutter running down one side of it, and a bay tree in a pot outside somebody's door.

There were six men on the inside of the gate with their hands on the timber. Beyond them a woman was carrying a basin across a courtyard. Further up, where the street bent, two children were sitting on a step being told something by a servant, and neither of them was crying, and the servant was not hurrying.

She stood and let all of it arrive and did not move for the length of about eight breaths.

What she wanted was to turn round and put both blades through the bar.

She priced it, because everything got priced. The bar was oak and iron and she could probably do it. Six men were standing on the inside of that gate and two of them would have to go down before she reached the timber, and they were her own side. Past the timber were thirty thousand people who would be through it inside a minute.

Which meant that if she did it, the wall fell at noon, and the judge had been right in front of every house on this island.

She put it away and went to work. Standing in a street being angry was not a thing a Vel did, and the eight breaths were already a failure she would account for later.

The district was one long spine of a street with the houses off it. It was full without being crowded, because the households were inside their own walls. What moved in the open was sworn men, in six or seven different sets of colours, none of them under one officer that she could see. That was the first real thing she learned. It was a bad one.

She found the Temple children in the second courtyard she looked into, which was the largest one, and which somebody had turned into a hospital in the last two hours by carrying every table in the house out into it.

There were not four hundred.

She counted while she crossed the yard, because counting was faster than asking, and she had ninety-one before the tables ran out. Some of those were on the tables.

"Val."

Aurelian Corvane was sitting on the edge of a horse trough with his left arm held against his body in a way she had watched him hold it for four days. He got up when she came over, which he did for her now and could not seem to stop doing, and it cost him something this time.

"Sit down," she said.

"I'm all right."

"Your arm has been not all right since the tournament, and you have used it today. Sit down."

He sat down. Elarine Sedrane was on the trough beside him with her back against the stone and her hands loose in her lap, palms up, which was not a thing Elarine did. There was a burn along her jaw that nobody had looked at yet. She had been mended in front of eleven thousand people the previous morning. There was nothing left in her. It showed.

"You came up the road," Elarine said.

"Yes."

"Then you know more about what is outside this wall than anybody standing inside it."

"Probably." Valeria looked back down the length of the street, at the sets of colours moving about in front of the gate. "Who is holding the wall?"

"The houses are holding the wall."

"Which of them?"

"All of them."

"All of them how?"

Elarine turned her head far enough to look at her properly, which cost her something she could not entirely keep off her face.

"Every house has its own men on its own stretch," she said. "Every house is giving its own men its own orders. There is nobody standing over the top of it. My mother has been at them since the second hour to put one man in charge of the whole wall, and they will not do it, because the moment they do, whoever is chosen is standing over everybody else's men. No house in this district is going to be the one that agrees to that today."

Valeria had read it off the street on the way in and had been hoping to be wrong about it.

"What word is there," Valeria said. "Who they are, and why."

Aurelian answered. "Eastern. The colours are eastern, somebody said that at the wall. There's a count going round that says nine thousand off the boats and another that says thirty. My uncle's man says the garrison is coming up from the south and will be here by the afternoon."

"There is no garrison coming up from the south."

"You've seen the south road?"

"I came through the village on the inland road. The garrison post at the lane mouth was gone before I got to it. Not fallen. Gone. They went somewhere in the first hour and nobody has seen them since." She heard her own voice doing the flat thing it did and let it. "Nine thousand is wrong. Thirty is wrong. Nobody in this yard has been outside this wall since it shut. Every number in here was made up by somebody inside it."

Nobody said anything to that. A boy she did not know was being sick into the gutter at the far side of the yard and being held up by a girl who was not much bigger than he was.

"Where is the First Master," Valeria said.

"With the houses. They have been in there since before you came."

"How long."

"An hour. Perhaps two." Aurelian moved his shoulder and stopped moving it. "They will not send anybody out, if that's what you're going to ask. They have been asked. Three times, by us."

"I am not going to ask them to send anybody out."

"Good," said Elarine, "because they won't," and then she looked up, and her eyes did the thing they did, which was arrive on you already having decided something.

"I saw him," she said.

Valeria did not answer.

"On the road below the wall. About a hundred and forty out. I could not see his face."

"No."

"He was on his own."

"Yes," Valeria said.

Aurelian was looking at the flagstones between his boots. He said, in the voice of a man reading out a weight, "He hasn't got a house."

"No."

"That's all it is," Aurelian said. "There isn't a second part of it. My family's men are on that wall. If I went and stood in front of them and told them to open it, they would be very sorry. They would not open it. They would be right, because a house stands for its own. Yes. Of course."

He said it well. He spared himself nothing, which was the thing about him, and it was worth nothing at all.

There was a place in the middle of it where she came very close to telling him what she thought of his family's men, and of the wall, and of him.

She did not. She had done that once already today, at a gap eighteen inches wide, at the top of her voice, with her name and her orders and everything eleven generations had put in her behind it. It had moved a grown man's hand not one inch. She had gone up a lit street backwards like a chair being dragged across a floor, in front of forty people, and the last thing she had managed was his name.

Valeria thought about the eighteen inches. The boy's hand on the edge of the leaf. *My mother is on the mountain.* She had spent her name at that gap, and her orders, and herself, and had been answered correctly at every rung.

She did not tell them any of it.

"His mother is having a child," she said, which was the only part of it that belonged to anybody but her, "and he has gone east."

---

They came for her in the middle of the afternoon.

A man in Corvane grey stopped in front of her, looked at the blood on her forearms, and said, "The Line of Vel?"

"Yes."

"Inside, please."

That was the whole of it.

Her name had opened every door on this island. She thought about that for as long as it took to cross the courtyard, which was long enough.

The room had been somebody's dining hall and there were nineteen people in it.

She read it in the order she had been taught. Exits: two, the double doors behind her and a service door in the corner, and the service door had nobody on it, which was wrong. Weapons: everywhere, worn, none of them ready. Ground: a long table down the middle with nothing on it, chairs pushed back, everybody standing.

Then the people.

The houses had sent their heads. The heads had brought their sons. The Drusane man was at the near end with his hands on the back of a chair. She had watched him from the competitors' gate two mornings ago while his son was being carried out on a board.

The princess was seated at the far end, and she was the only person seated.

Then she priced her. It took about four seconds. It was not a thing she could have explained to anybody who had not been raised to do it. Height, weight, reach. Whether she could run, how far, in what. The answer was that she was fourteen years old, had never carried anything, and wore the shoes of somebody who had never once had to run. If it came to it she would have to be carried. She would have to be carried by somebody who was not also fighting.

She had been pricing bodies like that since she was six.

Eleven generations of her house had stood in front of that girl's house, and the best of them stood over the king, and being the best one meant that and nothing besides. She had known what she was for since before she could read.

If that girl had to be taken off this island, Valeria would take her off it, or she would be lying somewhere behind her having failed to. There was no third arrangement and there never had been one.

She wanted to stand in front of her. She had wanted it her whole life, and nobody had ever had to ask.

The judge was against the far wall with his hands folded, broad and comfortable, saying nothing. He had come in ahead of her and had not looked at her since.

She looked at him long enough to fix where he was standing and what was inside the reach of his hands, which is what you do with everybody in a room. Then she stopped looking at him, and went on not looking at him for the next two hours, and it was the hardest work she did that day.

And the First Master was in the middle of the room with his sleeves down and his hands behind him, being spoken to by three men at once.

He was grey all the way through. Yesterday morning she had watched him come off the front of the host's box, take two children apart, and put them back together in front of the empire. The man in this room was a different order of thing altogether: gone thin about the face, with the stoop that was ordinarily a scholar's now sitting on him as weight. His eyes were very dark in it. It was the only dark thing left in that face, and it gave out precisely nothing. She filed him as the most dangerous person in the room. Then she went and stood against the wall four feet from the service door, because somebody had to.

---

"—and I am telling you the wall will not be there tonight," Severin said.

"You are telling us. On what."

"On what came over the arena." He did not raise his voice. He had a voice like a man reading a list of stores. "Eleven stones in the first four minutes and none of them short. That is not a crew learning a range. That is a crew who had the range before they sailed, which means somebody paced this island out for them at leisure, and they have done the same for this district, and they will start on it when they are finished with the hill."

"The hill is not a wall."

"No," Severin agreed. "The hill was thirty thousand people standing in the open."

The Drusane man said, "The engines will not come up that road."

"Some of it is not engines." He said it without any weight on it at all. "I stopped one stone over four hundred children yesterday morning. I did not stop the second. I want you to hear the reason, because it is the only useful thing I know. I could not hold two. There is at least one among them who can put fire onto a roof from beyond bowshot; I watched three separate roofs go in the village in a way no engine does it. Their Resonants are fire, most of them, and there are a great many of them. I am one man. I am an old one, and I have been spending since the third bell yesterday."

"Then what are you proposing," somebody said, "that we walk out of our own houses?"

"I am proposing that the island is lost and that you get your children off it tonight."

The room did not go quiet. That was the thing she noted. It got louder, in the particular way a room gets louder when everybody has already had the thought and is now hearing it out loud.

Outside, past the shutters, the sound off the hill was still going.

It had been going since before she came in. It was not loud. It came in under the talking the way rain comes in under talking, and every so often a piece of it stood up out of the rest and had one person inside it, and then went back down again.

Nobody in that room had mentioned it once.

"There are no ships," said a man in green. "The harbour is theirs. You have just finished telling us so yourself."

"There is a boat," said Severin.

Valeria watched the room turn.

"On the far side of the mountain, on the eastern water, in a cut below the goat ground. It is not on any quay and it is not in any ledger and it will take about forty."

"Whose is it?"

"Mine."

"Since when?"

"Since the autumn."

There was a pause of the length that pause needed to be.

"You have had a hull hidden on the far side of this island since the autumn," said the Drusane man.

"Yes."

"Why."

"Because I am the First Master of this Temple," Severin said, "and I have four hundred of your children in my keeping eleven months of the year, and I would be a poor keeper of them if I had nothing at all." He let that sit for exactly as long as it took, and then he said, "You may ask me the rest of it when we are standing on the other side of that water. I will still be here to be asked."

She put it down as *a man in his position keeps a hull,* and moved on, because it was reasonable, and because there was nothing else in the room to put it down as.

---

The houses said no.

It took them the better part of an hour and they were not stupid about it, which was the part she had not expected. She had come in ready for pride. There was pride in it. Underneath the pride the argument they made was a real argument, and she could not find the hole in it fast.

The wall had held all morning. That was the first thing, and it was true. It had held on its own, against everything that came at it. Every man on it had been paid and drilled by somebody in this room for years. Walls are the one thing anybody knows how to do.

Beyond the wall was open ground for six miles in three directions, with an enemy on it in numbers nobody could give, moving in ones and twos and fours. Any party leaving this district would be leaving into that with children in it. Behind a wall the arithmetic was known. Outside it, nothing was known at all.

And the empire was coming. That was the third thing. A princess of the blood was inside these walls. When the capital learned it, the capital would come for her with everything it had, and everything it had was not small. Hold, and the empire arrives. Run, and it arrives to find an empty district and a road with children on it.

"Securing Her Serene Highness is the whole of the question," said a woman in Sedrane blue. "There is no second question. Behind stone, with four hundred sworn men, is where she is secured."

"Behind stone is where she is *found*," Severin said.

"By our own people."

"By whoever gets here first."

The princess said, "How long would the crossing take?"

It was the first thing she had said. Every head in the room went to her, and Valeria watched the room do it, and watched the girl not notice that a room had done it, because a room had done it every day of her life.

"Six hours in the dark, moving carefully," said Severin. "Perhaps eight."

"And my guard would be with me the entire way."

"Yes."

"Then I do not see the difficulty," said Aeliana Cassivara Vessantine, and turned her hand over on the arm of the chair, and that was the end of her contribution to it.

Nobody corrected her. Valeria noticed that too, and filed it under the same heading as the service door with nobody on it.

The talking went round again. It went round a third time. A man had begun a sentence about the north bastion when he stopped, went to the window himself rather than sending anybody, closed the shutter, came back, and finished the sentence.

Nobody remarked on it.

Then the judge came off the wall.

---

"May I."

He said it into a gap, the way a man asks for the salt, and the room let him have it, and Valeria understood something about the room from how easily it did.

"I am not a soldier," Halden said, "and I am not going to stand here and tell soldiers their business. I am a judge. I was in a box above that sand when they came, which makes me the man on this island with the best view of the sea. I would like to give you what I saw. Then I will sit down."

He had a voice like a warm room. He did not hurry.

"There were eleven ships in the first line. I counted a second line behind it. I did not finish counting it. They came in on the beaches and into the harbour together, which nobody does unless they have enough for both. The harbour went in the first hour. Everything in it is burned or is being crewed by them now. I watched two of our own hulls go out under sail at midday with the wrong men on them. They went east." He turned his hands over. "There is nothing to sail off this island. That is settled and it is not a matter of opinion.

"On the numbers. Every estimate this room has was made by somebody counting boats. I counted boats too. I was wrong. They are not carrying what a raid carries. There is no baggage on that beach, no pens, no ground marked out for anything. They are not stopping anywhere. They went into the village and did not stop in the village. I have been trying all afternoon to make it read as a raid and it will not."

"Then what does it read as," said the Drusane man.

"It reads as men clearing ground," Halden said.

He said it in the voice you would use about a field.

"So I find that I agree with the First Master. I want to be plain about the part that matters, because it is not comfortable and somebody should say it. He is the strongest man on this island by a long way. There are others in this room who are not nothing. The Temple has ninety-odd children in that courtyard, and some of them are very good. Add all of it together, every one of us, and stand it on that wall tonight." He paused, and it was not for effect. "It is not enough. It is not nearly enough, and I would rather be told I was a coward in the morning than have this room decide otherwise on the strength of a number I gave it two days ago."

Nobody called him a coward.

Valeria had watched two thousand people be moved by better speeches than that at the games, and had priced every one of them. She knew, standing against that wall with her back on the stone, that this was the moment the room turned. She knew exactly why it turned, and it had nothing to do with the sense of it.

The old man was not a house. The judge was. He had a warrant, a name, and business he did for people in that room. When he said the thing they had already worked out for themselves, it stopped being a schoolmaster's fright and became information.

Severin had said all of it first. He had said it better, and with the numbers, and he had been standing in front of them for two hours saying it.

The same voice had said *come through, please* to her this morning, and *he does not*, and had been correct then as well.

She stood against the stone with her hands where her hands went, and got through it.

Severin knew it too. She was watching him when Halden finished, and there was nothing on his face at all, and she thought that a man who has just been agreed with does not usually look like that.

---

They came to it inside twenty minutes, once they had turned.

The houses would hold the district. All of them, all the sworn men, every gate and every stretch of wall, for as long as it could be held and then a while after that. Under cover of the holding, a small party would go out the far gate at full dark, onto the road that had been closed since the second hour, and cross to the eastern water, and take the boat.

They did it well. She would give them that for the rest of her life and never once be able to say it out loud without the other half arriving with it.

There was no argument about who would stay. There was no argument at all. Men and women stood in that room and said their own names and the names of their houses, in an ordinary voice, one after another, the way you take a duty roster. One man said *and my brother's men; he is not here to say it, he would say it.* That was the closest anybody came to speaking loudly.

The Sedrane woman said her daughter's name when it came to her, and got most of the way through the sentence, and finished it.

They loved their children. That was true, and it was the whole engine of it. Valeria, who had been raised inside a house that had never once said such a thing to her, stood against the wall and watched nineteen people put their names to a wall they had just been told would not hold. She did not find any part of it ridiculous.

Beyond the closed shutter, the sound off the hill was still going.

It had been going for four hours. Somewhere out there under it were the people who had come to this wall in the morning, and had queued, and had looked after one another while they queued, and had been told there was no line for them. The nineteen people in this room had not said one word about them since she walked in. They were not going to. It was not because they were hard.

It was because there had been nothing to say. They were not in the arithmetic. They had never been. A house stands for its own, and the sums are done in houses, and it is not possible to be cruel to a quantity you have never once had occasion to write down.

She had been angry since the middle of the morning, and had been holding it down all afternoon with both hands.

It had never once been about *them*.

The party was made in front of her.

The First Master would lead it, because he was the strongest thing they had and because the boat was his. The princess and her twelve guards. And the Temple children who had reached this wall with a house behind them to be counted with. That came out at seven, because seven was how many were left of the ones this room had names for who could run.

Valeria Vel. Elarine Sedrane. Aurelian Corvane. Lucan Drusane. Ostrian Vessaline. And two more: a Kaeth cousin, and a girl from the northwest school whose name Valeria did not have, and made a point of getting before she left the room.

Twenty-one, with the guard. Severin said he would take four more from the courtyard and did not say which four, and nobody asked him.

"And you," the Drusane man said to Halden.

"I'll stay."

He said it the way he had said everything else, level and without any weight on it, and he did not offer anything after it. The Drusane man waited two beats for the rest of the sentence and did not get it.

"Corvane has men on the eastern stretch," Halden said, "and Corvane's business on this island is mine to answer for while I am on it. It would be an odd thing to answer for it from a boat."

---

They put her on the princess.

It was not an order, and nobody phrased it as one. The captain of the twelve came over to where she was standing, a grey-headed man with a countryman's face. He did not ask her anything about the road, or the village, or what was on her arms. He asked her how long she had been on the island, and who she trained under here. She told him. He looked at her forearms, and at how she stood.

"You will walk on Her Serene Highness's left from the gate," he said, "and you will give your life for the princess."

"Understood," Valeria answered.

It was her purpose. A Vel walks on the royal left. There is not a word in the language of her house for wanting to be somewhere else. And she did not want to be somewhere else.

She went out into the courtyard to find the seven and count them properly. The light had gone orange along the top of the eastern wall.

The sound had stopped.

She stood in the middle of the yard with the tables around her and worked out when.

It had been going when the shutter was closed. It had not been going when they made the party. Somewhere in between, in a room with the shutter shut and nineteen people putting their names to a wall in an ordinary voice, thirty-one thousand people had finished. She had not been able to hear it stop. Neither had anybody else in that room. That was what the shutter was for, whether the man who closed it had known it or not.

She wrote the time down in her head under the heading it belonged to and went to find Elarine.

She found her in the corner of the yard by the cistern head, with her mother, and stopped where she was, because you do not walk into that.

The Sedrane woman had her daughter by both shoulders and was giving her instructions.

That was what it was. Instructions. Which of the factors could be trusted and which could not, and what to say to her uncle, and in what order. The woman had put her own name to that wall an hour ago and had not mentioned it once since.

"Write it down tonight, on the water, while you still have it exactly. Say it back."

Elarine said it back. All of it, in order, without a word out of place, because she did not repeat her work and did not ask twice and never had.

"Good."

"You could come," Elarine said.

"No."

"He said he would take four more out of the courtyard. There is room."

"There is room for four children out of that courtyard." Her mother did not let go of her shoulders. "I am not a child out of that courtyard."

"That is not a good argument."

"No," her mother agreed. "It is not, and you knew it was not before I finished saying it. That is the part I gave you."

Something moved in Elarine's face and did not get anywhere.

"Listen to me now, because I am not going to be able to say it twice. If this goes the way the old man says it will, you are going to be what is left of this house. You are thirteen. Every man who ever did business with your father will come at you inside the year, and most of them will bring somebody kind to do the talking."

"Yes."

"Not one of them is good enough." Her mother's hands did not move on her shoulders. "Not one of them has ever been good enough. I have known it since you were four years old, and I have never once said it to you, because you did not need saying to and I was not going to have you softened."

Elarine did not answer that.

"I am not encouraging you. Encouragement is for people who need it. I am telling you what I have watched."

Then she put her hand flat against the side of her daughter's head and left it there, and neither of them said anything more.

Valeria watched, and priced it, and got nothing.

She knew what she was looking at the way you know what a foreign coin is. It was the same shape as the thing that came down the wall at her out of her father's letters, and it was not the same substance, and she could not have said where the difference lived.

It was going on all over the yard.

Corvane had his son by the back of the neck and was talking fast into his ear. "—and you will not decorate it. He will want it plain."

"Yes."

"He will ask what I did. You will tell him I stood on the eastern stretch with our own men, and you will not make it better than that."

"No."

Then the man stopped. He was big and gone heavy, with his son's colouring and thirty years on top of it, and Valeria watched him go looking for something and not have it anywhere about him.

"You carried that arm through two days of games and told nobody."

Aurelian said nothing.

"Your brother would have had it round the whole building inside the hour." Corvane's hand tightened on the back of his neck. "I have two sons. I have never once had a favourite, and I have let a great many people believe otherwise, because it was useful. It was useful to me. It was not useful to you." He let go. "That was badly done, and I am saying so now, because there is not going to be a later."

Aurelian Corvane, who answered everything, did not answer that.

Nearer the gate, Ostrian Vessaline was being held on to by two people at once and was managing none of it.

"—and you eat, Ostrian. They will not think to tell you and you will not think of it yourself—"

"Father—"

"—and you keep the coat. Whatever they say about it. It is a good coat, it is warm, and I do not care in the least what it looks like—"

"Father."

"What."

Oss could not get anything out at all.

His father took him by both arms. He said nothing about houses. He did not mention the name once, the name that had cost him most of what he owned and had been the whole subject of his life, and Valeria, who had no equipment whatever for noticing a thing like that, noticed it.

"You were always enough," the man told him. "You were always plenty."

Every old house along that wall did the same thing at the same moment, which was to look at something else, and it was the only kindness Valeria saw an old house do all day.

And at the near end, by the tables, the Drusane man stood in front of his son.

"You will be no use to them on that road."

Lucan Drusane did not answer.

"You have been on your back two days. You will slow them, and they will carry you, and one of them will be killed doing it." His father's hands hung at his sides. "That is what I said to the room. I said it in front of the First Master, and I said it twice."

"I know."

"They kept you in anyway."

"I know."

Valeria waited for the rest of it, because there is always a rest of it.

"Good," said Drusane.

That was all of it. Then he took his son's face in both hands and held it, and Lucan stood and let him, and after a while the man let go and turned round and walked out toward the wall without looking back, and the boy stayed where he was for a long time after that.

Then she went to find the princess, because she would be walking on her left and it was time to begin.

Aeliana Cassivara Vessantine was standing near the arch inside her twelve, with nobody's hands on her at all. There was no one in this district to say goodbye to her. Her people were on the other side of the sea and did not know yet.

Valeria looked at the yard, and then at the girl by the arch, and did the arithmetic before she could stop herself.

Out of everybody going through that gate tonight, there were two that nobody had hold of.

Two of them had both hands free.

---

The captain took her report at the far gate at the end of the light, standing up, with a lamp held by somebody else.

She gave it in order, because that was how it was given. The arena, the seaward arch, the timing of the collapse in the north curve. The inland road off the headland and the four men at the second bend. The colours, which were eastern, and what that meant about how far they had come and how little they intended to hold. The garrison post at the mouth of the village lane, empty before the first hour. Nothing had come up the south road since. The village. The wells, and the fifteen men at the wells, and the shape of the ground there. The count on the hill, thirty-one thousand at the low end and thirty-six at the high, taken off a strip and checked against a second strip.

He stopped her twice to make her repeat a number and she repeated both without changing them.

At the wells she said *we* twice, and did not say who, and he did not ask.

Then she asked him the only question she had.

"The north side of this district. Between the bastion and the corner where the terraces come up. Is there anything on it."

He thought about it properly, which she had expected of him by then.

"Nothing built. The ground goes up in terraces and dies against the foot of the wall. No gate, no postern, nothing to come at. We have four men on that stretch and they are four men because there is nothing there to want." He looked at her. "Why."

"I like to know what is behind me," Valeria said.

"That is the correct answer," said the captain, and went away up the line.

She stood at the far gate with the lamp going away from her.

Her report had been complete. She had gone over it while she gave it and there was not one thing in it she had left out, not one number softened, not one distance she was unsure of that she had not marked as unsure.

There had been a boy on that road with her from the first bend to the wall. He had read the road for her when she could not read it, and had walked her through thirty thousand people who would not have moved for anybody else on this island, and had understood every word the enemy said within a hundred strides of them and had told her all of it, exactly as she had ordered him to, and had not once asked her for anything.

Somewhere out east of this wall, in the dark, six miles up a mountain that was on fire, he was going home to a house she had never asked him a single question about. And now she would have to cross that boy's mountain to get off this island, and she had left him standing outside a gate that morning.


---

# Chapter Thirty-Two — The Mountain

His father did not slow until the split rock.

Then the ground went up in the way Kael's legs had known since he could walk, and the rest of him had stopped being able to feel anything about it at all.

"Kael."

"Yes."

"Breathe out. All of it."

He breathed out. It went about two-thirds of the way and stopped, and something on the right side of his chest put a hand up.

"Again."

He did it again. It stopped in the same place.

"Say a long sentence."

"There were four men in the yard and one of them was standing on the threshing floor when I came over the wall."

"Good," his father said.

They went on up, Kael's head against his father's shoulder. He could not have lifted it if the house had been burning at the top of the path. The coat under his cheek smelled of wet wool, of other people's blood, of the mountain. It had smelled of the mountain his whole life.

"Are you all right," his father said.

Kael did not answer, because he was working out what had been asked him.

It was not a question anybody could do anything with. It did not price a thing or set a thing or fix a thing.

"I don't know," he said.

"No," said his father. "Nor do I."

They came up out of the last of the trees and the wind found them, and Kael turned his face into the coat and got it out anyway.

"Is mother by herself."

"Yes."

He waited for the rest of it. There was not any rest of it.

"Father."

"Yes."

"Nothing," Kael said.

Above them the cloud had come down onto the top of the mountain and stayed there, and the underside of it was orange from one end of the island to the other, and it moved the way water moves.

The door was standing open.

It had never in Kael's life been standing open at night. The bar went into the stone every night. The wind came and pushed at it. The door held. That was a thing his mother did last, with her hand flat on the wood, and it was the sound the end of the day made.

His father did not remark on it. He turned side-on and carried his son in through his own door without breaking stride.

---

The fire was up higher than it had ever been allowed to go.

That was the first thing. The fire in that house lived down in its hole where the wind could not find it, and it was kept small because small was what you kept, and it was standing up now nearly to the height of the pot chain, and the whole room was moving with it.

The second thing was the water. Every vessel in the house was full and standing in a line along the hearth stone, the two pails, the pot, the wash-basin, a thing Kael had only ever seen used for salting.

The third thing was his mother, on her feet, with both hands on the end of the table.

She turned her head. She had her hair pushed back off her face with the flat of her wrist, and there was a long moment where Kael watched her go through the whole of it — the two of them in the doorway, the blood, the way his father was carrying him, the fact of him being carried at all — and get to the end of it and find that he was alive.

He had seen his mother pleased. He had seen her satisfied, and once or twice, when a thing he had built came out true, he had seen her proud all the way through with nothing over it.

He had never seen this. She made a sound that had no word anywhere in it.

Then a pain took her. She went white round the mouth and got her weight forward onto her arms, and her chin went down, and she was not in the room any more.

Kael watched his mother go somewhere he could not follow, and stay there, for a count of forty.

"Put him down," she said, when she came back. "By the fire. Not on the boards, get the fleece under him."

"Neris—"

"I heard you come up. I have been listening to that path since the light went." She was already moving. "Put him down."

---

She went over him the way she went over everything.

Fingers along the ribs, one at a time. She found it on the fourth and did not stop when he made the noise, because stopping teaches you nothing. Both hands flat on his chest to feel the fill. Her thumb pulling his eyelid up, and the lamp brought over so that the light came across and not into.

"Three," she said. "One of them is not where it should be and it is sitting on the lung. Has there been blood in your mouth."

"No."

"Say it again."

"No. I checked."

"Good boy." She went down his arm, and along the burn, and past it. "This is old."

"Yesterday."

"Yesterday," his mother repeated. Something went over her face and went away again. Her hands got to the thigh and he came off the fleece about an inch. "And this."

"It stopped answering on a wall. It has not come back."

She sat back on her heels.

His father was standing over the both of them. He had put the longsword against the wall by the door on his way past and had not let go of it until it was standing. Now his hands were empty, half-curled, and he did not seem to know where to put them.

"Vask," his mother said. "Hold his shoulders."

"Neris."

"Hold his shoulders."

There was a space then that Kael did not understand at the time and understood perfectly well about four seconds later, in which his father did not move, and his mother did not look up at him, and neither of them said anything at all.

Then his father knelt down on the boards and put his hands on his son's shoulders.

---

Kael had felt his mother carry something exactly once, on a flat rock above the pens, with the sea going on underneath them and her palm empty and the whole thing so small that he had spent a year afterwards not being sure.

This was not small.

It came up under his ribs like a hand pushing at a door that had swollen in the wet, and it did not go through, and it came again, and the third time something inside him moved and went on moving, and it hurt worse than the breaking had by a great deal. He got most of a shout out. His father's hands did not tighten and did not let go.

It was not clean. That was the thing he had nowhere to put. He had stood eleven feet from the First Master of the Temple the morning before last, in front of eleven thousand people, and watched him take two children apart and put them back together, and nothing had shown. No light. No sound. It had been like watching a man decide something.

This showed.

His mother's breath was going in short through her teeth. There was sweat standing out along her hairline that had not been there when she knelt down, and her hands were shaking on him, and she went on anyway, slowly and badly, the way you do a thing with the wrong tool because it is the only tool you have.

"Mother," he said. It came out with no shape on it.

"Be quiet," his mother said. "I am counting."

There was a piece of her hair hanging down beside his face. He had been looking at it because it was what there was to look at from where he was lying, dark and thick and coming loose out of the wrist she had pushed it back with, swinging a little every time she leaned.

While he watched it, the colour went out of it.

It did not go grey. Grey happens to a man over twenty years. It comes in stages. It is the colour of the sea in the cold term, of wet stone, of his father's temples. This was not that. It came up out of the roots and ran down the length of the piece the way frost runs across the top of a pail. Then it stopped, and it was white. Not the white of anything alive. The white of the seam in a stone when you split it.

Kael knew exactly what he was looking at.

"Stop," Kael said.

She did not stop.

"Mother. *Stop.* I can breathe. It's enough, it's—"

"You will be running tonight," his mother said, "and you will be doing it on a mountain, in the dark, and I have seen how you go up that scree when you are whole."

A second piece went. It went while she was talking about the scree, in the ordinary voice she used for facts, and she did not seem to feel it happen.

Then it was over. Her hands came off him and she went sideways, and his father took her weight without any part of him appearing to move first, and got her down onto the fleece beside her son.

The next one came on top of that, before she had got her breath back at all, and it was a bad one, and it went on for a long time.

Kael lay on his back six inches from his mother's shoulder with his chest filling all the way to the bottom for the first time since a threshing floor, and could not do one single thing.

---
