It did not feel like anything. There was no pressure behind his eyes and nothing in his throat and no wish to make a sound. His face was simply wet and going on being wet, the way the count went on counting, in a part of him that had never once asked his permission for anything.

He turned his head to check the leg's line on a bad step and looked at his mother instead.

Her face was wet all the way through.

She had it up and forward and she was watching Severin's back for the road, and it was coming down both sides of her nose and along her jaw and going into the collar of the coat, and her mouth was doing nothing at all.

She felt him look. She turned her head about two inches and their eyes went over each other for something under a second.

Neither of them said one word.

---

The first light on the slot came up behind them in the third mile.

Kael saw it because he was the one who kept looking, and he kept looking because his father had spent five years teaching him that the thing behind you is the thing that kills you and the thing in front of you is only the thing that worries you.

"Master," he said.

Severin did not stop. "Where."

"Top of the slot." Kael took it and did not want it. "Forty. Sixty — it has not stopped. A hundred and twenty. A hundred and sixty, and there is more of it still behind the lip that I have not got yet. They are coming down it in a body and they are not being careful about it."

His count went on doing what it did, which was work, and gave him the rest of it, and he gave that away too.

"And there is a second one. Round the head of the slot, along the old terrace. That is longer by about a mile and I cannot see the end of it from here, and it is bigger than the first."

"How long."

"For the slot, twenty minutes. They will run us down before the runnel. For the terrace, forty."

Severin ran three more strides.

"Then it is here," he said.

He turned off the line without warning and went up onto a shelf of ground on their right where the goat track came round the shoulder of the terracing, and stopped, and turned round, and the whole party arrived on it behind him in a ragged knot and stood there breathing.

Below them the goat ground fell away in a long shallow bowl about three hundred strides across, all stone and burnt spring stubble and old thorn, with the black line of the slot coming down into the top of it. There was one way across that bowl at a run and it was the one they had just come along.

Kael read it in about four seconds and got a complete answer.

The answer was that this was the best ground on this side of the mountain to be killed on, and the second best to hold, and there was no third thing.

"Vel," said Severin.

"Yes."

"The lip under us. When they come off it."

"Yes."

"Sedrane. How much have you got."

Elarine Sedrane was bent over with her hands on her knees. She came up off them and looked down into the bowl for a while, and Kael watched her.

"How much do you need."

"All of that ground."

"Then you will have it once," said Elarine. "After that I am carrying nothing."

"That's plenty."

She went to the front of the shelf and began pushing her sleeves back off her hands.

"There has been no rain since the storm-weeks," she said, to nobody in particular. "There is thorn all through the bottom of it and nobody has turned that stubble in ten years. It will take everywhere at once."

"And it will still only be a fire," said Neris, from behind her, "until I get to it."

Everybody on that shelf turned round.

She had her hand inside the front of her coat, taking the child out of it.

---

Kael watched his mother hand his brother to a princess of the empire.

She did it the way she did things, which was quickly and without any ceremony in it: she got him out through the front of the coat with one hand under the head, turned, and put him against Aeliana's chest before the girl had finished understanding what was being walked toward her.

"Both hands," his mother said. "Under the head. Do not hold him tight, hold him *close*, they are different. If he goes stiff and arches, that is wind and it is nothing. Sit down on the stone. Do not stand up with him."

"I do not—"

"You can, you will," said Neris. "You will be extremely good at this."

She took the girl's elbow and moved it about an inch, and looked at the result, and let go.

Then she walked to the front of the shelf.

Kael went after her. He got four strides and Severin's hand came onto his chest.

"No."

"She's—"

"I know what she is." The old man did not look at him. "Stand there."

---

Below them, the front of the column came out of the bottom of the slot and onto the goat ground and spread itself out along the foot of it, and stopped, and went on arriving behind itself for a long time.

They were four hundred strides out. They spoke to each other and the wind brought some of it up.

*Nothing on the flat.*

*They are on the shoulder. Up there. Look at the shape of it.*

*Let's go.*

Kael understood every word, and it did nothing for him, and he had stopped expecting it to.

Valeria Vel walked down off the front of the shelf about ten strides and stood on the lip where the goat track came round.

She did it one-armed. Kael watched her set her feet — the right one forward and turned out, the left one back and flat, which is not a fighting stance, which is a stance for putting something into the ground — and then he watched her go down into the stone.

For about two seconds nothing happened.

Then the lip of that terrace stood up.

It came up in three columns the thickness of a man's chest, out of the made ground the terrace wall was holding back, and it came up fast and rough and it went through the wall on the way. The first one took the leading man on the underside of the jaw and put him about twenty feet into the air. The second and third came up under the two behind him and did not throw them anywhere: they simply arrived, and the men were where the columns were going, and afterwards the men were part of the arrangement.

The whole face of that terrace slumped forward into the bowl with the wall gone out of it. Nobody was going to walk up it now.

The other six went sideways along the bottom, fast.

Valeria came back up onto the shelf, and she was grey around the mouth, and she stood with her right hand on her knee for a moment before she straightened.

"That is one," she said. "I have perhaps two more of those in me and then I am just blades."

"Sedrane," said Severin.

Elarine Sedrane put both hands out over the bowl with her fingers spread, and Kael, eight feet behind her, understood about half a second too late that he should have been watching the ground and not the girl.

Nothing came off her hands. Nothing crossed the air.

What happened was that the bottom of that bowl caught.

Not in one place. Three hundred strides of it, corner to corner, in the same instant: every stand of dead thorn, every foot of unturned stubble, the grass in the cracks of the old terrace wall, the roots under the lip, the dry stuff banked in the gullies where the winter had put it. It did not spread from anything. There was no first flame anywhere for a second flame to have come off. In half a second there were four hundred separate fires down there and every one of them was exactly where the fuel was.

The light came up onto the shelf and took eight people's shadows and threw them back up the mountain.

Two days ago Kael had stood on white sand and watched this girl open the whole of her treasury in one working, and what she had made with it was one enormous thing.

This was the other half of her and it was worse. She had gone through three hundred strides of black hillside item by item — in the dark, off a shelf, in about ninety seconds — and found every single thing in it that was willing to burn, and then lit all of them at once, and not one grain of what she spent had gone anywhere that did not want it.

The heat arrived four seconds later and he put his forearm up across his face.

"That is the whole of it," said Elarine Sedrane, and took her hands down.

Then she sat on the stone with her back against it and her palms turned up in her lap, and did not get up again for a long time.

And Kael's mother put both hands out in front of her, palms down, and gave that fire a wind.

---

He had watched a great deal of the Art by then.

He had watched a boy from a capital house put an air column through the middle of a bout and a girl turn sand into glass, and he had stood eleven feet from the First Master of the Temple while the man caught a burning stone out of the sky over four hundred children, and eleven hours ago he had seen his father hold lightning in the whole of his body for an hour and forty minutes without once letting it go thin.

He had never seen anything like what his mother did to that bowl.

It came off her flat and it came off her *level*, out of both hands and out of the whole front of her at once, and the first thing it did was take the ground bald for eighty strides in a line as clean as a scythe cut.

The second thing it did was pick up Elarine Sedrane's four hundred fires and put them together.

They came off the ground sideways, all of them, at the same moment, and stopped being four hundred things. What stood up out of the bottom of that bowl was one thing eight feet high and two hundred strides wide.

There were nine old olive trees on the terrace under the shelf, planted by somebody dead in the reign of somebody else dead, thick through the trunk as a man is thick through the waist, and his mother turned her hands about fifteen degrees and put the wind across them.

The first three came apart.

Not uprooted. Not blown over. They came apart along the grain from the top down, in long white splitting strips, and the strips went out into the bowl flat and level and did not slow down, and where they went into the fire they came out of the other side of it still going.

There were a hundred men and more in the bottom of that bowl by then, strung out along it, looking for the next way up.

Kael watched a piece of olive the length of a cart shaft go through four of them and not change direction. He watched the front of them stop being a front. Some were taken off their feet and some were not, and the ones who were not were the worse to look at, because a strip of that wood at that speed does not knock a man down: it goes through the part of him it meets and leaves the rest of him standing there for a moment, deciding.

The ones behind went flat and stayed flat while a hundred and fifty years of somebody's orchard went over the top of them in pieces.

And then his mother stopped using the mountain and did it herself.

She brought her hands in and turned them over.

There was no gust. Nothing on that shelf moved, and nothing came across Kael's face, and there was no roar going away from them — it was quieter than the olives had been by a long way. From where he stood it looked like a field going over under a wind that was not touching one other thing in the world.

Where it went across them, men came apart.

Not down. Not backwards. Apart, at the places a body has, which were the places his mother had laid a hare open on her own table and named for him when he was eight: the neck, the shoulder, the hip, the two joints of the arm. A man ten strides ahead of the fire turned to run and his head came off him on the turn and went out into the burning stubble, and the rest of him did four more strides before it found out. Two more went through at the waist in the same instant, and the top halves went one way and the legs stayed standing where they were and then folded. Something crossed the trunk of the nearest olive and took an arm off at the shoulder on the way past, and the arm hit the tree, and stayed on it.

The blood did not spray. It went out flat, in sheets, and where it went into the fire it came off the fire as steam, and where it went into the trees it went up those nine trunks past where Kael could have reached and stopped in a line, and the burnt ground underneath took the rest of it and went black and shining.

It lasted about nine seconds.

Then his mother closed her hands and it stopped, and the noise came up out of the bottom of that bowl and arrived on the shelf.

Kael had heard men die that morning at a gate and that afternoon in a lane and that night in his own yard, and he had heard thirty thousand people on a hillside from a mile away, and none of it had prepared him for one single second of this.

It had no shape. There were no words anywhere in it. It came up off two hundred strides of burning ground all at once and it did not stop when the wind stopped, because a great many of the people making it were not dead and were not going to be for some while yet, and a man in that condition does not go quiet, he goes on. Some of them were calling other men by name, over and over, the way you call across a field. One of them was screaming for his mother, down in the burning, in a language Kael had been taught on a flat rock above the pens by the woman standing eight feet in front of him.

The bowl went on burning from one side of it to the other, and the men in it who could still get up did not get up, because there was nowhere down there to get up into.

Kael stood on the shelf and did not have one single thing anywhere in him to put on what he had just watched.

He tried, because that was what he was, and it came back with nothing. He had a unit for a column of air, because he had been hit by one. He had a unit for a fan of fire, because he had put a blade through the middle of one and taken the skin off his hand doing it. He had no unit at all for eighty strides of ground going bald in one movement, or for what an olive trunk does when it is asked to be a different shape at that speed.

He looked at the back of his mother's head.

She was standing at the front of the shelf with her hands down at her sides and her shoulders going, breathing in a way that frightened him more than the bowl had. The white in her hair caught the light from the fire below and it went all the way along the two places it had been going all night and it did not, as far as he could tell, go any further.

She did not turn round.

He waited for her to. Everybody on that shelf waited for her to. She stood at the front of it with her back to eight people and the whole of that bowl burning in front of her, and she took as long as she took, and nobody said her name.

When she did turn round she was his mother.

She looked at him for a moment across the shelf. Then she went to the princess and took her son back out of her arms and said something to her that Kael did not hear, and the girl said something back.

"Move," said Severin.

---

They moved.

It bought them eleven minutes. Kael knew it was eleven minutes because he counted from his mother closing her hands to the first order shouted in the dark behind them, which is a different sound from the one that had been going on the whole time and much worse to hear, and eleven minutes was what four Resonants and a burning hillside had been worth.

His mother had emptied a bowl of everything standing in it. Elarine Sedrane had emptied herself. Valeria Vel had taken the front off a terrace. And on the other side of that fire the number had not meaningfully changed, because the number had never been the hundred in the bowl; it was whatever was left of the four hundred who had come up his father's path, and they were all still on this mountain, and they were all still walking this way.

The forty for the others had become twenty-five. The rest were coming round the head of the slot on the old terrace and they had not been slowed by any of it, and they would be onto this ground before the runnel.

He gave Severin the number without being asked for it.

"Twenty-five minutes to the runnel and they will be on the top of it in nineteen."

"I have it."

"Then you know we do not get eight people down it single file."

"I know."

"Master—"

"Boy," said Severin, and there was nothing in it, "I have known that since the yard."

They ran.

The old man took them off the goat track at a place with nothing to distinguish it and down a fold Kael would not have called a fold, and then the ground did the thing he had said it would do in a yard six hours ago, which was that it went *down* before it went up, into a black cut in the hillside that felt exactly like walking into a wall.

Halfway along the bottom of it Severin stopped and put his hand on the rock.

"Here," he said.

Kael looked at where they were standing and understood what the old man was going to do about nine-tenths of a second before he did it, and even so it was not enough.

Severin knelt down on the floor of the cut and put both palms flat on it, and the roots came up.

They came up out of the walls on both sides and out of the floor between them: olive root and thorn root and the fine grey stuff that lives in a crack and holds a whole hillside together, and it came up through the stone the way worms come up through ground, in no hurry and with nothing able to be done about it. It went across the mouth of the cut and it went on going. It laced. It thickened at the crossings and put out fresh growth on the far side of itself, pale in the dark and wet-looking, with three-inch thorns standing off it in every direction.

In about thirty seconds the way they had just come was a wall of live wood eleven feet deep.

Then the old man moved his hands and a dozen of the standing pieces went out of the wall and through the gap on the far side of it.

There was a sound out there. It was short.

"That is not a wall," said Severin, getting up crooked. "That is a delay. They will burn it or they will cut it. It is green and it will take them a while."

"How long," said Valeria.

"Longer than eleven minutes." He was bleeding out of the nose again and he did not touch it. "Shorter than I would like. Go."

Kael went past the wall of it at a run with his father's sword in both hands.

He looked at it as he went. He could not help looking at it. Eleven feet of living wood driven up through solid rock in half a minute by an old man who had dropped four hundred feet of mountain an hour before that and had blood coming out of his face about it.

And Kael had, in the whole of him, a line of earth about two fingers wide that he could put down the outside of his own palm, and a bundle of lightning he could hold in a piece of steel.

He had been proud of those.

That was the thing that arrived, at a run, in the dark, and it arrived whole and it did not need any working out.

Two days ago he had stood on white sand in front of eleven thousand people and done two things at once for the first time in his life and put a girl's whole treasury on the ground with it, and he had gone up that hill afterwards with his ears hot, and eleven thousand people had made a noise for him.

He had thought that was the top of something.

He had thought — and there was no dressing this up, running down a cut in the dark with his face wet — that he was becoming one of the serious things in the world.

His mother was forty feet in front of him with her second son inside her coat and a hundred and fifty years of olive trees behind her in pieces, and she had never once, in thirteen years, done anything on that mountain but teach him arithmetic and boil water.

He had wanted to know what was on the other side of that sea since he was four years old.

It had now come over and told him. *You are nothing special.*

---

The last mile was the worst mile of it.

The ground under the runnel went from stone to broken stone to a kind of scree, and Aeliana went down on it twice more and the second time she did not get up straight away.

Elarine got to her first. Elarine was going on nothing at all by then and she got down onto both knees on the scree beside a princess of the empire and took her by the shoulders and said, "Up. Up. I am not going to be able to do it twice, so please do it now," and the princess got up.

Her hands were in a state Kael could see from fifteen feet. She had been catching herself on stone with them for four miles. She had not said one word about it.

Behind them, back up the cut, there was a light through the wall of roots and somebody was shouting instructions at somebody else about it in the downhill language, and it was not panic and it was not anger; it was a man telling three other men how to get through a hedge.

Kael's leg stopped negotiating at about the same time.

It went from a leg that hurt to a leg that did something else, and he got twenty more strides out of it and then it put him down on his hands on the scree with the sword going out of his fingers and skittering away downhill.

He got up onto one knee. He put a hand out for the sword and it was three feet further than his arm.

Then somebody had him under the arm.

His mother had come back up eleven strides of that slope to do it, with the child against her, and she got him onto his feet with a hand under his armpit and a fistful of the back of his coat, and it was not gentle at all.

"Get it," she said.

He got the sword.

"Now listen to me." She had his coat and she did not let go of it. Her face was six inches from his face and it was still wet and the front of her hair was stuck to it, and she was not out of breath, which after four miles was its own kind of information. "One mile. It is one mile from here and it is downhill the whole of it and at the bottom of it there is a boat. Do you understand me?"

"Yes."

"Say it."

"One mile."

"Kael." Her hand came off the coat and onto the side of his head, and her thumb went along under his eye and took the wet off it, once. "*I have got you. I love you.*"

He could not answer that.

"One mile," said his mother, and turned him downhill by the shoulders and pushed him into it.

---

The runnel let them out about six hundred strides above the water. Kael's body and mind were more pain than flesh. And he went on.

It came out of the hillside low and sideways and without any warning, the way Severin had said it would, and Kael went out of the mouth of it into open air and cold and the smell of weed and salt, and the ground fell away in front of him in three big shelves and at the bottom of the third one was the sea.

It was black. There was a long way of it and it went out to a horizon he could not see, and the only thing on the whole of it was the orange lying along the underside of the cloud, which followed them all the way out and did not stop where the island stopped.

He had looked at that water every day of his life from the other side of the mountain.

He had never once been down at it.

"There," said Severin.

Below them, in a cut in the rock where the third shelf came down to the water and folded in on itself, out of any line of sight from anywhere, tied fore and aft to the stone with two ropes that had been put there by somebody who intended to come back — was a hull.

It sat low and it was dark all over and it had one mast down along its own length and nothing standing anywhere on it. It looked like a piece of the rock.

Nobody cheered. Nobody said anything.

They went down the three shelves in a line without a word being said, and Aurelian carried the princess of the empire down the last of them on his good side, and Valeria came off the second shelf into the water to her knees to get her hand on the stern rope, and Elarine sat down on the stones of the cut when she reached them and did not get up for a while.

Kael came down last, behind his mother, with the sword in both hands.

He stopped on the last shelf.

The others were down at the boat. Severin was already up on it, doing something at the mast with his hands, and Valeria was at the rope, and the whole of it was going forward without him for four or five seconds.

He turned round and looked back up the mountain.

He could not see the house. It was on the other side of the shoulder and had been for hours and there had never been any question of seeing it. What he could see was the top of the island, black against a sky the colour of the inside of a furnace, and a great deal of smoke going east, and the ridge line he had known since before he could walk, with nothing on it that he recognized at all.

Somewhere up there, on trodden dirt in a yard, facing the door of his own house, was his father's head.

Kael stood on a rock above the water and did the only thing he had left, which was to be exactly accurate about it.

He had let it happen. He had been standing eleven feet away with two blades and both his legs when his father put a sword into his hands. His father had come six miles across that island in the afternoon to pick him up off the ground in somebody's yard, and had gone back to that house carrying him, and had held that path for an hour and forty minutes standing between it and his own door, and every part of that had a boy in the middle of it, and the boy was him.

Nobody had said any of that to him. He did not need anybody to. He had watched the whole of it with his own eyes and he had a machine in his head that would not stop putting things beside other things, and it had been putting them beside each other all the way down that mountain.

"Kael."

His mother was on the stones below him with one hand up.

He went down.

She took the sword out of his hands, which nobody had managed all night, and looked at it once, and handed it up to Severin without any expression on her face at all. Then she got hold of the front of his coat and walked him to the side of the hull.

"Up," she said.

He put his hands on the wet stone and the wet wood of it and got a knee over, and the whole party of them was aboard and there was very little room and everything smelled of tar and old fish, and he sat down on the boards with his back against the side of it, next to Elarine Sedrane, who had not opened her eyes since she sat down in the cut and had had to be helped over.

The stern rope came off the rock.

Then the bow rope, and Valeria Vel came over the side of the hull with the wet running off her, and the water took them, and the black shelf of rock they had been standing on began to go away.

Kael sat on the boards of a boat with his father's sword lying beside him and his brother crying somewhere behind him and his mother's hand on the top of his head, and watched the island he had never once left come off the front of the world.

It was the first time in his life he had been on the water.

*I got what I wanted.*


---

# Chapter Thirty-Five — The Range

They came out of the cut on two sweeps, and the boat made almost no sound doing it.

Kael sat where he had been put, on the boards with his back against the side, and the whole of it came up through the wood into him: the sweeps turning in their holes, the water going along the outside of the planks a hand's width behind his spine, a rope knocking somewhere forward. He had listened to the sea every night of his life. He had never once heard it from this side.

Valeria Vel had the forward sweep. She worked it with her right arm and her back and kept the left one where it had been all night, and every fourth stroke she was a beat late and every fourth stroke she made it up. Aurelian Corvane had the other. Between the two of them they had two good arms and they got a hull that took forty out of a hole in the rock and into open water without touching either wall.

Severin sat in the stern with the tiller under his forearm and his head down. In the last of the light off the island his face was the colour of the boards.

"Ship the sweeps," he said. "Boy. The mast."

Kael got up. His leg had gone somewhere else while he was sitting, and it came back badly, and he stood on it and went forward with a hand on the side.

The mast lay down the middle of the hull along its own length, with the sail furled onto it and the whole of it lashed to a pair of crutches.

They got it up. Aurelian took the foot of it against his good shoulder and swore steadily. Elarine Sedrane, who had not opened her eyes since the cut, opened them and put both hands on a rope and hauled with the others.

The sail came off the spar and filled, and the boat put its shoulder down.

The wind was coming off the top of the mountain, cold, the way it came down the runnel every night of the year, straight out to sea. Kael sat down on the boards again with his father's sword beside him and let it happen to him.

His mother was in the middle of the boat with her back against the strake and her son inside her coat. She had not moved since Severin lifted her over the side. Her eyes were open and she was watching the sail.

Aeliana sat on the boards opposite with her ruined hands turned up in her lap, looking at them.

Nobody said anything for a while. The land went slowly along on their right, black, with the top of it orange, and the smell of the burning came out over the water after them and did not stop.

Kael's body began to report.

The leg first, which he already knew about. Then the ribs. Then his hands, which he could not entirely feel, and then his throat, which was ruined, and then everything else at once, arriving with no order to it.

He put his head back on the strake.

He had been standing up since the third bell that morning, in a fog, on white sand, waiting for a girl and a judge's hand.

Then, because that was what he was, he looked back at the island.

---

There was a light.

It was small and orange and a long way up, at the mouth of the runnel, and it had not been there when they went past it. The land behind them came down to the sea in three great steps, and the runnel let out at the top of them, six hundred strides above the water.

While he was looking at it there was a second one beside it and then a third, and the three of them began to come down the rock together.

Kael sat up.

"Master."

"I see it."

"There are eleven."

Severin's head came up.

"There are eleven now," Kael said. "They are halfway down. Master, they are carrying torches down the hillside in the dark with our boat six hundred strides below them, and they do not care what we can see."

"Yes," said Severin. "They know where we are."

Valeria Vel came aft along the boards without being told to. She looked at the lights coming down the shore for about two seconds and then she looked at the sail and then at the water going past the side, and Kael watched her do the same sum he had just done and get the same answer.

"How fast does this go," she said.

"Not fast," said Severin.

"We should lay low. They still have arrows and fire."

"Yes," said Severin. "Everybody, down."

They got down. Aurelian took the princess by the shoulder and put her flat on the boards between the thwarts with a sort of enormous carefulness, and lay down beside her with his bad arm on top of him. Elarine went down where she was. Valeria did not lie down. She got onto one knee at the mast with her right hand on the wet wood of it, facing the land, and stayed there.

Kael did not lie down either.

He knelt on the boards with his forearms over the side and his chin on the strake and watched the lights come down the last of the rocks to the water, and put them in an order, and counted them again.

Nineteen.

Then the first one arrived.

---

It did not come like a thing thrown.

It stood up off the shore, all at once, wider than the boat was long, and it came out over the water without going up first and without dropping, keeping its shape the whole way, and the whole of the sea between the island and the hull went the colour of the sun.

A fireball.

Kael saw the mast's shadow lie down flat across the deck and run out over the water on the far side.

It fell short. It went into the sea about forty strides off the bow and the sea took it, and the noise it made going in was long and flat and did not stop for some while, and a wall of steam came up off the water white and went over the boat, and it was warm, and it stank.

Nobody made any sound at all.

"That was short by forty," Kael said, into the steam, to nobody. "The one after it will not be."

Then his mother was beside him.

She had come up the boards on her knees with one arm across the front of her coat. She got the child out of it with her free hand under the head, and she put him into Kael's arms and pushed both of Kael's hands where they had to go with her thumbs.

"Do not put him down," she said. "Whatever happens on this boat, you do not put him down."

"Mother, I can hel—"

"Kael. Say it."

"He'll be safe. I won't put him down."

She got up. She went forward past the mast, past Valeria, all the way to the bow of that hull, and stood up in it facing the island with her hands loose at her sides.

Kael sat back against the strake with his brother against his chest and did not see her face again.

---

The second one came off the shore twenty seconds later and it was on line.

It came at the boat the way the first one had, keeping its shape. It was going to arrive somewhere between the mast and the stern. Kael did the arithmetic without wanting it and got about four seconds.

Then it stopped being one thing.

Nothing came off his mother. Nothing crossed the air between her and it. It simply stopped travelling toward them, at the top of its come, and went sideways and down instead, all of it together, and lay along the water eighty strides out from the side of them and went out in pieces.

The light on the water lasted longer than the fire did. Kael watched it go along under the surface and die, and the steam came off the sea in a line where the thing had gone in, standing up in the cold with no wind in it to take it anywhere.

"Oh," said Aurelian Corvane, from the boards, with his face against the wood. "Oh, that is—"

"Quiet. Let the woman focus," said Elarine.

Kael got his knees under him.

He put his brother up onto his shoulder with his left hand flat on the small of the back, which was where the child had been the whole time on his mother's chest, and got his right forearm onto the strake, and looked at the island.

He found the source of it at once.

He was down at the water, out on the end of the rocks, enormous through the shoulders. There were four torches standing near him and a good deal of light on him. He was standing with his weight on his back foot and his hands down. While Kael watched, he turned his head and said something to somebody behind him. Then he turned back, and put his weight forward.

And his hands came up, and toward the boat.

Kael had the whole of it before the thing was halfway to them, because he had seen the man do it in his own yard.

"MOTHER!" Kael said. "LEFT OF THE MAST. HIGH AND SLOW. IT IS LONG — IT GOES OVER—"

The back of her head did not move.

"He goes to your left every second one! He loads the next before that one lands! Watch his feet, his feet go first!"

Nothing came back. The third one went over the top of the sail and out into the water on the far side of the boat, low and wide, and the boat rocked once as the sea underneath it decided what to do about it.

"Right of the mast!" said Kael. "Level, and it is not dropping. It is ON THE MAST—"

The fourth came apart above the mast.

He never saw what she did to it. It arrived and then it was in four or five pieces going in four or five directions. One of them came down on the water twenty feet from where Kael was kneeling, and the heat of it went across the deck like a hand. The ropes on the near side smoked. Valeria Vel came off her knee with a wet coat in her one good hand, beat them out, and went back down.

The child was screaming.

He had started somewhere in the middle of the second one and Kael had not heard him begin. He was screaming with his whole self, thin and furious and going up at the end and not stopping to breathe when a child that size ought to have to, with his bound face turned into Kael's neck and his fists shut, and there was nothing to be done about any of it, and Kael went on calling the man's feet over the top of it.

"Short. That one is short — no. NO. It is on."

She took it. It went down into the sea beside them. The water came up over the side and across the boards and put out everything that was burning on that side of the boat, and Elarine Sedrane, flat on the deck, got a mouthful of it and choked and did not move.

"Left, low, fast," said Kael. "Left again behind it, higher, slower. The fast one arrives first."

He had begun to hear his mother's breathing between the throws, the way a person breathes when the breath itself is a thing that has to be decided on and got through, and it was coming up from a long way down, and it was slower than it should have been.

Then the wind came off the land in a long push and the sail went hard, and the boat leaned and began to go, properly, for the first time.

"We need to hold. Just a bit further, and they won't hit us," said Severin.

---

Then, a woman came down onto the water's edge on the last of the rock of the shoreline.

Kael saw her.

She came down the last of the rocks alone, quickly, with her sleeves pushed back, and she stepped out onto the flat stone at the very edge of the water, which was the nearest piece of the island to the boat, and she stopped.

A line of fire about the thickness of a finger came out of her right hand and lay itself down along the rock behind her, and did not spread, and did not go out.

Kael's hands went cold on his brother's back.

"Mother." It came out of him wrong and there was nothing he could do about that. "The woman on the low rock. She does not put things out. She goes across them—"

"I see her."

It was the first thing his mother had said since the bow.

"Keep counting."

Then the enormous one stopped taking turns.

The seventh and the eighth came off the rock together and they were not the same shape as each other. One stood up wide and flat and kept its shape the way every one before it had. The other came low and fast with its belly in the water, throwing a wall of steam up behind it the whole way across, and it was still climbing when it arrived.

"Two of them! Left of the mast, high and slow — and one off the front, low and fast, the low one gets here first, THE LOW ONE FIRST—"

His mother took them both.

She took the flat one first and put it down into the sea on their left. Then she took the low one out of the air with her hands going the other way, and it broke across the front of the boat and went over them in pieces, and every piece of it that came down came down in the water and not on the deck.

"Nine, left, standing up and turning — it will not go where it is pointed—"

Nine was a column. It came off the rock standing on its end, turning, taller than the mast and thinner than the mast, and it did not travel like the others; it walked.

"Ten, right and high. Eleven under it, low. Twelve — twelve is behind both of them and I have not got it—"

The sea on both sides of the hull was going up.

That was the thing he could not stop looking at afterwards, when there was time to have looked at anything. Not the fire. The water. Every one of them his mother put down went into the sea within thirty strides of the boat, and every one of them took a piece of the sea up with it when it went, and the steam stood along both sides of that hull in walls higher than the mast, and the light came through the walls from underneath and moved, so that eight people lay on the boards of a boat inside a white room with the walls of it going up and going out and going up again.

"Fourteen, left, fast. Fifteen, left. Two more behind fifteen—"

He had lost thirteen. He did not know where thirteen had gone.

He had begun the night giving her a side, a height, a speed, and a word about what the thing was going to do when it got there. Somewhere before fifteen he stopped being able to afford the last of those. Then he stopped being able to afford the one before it.

His voice had stopped being a voice with sentences in it and had become the noise a man makes counting a herd through a gate, flat and fast and without any breath under it, and he could hear himself doing it and could not have stopped if the boat had depended on it, which it did.

The child was screaming against his neck. The old man was shouting something in the stern that had the word *down* in it. Aurelian Corvane had both hands over the back of the princess's head and was talking to the boards. None of it went anywhere near the part of Kael that was working.

"Left. Left. Right and low — LOW—"

"Front of the mast. TWO — no, three—"

"TWENTY. Twenty, and there is another one—"

His mother stood in the bow with her arms going and the whole of the shore coming at her, and she was not choosing any more. The choosing had been in the first six, when there had been a second between them and a decision to make about what to do with it. Now she was simply taking whatever arrived, in the order it arrived, with whatever it was that made her stop these.

Twenty-two. Twenty-three, two together. Twenty-six.

He stopped saying them out loud somewhere in there, because he was telling her about things that had already been and gone, and she had answered them without him, and a number is only worth anything if it arrives before the thing does.

The count did not stop when his mouth did. It never stopped. It went on running behind his eyes at the speed the shore was going, and it gave him thirty, and it gave him thirty-four, and then it gave him a number he did not believe and gave it to him again.

And down on the flat stone at the very edge of the water, eighty strides off, the woman with the line put her arm out sideways and drew it across.

There was no noise. Whatever it was his mother had up in front of the boat folded along the line the woman had drawn, from the top down, in two pieces, and the two pieces came off their own weight and went into the sea and were gone.

The white room went out.

The dark came back down on that boat so fast that Kael's eyes gave him nothing at all for a second and a half.

Which was exactly as long as it took the next one to arrive.

It came in whole, on line, at the height of the mast.

His mother got a hand up.

Most of it went past the boat. Kael felt the whole front of himself scorch through his coat, and he got his shoulder round so that the child was behind him and put his face down, and something enormous went over the top of the hull with a sound like a wave arriving in a cave.

The rest of it took the sail.

The canvas went all at once, top to bottom, with a soft heavy noise, and it stopped being a sail and became a thing standing up out of the middle of a boat that was made of fire. The light of it was yellow and moved on everything. Then the mast went — not down, not at first: it burned along its own length up the whole way, and the ropes on the left of it let go one after another and whipped down across the deck, and one of them came across the boards a hand from Aeliana's head and she did not know it.

"OFF," Severin shouted. "GET IT OFF THE BOAT—"

They got it off the boat.

Valeria Vel went up onto the thwart with the wet coat and got the burning sail down off the spar and Aurelian Corvane took the bottom of it one-handed and the two of them walked it over the side into the water while it was still going, and the sea put it out with a noise like an indrawn breath and it went away behind them, black and steaming.

The mast came down about four seconds later. It came down across the left side of the boat and the top third of it went into the water and it hung there, burning in three places, held on by the ropes that had not gone yet, and it began to pull the boat's head round.

"Cut it," said Severin. "CUT IT NOW!"

Elarine Sedrane got up off the boards, gripped her sword with all she had, and did it.

The boat came upright.

It sat down on the water and stopped doing anything at all.

Kael had his brother against his chest and did not know what number he was on.

---

"Down," said Severin. "All of you. Down and stay down."

They went down.

Kael lay on the boards on his side with his brother between his chest and the strake. The boat went along on its own weight, slowing, with no sail on it and no mast standing and the wind going straight over the top of everything.

Then another one came off the shore.

It fell short.

He heard it go into the water and he heard how far away that was without needing to look, and he lay there and did the sum anyway, because there was nothing else to do with any part of him.

"Sixty," he said. "That was sixty short."

The one after it was shorter than that.

He lifted his head at the one that never came, and looked back over the strake at the island, and found that the whole of that shore had got smaller and that the light on it had begun to look like something a long way off.

There were nineteen of them standing on that rock.

The enormous one had stopped throwing. He had his hands down and he was looking out at the water, and even from here the shoulders on him were a shape Kael was not going to lose. The woman had put her line out. She had crouched down at the water's edge and was doing something to her own hand with the other hand.

And behind them both, further up, where the light was thinner, a small neat man stood on his own with his hands behind his back and did not move.

Kael lay along the strake of a boat with his brother screaming against his collarbone, and watched the man who had killed his father get smaller.

Then the rock went behind the shoulder of the island and took the light with it, and there was nothing on the water behind them at all.

His brother had finally calmed. Almost as if this day-old baby knew that he was safe.

Then, silence.

For what felt like an eternity of chaos and screaming and crying.

Silence, finally, arrived.



---

# Chapter Thirty-Six — The Quiet

The wind went out an hour before first light.

Kael felt it go. He was lying on his side on the boards with his brother inside his coat, and he had not slept, and he felt the hull stop leaning and come level and sit down on the water and stay there.

He waited for it to come back. It had come down off that mountain every night of his life and it had never once stopped before the sun did.

It did not come back.

"That's the land breeze finished," said Severin, from the stern. "We will not have another until tonight."

"Is that bad."

"It is what it is."

Kael lay still and listened to the boat instead. Without the wind in it the hull had a great many small sounds that had been underneath the other noise all night: water knocking along the outside of the planks, something wooden turning over somewhere forward, a rope working against wood with nobody touching it. And people breathing. Seven of them, at seven different speeds, in a space about the size of the yard at home.

Eight, with his brother.

He put his hand flat on the front of his coat and felt the small ribs go up and down under it, quick and shallow, the way they had been going since he had been handed the child on a burning boat and told not to put him down.

Then the sky went grey, and he could see the boat.

It was worse in the light.

There was water over the boards, a hand deep at the low end, going back and forth with the roll. Three of the strakes on the left side were burned through in patches and one had a hole in it the size of a fist that Severin had stuffed with somebody's coat. Where the mast had stood there was a stub about as high as Kael's knee, black and split and still smelling. The rope ends lay about the deck where they had been cut, and every one of them ended in char.

Aurelian Corvane was asleep sitting up against the side with his mouth open and his bad arm across his body. Elarine Sedrane was asleep against Aurelian, which was not something either of them would have arranged. Aeliana lay on her back on the boards with her ruined hands turned up on either side of her, and she was not asleep, and she did not move.

Valeria Vel was not asleep. She was sitting on the thwart nearest the princess with her blades on and her eyes open, and Kael would have bet a great deal that she had been sitting exactly like that since the shore went behind the shoulder of the island.

And his mother was in the middle of the boat with her back against the strake and her knees up.

The light came onto her face and then onto her hair, and Kael, who had been looking at her for a while without anything happening in his head at all, saw the quartz white her hair and mixed into.

---

"Boy."

He got up. His leg had set overnight into something that took about eight strides to argue out of.

"There is water in the boat and it needs to come out," said Severin. "There is a scoop under the after thwart. When you have got it down to the boards, tell me, and I will show you where it is getting in."

"Yes, Master."

It was the best hour of that day.

It was a stupid job and it did not stop, because the sea came in as fast as he took it out for the first while, and then slower, and then he was ahead of it. The scoop was a cut-down wooden pail with a handle burnt onto it. He knelt in the water with his knees going numb and worked the low end of the boat and did not think about one single thing.

Aurelian woke up and watched him for a minute and then came and did it with him, one-handed, badly, and neither of them said anything about the arm and neither of them said anything at all.

When Kael got down to the wet boards, Severin came forward on his hands and knees and put a thumb on the two places the water was coming in, and showed him how to plug one with tallow and rag, and how the other could not be plugged and would have to be lived with.

"That one is above the water while we sit level and under it when we do not," he said. "So keep us level. That is the whole of it."

"How do I keep us level."

"You put the people where I tell you and you leave them there."

The old man's hands were shaking. Not much. 

Nobody said anything about that either.

---

There was a box under the stern thwart with a lid that fitted.

Severin got it open about the middle of the morning, and everybody who had been trying to be asleep stopped.

There were two small casks of water. A stone jar of oil, a bag of hard biscuit, a smaller bag with about four pounds of dried figs in it, a coil of line, a fish hook wrapped in cloth. And one loaf.

Severin took the loaf out and looked at it and put it on the thwart.

"That is a day old," he said. "It will not be worth eating tomorrow. So it is eaten now."

He broke it with his hands and passed it down the boat, and it went from hand to hand, and every person aboard took a piece off it and sent the rest along.

Kael got his and put it in his mouth and stopped.

"How much water is there," said Valeria.

"Two casks."

"How much is that."

Severin looked at the casks. Then he looked at Kael, and did not say anything, because he did not have to.

Kael put his hand on the nearer one and rocked it, and listened to it, and did it again.

"They are both full," he said. "Call it eleven gallons together. Eight people. In this weather a grown man wants three pints a day and will live badly on two, and my mother needs more than that, because she is feeding him." He stopped. He had been going to say the next part in the same voice as the rest of it. "Nine days. Ten, if nobody is stupid. If it rains it is a different number and I do not know what number."

Nobody said anything.

"Thank you," said Severin.

"That is not a good number, Master."

"No," said Severin. "It is not." He put the lid back onto the box and sat with his hand flat on it. "It was not stocked for eight."

Nobody asked him what it had been stocked for.

And that was all anybody said about it, for the rest of that day and the whole of the night after it.

---

Aurelian started talking again a little before noon, and Kael only noticed because of the silence that had come before it.

"—so it is south from here, or it is nothing," Aurelian said, to the sky. "You would go south and then east along the shoulder of the continent. There is no other road. You cannot go north. North is a month of nothing and then it is theirs."

"You have never been on a boat in your life," said Elarine.

"I have been on a great many boats."

"You have been carried onto a great many boats and then sat down in the middle of them by somebody who was paid to do it."

"That is true," said Aurelian, "and it does not make the map different."

Kael came and sat down against the side near them, because there was nowhere on that boat to go, and because he wanted to.

Aurelian looked at him for a moment. Then he went on.

"The princess has to reach the capital," he said. "That is it. Whatever else happens, and however it happens, she reaches the capital, and every one of us on this boat is part of how she does it now. That is not a duty anybody here was given. It is one that arrived." He was not talking to the sky any more. "My father's men were on the eastern stretch of that wall. Sedrane's were on the north with hers. There were seven houses' colours on that wall and I know what five of them are worth in a fight, and I have not stopped doing that sum since the middle of last night. That is what I have instead of knowing."

"Aurelian," said Elarine.

"I am all right."

"You are not, and I did not say you were. I said your name."

He shut his mouth and sat with it.

Kael did not know what to do with his hands, so he did what his mother did, which was to ask about the practical thing.

"Your house is not on the island," he said.

"No. My house is at the capital, and my house is at Corvane, and there is a good deal of my house in the east where it should not be." Aurelian's mouth did something. "Two of us were on that island. My father, and me. Twenty-nine people carry that name and two of them were there, and I have spent four years being extremely tiresome about the other twenty-seven, and this morning it turns out they are all perfectly safe and it is not the smallest comfort to me."

"Sedrane is four hundred miles from any of this," said Elarine, without being asked. "My grandfather is alive. Two of my uncles are alive. The seat is alive. My house will go on with me."

She said it the way she said everything, as though she had already been over it twice and found it correct.

"Only my mother was there," she said.

Kael waited.

"She said a thing to me at the far gate. I have been holding on to it since about the third hour of that morning." Elarine turned her hands over in her lap and looked at them. "And I have worked out that she said it because she knew she was not going to see me again. Which means the thing itself was true, and the reason it was said out loud is that she was dying. So I do not know what to do with it. I have never once not known what to do with a piece of information."

"They are probably dead," said Aurelian.

"Yes."

"Both of them."

"Yes."

"Then say it, and let us have it said."

"They are probably dead," said Elarine Sedrane.

Kael sat with the two of them and did not offer them anything, because there was nothing to offer, and because the one thing he had that they did not was certainty, and it was not the better thing to have. He had watched his. These two would go a month or a year not knowing, and would build something on the not knowing, and part of him wanted to tell them exactly how much he would have given to trade.

He did not say that either.

What he said was: "My father is dead."

"I know," said Elarine Sedrane. "I am sorry."

"I am as well," said Aurelian. "I am extremely sorry. I have been trying since about the middle of the morning to find a way of saying that which would be of any use to anybody, and there is not one, so I am saying it badly and you will have to take it as it comes."

Then he moved along the boards until his shoulder was against Kael's shoulder, and left it there, and did not say anything else, which for him was an enormous undertaking.

Elarine put her hand flat on Kael's back for a moment and took it away again.

Kael did not know where to put any of that, so he sat still and let it happen to him, and found that he was glad — flatly and simply, with nothing in the way of it — that the two of them were alive and on this boat.

---

"You are talking about the capital," said Aeliana.

She had come up onto one elbow. Valeria came off the thwart and onto her feet in the same movement, without hurrying, and stood where she had been standing since the cut.

"Your Serene Highness," said Aurelian, and got most of the way up before he remembered the boat and sat down again.

"Do not do that. There is nowhere for you to do it." Aeliana pushed herself the rest of the way up and put her back against the side. Her hair had come down in the night and salted into ropes, and her hem had been through six miles of goat ground, and none of it made the smallest difference to what happened on that boat when she spoke, which was that every person on it stopped and turned. "You are right. We must go to the capital."

"Yes, Highness."

"I have been thinking about who is to be told what, and in what order, because my father will want it in an order." She said this as another person would say they had been thinking about the weather. "You will carry your own houses' news. I shall carry my guard's."

"Yes, Highness."

"They will be spoken for properly. My guard, your houses. I want you to know that. It will be done at court with their houses standing, and their names will be read." Her chin came up a little. "My guard did what my guard is for. Twelve of them, and the captain last, and there is nothing to be said about that except that it was correct, and it would be an insult to them to say more."

"Yes," said Valeria Vel.

"It is the same for the rest," said Aeliana. "The men on that wall. The Temple's children. Everyone who was lost getting me off that island was lost getting me off that island, and I am not going to pretend it was some other thing, or be delicate about it, because that would make it smaller than it was."

"They came for you," Kael said.

He had not decided to say it. It came out in the voice he used for a number.

Valeria Vel did not move.

"I should think so," said Aeliana. "There is nothing else on that island worth a fleet coming for."

"There were eleven thousand people in that bowl and they started putting stones into it before the bell had finished ringing." He was going faster. He could hear himself doing it and could not stop. "There is a boy of about six lying on the village road who pointed at me two days ago with his whole arm. There were thirty thousand people standing outside that wall and I did the sum on it four times looking for a way that any of them lived, and there was not one, and then I went east and listened to them stop. And then they came up my father's path, four hundred of them, all night, because you were standing in his yard." His hands had gone cold on his knees. "He put himself between them and his own door and he stayed there until they took his head off, and I watched every second of it. And you are not worth it."

Nobody on that boat said anything at all.

Aeliana did not answer him. She sat with her back against the side of the hull and looked at the water going along it, and she looked at it long enough that Elarine Sedrane began examining her own hands and Aurelian Corvane found something to do with the sky.

"The big one," she said, at last. "With the sword."

"Yes."

"Then he did well," said Aeliana Cassivara Vessantine, kindly, and she meant it entirely. "You should be proud of him. I shall tell my father about him when we reach the capital. I shall say all of their names, and I shall say his, and I shall have it written down."

Kael's body went before he did.

He was not aware of standing up. He got about a stride and a half and his arm was already going, and there was nothing whatever in his head — no plan, no anger he could have named, nothing that had produced any of it — and then Valeria Vel was in front of him and she had his wrist.

She had not appeared to move quickly. She had simply arrived, the way she arrived, and her hand closed and stopped, and the arm stopped with it, and Kael found out in about a quarter of a second that there was nothing he could do about that at all. She was standing closer to him than she had ever stood. Half her face was burned red and shining down one side, and her left arm was still carried in against her ribs, and she had done it with the other one.

She did not let go.

"Kael, stop it. Please," said Valeria.

He pushed.

"Kael," said his mother.

That was all she said. She said it from the middle of the boat, in the voice she had used across the yard when he was small and going near the block, and everything in him came down out of wherever it had gone.

Valeria opened her hand.

He sat down on the boards.

"What is the matter with him?" said Aeliana.

Kael was looking at the wood. He heard Valeria Vel put her feet back where they belonged, which was on the princess's left, at the distance she kept.

"He is tired, Highness," she said. "We are all tired."

"Yes," said Aeliana. "It has been a very great deal."

And that was the end of it, and nobody on that boat ever said one word to him about it, then or afterwards.

---

Later, with the sun off the top of the day and the water lying like a floor, Kael went aft and sat down near the tiller, because it was the only place on that hull nobody else was.

Severin had the tiller under his forearm out of habit. There was nothing for it to do. The boat lay where it lay and turned very slowly all day, so that the island went round them by degrees, like something on a table.

"What is this called," Kael said, and put his hand on the piece of wood he was leaning against.

Severin opened his eyes.

"That is a strake. All of them are strakes. That is the top one, so if you want to be understood you call it the gunwale." He watched Kael take it. "The cross-pieces you have been sitting on are thwarts. The bench at the back there is the stern sheets, which is not a sheet at all; a sheet is a rope. The front is the bow and the back is the stern, and if you say front and back nobody will hang you, but nobody will hurry either."

"And left and right."

"Larboard and starboard. They do not swap round when you turn about, which is the entire reason for having them." The old man's mouth moved very slightly. "Which side is starboard?"

Kael thought about it and got it right.

"Good," said Severin. "Tomorrow I'll teach you some more."

He gave him about twenty words in an hour, and made him say each one back, and then made him say them back in a different order, and then sent him forward to touch each thing and name it out loud to nobody, which Kael did, in front of six people, without feeling anything about it whatsoever.

He came back and sat down.

"That is a small language," Kael said.

"It is."

"There is one word for each thing and it only does that thing."

"Yes."

"Why is it like that?"

"Because a man shouts it once, in the dark, in weather, and the boat lives or it does not." Severin shifted his shoulder against the stern, and something in him hurt when he did. "There is nothing clever in any of it. There has never been one clever word in a boat. It is hundreds of years of people finding out what has to be said quickly, and then never changing it again."

Kael turned that over.

"My mother would like that."

"Your mother," said Severin, "would have had the whole of it off me by supper, and would have found two of the words redundant, and would have been right, and I should have gone on using them anyway."

Kael laughed.

It came out of him before he knew it was in there, one note, ugly, and it stopped as fast as it started, and then he sat with his hands over his face for a moment and the old man let him.

"Master."

"Mm."

"How many of the students got out?"

Severin was a long time answering.

"There were four hundred and six children in my Temple on the morning of the third bell. I walked as many of them as I could find toward the northern arch, and I did not look up while I did it, because if I had looked up I would have stopped walking. Ninety-one of them were standing in a courtyard in that district by the evening. That is the number I know. There will be others. There were boats that morning, and there were households that took children in, and there was a great deal of that island I could not see." He looked out at the water. "I do not know how many live. I have stopped doing that sum at night, because it does not have an end. I would recommend you do the same with yours, and I know perfectly well that you will not."

"No."

"No."

Then Severin reached under the stern sheets and brought out the sword.

It had been lying there all night wrapped in a piece of the burned sail. He took the cloth off it and held it across both hands, and did not hand it over at once, and Kael understood that he was not being made to wait for anything, only that an old man's hands were slow.

"This is yours," said Severin. "It was put into your hands, and I took it off you, because you had been holding it four hours and your fingers had stopped working. You will not use it for a year and possibly two. It is far too much sword for you."

"I know."

"Then keep it where it will not roll."

Kael took it. He laid it along the boards beside him with the grip toward him, the way his father had shown him with everything sharp for as long as he could remember, and put one hand flat on it, and after a while he stopped noticing he was doing it.

---

His mother had the child out when he came forward.

She had her back against the strake and her knees up, and Wren lay along her thighs with the linen still across his eyes, and she had one hand under his head, and she was doing nothing else. She had been doing nothing else for a long time.

Kael sat down beside her.

"What have you got in your coat," she said.

He went through it. His father's knife, which had come off a wall in a room that no longer existed and had been in that coat since a morning on the other side of the world. A length of cord. A flat grey stone about the size of a coin, which he had no memory of picking up and must have picked up somewhere between the arena and the water.

He put the three of them on the boards between them.

His mother looked at them for a while.

"I packed two bundles for this travel," she said. "I roped them and stood them by the door on the morning the games opened, and they stood there for three days. The books were in one. In the other there was salt, and needles, and every seed I have kept for eleven years." She turned her hand over. "Not one thing out of them is on this boat."

Kael did not say anything.

"That is what I have," said his mother, flatly and evenly, with no grief anywhere in the voice, in the tone she used for the result of a trial. "You have a knife, a bit of cord and a stone. And I have you, and I have him. That is the inventory."

Then she stopped, and did not add anything to it.

After a while she said, "Do you want to hold him?"

"Can I?"

"Sit back. Put your arm along the top of your leg. No — flat. Like that."

She put the child into the space he made.

He weighed almost nothing and he was extremely warm, which Kael had not expected either time and had forgotten in between. His head had no neck under it worth the name; the whole thing worked because Kael's hand was under it. His fists were shut up beside his face, the way they had been shut through the whole of a barrage. Under the coat and the linen he smelled of nothing Kael had a word for, warm and faintly sour and completely particular, and Kael understood that he would be able to find this in a room for the rest of his life.

His breathing was wrong. That was the first thing. It went fast, and then very fast, and then stopped for long enough to close Kael's whole chest, and then started again.

He counted it, because he could not do anything else.

He counted for four minutes and got the shape of it, and found that it repeated, and that it was not wrong at all, and that a thing this size simply breathes like that.

Then he went on counting for another ten minutes anyway.

His brother's hand came open and shut on Kael's finger and stayed shut.

He did not have much grip. He had exactly as much as a thing that has been alive for a day and a night has, which is not enough to hold anything, and he held on with it anyway, and Kael sat completely still on the boards of a drifting boat and did not take his finger back, because he did not want to be the one who did that.

The linen was still across the eyes. Kael did not touch it. Nobody on that boat had touched it, and nobody had said one word about why it was there.

"Kael," said his mother.

"He's holding my finger."

"I know," she said. "He does that."

---

Aeliana came forward in the middle of the afternoon, which took her past five people in a boat forty feet long, and stood there.

"May I hold him?"

Kael looked up.

She had asked. He turned it over twice looking for the other thing in it, and there was no other thing in it. She had been given a newborn on a hillside by a woman who told her she would be extremely good at it, and she had held him through the length of a fight and given him back, and nobody had said anything to her about it since, and she had crossed a boat to ask.

"Sit down first," said his mother.

The princess sat down on the wet boards in what was left of a gown, and put her arms where she was told to put them, and Kael leaned across and put his brother into them.

She got the head wrong, and corrected it before anybody said anything.

"There," said his mother.

Kael sat back and watched his mother watch a princess of the empire hold her son, two hours after her elder son had come up off the boards to hit that girl in the face.

His mother said nothing about that. She had said his name once, and she had never said anything else about it, and Kael understood, sitting there, that she was not going to, and that this was not the same thing as her not having seen it.

"He is warmer than I remember," said Aeliana.

"They are."

"Is he well? I do not know what a well one looks like."

"He is well."

The princess sat on the floor of the boat with her ruined hands under a herder's child and looked at him for a long time, and did not say anything, and did not appear to want anything from anybody, which was, Kael thought, the first time he had seen that happen to her.

---

She gave him back before the light went.

His mother took the child and put him inside her coat, and then she said, "Tell me the night."

Kael had never once not started.

He started. He gave her the top of the slot and the shape of the party coming down it, and the interval on the crying and the six hundred strides it carried and what he had done about that arithmetic, and the four holdings, and the forty and the sixty and the hundred and sixty, and the second column round the head of the slot. He gave her Valeria's terrace and Elarine's bowl in the right order with the right numbers on them, and his voice did what it always did in a debrief, which was go flat and get faster.

He gave her the shore, and the nineteen lights, and the first one falling forty short.

He gave her the count coming apart. He told her he had lost thirteen entirely, and that he had been at twenty with nothing behind him between fifteen and twenty, and that he had stopped saying them aloud because they were arriving after the things did, and that he did not know what number he had been on when the mast went.

"Good," said his mother. "That is the correct thing to have stopped doing."

He gave her the woman on the low rock, and what she had done, and how long the dark had lasted afterwards.

Then he went backwards, because she had said the night, and the night had a beginning, and he came up the mountain into the yard.

He got Lucan Drusane out. He got the column and the four seconds and the store wall and *did I get him* out, all of it, in order, correctly.

Then he said, "Oss stopped at the top of the slot."

"Yes."

"He put a wall up. Four feet, eleven strides, out of nothing at all, and it held four seconds. I have never seen him do anything worth anything." His voice was still going flat and fast, which was the strange part, because there was water coming down his face while it did it. "And they went round it. And I was forty feet away."

"Yes."

"I never said one word to him. Not this year. Not on any day of this year, and I saw him on both days of the games and I found him in about a second and a half both times and I did not go over, and then he was in my own yard and he got half a step and the line moved him on, and I never said one word to him, and now I am not—"

And that was where it stopped being a debrief.

He got no further than that. He sat on the boards with his hands over his face and the whole of it came up out of him at once, thirteen years old, in front of six people who all looked at the water at the same moment, and his mother pulled him in against her with one arm and put her hand on the back of his head and held it there.

She did not tell him it was not his fault.

She did not say that Oss had chosen it, or that there had been no time, or that there was nothing he could have done, or any of the other things that are true and are said to people and do not do anything.

She held on to him and let him get through it, and when he had got through it she went on holding on to him a while afterwards, and Kael, who had been carried up that mountain by his father two nights before and had been thinking about it ever since without letting himself, lay against his mother on the boards of a boat and was, for about four minutes, nothing whatever except a person's child.

The sun went down while he was there.

---

He came out of it slowly and did not move for a while.

His mother's hand was on the top of his head. Inside her coat, three inches from his ear, his brother went on breathing in his fast broken pattern with the long stops in it, and Kael counted twenty of them, and it held.

*Ask me on the water.*

She had said it with the shore coming down at the boat and nothing left in her, and she had given him the reason, and the reason had been honest: that she had nothing to spend on it, and that he would get it badly and would have it badly for ever.

They were on the water.

He had the question sitting whole and finished at the front of his mouth. He had had it there since the middle of the afternoon.

He looked up at her instead. He looked at the day-old white in the front of her hair, and at what four miles of that mountain had done to a woman who had opened on her own floor eighteen hours before she ran them, and at the way her hand had shaken very slightly when she put the child back inside her coat, and he ran the sum he had been built to run.

Then he put it down.

She had felt him take the breath. He knew she had, because her hand stopped moving on his head for about a second and then went on, and because she did not offer him anything, and because his mother had never once in her life failed to notice a thing like that.

Neither of them said one word about it.

---

The last of the light went off the water and the island came up black behind them.

It had been out there all day, going round them by degrees, never getting any further off, because there was no wind to take them anywhere and nothing under the boat but the slow crawl of the water. It sat on the horizon at about the width of two hands. The smoke had stopped being smoke and become a smear in the air with no shape to it, going east, and the orange under the cloud had gone out at some point in the afternoon without anybody watching it go.

Kael put his back against the gunwale and looked at it, and let himself want things, which he had not done since the third bell of the morning before.

He wanted his mother alive in a year. He wanted his brother alive in a year, and grown, and with the linen off. That one came first and it did not need any working out and it had nothing underneath it.

He wanted to know what his parents had been.

He had refused it twice. His father had given him three sentences in eleven years and he had taken them and not pushed; his mother had shut the door with the same words every time, and he had let her, and he had told himself both times that this was a kind of discipline. It was not, and he had known that as well. It had been comfortable. A boy who does not ask does not have to hold the answer, and Kael had been extremely comfortable for a very long time, and then men had come up his father's path who knew his father's face and had a name ready for it. There was no version of the rest of his life now in which he did not find out. He would find out from her if she ever wanted to tell him. He would find out some other way if she did not. He would be a long time about it and he did not care how long.

And he wanted to find the small neat man who had walked round the edge of his own ruin with his hands behind his back, and he wanted to kill him.

He turned that one over, because he turned everything over, and it did not change shape under the handling. It was not hot. There was nothing in it that made his chest go, the way the wanting had gone through him on that mountainside when he went up the slope with nothing in his head at all. It sat where he put it, flat and cold and extremely patient, and it was going to be there in the morning.

He did not say any of it out loud. He did not swear anything to anybody. There was nobody to swear it to and he would not have known the words.

The men who had done all of this had come across this water and burned an island, and they were the enemy, and that one Kael did not turn over at all, because there was nothing in it that needed turning over.

Behind him somebody was bailing. Somewhere forward, Aurelian Corvane had started talking again, quietly, to Elarine, about roads.

Kael sat against the side of a boat with a sword he could not lift properly lying along the boards at his feet, and watched the island go dark.

He had seen that shore come alight every night of his life. The two by the boat sheds first, always, and then the long scatter along the water, and then the ones going up the hill in no order at all as the houses got round to it, until the whole of it lay along the black in a shape he could have drawn from memory at four years old, and had.

He waited for them.

He knew before he started waiting. He waited anyway, with his eyes open, and he went on waiting a long time after the light had gone completely, and no lights came on anywhere on that island at all.


---
